There is no such thing as a true, unarguable starting line for any story we live or tell. Even the strictest, most exact science fudges the zero point on its star-calibrated clocks, picking an arbitrary spot in the endless drift of the constellations to call the first second. Poetry, that wilder, older grandmother of science, has always leapt straight into the thick of things, and it turns out both do much the same: look back far enough, and you find Science, too, splitting its arbitrary starting unit into a billion tiny fractions, and the moment you thought was zero is already halfway into the messy, lived-in thick of life. No prologue, no matter how grand, can wrap up all the unspoken history that tugs at every person we meet. So we begin right here, mid-breath, mid-rattle of the roulette wheel, with the question looping in Daniel Deronda’s chest the second his eyes landed on her: Was she beautiful? And what was that strange, thrumming pull in her gaze that made it impossible to look away? Was the fire in her glance bright, or sharp-edged, the kind that burns more than it warms? Probably sharp, he thought, when every glance back at her left him restless, not softened—when the urge to look again felt less like a gentle longing, and more like something he could not quite resist.

The woman who held all his attention was gambling, not barefoot on sun-warmed ruins in the south of France, but in one of those gilded, heavy pleasure palaces centuries of wealth had built for the exact same thrill: all gilded moldings, dark polished wood, plump marble nudes on every mantel, thick enough to trap the breath of all the high society guests inside, far away from the ordinary world outside. It was nearly four o’clock on a soft September afternoon, and the air had thickened into a visible, hazy glow, the only sounds the light rattle of the roulette ball, the chink of coins, the soft sweep of the croupier’s stick, and the flat, mechanical French patter rolling off their tongues like it came from a perfectly built automaton.

Fifty or sixty people huddled around the two long tables, every single one of them fixed on the spinning wheel save one: a sad little boy in a fancy costume, his bare calves peeking out under frills, standing right behind the woman absorbed in her game, his face turned toward the doorway with the blank, glazed stare of a child hired to stand outside a traveling show. The crowd at the table was a patchwork of every European type: Livonian and Spanish, Graeco-Italian and half a dozen kinds of German, English aristocrats and English tradespeople, all rubbing elbows the way they never would anywhere else. An English countess’ jewel-encrusted white fingers nearly brushed a bony, yellow, crab-like old hand that stretched out to clutch a pile of gold coins, attached to a gaunt square-faced woman with deep-set eyes and grizzled eyebrows who looked like she was one step away from turning into a vulture. A sleek, soft-handed London tradesman sat right beside the countess, his hair parted perfectly front and back, holidaying in high society on the back of his successful shop that counted half the nobility as clients. A statuesque handsome Italian leaned over him to set down a whole pile of napoleons fresh from a new money bag, his face so impassive you would never guess he was chasing a win, certain his foolproof system could beat chance. Even the worn-out old libertine squinting through a single monocle, his hand shaking as he asked for change, moved through the game with the same vacant, mask-like look on his face as everyone else, as if they had all eaten of some root that narrowed every thought down to the same single narrow track.

Deronda’s first idle thought was that ragged Spanish shepherd boys gambling coppers on a stone wall looked far happier than this crowd of gilded zombies—for once, Rousseau might have been right that art and progress had done the human race no favors. Then suddenly the whole room felt like it shifted into a scene from a play. His gaze landed on the young woman standing not far from him, and he could not look away. She bent to murmur a sentence in English to the middle-aged lady sitting beside her at the table, then turned back to the game, and he got the full view of her graceful, tall figure, and a face no one on earth could pass without a second glance. His scrutiny deepened, no longer the vague warm glow of casual admiration, but sharp, curious, trying to untangle what made her so unignorable. She bent to place her bet, her pale grey gloved fingers moving with quiet, sure confidence, then leaned back to watch the wheel, her face completely unguarded. She was winning, stacking the coins pushed toward her with steady fingers before sliding them right back onto her chosen spot, then glanced around the room with a cool, neutral survey that barely hid the bright spark of triumph thrumming under her skin.

Then her eyes locked straight onto Deronda’s. She meant to look away, to pretend she had not seen him, but she could not. A hot, stinging resentment flooded her chest: he was not just looking, he was weighing her, seeing past all the charm that made everyone else fall over themselves to please her, as if he existed in some higher, separate space outside the crowd here, and she was just a curious, lesser specimen to him. The color did not rise to her cheeks, but it drained all warmth from her lips. She turned back to the game, defiantly, and her stake vanished. No matter—she had been winning ever since she first sat down with a handful of napoleons, she had a big reserve, she was sure her luck could not break. She had even started imagining a little following of people who would watch her play like she was a luck goddess, predicting their wins for them.

But every stake she placed after that was swept away. Her eyes felt hot, and she did not even need to look to know that Deronda was still standing there, watching her, that his gaze was pressing down on her like a weight that was starting to hurt. The more reason not to flinch. When her chaperon touched her elbow and suggested they leave, she answered by slamming ten louis down on the exact same spot. She was past caring about winning or losing; all she cared about was defying both the bad luck she was sure his gaze had cursed her with, and him. She doubled her bet every time the croupier swept it away, no tremor in her hands, no shake to her lips.

“Faites votre jeu, mesdames et messieurs,” the croupier droned, and she stretched out her arm to put down her last poor heap of napoleons. “Le jeu ne va plus.”

Five seconds later, every last coin was gone. She turned from the table, and instead of hurrying away in shame, she turned directly to face Deronda, and stared right back at him. There was a faint, ironic smile in his eyes when their glances met, and it was far better than being ignored like she was just one tiny nameless insect in the swarm of gamblers. She did not even mind the superciliousness in his face; he was young, handsome, clearly from a good family, not one of those sour boring prudes who gave the casino a disgusted look before they hurried past. She had always taken for granted that everyone found her admirable, that she knew exactly what was worth liking in the world, and that little unshakable belief had wobbled, but it was not broken.

By evening, the same room was even more stifling, glowing bright with gaslight, full of ladies sweeping past in their ballgowns, trains trailing on the carpet. Gwendolen Harleth was the Nereid of the hour, dressed in sea-green silk dotted with silver ornaments, a pale sea-green feather tucked into the green hat on her light brown hair, falling back over her shoulders. She drifted along on the arm of her chaperon, the baroness she called her cousin, next to the stiff, white-mustached German baron, and every seated group turned to stare and whisper as she passed.
“Striking girl, that Miss Harleth. Unlike anyone else here.”
“Dressed up like a serpent tonight, all green and silver, holding her neck like she’s winding around something.”
“I swear she always does something outrageous. You think she’s pretty, Mr. Vandernoodt?”
“Very. A fool might risk hanging for her, easily.”
“It’s that Lamia, cold fairy beauty she’s got, no color in her cheeks at all.”
“Nonsense, that warm pale skin is her best feature, that little turned-up nose, that perfectly curved mouth—”
“Ugh, I hate that mouth. It looks so self-satisfied, like it knows exactly how pretty it is.”
“I think she’s odious,” huffed an old dowager. “And who even are these Langens?”
“They’re perfectly respectable. The baroness is English, you know, and Miss Harleth is the cleverest girl you’ll ever meet.”

Gwendolen held herself extra straight that night, craning her neck every chance she got to spot Deronda, still wincing a little under the memory of that measuring gaze of his. She found her chance with the famously well-connected Mr. Vandernoodt, leaning in with a lazy, clear soprano that did not sound nearly as eager as she felt. “You know everyone,” she said. “Who is that dark-haired young man by the door, the one with the dreadful expression?”
“Dreadful? He’s one of the handsomest men in the room. That’s Deronda, Mr. Deronda, here with Sir Hugo Mallinger, they say they’re very close.”
Gwendolen colored faintly. She knew Sir Hugo’s estate was not far from her own family’s home, though he never visited it. “Deronda,” she said, soft. “What a lovely name. He’s nothing like the usual boring young men I meet. I can never guess what they’ll say next, but I can’t begin to imagine what he’d say.”
“Mostly he says nothing,” Vandernoodt laughed. “I sat with his party on the terrace last night for an hour, he never spoke a single word, didn’t even smoke. Looked half bored to death.”
“All the more reason I want to meet him,” Gwendolen said. “I’m always bored.”

But Vandernoodt never managed to pull Deronda over to their group that night, and when Gwendolen finally slipped back to her private room, there was a sealed letter waiting on her dresser, summoning her home immediately, before she had ever gotten the chance to speak to the strange, dark-eyed stranger who had already burned himself into her thoughts.

This man weaves a secret between us two, that he might subdue me with the weight of his gaze, as one tames a lioness backed into a corner.

The cream envelope was propped against her pearl-handled hairbrush when Gwendolen stepped back into her sun-dappled Leubronn lodgings, still thrumming from the previous night’s whirl of waltzes and the sharp, sour sting of bad luck at the roulette table. It was her mother’s handwriting, its wobbly loops instantly familiar, and she sank onto the plush red velvet sofa by the window to tear it open, no premonition of the ruin spilling across its pages.

*My Dearest Child*, it began, *I have waited a week to hear from you. In your last letter you said the Langens were thinking of leaving Leubronn for Baden – how could you be so careless as to leave me guessing your whereabouts, my heart sick with fear this note might never reach you? You were meant to come home at the end of September, but I beg you to return as fast as you can. If you’ve spent all your allowance, I have no more money to send you. Do not borrow from the Langens, for I could never repay them. The sad, terrible truth is this: a calamity has fallen on us all. You know nothing of business, and will not understand the details, but Grapnell & Co. have failed for a million pounds, and we are utterly, irrevocably ruined. Your aunt Gascoigne and I are both stripped bare, though your uncle still has his church benefice; they can scrape by, giving up their carriage and putting the boys to work. Every last penny our poor father saved for us is gone to pay the firm’s debts. I have nothing left I can call my own. It rends my heart to tell you this, but you deserve to know it at once. Of course we all cannot help but grieve that you left for your holiday right when this disaster struck – but I will never reproach you, my love. I would shield you from every hardship if I could. You will have time on the journey home to steel yourself for what waits. We shall leave Offendene at once, we think; Mr. Haynes, who wanted to rent it before, may take it off my hands. There is no spare corner of the rectory for us to stay, so we must find some tiny cottage to shelter in, and live off your uncle’s charity until I can find some way to earn money. I cannot pay the tradesmen’s bills, or the servants’ wages. Summon your courage, my dear. We must resign ourselves to God’s will, though it is cruel to accept the recklessness of that wicked Mr. Lassman who brought this all down. Your poor sisters only weep with me, they cannot help. If you were here, perhaps the clouds would break – I have always known you were never meant for a life of poverty. If the Langens wish to stay abroad, find some other respectable lady to chaperone you on the journey. Come home as soon as you can, to your loving, broken
Mamma, Fanny Davilow.*

For long minutes after she finished reading, Gwendolen could not move. The unshakable, youthful certainty that her life would always be soft, luxurious, every inconvenience neatly swept away by money and her own charm, had defined her for as long as she could remember. It felt as impossible to accept she was now poor, dependent, humiliated, as it would have felt to imagine her own bright, blooming life ending tomorrow. She stood at last, tossed off her hat, and stared blankly at her reflection in the glass: her light brown hair was still coiled in perfect, glossy loops, fit for a royal ball. Usually she would linger to admire the curve of her cheek, the glint in her eyes, but today she did not see her own beauty at all. She only stared, as if waiting for some cruel, wrong noise to end, to wake her back to the world she had known an hour before.

She sank back onto the red sofa, read the letter twice more, and let it drift to the carpet at her feet. Her hands were clasped tight in her lap, and she shed no tears. She did not think of her mother’s grief first – Mrs. Davilow had never been the sort to savor joy, and Gwendolen had always been the apple of her eye, the one she spoiled and doted on above all others. It was hot, sharp anger that flooded her first, bitter vexation that her luck at roulette had run dry the night before. If she had won just one more big stake, she could have come home with a fortune large enough to fix all their troubles.

Wait, she thought, why not still try? She had only four napoleons left in her purse, but she had the Etruscan turquoise necklace her father had left her, an old piece she rarely wore. Pawning jewelry was common, even fashionable, for wealthy tourists at the German baths – no one would bat an eye. Thirteen louis would be enough to get her home, and if she risked three of them on the roulette table, with the luck that had always seemed to follow her, she could win enough to lift her family out of ruin entirely. The only catch was the Langens: she could not let them find out her family was ruined, could not bear their pity. She resolved to slip out at dawn, pawn the necklace, tell the Langens her mother had called her home urgently without giving details, and take the evening train to Brussels.

She did not go to bed at all. She lit every candle in the room and packed furiously, her thoughts swinging wildly between visions of arriving home with a sack full of winnings, and the unavoidable memory of the tall, dark stranger who had stood watching her at the roulette table the night before – Daniel Deronda, his gaze heavy with a quiet, unreadable irony, the same man who had been there to see her lose every last one of her big bets. The thought of him seeing her back at the tables, failing again, made her pride burn so hot it nearly settled her resolve to leave at once. By the time she had zipped her last traveling bag shut, pale dawn was seeping through the white blinds, dimming the guttering candles. She did not bother sleeping. A quick cold bath refreshed her, and the faint dark circles under her eyes only made her look more striking, more romantic. By six o’clock she was fully dressed in her soft gray traveling gown, her felt hat pinned on, ready to slip out the moment other ladies began their morning walks to the springs.

She paused sideways before the long mirror between her windows, leaning her elbow on the back of her chair in a pose fit for a portrait. Even with her whole world falling apart, she could not help the slow, soft smile that curved her lips as she stared at her own reflection. How could such a bright, lucky girl as her be made for unending sorrow? She felt strong enough to fight any hardship, to outrun it, as she had outrun every small inconvenience of her life so far.

Madame von Langen never left the house before breakfast, so Gwendolen could take the long way home past the Obere Strasse, where Mr. Wiener’s jeweler’s shop would open just after seven. The Czarina Hotel – Deronda’s hotel – loomed at the end of the street, and it was possible someone staying there might see her walk into the pawn shop. But what of it? She could say she was popping in to buy a souvenir. She walked with her usual graceful, gliding step, not looking left or right, and transacted her business quickly. Mr. Wiener, the little jeweler, fawned over the three big turquoise stones set in the old necklace, but he gave her only nine louis for it, far less than she thought it was worth. Still, thirteen louis total was more than enough to get her all the way home, and she wavered again on her way back to the Langens’ apartment, half-ready to stay one more day and try her luck.

She sank into the salon sofa, tired and hungry, eyes half shut, waiting for her friends to come down for breakfast. She expected to see one of the Langens walk through the door – but it was only the servant, bearing a small wrapped parcel for Miss Harleth, that had just been left at the door.

Gwendolen’s heart jumped to her throat. She grabbed the parcel and ran to her own room, her hands shaking. Before she even unwrapped it, she knew what it was: her turquoise necklace. It was wrapped in a fine cambric handkerchief, with a scrap of pencil-scrawled note tucked inside: *A stranger who has found Miss Harleth’s necklace returns it to her with the hope that she will not again risk the loss of it.*

Waves of hot, burning humiliation flooded her. She knew at once who the “stranger” was. Deronda must have seen her walk into the jeweler’s shop, must have gone in right after her, bought the necklace back, and sent it to her anonymously. He had dared to pry into her secret, to put her in this impossible, humiliating position. She could not send it back to him – that would mean admitting she knew it was him, admitting she had pawned her own father’s necklace to gamble. He thought he was some sort of saintly mentor, smiling down at her with that ironic, superior look, and she could not bear the thought of facing him ever again. Bitter, angry tears streamed down her face, the first tears she had shed since reading her mother’s letter. No one had ever dared to treat her like this.

There was a sharp knock at the door. Breakfast was ready. Gwendolen stuffed the necklace, the handkerchief, and the scrap of note into her vanity case, pressed her own handkerchief to her eyes to wipe the tears away, and summoned back her usual proud, steady poise before she stepped out to join her friends. The faint signs of her fatigue and red eyes lined up perfectly with the lie she told – that she had stayed up all night packing, too restless to sleep. The Langens protested fiercely that she should never travel alone, but Gwendolen was firm, unstoppable. She would take the ladies’ compartment, she told them, and sleep the whole way, no chaperone needed.

That Thursday evening, Gwendolen’s train pulled out of Leubronn’s station, and she never set foot in the roulette hall again. By Saturday morning, she was stepping onto the gravel drive of Offendene, the home she and her family would soon have to leave forever, the strange, unexpected gift of the stranger’s turquoise necklace tucked safe in her case.

*“Let no flower of the spring pass by us; let us crown ourselves with rosebuds before they be withered.”* — *Book of Wisdom*

It is a small, soft tragedy that Offendene was never stitched into the fabric of Gwendolen Harleth’s childhood, never made dear to her by the giddy rush of summer barefoot runs across its lawns, the familiar cluck of the hens in the backyard, the face of a long-dead neighbor who once slipped her a honey cake on the way home from school. A human life, after all, deserves to root itself deep in one small patch of native earth: a place where the stars above your bedroom window feel like your own private constellation, where the sound of the local blacksmith’s hammer drifting over the fields is as much a part of you as your own heartbeat. At five years old, no one is born a citizen of the world, hungry for abstract ideas and impartiality; that blind, fierce love of your own home milk, your own home hearth, is the very stuff that nourishes body and soul.

But that sweet, unthinking habit of affectionate belonging had never grown in Gwendolen’s life. Offendene had only been chosen as her mother’s home a year before they returned from Leubronn, picked for no more romantic reason than its proximity to Pennicote Rectory. On a late October afternoon, the whole party — Mrs. Davilow, Gwendolen, her four little half-sisters, and the governess and maid trailing behind in a second carriage — had first driven up its long avenue, rooks cawing loud overhead, golden elm leaves whirling and sticking to the hems of their black traveling dresses.

The season suited the old oblong red-brick house perfectly, its stone carvings tracing every line of its facade, down to the rows of narrow mullioned windows and the heavy square portico out front. The stone was crusted over with soft green lichen, the brick dusted with pale gray, so that for all its rigid, straight lines, it felt warm, softened by decades of weather. Three avenues of old plantation trees stretched east, west and south from the grounds, and though the house sat tucked behind their screen of foliage, it still offered far-off glimpses of the tall, graceful curves of the chalk downs, glowing honey-gold under the low sun. It was just large enough to be called a proper mansion, moderately rented, no grand manor lands attached, its dark faded upholstery and sombre old paintings making it hard to let to newer, showier tenants. But no one glancing up at its walls could ever mistake it for the home of a retired tradesman: that quiet air of old, gentle birth was worth more to Mrs. Davilow than a dozen modern conveniences, for she and her daughters occupied that delicate border territory of rank where social respectability was a prize worth chasing. To live in a house that had once belonged to a dowager countess gave her a quiet, unspoken thrill of satisfaction, finally having a proper establishment of her own.

The sudden death of her last husband, Captain Davilow, who had only drifted in and out of their lives for nine long years, had made this new life possible, but Gwendolen cared far more for the change itself than the unspoken, awkward explanations behind it. She had always hated their old wandering life, drifting from one foreign watering place to the next, from Parisian apartment to German boarding house, always surrounded by strangers’ scratchy hired furniture, meeting strangers who never treated her like the extraordinary person she knew she was. Even two years spent at a fashionable showy school, where she was always pushed to the front at every recital and prize day, had only deepened her certainty that someone as exceptional as her was never meant to muddle through ordinary, unremarkable circumstances. All that anxiety vanished the moment they settled on Offendene. She never thought to question how her West Indian grandfather had made the fortune inherited by her and her mother, nor did she wonder why her father’s lofty, aristocratic family had never spoken to her mother again — the last time she had tried to ask about her mysterious, long-dead father, at twelve years old, her mother had shut the old memorial box of his miniatures with a sharp, uncharacteristic snap and snapped that she had no feeling at all, and Gwendolen, who loved her soft mother dearly, had never dared bring the subject up again.

She was not a cruel girl, not really: even as a child she had fished drowning insects out of puddles to nurse them back to life, and when she had once strangled her little sister’s canary in a fit of overwrought fury, its shrill singing interrupting her piano practice for the tenth time, she had bought the child a soft white mouse as penance, though she still winced a little to think of that moment even now, at twenty. Her whole life she had been the household’s little princess, waited on hand and foot by her mother, her sisters, the governess, and the maids, and it had never occurred to her that her own pleasure was not the most important thing in the room. Now, as a young woman, that old fiery will had hardened into easy, practiced charm, sharpened with a quiet calculation no one else quite saw beneath it.

On that first day of arrival, none of them had seen the house before — Mrs. Davilow’s brother-in-law Mr. Gascoigne had arranged the rental for them. They all stood clustered under the portico as the front door swung open, the glow of a bright wood fire spilling out into the stone hall, walls hung with old dark paintings, and no one spoke: the four half-sisters, ranging from sixteen-year-old Alice down to ten-year-old Isabel in their shabby black dresses, the quiet, neutral Miss Merry the governess, all of them turned their eyes to Gwendolen, as if their entire future happiness rested on what she thought of the place. Her mother’s worn, gentle face looked up at her with a total, soft appeal, and Gwendolen gazed around her, head held high, the picture of a sleek young racehorse prancing in the paddock among plodding, unremarkable hacks.

“Well, dear, what do you think of the place?” Mrs. Davilow asked at last, her tone soft and tentative.

“It’s charming,” Gwendolen said at once, bright and delighted. “Romantic. Any wonderful, unexpected thing could happen here. It’s the perfect background for anything grand — no one would ever need to be ashamed to live here.”

“There’s certainly nothing common about it,” her mother agreed.

“Oh, it would suit fallen royalty perfectly, grand genteel poverty exactly. It would be far more romantic if we had once lived in a palace and come down to this, wouldn’t it?” Gwendolen laughed, then her tone shifted, sharp with surprise. “But where are uncle and aunt Gascoigne? And cousin Anna? I thought they’d be here to meet us.”

“We’re early,” Mrs. Davilow said, stepping into the hall to greet the waiting housekeeper, who assured her the Gascoignes had been by the day before to check the fires, every room had been aired for a full week, all the brasses polished until they shone, and they would arrive sharp at five o’clock.

Satisfied, Gwendolen tripped up the matted stone staircase and back down again, leading her sisters on a quick tour of the ground floor: the dark oak-paneled dining room hung with a painting of snarling fighting dogs over the sideboard, the library thick with the old, comforting smell of worn brown leather, and last of all the drawing room, reached through a small antechamber crammed full of dented old knickknacks.

“Mamma, come see!” Gwendolen called, throwing off her hat and gloves, spinning to point at the organ tucked against the far wall. “There’s an organ! I shall be Saint Cecilia — someone must paint me as Saint Cecilia. Jocosa, unpin my hair, quickly!” The meek governess fished the comb holding her coils of light brown hair free, and shook the mass out until it flowed smooth and thick far below her slim waist. Gwendolen seated herself on the organ bench, head tilted up in the perfect saintly pose, and her mother laughed, charmed to see her favorite daughter looking so radiant, even in front of the housekeeper.

Then little Isabel gave a small, gasping cry from the other end of the room. She had pried open a hinged wainscot panel, and behind it was a hidden painting: the upturned face of a dead man, an obscure shadowy figure fleeing away from it, arms outstretched in horror. Mrs. Davilow made a face of disgust, but Gwendolen shuddered, cold down to her bones.

“You’ll never dare stay in this room alone now, Gwendolen,” Isabel said, her small voice ringing out.

“How dare you pry open things that are meant to stay shut, you naughty little thing?” Gwendolen snapped, her face flushing hot with anger as she snatched the panel from the little girl’s hand and slammed it hard shut. “Find the key for this lock at once. If there isn’t one, make a new one, and no one — no one — is to open this panel ever again. Bring the key straight to me.”

They hurried up to their black and gold bedroom soon after, the tall mirror between the windows reflecting Gwendolen in all her bright glory, the mass of her loose hair falling over her shoulders. She preened a little, brushing the strands back from her face, laughing that her nose turned up just a little too much to be a proper saint’s nose, but that a crown of white roses would fix it perfectly. When her mother sighed, heavy and weary, she turned to her, sharp with gentle reproach.

“Now mamma, don’t start being dull already,” she said, wrapping her arms around the older woman’s waist. “We can be happy here. Everything is going to be wonderful now. You must stop moping, you hear? I’m never going to waste my life muddling through like everyone else does. I’m going to be happy, no matter what. And I promise I’ll be nice to uncle and aunt, and cousin Anna, I swear it.”

No sooner had they finished dressing than the Gascoignes arrived. Gwendolen greeted them with all her practiced, graceful charm, determined that the relatives who had not seen her since she was sixteen would admire every inch of the woman she had grown into. Mrs. Gascoigne, the rector’s wife, was sharp and capable, used to running a parish and a household of eight children with quiet beneficent authority. Mr. Gascoigne, once a army captain who had only taken orders a few years before his marriage, was a strikingly handsome man of fifty-seven, iron gray hair, a fine straight nose, the kind of man everyone at the local sessions trusted implicitly, who could preach a better sermon than any clergyman in the diocese, and who knew the history of every European campaign like the back of his hand. He looked at Gwendolen with unmistakeable admiration, then drew his shy tiny daughter Anna forward, the girl’s face a soft miniature copy of his own.

“You’ve quite outgrown Anna, my dear,” he said, smiling. “She’s a year younger than you, and she’s done growing long ago. I do hope the two of you will be the very best of companions.”

Gwendolen leaned in to kiss her quiet cousin, warm and genuine, already certain that she would be the brightest, most beloved star of this quiet little corner of Wessex, and that some wonderful, glittering fate was waiting for her at Offendene.

*Gorgibus. — I tell you marriage is a holy, sacred thing, and it is only proper for honest folk to begin their lives together by it.
Madelon. — Good heavens! If everyone thought like you, a romance would be over in five minutes! What a dull world it would be, if Cyrus married Mandane right off the bat, and Aronce tied the knot with Clélie before the first page was half-read! * Let us spin out the thread of our story at our leisure, and don’t rush us to the ending so soon.*
— Molière, *Les Précieuses Ridicules*

No one could fairly blame the rector of Pennicote for, in the course of weighing every side of his niece’s prospects, concluding that Gwendolen Harleth was exactly the sort of girl destined for a brilliant, envied marriage. Why should he be expected to stray far from the common good sense of his day, and wish his charming niece a worse outcome for her maidenhood than the whole of polite society agreed was the very best a girl could hope for? It speaks well of him, in fact, that every part of that hope was rooted in pure, unselfish good nature. He was far too rational to chase silly, fairy-tale fantasies: he would never have recommended Gwendolen dress in rags like Patient Griselda to draw a marquis’s eye, or hidden her away from all company on the off-chance some wandering noble would stumble across her. Mr. Gascoigne had no interest in contriving silly near-accidents to force a rich man to rescue her, either. He simply wanted what was best for her: to make sure Gwendolen was seen to her full advantage in every drawing room and social gathering across their corner of the county.

Her uncle’s plans aligned perfectly with Gwendolen’s own quiet wishes — though do not mistake her intentions. She did not ride her horse with that effortless, spellbinding grace, or practice her music and French, for the explicit end of landing a grand husband. She would, if pressed, admit that marriage was in her distant future, and she felt, without ever stopping to argue the point, that her union would never be the dull, middling sort that most girls settled for. But her ambitions never centered on marriage as their final fulfillment. The dramatic daydreams where she cast herself as the leading lady never wrapped up with a wedding. To be courted desperately, to be the object of hopeless, reverent sighs from half a dozen suitors, that was the sweet, necessary proof of her power over other people. But to become a wife, bound hand and foot by all the tedious domestic rules of that state, struck her on the whole as a vexing, unavoidable chore. All the married women she had observed seemed trapped in a dreary existence: no freedom to do as they pleased, stuck with far more children than they cared for, growing dull and plodding and swallowed whole by endless humdrum routine. Of course marriage was a step up the social ladder, and she had no interest in spending her life as a poor, unmarried spinster — but promotions often came with bitter side effects. A peerage could never give a man the thrill of leading a movement he had dreamed of, after all, and this slight, sylphlike twenty-year-old had no intention of being led. That hunger for power and admiration burns in feminine breasts too, and in Gwendolen’s it nestled gently among all the soft, lady-like trappings of her upbringing: she had no grand designs to advance scholarship or reshape government, no illusions her wits could move the world like Archimedes’ lever. She only wanted to live in a way that was striking, that made everyone around her gasp in admiration, so she could feel that bright, burning rush of being truly alive, reflected in every other person’s awe.

“Gwendolen will never rest until she has the whole world at her feet,” her meek little governess Miss Merry once said, and the words were hardly hyperbolic for their quiet little country circle: after all, everyone who lives in a small genteel suburb knows what it means to have “the world at your feet” — it is just six or seven people fawning over you, all your little world’s most flattering attention gathered in your palms. That hazy, grand prospect hung like a soft golden glow around Gwendolen in the height of her young, self-assured exultation. Everyone else around her let themselves be dragged about like empty ships at the mercy of the tide, their lives directed by anyone stronger than them. Not her. She would never be sacrificed to people far less clever and far less worthy than she was. She would take every chance life handed her, and conquer every circumstance, all by her own extraordinary wits.

Their new home at Offendene, with Lady Brackenshaw’s occasional notice, the local archery club, and the rare dinner invitation to the wealthy Arrowpoints’ estate, hardly seemed like a setting that would grant her grand opportunities — but Gwendolen’s confidence never depended on her surroundings. She was certain she had every tool she needed to master her own fate. She agreed that her education had left her with no disadvantages: in the schoolroom, her sharp, quick mind had soaked up all the firm, unyielding starch of unexplained rules and disconnected facts that keeps ignorance from ever feeling flimsy or unconfident. All the rest of the world’s stored knowledge, she was sure, she already knew well enough from the novels, plays, and poems she devoured. She had no doubts about her two core lady’s accomplishments: her French was fluent, her piano playing was graceful and impressive. Add to that the natural, unshakable sense of capability some lucky people are born with, the kind that makes them certain they can form a perfect judgment on any subject the second they turn their attention to it, and who could blame Gwendolen for feeling fully ready to steer her own destiny? There were dozens of subjects she found boring, of course — most subjects feel dull to the bright young, the same way bright light feels harsh to the old — but she never felt helpless to hold her own in conversation about any of them.

No one in her life had ever disputed her superiority, or her right to have things her way. From the very first day they moved into Offendene, everyone around her asked first: what will Gwendolen think? If the footman’s heavy boots creaked too loud down the hall, the maid would murmur that the racket would never do for Miss Harleth. If the laundry came back wrinkled and ill-done, it was not seen as an inconvenience for the rest of the family — it was unacceptable for Gwendolen. If the bedroom fireplace smoked so bad it stung Mrs. Davilow’s already weak eyes, her mother apologized to Gwendolen first for the discomfort. If they were traveling and Gwendolen came down to breakfast an hour late, long after everyone else had finished their meal, the whole household scrambled to make sure her coffee was still scalding hot, and her toast still perfectly crisp. When she stepped into the room then, her light brown hair freshly brushed and streaming down her back, waiting for her mother to twist it up into its usual style, her large brown eyes bright as a wave-polished onyx under their long dark lashes, she was always the one to be tolerant: chiding Alice not to hunch her shoulders in that frightful way, sending Isabel off to sit with Miss Merry instead of pestering her with a hundred silly little questions.

She was the exiled princess, who even in times of famine deserved a breakfast roll made of the finest flour milled from seven thin meager ears of wheat, who if the whole household had to flee in a hurry, would have her silver fork kept safe and separate from all the messy, jumbled baggage. The reason for this little domestic reign seems obvious, at first glance: her beauty, that rare, unplaceable charm about her, that steady will that hummed through every graceful movement of hers, every clear, unhesitating tone of her voice. On a rainy, gray day when everyone else in the house felt limp and listless, when no one could see the point of keeping up any small formality, all Gwendolen had to do was step into the room, and suddenly there was a bright, compelling reason to keep life beautiful and ordered. Even the waiters at inns lit up with extra energy when she walked in, hurrying to wipe away crumbs, smooth out tablecloth creases, dump out the half-empty glasses of dregs with drowning flies in them. Add that magic to the fact that she was the eldest daughter, and her mother had spent years feeling apologetic to her over all the misfortunes that cruel stepfathers had dragged the family into, and it would seem you did not need any other explanation for her little domestic empire — like explaining daylight by pointing at the sun, it seemed too simple to argue with.

But do not jump to conclusions before you compare. The narrator has seen that same fawning, trembling, apologetic deference given to people who were not beautiful, not unusual at all, whose will showed itself in harsh, graceless tones, who were not eldest daughters with soft, timid mothers wracked with guilt. Some of them were very ordinary, unremarkable men. The only trait all of them shared, the one common thread, was an unshakable determination to get whatever pleased them, and a total, unafraid willingness to make themselves thoroughly unpleasant and difficult if they did not get their way. What household full of soft, tender-hearted women will not fawn over the unscrupulous man who will go off and do far worse things if he does not get his way at home? So it seems perfectly possible that Gwendolen, even without her rare charm, even without her mother’s gentle guilt, could still have ruled as that exiled princess — if she only held onto that fierce, unapologetic egoistic desire, that quiet power to make everyone around her fear what she might say, or what she might do. But of course she had the charm, too, and the people who were a little afraid of her loved her just as fiercely. Both the fear and the fondness were heightened by the shimmering, iridescent quality of her character: that play of conflicting, opposing traits inside her. Macbeth’s famous line about being unable to be two opposite things at the same time only applies to the rough, clumsy work of physical action, not the far subtler workings of human feeling. You cannot speak a loyal word and stay cruelly silent in the same breath, you cannot kill a man and not kill him in the same moment — but a single heartbeat is wide enough to hold both a loyal impulse and a selfish one, a flash of murderous rage and the sharp, quick tug of immediate repentance.

Gwendolen’s reception across the neighborhood unfolded exactly as her uncle had predicted. From the grand gilded halls of Brackenshaw Castle to the warm, generous home of banker Mr. Quallon at the Firs in Wanchester, she was met with unmistakeable, eager admiration. Even the ladies who privately decided she was a touch too sharp, a touch too unapologetically radiant, could not resist adding her to their guest lists: hostesses who entertain often curate their parties the way statesmen build cabinets, choosing guests not only out of personal fondness, but to make the whole affair sparkle. There was no tedious baggage that came with inviting Gwendolen, either: her mother Mrs. Davilow was a quiet, gently picturesque chaperon who never demanded fuss, and her uncle Mr. Gascoigne was welcome everywhere on his own merits.

One of the first houses to send an invitation was Quetcham Hall, where no one quite warmed to her but no one dared leave her off the list for their massive dinner party: an event that acted as a formal, broad introduction of the new arrivals to every visitable family in the district, with thirty carefully selected guests spanning every relevant generation. No figure that night outshone Gwendolen as she drifted through the long suite of rooms draped in garlands and lit by hundreds of soft candles. At first she was nothing but a slim, floating silhouette in layers of flowing white drapery, drawing closer through one wide carved doorway after another, growing brighter, sharper, more unmistakeable with every step. She had never before been the centre of that kind of slow, grand public promenade, and she exulted in the knowledge that it suited her. A stranger glancing over could have sworn she had been raised on long gilded galleries and liveried footmen, while her cousin Anna, who was far more familiar with such aristocratic households, fidgeted so nervously she might have been a rabbit dropped suddenly into a brightly lit ballroom.

“Who is that with Gascoigne?” the archdeacon cut off mid-conversation about military maneuvers, a topic every guest naturally deferred to him on as a clergyman. His son, the brilliant young classicist who had already published several elegant, ingenious new emendations of ancient Greek texts, spoke at almost the exact same second, muttering under his breath: “By God, who is that girl with the perfectly held head and that glorious figure?”

To anyone who wanted every guest to feel flattered and at ease, it was almost exasperating how thoroughly Gwendolen eclipsed everyone else. The famously handsome Miss Lawe, daughter of Lady Lawe, suddenly looked broad, heavy, and completely lifeless beside her. Poor Miss Arrowpoint, who had unfortunately also chosen a white gown that night, suddenly resembled a tiny cheap carte-de-visite portrait where only the skirt had been paid for. Since Catherine Arrowpoint was universally beloved for her gentle, unassuming warmth and her endless patience softening her mother’s more eccentric quirks, many guests thought it oddly unfit that this new stranger should carry herself like someone born to far higher social standing than their well-liked local heiress.

“She is not really nearly as handsome if you stop to examine her features,” Mrs. Arrowpoint confided to Mrs. Vulcany later that evening. “It’s just a certain bold bearing that stuns you at first, but she grows far less pleasant the longer you speak to her.”

Gwendolen had not meant to offend her hostess, though she had. The lady of Quetcham was not a spiteful woman, but she had her sensitive spots. Everyone in the neighborhood loved to connect all her little oddities to the fact that she was heiress to a fortune made in some vague city trade: people joked that her short squat figure, her sharp parrot-like squawk of a voice, her elaborate towering headdresses, and her fondness for writing literary treatises were all inevitable side effects of that mercantile fortune. (A little observation would have shown how little sense that made: aldermen’s daughters were often tall and graceful, pretty women often had harsh voices, and terrible poetry was written by every sort of person, man or woman, across every class.)

Gwendolen, who had a sharp eye for the absurdities of others, had been fully determined to win Mrs. Arrowpoint over, showering her with more interest than most other guests would bother to offer. But self-confidence often tricks people into assuming everyone less self-assured is also slow-witted: rich people coo patronizingly at the poor, vibrant young people raise their voices and speak in silly simplified tones to seniors, assuming they must be deaf and dim. Even clever, kind Gwendolen fell for that trap. She unthinkingly decided that because Mrs. Arrowpoint was a little ridiculous, she must also be easily fooled, and began performing a little act of wide-eyed, girlish naivete without ever suspecting her hostess was noticing every tiny shift in her behaviour.

“I hear you love books as well as music, riding, and archery,” Mrs. Arrowpoint said, pulling her aside for a private chat in the drawing room after the men had finished their port. “Catherine will be so glad to have such a sympathetic new neighbour.” The words would have sounded perfectly graceful if they had been spoken in a soft, mellow voice, but with that sharp loud twang, Gwendolen found herself shifting into patronizing mode without thinking, replying sweetly: “It is I who am fortunate. Miss Arrowpoint will teach me what good music really is. I am a complete beginner, I hear she is a master of the craft.”

“Catherine has certainly had every advantage. We have a first-rate musician staying in the house right now, Herr Klesmer—you must know his compositions. Let me introduce you later. You sing, I believe? Catherine plays three instruments but she does not sing. I hope you will let us hear you, I understand you are an accomplished singer.”

“Oh no, ‘die Kraft ist schwach, allein die Lust ist gross’ as Mephistopheles says.”

“Ah, you read Goethe. Young ladies are so advanced these days. I suppose you have read every important book.”

“Truly no. I’ve been rummaging through the library at Offendene and there’s nothing readable at all. The pages all stick together, and the whole place smells musty. I wish I could write books to amuse myself the way you do! How delightful it must be to write exactly what you want, instead of reading other people’s ideas. Home-made books must be so nice.” For a split second Mrs. Arrowpoint’s glance sharpened, but before she could take offence, Gwendolen gushed that she would give anything to write a whole book of her own, and the comment read as nothing more than silly girlish enthusiasm.

“Why shouldn’t you?” Mrs. Arrowpoint encouraged her. “Pen, ink, paper are there for anyone to use, just as they were for me. I will send you all my own published work with pleasure.”

“Thank you, I shall be so glad to read them. It must be so special to know an author personally, you’d be able to tell for certain which bits are meant to be funny and which are serious. I always laugh in the wrong places, I swear.” Gwendolen noticed she was treading on dangerous ground, and quickly added: “With Shakespeare and all the other great writers we can never meet, you know. I always want to know more than what is written on the page.”

“If you are interested in any of my subjects, I can lend you piles of unpublished manuscripts,” Mrs. Arrowpoint said, as Gwendolen suddenly felt like the girl who had foolishly announced she was wildly fond of potted sprats. “I will publish most of them eventually, so many friends have urged me to, and one does not like to be stubborn. My work on Tasso, for example— I could easily have made it twice the size.”

“I adore Tasso,” Gwendolen said.

“Perfect, you shall have all my papers. Everyone who has ever written about Tasso has got it all wrong: the nature of his madness, his feelings for Leonora, the real reason he was imprisoned, what Leonora was really like— I think she was cold-hearted, otherwise she would have married him even against her brother’s wishes. No one else sees it that way.”

“How fascinating!” Gwendolen said. “I hate agreeing with everyone else. It’s so boring to just parrot other people’s opinions. That’s the worst part about publishing your work: you make everyone start agreeing with you.” That line made Mrs. Arrowpoint suspicious again, her gaze probing for a trace of sarcasm, but Gwendolen looked so wide-eyed and innocent, leaning in like an eager student, that she let it pass. “I know nothing of Tasso except *Gerusalemme Liberata*, which we had to memorize at school.”

“His life is far more interesting than his poetry. I have written the early part of his story as a full romance, accounting for his father Bernardo and all the rest, all the parts that must be true.”

“Imagination is often truer than fact,” Gwendolen said decisively, though she could no more explain that glib phrase than she could read Coptic. “I can’t wait to learn everything about him, especially his madness. Poets are always a little mad, aren’t they?”

“Of course—‘the poet’s eye in a fine frenzy rolling,’ and someone wrote of Marlowe: ‘For that fine madness still he did maintain, Which always should possess the poet’s brain.’”

“But no one always noticed it, right?” Gwendolen said innocently. “I expect some of them rolled their eyes in private. Mad people are often very cunning.” A flicker of irritation crossed Mrs. Arrowpoint’s face, but the doors swung open then and the rest of the guests filed in from the dining room, stopping any potential argument dead.

“Ah, there is Herr Klesmer,” Mrs. Arrowpoint said, standing to lead him over to Gwendolen. The brilliant musician was a wonderful, rare mix of German, Slav, and Jewish heritage, with grand striking features, wavy brown hair styled in soft artistic disarray, and dark eyes behind round spectacles. His English was nearly flawless, no thick foreign accent to be heard, and his intimidating genius was softened just then by the foolish, gentle eagerness any great artist feels when he wants to charm a beautiful woman.

They soon moved to the pianos, where Miss Arrowpoint and Herr Klesmer played a four-handed duet that went on so long the whole general audience was quietly grateful when it finished. Gwendolen, in particular, walked away from the performance realizing that quiet, placid Catherine Arrowpoint had a mastery of the piano that would put all her own casual practice to shame—though she did not lose confidence in her much-praised singing voice.

Soon every guest was begging Gwendolen to sing, especially Mr. Arrowpoint the host, a perfect gentleman who was beloved by all for nothing more noteworthy than marrying Miss Cuttler and importing the very best cigars. He led her politely to the piano, where Herr Klesmer opened the lid for her, smiling as he stepped back a few feet to stand where he could see her clearly as she performed.

Gwendolen was not nervous. She never trembled when she set out to do something she knew she was good at, and singing was pure joy to her. She had a moderately powerful soprano that people often said sounded just like Jenny Lind’s, a perfect ear for pitch, and she had never known anything but unreserved applause for her performances. She had the rare gift of looking even prettier when she sang than she did at rest, and having the great Herr Klesmer watching her did not feel unpleasant at all. She sang her carefully pre-planned favorite Bellini aria, sure of every note.

“Charming!” Mr. Arrowpoint exclaimed from where he stood nearby, and the praise echoed around the room, the usual small amount of harmless social insincerity no one bothers to call out. But Herr Klesmer stood as still and silent as a statue in spectacles, not a word, not a clap. The guests begged her to stay seated and sing a second song, and she was not inclined to refuse—until she turned to Klesmer with a soft, smiling appeal. “It would be far too cruel to make a great musician listen to poor amateur singing.”

“No, that means nothing,” he said, and suddenly his voice shifted into a sharp, staccato thick German accent he had hidden until that point, the kind of brogue a man slips into only when he is angry, passionate, or fiercely sincere. “That makes nothing. It is always acceptable to see you sing.”

It was the most unexpected assertion of superiority she had ever encountered. Gwendolen flushed bright crimson, but with her usual quick presence of mind, she did not storm off in ungraceful resentment. Catherine Arrowpoint, who had stood close enough to overhear, and had noticed Klesmer’s admiring gaze on Gwendolen was far more focused on her than polite society permitted, stepped in to smooth things over with perfect kindness. “Imagine what I have to put up with from this professor! He can barely tolerate any of the English attempts at music. We all just put up with his severity, because it is almost a relief to know exactly the worst thing anyone could ever say about your playing, when everyone else is fawning over you.”

“I would be so grateful to him if he told me the worst,” Gwendolen said, recovering her wits instantly. “I expect I had very bad teachers, and no real talent—only a great love for music.” It was a very graceful line, for a thought that had never once crossed her mind before that second.

“That is true, your training is terrible,” Herr Klesmer said quietly. He adored beauty, but music meant far more to him. “Still, you are not completely without gifts. You sing perfectly in tune, and you have a fair, pleasant voice. But you produce your notes incorrectly, and that music you just sang is beneath you. It is that sort of sappy, see-sawing melody made for people with no breadth of horizon, no deep passion, no sense of the universal. It makes everyone who listens to it smaller. Sing something larger, now, and I will see what you are really capable of.”

Gwendolen’s heart sank. She had spent the whole night being treated like the brightest, most special guest in the room, and now this genius had ripped apart one of the things she was proudest of. “Not now, later,” she said, a little unsteadily. Catherine stepped in to save her again. “Yes, later. I always need half an hour to summon my courage after he criticizes my playing. Let us make him play for us now, he owes it to all of us to show what great music really sounds like.”

Klesmer agreed, and played one of his own compositions: the fantasia titled *Freudvoll, Leidvoll, Gedankenvoll*, a sprawling, richly layered piece that drew every possible thread of passion from the piano, his fingers working a kind of imperious magic that sent thrills up every listener’s spine, making the strings sing with a raw, quivering urgency few pianists could pull off. Even with her ego bruised, Gwendolen felt the full weight of that performance. Her mortification melted away into bright, restless excitement, until she was almost completely indifferent to how badly she had just been judged, determined to laugh off her own small failings like they belonged to some other girl. Her eyes sparkled, her cheeks flushed pink, and her tongue was sharp and ready for mischievous remarks.

“I do hope you will sing for us again, Miss Harleth,” young Clintock, the archdeacon’s classicist son who had walked her into dinner, said as soon as Klesmer finished. “That fancy complicated stuff means nothing to me. It’s like a jar of leeches, you can never tell where the beginning ends and the end starts. I could listen to your singing all day.”

“Yes, we would all love something popular now, another song from you would be such a nice relaxation,” Mrs. Arrowpoint added, moving over with polite intentions.

“That is because all of you are stuck in a puerile state of culture, with no breadth of horizon,” Gwendolen said, completely ignoring Mrs. Arrowpoint and grinning bright up at Clintock. “I just learned that. I’m having growing pains right now, and they are never pleasant.” Mrs. Arrowpoint noticed the intentional rudeness, but simply stepped back, saying no more.

“I’m so glad you like our little neighbourhood,” Clintock said, pleased that he had her full attention.

“It’s perfect. There is a little of everything, and not too much of anything.”

“That’s rather ambiguous praise.”

“Not to me. I love having a little of everything. A little absurdity, for example, is so much fun. I am thankful for a few odd, quirky people, but too many of them get boring.” Mrs. Arrowpoint, who could still hear their conversation, realized she was right to be suspicious of Gwendolen’s earlier gushing about Tasso, and decided the girl was double-faced and far more sarcastic than she pretended.

“I think we need more croquet, for one thing,” Clintock went on. “I’m usually away studying, but if I was home more I would start a whole croquet club. You’re in the archery club, I think, but trust me, croquet is the game of the future. It just needs someone to write it up properly. One of my friends wrote a whole four-canto poem about it, as good as Pope. I keep begging him to publish it. You’ve never read anything better.”

“I will study croquet tomorrow,” Gwendolen said. “I will give up singing and take up croquet instead.”

“No no, don’t give up singing! But do take up croquet. I can send you Jenning’s poem if you like, I have a handwritten manuscript.”

“Is he a very close friend of yours?”

“Just about, yes.”

“Oh, if he’s only just about, I think I will decline. Unless, if you send it to me, you promise not to quiz me about it afterwards, asking which part I liked best. It’s far easier to pretend you know a sermon you never listened to than pretend you loved a poem you never read.”

But Quetcham Hall continued to send the Harleths warm, polite invitations all the same. It was not just that social calls are never based entirely on personal liking: gentle Catherine Arrowpoint, who managed all the household’s social arrangements while her mother was busy writing her manuscripts, had felt a soft, protective fondness for Gwendolen after that tense little scene at the piano, and was determined to make sure that dazzling, sharp, bright young girl who carried the whole world at her feet got all the kindness and chances she deserved.

“Croyez-vous m’avoir humiliée pour m’avoir appris que la terre tourne autour du soleil? Je vous jure que je ne m’en estime pas moins.” —FONTENELLE: *Pluralité des Mondes*.

That cutting, lofty bit of criticism from Herr Klesmer had stung Gwendolen in a way no silly local slight ever could. She would never have breathed a word to anyone of how bitterly she resented never having Miss Arrowpoint’s years of rigorous musical training, training that would let her argue with the great master from a place of earned confidence instead of the shaky, untested charm she usually relied on. She would not even admit to herself that every meeting with quiet Catherine Arrowpoint sparked a strange, hot flash of unaccustomed jealousy in her: not for the girl’s fortune, but because it was infuriating that someone no one would have spared a second glance for on a crowded street—slight, average height, small plain features, sallow complexion—could carry that unshakable, unarguable mental superiority, that perfect, meticulous skill on the piano, that fastidious good taste that no amount of Gwendolen’s charm could coerce into gushing over her half-learned ballads. That perfectly polite, unassuming girl of four-and-twenty was clearly quietly judging Gwendolen’s accomplishments as no more than common, and the knowledge festered, no matter how much Gwendolen tried to brush it off.

She did not like to dwell on facts that cast her in an unflattering light, of course. The “musical magus” who had so suddenly widened her expectations of what talent could be was not always lingering nearby, and as he shuttled back and forth between London and Quetcham, Gwendolen began to tell herself she could surely win him over, make him see how much she shone, with a little time. The unreserved delight her singing drew from guests at Brackenshaw Castle, the Firs, and every other country house gathering soon soothed her ruffled ego. She trusted their unreserved praise far more than Klesmer’s sharp critique, and she was not one of those rare, tormented souls who craves a perfection no one around them ever asks for. Outwardly, at least, there was nothing especially unusual about her beyond her rare, effortless grace of movement, her striking bold wit, and that little spark of daring that made a very ordinary, self-centered hunger to stand out feel far more vivid and exciting than the same ambition smoldering under a hundred dull, conventional exteriors. The way a girl holds her head does not change how fiercely she craves supremacy, after all—it only changes what she thinks she can achieve, and how. For Gwendolen, that hunger was bounded firmly by the rules of genteel romance: she wanted to do something remarkable, something unforgettable, without ever stooping to sordid, demeaning paid work, without ever stepping outside the glittering sphere of polite society. She raged against the small constraints of her family’s reduced circumstances in her head, ignored all unasked obligations to chase what she wanted, and yet she had no radical theories, no desire to reform the world, and would have mocked any woman who claimed to want to do such a thing as a ridiculous bluestocking. Her whole world was bounded by the kind of romantic story where the heroine’s journal is full of vague dreams of power and originality and rebellion, but she still walks every path in satin shoes, so that even her misadventures feel soft, beautiful, and entirely proper.

That very ordinary, unremarkable outcome was what Gwendolen found herself drifting toward that first quiet winter at Offendene. She knew for certain she did not want to live the same dull, repetitive life every other young lady in the neighborhood was trapped in—calling on neighbors, painting bad watercolors, stitching endless samplers—but she had no clear plan for what to do instead, no idea how to assert the freedom she was so sure she was entitled to. Offendene was a fine, grand old house to be the setting for something exciting, but the surrounding neighborhood, she decided, was far too boring to ever give her that adventure.

Beyond the predictable thrill of turning every head at her first few invitations, there was little to amuse her, so she came home after bouts of sharp, knowing satire that had already ruffled Mrs. Arrowpoint’s feathers, to fill her days with very girlish, unapologetic fun. The boldest act of rebellion she could think of was to scrap young Alice’s lessons entirely—on the logic that Alice would only be happier, and likely more talented, running wild than being forced to cram dull books. She roped the governess Miss Merry, and the maids who waited on all the ladies of the house, into sewing up elaborate dramatic costumes, all ready for future charades and home theatricals she was determined to make happen, by force of will if she had to. She had never acted in her life, only posed in tableaux vivants back at school, but she knew with absolute certainty she would be brilliant at it. She had visited the Théâtre Français once, and heard her mother rave for hours about the great tragedian Rachel, so her daydreams often drifted to the idea of becoming a star actress even more famous than Rachel—after all, she was far more beautiful than that thin Jewish performer. The wet weeks before Christmas slipped by happily, stitching flowing Greek gowns, rich Eastern robes, all kinds of hybrid fantastical outfits, and she would pose and recite grand tragic lines for a small domestic audience that once even included the housekeeper. The woman had been invited to cheer her on, but ruined her welcome the first time by blurting out that Gwendolen looked far more like a queen in her everyday silk gowns than that baggy tunic that left her arms bare, so she was never asked to join again.

“Do I look as well as Rachel, Mamma?” Gwendolen asked one day, parading in her Greek drapery for Anna, reciting scraps of tragic scenes with great, fiery dramatic purpose.

“You have far better arms than Rachel,” Mrs. Davilow said, drowsy from the sofa. “Your arms would suit any part, Gwen. But your voice isn’t deep enough for real tragedy, not like hers.”

“I can make it deeper if I want,” Gwendolen shot back immediately, then shook her head, decisive. “No, actually, a higher voice is more tragic. It’s more feminine. And the more feminine a woman is, the more devastating it feels when she does something desperate and brave.”

“There may be something to that,” Mrs. Davilow said, half-listening. “But I don’t see the point of making everyone’s blood run cold. And if there’s anything horrible to be done, it ought to be left to the men.”

“Oh Mamma, you are so dreadfully prosaic! All the great poetic tragic heroines are women, everyone knows that. Men are just cautious, boring little creatures.”

“Well, dear, you’re still afraid to be left alone in the dark at night, so thank goodness I don’t think you’ll be pulling off any great crimes anytime soon.”

“I’m not talking about real life,” Gwendolen said, impatient, and as soon as her mother was called out of the room, she turned quickly to her cousin, seizing the chance. “Anna, go ask your papa if we can put on charades at the rectory. Mr. Middleton and Warham can act with us, just for practice. Mamma won’t let us have Mr. Middleton rehearsing here, but he’s a stick, we can give him all the boring parts that suit him. Go ask, or I will.”

“Oh not until Rex comes home! He’s so clever, and such a dear, he’ll act Napoleon staring out over the sea, he looks exactly like him. Rex can do absolutely anything.”

“I don’t believe a word about this perfect Rex of yours,” Gwendolen laughed, teasing her. “He’ll turn out to be just like those terrible lopsided blue and yellow watercolors you hang all over your bedroom and worship.”

“Fine, you’ll see,” Anna said, huffing a little. “It’s not just me that thinks he’s clever. He already has a university scholarship, Papa says he’s sure to get a fellowship, and no one is better at games. He’s far sharper than Mr. Middleton, and everyone but you calls Mr. Middleton brilliant.”

“He may be brilliant in a dark-lantern sort of way, but he’s still a total stick. If he had to say ‘Perdition catch my soul, but I do love her,’ he’d deliver it in exactly the same flat tone as he’d read out ‘Here endeth the second lesson.’”

“Oh Gwendolen!” Anna said, shocked by the reference. “That’s so unkind to say! He admires you more than anything. I heard Warham say to Mamma the other day, ‘Middleton’s properly spooney on Gwendolen.’ She told him off for saying it, but I know what that means. That’s what they say at university for being head over heels in love.”

“What am I supposed to do about it?” Gwendolen said, half-contemptuous, half-amused. “Perdition catch my soul if I ever love him.”

“Of course you don’t! Papa wouldn’t want it anyway, and he’s leaving soon. But it makes me sad when you tease him.”

“What will you do if I tease Rex when he gets here?” Gwendolen asked, grinning wickedly.

“Gwendolen, dear, you won’t, will you?” Anna’s eyes filled with tears immediately. “I couldn’t bear it. There’s nothing at all to make fun of in him. No one ever laughed at Mr. Middleton before you, everyone thought he was handsome and perfectly mannered. Don’t laugh at Rex, promise me.” Her beseeching little face touched Gwendolen, and she softened at once.

“You’re such a sweet little coz,” she said, tapping the tip of Anna’s chin gently between her thumb and forefinger. “I’d never do anything to upset you. Especially not if Rex is going to make all our charade plans work out perfectly.”

When Rex did finally arrive, the bright, warm, lively energy he brought to both Offendene and the rectory, and his wholehearted willingness to throw himself into every one of Gwendolen’s plans, left her no urge to tease him at all—all her jokes for him were the affectionate, flattering kind he loved right back. He was a fine, open-hearted young man, with a handsome face that resembled his father’s and Anna’s but softer in expression than the rector’s, and broader, brighter than Anna’s. His nature was warm, healthy, loving, so full of joy for simple, innocent fun that vice held no allure for him at all; what little he knew of it felt like a distant, stupid thing “some other fellows did” that he was perfectly happy to stay far away from. He loved Anna as deeply as a brother could, but he had never felt anything for anyone else even close to the blazing, instant passion that took over his whole life the second he met Gwendolen.

The cousins were together constantly, at one house or the other—mostly at Offendene, where Gwendolen reigned unchallenged, and everything she wanted automatically became Rex’s top priority. The charades unfolded exactly the way she planned, and there were dozens of little impromptu scenes she had never anticipated, where Gwendolen got to show off her natural talent for acting even more vividly. The rehearsals and performances were held at Offendene, and Mrs. Davilow saw no reason to object to inviting Mr. Middleton to join now that Rex was there—especially since Warham, who was cramming for an exam that would send him to work in India, had no spare time at all, his head so full of random facts he could barely spare a thought for anything that wasn’t a potential exam question.

Mr. Middleton was easily persuaded to take all the grave, silent parts Gwendolen assigned him, after she flattered him endlessly for his enviable still, unchanging countenance. At first he was a little hurt and jealous at how easily Gwendolen laughed and joked with Rex, but he soon convinced himself that cousinly casual familiarity like that could never lead to a serious romance. He decided her more formal, polite treatment of him was actually a sign of favor, enough to make him dare to hint at his feelings before he left Pennicote, even though he’d originally planned to wait until his career was far more established. Gwendolen was fully aware the quiet young curate worshipped her, and she didn’t mind at all. She turned her calm, unreadable eyes on him and gave him dozens of small, gentle reasons to hope, deliberately avoiding any dramatic physical contact with him, knowing full well every little small gesture could be read into whatever romantic interpretation he wanted.

A lot of people might have assumed beforehand that a pious young Anglican curate who thought even mild swearing was rude would never dream of marrying a sharp, daring girl who mocked convention so freely, who had none of the soft, meek piety everyone expected from a clergyman’s wife. Or that a young man who’d spent years steeped in theology would realize he could never keep a bright, restless girl like Gwendolen happy. But no one ever asks why their assumptions turn out to be wrong, do they? The mistake is on their side, not the world’s.

As for Rex, he would have felt sorry for poor Mr. Middleton if he’d noticed the curate’s quiet heartache, but he was so completely swept up in his first all-consuming love that he barely noticed anyone but Gwendolen existed. He didn’t watch her, he just felt her—he could tell exactly when she walked into a room even if his back was turned. Before the end of the first fortnight he was so deeply in love he couldn’t imagine a future that wasn’t tied to hers. He saw no obstacles at all; his own love felt so bright, so perfect, he assumed Gwendolen must feel exactly the same way. He could no more imagine her breaking his heart than an ancient Egyptian could imagine snow. She sang and played for him whenever he asked, was always happy to ride out with him even when the horses he borrowed were shaggy, ridiculous old nags, laughed at all his jokes, and was endlessly kind to Anna. No single sign of sympathy felt missing. It never even crossed his mind that someone as perfect as Gwendolen would make a grand, wealthy marriage far above his station. He had no overinflated ego, just the quiet confidence of a happy young man who’d never known anything but love and success, and he took Gwendolen’s perfection as a natural, obvious part of the good, bright world he’d always lived in.

One small incident that happened mid-rehearsal struck Rex as a perfect, unexpected sign of how sensitive and deep Gwendolen really was, a side of her nature no one would have guessed at who’d only ever seen her fearless on horseback or giddy and sharp at parties.

After weeks of practice, they decided to invite a small, select group of guests over to Offendene to watch their little show, which the cast were all convinced was far better than anyone expected. Anna surprised everyone, playing her small parts with a neat, sly charm no one had suspected from her shy, quiet demeanor. And Mr. Middleton was perfect, because he never once tried to be funny or overstep his role. The only problem they’d hit was Gwendolen’s stubborn desire to appear on stage in her favorite Greek gown. She couldn’t think of a single word or theme for a charade that would let her stand in that statuesque pose without looking ridiculous. They couldn’t do a scene from Racine, because none of the others could declaim French verse well, and improvised speeches would turn the whole thing into a joke. And Mr. Gascoigne had forbidden them from acting scenes from actual full plays, even though he always argued that home theatricals were perfectly fine fun for ordinary people, he wouldn’t push the bounds of local Wessex decorum so far. He was happy to let them act harmless charades in his sister-in-law’s house, which was an entirely different affair.

Everyone was determined to make Gwendolen’s wish come true, and Rex came up with the perfect idea: end the whole performance with a tableau, where no one would have to speak, so all anyone would notice was how breathtakingly regal Gwendolen looked in her gown. She loved the idea immediately, and the only thing left was to pick the right theme for the tableau.

“Something nice, children, I beg of you,” Mrs. Davilow said. “I won’t have any of that horrid Greek wickedness on display.”

“It’s no worse than Christian wickedness, Mamma,” Gwendolen protested, still thinking of her Rachel-like tragic heroines.

“And far less scandalous,” Rex added. “Besides, it all happened thousands of years ago, it’s all over and done with. What do you say to Briseis being led away? I’ll be Achilles, and you can look back over your shoulder at me, just like that print we have hanging at the rectory.”

“That would be a good pose,” Gwendolen said, nodding, but later she shook her head, decisive. “No, it won’t work. We’d need three other men in proper costume, and it’ll just look silly if we don’t have them.”

“I’ve got it!” Rex said after a moment’s thought. “Hermione as the statue in *The Winter’s Tale*. I’ll be Leontes, and Miss Merry can be Paulina, one on each side. Our costumes don’t even matter,” he laughed. “It’ll be extra Shakespearian and romantic if Leontes looks like Napoleon, and Paulina looks exactly like a regular modern spinster.”

They settled on Hermione, and everyone agreed the age gap didn’t matter at all. Gwendolen pushed for them to act just a tiny bit of the scene, enough that when the music struck up as a signal, she would step down from her pedestal and walk forward. Leontes wouldn’t embrace her, he’d kneel down and kiss the hem of her gown, and then the curtain would fall. The big antechamber with its folding double doors made a perfect makeshift stage, and every single member of the household, even Jarrett the village carpenter, was roped in to help with the preparations for a show that everyone agreed was bound to be brilliant—after all, old fables tell us that an imitation of something often works out far better than the original.

Gwendolen was extra giddy with excitement about the whole event, because she knew Herr Klesmer was back at Quetcham, and she’d made sure he was on the guest list.

Klesmer showed up in one of his quiet, calm moods, sitting serenely in the audience, replying to every small conversation with soft, benignant grunts that sounded gentle, like a great big lion carefully

*Perigot: As the bonny lass passed by, Willie: Hey ho, bonny lass! P: She rode at me with glancing eye, W: As clear as the crystal glass. P: All as the sunny beam so bright, W: Hey ho, the sunnebeam! P: Glanceth from Phoebus’ face forthright, W: So love into thy heart did stream.*
—SPENSER, *Shepard’s Calendar*

“The kindliest symptom, yet the most alarming crisis in the ticklish state of youth; the nourisher and destroyer of hopeful wits… the servitude above freedom; the gentle mind’s religion; the liberal superstition.”

The first hint of the unforeseen snowstorm brewing in their quiet corner of the shire looked like nothing more than a wisp of bright white cloud, drifting soft against a perfect blue sky. Anna Gascoigne was the only soul who held Rex’s secret close, even though for the first time in their lives her brother had not breathed a word to her about the thing that consumed every waking thought of his. He simply assumed she knew, as siblings always do. And for the first time, Anna could not bring herself to voice the dread that was coiling tighter in her chest every hour.

If Rex had fallen for someone else more than her, it should have stung, but her worry for him drowned out every other feeling. Anna adored their cousin Gwendolen—she would say with perfect, simple sincerity that Gwendolen was always kind to her, that it was only right she should bend to her cousin’s brilliant, unyielding will.
