But Gwendolen was a creature of beauty and mystery to her: some wild, glowing thing, whose appetites no one could guess at, that might at any moment devour all the small, tender things Anna loved best. And now Anna was heavy with a truth she dared not speak aloud: Gwendolen would never, ever care for Rex the way he ached for her. All the soft, gentle, earnest things that mattered most to Anna, to Rex, seemed to slide right off Gwendolen like rain off a polished riding boot. It was far easier to imagine her scorning his devotion than returning it—she was always chasing some grand, extraordinary life far away from their quiet country parsonage. Poor Rex! Their father would be furious if he found out, Rex was far too young to be tangled up in a love so fierce it made him reckless, and Anna had always dreamed she would be his quiet little housekeeper for years and years before any romance ever touched him. How cruel a heart must be, she thought, that could not love her sweet, bright brother back. She was starting to hate her too-fascinating cousin, just a little, at the thought of how much he was about to hurt.

It felt to both of them as if those weeks hummed with a loud, obvious passion anyone could see, plain as day. If anyone had asked, Rex would have said he had no wish to hide it—soon he would have an official engagement to announce to his father, bright and proud. But for the first time he was quiet, secretive not just about his feelings but about little actions Anna had never seen him hide before. She, for her part, went tight with nerves every time her mother or father called her aside to speak privately, half-convinced they were about to confront her about Rex and Gwendolen. But none of the elders noticed a single thing. That roiling, tender young drama played out like a silent pantomime, crystal clear to the two people living it, invisible to the grown-ups who pored over their *Guardian* and *Clerical Gazette*, who saw the young ones’ antics as no more meaningful than the scurrying of busy little ants in the garden path.

“Where are you off to, Rex?” Anna called one soft gray morning, when their father and mother had set off in the carriage for the sessions, and she’d spotted her brother pulling on his thick antigropelos, the closest thing to proper hunting gear he owned.

“Going to watch the hounds throw off at the Three Barns.”

“Are you taking Gwendolen with you?” Anna asked, quiet and timid.

“She told you, did she?”

“No, I just thought… Does Papa know you’re going?”

“Not that I know of. I shouldn’t think he’d bother himself over something so small.”

“You’re taking his horse, then?”

“He knows I ride him whenever I get the chance.”

“Don’t let Gwendolen ride after the hounds, Rex,” Anna begged, a strange, sharp premonition settling heavy on her.

“Why not?” he said, smiling, teasing, already half annoyed.

“Papa and Mamma and Aunt Davilow all say it’s not right for her. They don’t want her doing it.”

“Why would you assume she’d do something she shouldn’t?”

“Gwendolen minds no one when she’s set on something,” Anna bolder now, prickling a little with anger.

“Then she won’t mind me trying to stop her,” Rex joked, brushing off her fear like it was trivial.

“Oh Rex, I can’t bear it. You’re going to make yourself so unhappy.” Anna burst into tears right there in the hall.

“Nannie, Nannie, what on earth is the matter with you?” Rex said, impatient now, hat on and whip in his hand, half out the door.

“She won’t care for you even a little bit—I know she never will!” the poor girl sobbed, whispering the words against her sleeve, every scrap of self-control gone.

Rex flushed bright red, turned on his heel, and hurried out the door without another word, leaving Anna miserable, knowing she’d made a fool of herself and said nothing to change his mind.

He thought of her words as he rode, the way you think of an unpleasant fortune-teller’s warning, even when you laugh it off. But he quickly told himself it was just little Anna being soft, being jealous, being overprotective, and soon he felt guilty for rushing off without soothing her. Every other feeling melted into fierce, stubborn certainty that she was wrong—and that very certainty, laced with the quiet, unspoken doubt he wouldn’t admit even to himself, made him resolve to say the words he’d been holding back for weeks, right there, that very day.

Gwendolen was already mounted, trotting her chestnut mare up and down the avenue, when he reached the gate. She’d brought the groom along as a backup, just in case Rex was late and she had to ride out alone, but as soon as she spotted him she dismissed the man, and the two of them set off free as birds. Gwendolen was giddy, bright as a spark, and Rex thought he had never seen her half so lovely: the clean lines of her riding dress showed off her long white throat, the soft curve of her cheek and chin perfectly. He could not imagine a more perfect girl. To a young lover like Rex, goodness, truth, and beauty were all wrapped up in her, alive and glowing right in front of him. Any neutral onlooker would have thought it only fair that Gwendolen should fall just as hard for him—his face was open, warm, handsome, none of that unnameable, sharp, almost dangerous trace of something wild that made people hesitate a little before fully surrendering to Gwendolen’s charm.

It was a perfect January morning, no threat of rain, the pale gray sky soft and kind over the mild winter land: grassy lane edges dusted with frost, hedgerows strung with bright red berries and full of tiny twittering birds, the bare purple branches of elm trees stretching overhead, the furrows in the fields rich dark brown. The horses’ hooves made a light, musical chime in the quiet, weaving in and out of the sound of their laughter. She teased him for his drab, un-fancy hunting gear, the exact opposite of a dandy, and he drank in that laughter like it was wine. The freshness of the morning tangled with the freshness of their youth, every laugh, every quick glance between them bubbling up from a hidden spring of joy. For them, the whole world was still bright, unwritten, still morning, inside and out. It’s hard not to wish, for just a moment, that the two of them could have promised each other everything right there, that they could have kept that bright joy forever, never to break. That goodness Rex believed in so fiercely was real there, tender as a green new blade of grass, ready to be crushed by one sharp gust of wind, one moment of careless choice.

“Anna’s got it into her head you’re going to ride after the hounds today,” Rex said, the words feeling far more daring, far closer to the big confession he was building up to, than he’d planned.

“Did she?” Gwendolen laughed. “What a little clairvoyant she is!”

“Will you?” Rex asked, half convinced she’d never go against what all the grown-ups had forbidden.

“I don’t know. I never know what I’m going to do till I get there. Clairvoyants are always wrong, aren’t they? They predict what’s likely, and what’s likely is always so dreadfully dull. I do the unlikely thing.”

“Ah, that’s a secret then,” Rex grinned. “Once I know what everyone else would do, I know you’ll do the opposite. That’s a pattern all its own. I’ll be able to predict you perfectly soon. You’ll never surprise me again.”

“Easy,” she laughed, her voice like chimes. “I’d turn right around and do the boring, expected thing just to catch you out.”

“See? You can’t escape being predictable. Contrariness is the strongest pattern of all. You have to give up this plan of yours.”

“No, I won’t,” she said, tossing her head, and every line of her pose was so perfect, so charming, that not a single sharp, arrogant word of hers landed wrong for Rex, it all felt like soft, pretty banter he was desperate to steer toward the confession he wanted to make. “My plan is to do exactly what pleases me.”

“Can you only ever feel what pleases you?” he asked, gentle.

“Of course not. Other people ruin that. If the world was nicer, we’d all only ever feel happy things. Girls’ lives are so stupid. We never get to do what we want.”

“I thought it was the opposite for men,” Rex said. “We’re forced to do all the hard, boring things, we get bored out of our minds, we get hurt. But if a man loved a girl more than anything else in the whole world, he’d do whatever she wanted. You’d get your own way, in the end.”

“I don’t believe that. I’ve never met a married woman who got her own way at all.”

“What sort of things would you want to do, then?” Rex asked, genuinely curious, leaning a little toward her in the saddle.

“Oh, I don’t know!” she said, flighty, giddy, her words spilling off her tongue before she could think them over. “Go to the North Pole, or ride steeplechases, or be a queen in the East like Lady Hester Stanhope!”

“You don’t mean you never want to get married, ever?”

“No, I didn’t say that. Only when I do marry, I won’t live the boring little life every other married woman lives.”

“You could do absolutely anything you wanted, if you married a man who loved you more dearly than anything else in the world,” Rex blurted out, his heart thudding, forgetting all his carefully planned words. “I know someone who loves you like that.”

“Oh, don’t talk about Mr. Middleton, for heaven’s sake!” Gwendolen said, her cheeks flushing bright red, sharp and sudden. “That’s Anna’s favorite story. I hear the hounds! Let’s go on.”

She kicked her chestnut mare into a canter, and Rex had no choice but to follow. Still, he felt encouraged. Gwendolen knew perfectly well her cousin was in love with her, but she never thought it mattered, not really. She’d never once felt that sharp, burning pain of loving someone who might not love you back. She wanted Rex’s little gentle devotion to fill up the rest of his vacation at Pennicote, no messy confessions to spoil the fun, and she was half repulsed, half thrilled at the thought of him saying the words out loud to her. For all her love of being adored, there was a sharp, unyielding maidenly fierceness in her that flinched from direct declarations.

But all those thoughts vanished the second they reached the Three Barns, the air thick with the hounds’ baying, the horses pawing at the ground, the hunt men shouting greetings. Several of the local gentry knew Gwendolen, and she laughed and chatted with all of them, so Rex never got the chance to say another private word to her. The thrill of the chase wrapped around her like a cloak. She’d never ridden to hounds before, she’d only ever said she wanted to, which made her mother fear for her safety, and her uncle the rector declare it was unseemly for a lady of good position—no one of quality in their part of Wessex followed the hunt, he said, except Mrs. Gadsby, the yeoman captain’s wife who’d once been a kitchenmaid and still talked like one. That last argument had held Gwendolen back for weeks, caught between her desire to prove she was free and her horror of being lumped in with Mrs. Gadsby.

But today, none of the respectable local ladies who usually came to watch the throw-off were there to stop themselves from following, and Mrs. Gadsby was nowhere to be seen, either. There was nothing to hold Gwendolen back from that wild, thrumming animal excitement: the hounds’ baying, the horses’ stamping, the men’s loud calls, flashes of bright hunting coats against the soft gray and green of the countryside. She felt half centaur, half wild creature, giddy with the thrill of the chase to come.

Rex would have enjoyed it all too, if he could have stayed close to Gwendolen, instead of being stuck watching her laugh and talk with a dozen other men on their lively, fast horses that swerved all around him, keeping him away.

“Glad to see you out this fine morning, Miss Harleth,” Lord Brackenshaw called out to her, the middle-aged peer in his stained pink hunting coat, easy and lazy as if nothing in the world could ever bother him. “We’re in for a first-rate run. Pity you haven’t joined us before. Ever tried that pretty little chestnut of yours over a ditch? I don’t suppose you’d be scared, would you?”

“Not the least in the world,” Gwendolen said, and it was true—she never feared anything when she was on horseback, in the thick of fun. “I’ve taken him over rails and small ditches all the time, near—”

“Ah, there we go,” Lord Brackenshaw said, casually reining his horse away, as the hounds gave tongue all at once. The whole field surged forward like the earth itself was spinning them along, and Gwendolen went with it, without a single backward glance at Rex. He followed right after her, without thinking. Could he let her ride off alone? Under any other circumstances he would have loved the run, but his head was still full of the confession he’d never gotten to say, and he was stuck on his father’s old slow gray nag, Primrose, a good steady horse for a parson, but too old, too stiff for a hard gallop after the hounds.

Gwendolen on her spirited little chestnut was right up with the front of the pack, feeling as invincible as a goddess, never once stopping to think she might get hurt, never once stopping to wonder where Rex was. If she had, she would have laughed at the silly picture of him falling further and further behind, craning his neck looking for gates. She never saw him at all, soon—Rex was so far back he might as well have been miles away. He was searching for a gate along a newly repaired lane when Primrose stumbled, went down hard, broke his knees, and threw Rex clean over his head.

Luckily, the blacksmith’s son Joel Dagge, who followed the hunt on foot (a habit even some frivolous people thought was improper) was trailing at the very back, and saw the whole thing. He ran over to help, and Rex was badly stunned, the first full jolt of sensation when he came to being white-hot pain in his shoulder. Joel was the perfect person to have around: he knew exactly what was wrong with the horse, exactly how far they were to the nearest public house and to the rectory, and assured Rex his shoulder was just dislocated, nothing worse.

“Lord, sir, let me shove that back in for you!” he said cheerfully. “I seen Nash the bone-setter do it a hundred times, done it myself for our little Sally twice over. Shoulders is all the same. If you trust me, hold real still, I’ll have it right in no time.”

“Go on then, old fellow,” Rex said, gritting his teeth, and Joel popped the shoulder back into place, even if it hurt so bad Rex went pale as a sheet. Joel helped him limp the rest of the way home as fast as they could, Rex all the while far more worried that Gwendolen might have had an accident than he was about his own bruises or the broken horse. He told himself everyone would look after her, someone would make sure she got home safe.

Mr. Gascoigne was already back at the rectory, writing letters in his study, when Rex stumbled in, pale and bruised, his face still as handsome as ever, just a little sheepish and hurt. Rex was his secret favorite, the spitting image of him as a young man, though he never showed any partiality to the boy, in fact he treated him with extra strictness. He’d already heard from Anna that Rex had ridden out to the hunt meet with Gwendolen.

“What happened?” he said sharply, not laying down his pen.

“I’m very sorry, sir. Primrose fell and broke his knees.”

“Where on earth did you take him?” Mr. Gascoigne said, his voice tight with severity, though he rarely lost his temper.

“To the Three Barns, to watch the hounds throw off.”

“And you were fool enough to follow them when they ran?”

“Yes, sir. I wasn’t trying to jump fences, but the horse stepped in a hole.”

“Did you hurt yourself, at least? That’ll be some small penance.”

“I dislocated my shoulder, but the blacksmith’s son popped it back in for me. Just a little banged up, that’s all.”

What name doth Joy most borrow
When life is fair? “To-morrow.”
What name doth best fit Sorrow
In young despair? “To-morrow.”

A far heavier, longer ache settled over the rectory than any small passing mischief of the younger Gascoigne children. Rex stumbled home at dusk, and threw himself face-down on his bed so still, so unresponsive to his mother’s soft calls, that they thought for hours he might not rouse at all. When he did stir the next day, it was only to show the sharp, burning signs of a fever that clung to his bones—too deep to be put down to cold, too heavy to be cured with broth and peppermint. His planned trip to Southampton was forgotten entirely. The whole household turned to tending the boy who no longer wanted to get better: the bright, teasing, apple-tree-climbing Rex who had always been the first to laugh through dinner, the first to carry the smallest of his siblings on his shoulders, was gone. In his place was a hollow, dull-eyed creature who met every affectionate offer with a hoarse murmur of “Let me alone.”

His father, the rector, saw past the fever to the heartbreak under it. He knew this sort of hurt would pass faster if it was not poked and prodded with lectures. He would slip in to sit by Rex’s bed in silence for ten minutes at a time, then press a gentle, warm palm to the boy’s blank forehead, and murmur “God bless you, my boy” before he left. The younger children, Warham and the toddlers, would peek wide-eyed around the bedroom door at this unrecognizable version of their lively brother, only to be shooed away immediately by Anna’s urgent shushing and wagging finger.

Anna never left his side, not for longer than it took to fetch a fresh glass of water. Rex would not squeeze her small hand back, but he did not pull away, and that was enough. Her heart was split half in raw, aching sympathy for him, half in hot, quiet resentment of her cousin Gwendolen. “Perhaps it is wicked of me,” she would whisper to herself over and over, her throat tight, “but I do not think I can ever love her again.” Even Mrs. Gascoigne found she could not hold her anger at Gwendolen back entirely, venting to her husband one evening as they sewed by the fire: “I know I ought to be glad she does not care for the poor boy, Henry. But really—she is hard. She has the heart of a coquette. She must have let him believe she cared, or this disappointment would never have settled into him so deep. And poor Fanny is utterly blind to what her daughter is.”

Mr. Gascoigne cut her off firmly, his tone gentle but unyielding. “The less said on that point the better, Nancy. I ought to have seen it coming myself. Be thankful no worse harm came of it. Let the whole thing die out as quickly as possible—especially where Gwendolen is concerned. Let it be as if it never happened at all.” For the rector, the quiet truth was that they had dodged a disaster: if Gwendolen had returned Rex’s feelings, the near-penniless girl with no fortune would have tied his son to a marriage that would have dragged his whole future down, and there would have been no way to untangle it.

Then one crisp, frosty morning, Rex asked for his bath, and dressed himself slowly, carefully, in his usual clothes. Anna nearly shook with excitement at the sound of his footsteps on the stairs, and ran to meet him at the bottom. He gave her a faint, wobbly smile, so sad on his pale face that she had to bite her lip to keep from crying. “Nannie,” he said softly, taking her hand to lead her into the drawing room. When his mother came to kiss his cheek, he mumbled, “What a plague I’ve been, haven’t I?”

He sat by the bow window, staring out at the lawn crusted white with hoarfrost, the sun sending thin, fleeting streaks of light across the shrubs that looked, Anna thought, exactly like that sad, fragile smile on his face. Rex felt as if he had woken up in a whole new strange world, where none of his old plans—his Oxford rowing matches, his summer trips, the books he had been looking forward to studying—mattered at all. Anna sat nearby pretending to sew, but never taking her eyes off him, her whole chest tight with longing to ease his pain.

Down the lane that ran past the garden hedge, a wagon piled high with oak timber trundled past. The great shire horses strained their muscles, their hooves slipping a little on the frost, and the driver cracked his whip before running along beside them to guide the lead horse’s head, terrified it would swerve and tip the whole heavy load. Rex stared until the last quivering trunk of timber vanished around the bend, then stood to pace the room once, twice, before sinking back down beside Anna. She dragged a little stool over to tuck herself against his knee, looking up at him with a quiet, pleading expression that said speak to me.

“I’ll tell you what I’m thinking of, Nannie,” he said. “I’m going to Canada. Or somewhere wild, like that. I’ll build a hut, clear the land for miles, work till I’m too tired to think, and have nothing but wide open quiet all around me.”

“Oh, Rex, not forever!” she gasped, big tears rolling down her cheeks.

“Where else would I go? I can’t stay here. I’ll never get the spirit back to study for Oxford, not after this. I’ll waste father’s money, I’ll fail my exams, and I’ll be miserable every single time I turn a corner and half-expect to see her.”

“Then I’m coming too,” Anna said, already certain. “I can make the fires, mend the clothes, bake bread. I’ll learn to churn butter before we sail. It’ll be like when we were little, remember? We built a tent out of the drugget rug in the attic, used our little toy plates and pretended we were settlers. It’ll be better than any of the silly parties they make us go to here.”

They talked it over until the sun was high, until they had worked out every small, practical detail that made sense to their young, heartsick minds. They agreed to go to the rector together, in his study when he was alone—they would break the news to their mother later, once their father had consented, so they would not distress her for no reason.

Mr. Gascoigne smiled when he saw them, glad to have Rex up and walking again. “Sit down, both of you,” he said, gesturing to the chairs by his desk. It was a striking little group, all three of them sharing the same distinct family face: the straight, high brow, the nose that might have been aquiline if it had just tilted a little higher, the short upper lip, the strong, square chin, even the same warm, deep set tone to their eyes. The grey-haired rector was solid, authoritative, the deep line between his brows deepening when he turned his full attention to you; Rex looked like a ghost of his father’s youth, stripped of all his father’s plans and composure, raw and heartsick; Anna was a tiny, soft copy of Rex, her face shifting in perfect mirror to every change of his expression, as if the two of them shared a single soul.

“You know what’s been wrong with me, father,” Rex began, and the rector nodded. “I’m no good for life here anymore. I won’t go back to Oxford. I can’t make myself read a single page. I just want your consent to take a small outfit, and go work the land in the colonies. I’ll earn my own bread, and I won’t be a burden on any of you.”

“And I’m coming too, papa!” Anna burst out, climbing onto his knee to nudge his arm. “Rex will need someone to keep house for him. We’ll never get married, either of us. We won’t cost you a penny, and you’ll have more money left to raise the younger boys. It’s perfect.”

Mr. Gascoigne did not smile, but he held Anna tight against his side, gentle, as he turned back to Rex. “You will admit my years of experience give me a better sense of what is good for you than your current grief-stricken state, don’t you?” he asked. Rex nodded, because it was true, even if it felt like a lie right now. “Then I am telling you: this feeling that your whole life is broken, that you can never go back to the path you were on? It will pass. If you run off to the colonies now, throwing away all the years of education we poured into you, you will wake up six months from now, or a year, and regret it for the rest of your life. You have no right to throw your whole future away over a heartache that most men work through by the time they are your age. I have arranged for you to miss the next term at Oxford. You will spend the next two months resting, no studying, no pressure. You will not go to the colonies.”

Rex had no arguments left to fight his father with. He knew, deep down, that part of him still cared for his family, still hated the thought of hurting his mother, but the ache in his chest was too big to ignore. “I’ll try,” he said at last, quiet. “But I can’t promise it will work.” He left the study without another word.

The rector held Anna back as she tried to run after him. “Now, you be as quiet as a mouse about this,” he told her. “Never mention the colonies, never mention what upset him, not to anyone, ever. It will all blow over soon enough.”

Anna nodded, but she had a little secret, tucked away where no one could see it. She was bitterly disappointed that she would not get to run off to the wilds with Rex, escape all the wide, bulky crinolines that were so fashionable that year—so big, everyone joked, that churches and ballrooms and even carriages would have to be expanded to fit them—escape all the stuffy dinner parties where she had to make small talk with strangers she did not care about. Anna Gascoigne’s small fourteen-year-old frame only ever needed the smallest size of those new giant skirts, and even that felt far too heavy for the quiet, wild little girl who dreamed of nothing more than her and Rex’s half-forgotten play tent, somewhere far away from the ache of unrequited love.

I’ll tell thee, Berthold, what men’s hopes are like:
A silly child that, quivering with joy,
Would cast its little mimic fishing-line
Baited with loadstone for a bowl of toys
In the salt ocean.

Eight months after the Davilow and Harleth household settled into Offendene, as the sweet warm haze of the following June settled over the Wessex countryside, an electric little rumor spread through every village, market town, and country estate within driving distance of Wanchester. It had nothing to do with the distant American war, but it sent ripples of bright, greedy excitement through every class: corn factors, brewers, horse dealers, and saddlers all praised it as proof of how valuable a landed aristocracy was to English prosperity, the blacksmith in little Diplow hamlet rubbed his hands over the promise of steady work for years to come, laborers’ wives daydreamed of their quick ten- and twelve-year-old boys getting steady, well-paid jobs as footmen in livery, and local farmers, even with a hint of gruff, bitter reserve, admitted they might finally get a fair price for a rick of old hay or a wagon of straw at last.

If the working classes had such small, bright hopes tied to the news, the gentry’s excitement ran even deeper, tangled up in all the soft, glittering pleasures of social life—and the rumor centered on marriage, that grand intersection of business and joy that makes even the most respectable household hold quiet, secret dreams. When news of a royal visit circulates, every city man tucks a quiet fantasy of a knighthood under his nightcap; and this news set off half a dozen floating, half-formed visions of a perfect, prestigious match across half a dozen well-bred drawing rooms.

The news was this: Diplow Hall, Sir Hugo Mallinger’s grand estate that had stood shuttered for two whole years, its rows of white window shutters half-closed like the squinting eye of a slumbering giant turning its back on its ancient elms, lily-fringed pool, and deer-dappled lawns, was being prepped for a new tenant. All summer, and through the entire hunting season, the house and its stables would be occupied in proper, lavish style. Not by Sir Hugo himself, but by his nephew, Mallinger Grandcourt, the presumptive heir to the baronetcy—since Sir Hugo and his wife had only had daughters. Fortune had stacked every possible advantage in young Grandcourt’s favor: the baronetcy would come through his father’s line, and his mother’s noble blood meant that if a handful of distant, little-known relatives happened to pass, he would inherit a barony and a seat in the House of Lords itself.

Fate is uneven in its gifts, of course, but even the most strict moralist could not argue that a man with that kind of fortune and peerage potential would not have a line of eager young women waiting to accept his hand—no one could blame those around him for quietly daydreaming that their daughter or ward might be the one to claim that title. Some readers may protest that no respectable, well-bred people would ever build entire marriage prospects on nothing more than the news that a wealthy unmarried man was moving within ten miles of their home, that such scheming is not real human nature. But we speak only of a small, tight-knit corner of Wessex, where every household was on visiting terms with the peerage, and every family had their own quiet stakes in the game.

Take the Arrowpoints, for example, who owned the sprawling, beautiful estate at Quetcham. No one could accuse them of being sordid about their daughter Catherine’s prospects, not when they would leave her a fortune of at least half a million pounds. But they were affectionate, anxious for her to have the perfect position in life—she had already turned down Lord Slogan, an otherwise perfectly respectable Irish peer whose estate was only lacking proper drainage and enough residents to pay its rents—and they found themselves quietly wondering, with more than casual curiosity, if Mr Grandcourt was handsome, of sound constitution, virtuous, or at least reformed from any wild youthful habits, and if his liberal-conservative politics were not too wildly extreme. They never wished anyone dead, of course, but they found themselves thinking his ascension to the peerage would be a most welcome event.

If the wealthy Arrowpoints ruminated this way, it is no surprise that the same thoughts crept into the mind of Reverend Gascoigne, the local rector. For all his clerical good sense, he was still a parent and guardian, already overburdened with the task of reining in his wild, willful niece Gwendolen, who would not be guided by bit, bridle, or any amount of judicious, gentle advice.

Of course, no one spoke these thoughts out loud. No one asked a neighbor if they had reliable intel on Grandcourt as a possible husband for their charming daughter, no one admitted out loud that they dared to hope for such a glittering match. Mrs Gascoigne only let one quiet remark slip to her sister Mrs Davilow: “It would be a true mercy, Fanny, if that girl Gwendolen were settled well and properly.” Soft, mild Mrs Davilow caught the faint hint of criticism of her darling daughter in her sister’s tone, and said nothing out loud, though she thought privately, No one will get her to marry just to suit you—her gentle temper turned sharp the moment anyone dared question Gwendolen’s perfection. Even to his own wife, Mr Gascoigne never admitted he had thought for a second that Grandcourt might meet Gwendolen at the upcoming annual Archery Meeting and be immediately captivated, though Mrs Gascoigne’s own mind was running along exactly that same track.

When she casually asked her husband what kind of man Grandcourt was, the rector brushed off the question: he thought it foolish, even improper, to pry into the wild youth of a man of such high birth and vast wealth, whose position excused a dozen small vices that would have ruined a man of lesser station. Any rich man who gambles and stops before he loses his own fortune, he reasoned, is practically a model of reformed virtue—no one would subject a future baronet to the same suspicious vetting one might give a new applicant for the position of footman.

Mrs Davilow, for all her quiet pride in Gwendolen, could not help but daydream a little too. She pictured Grandcourt first as a handsome, accomplished, perfect gentleman who would make any girl a wonderful husband—then stopped to wonder if that would be enough for Gwendolen, whose tastes were so wild and unreadable no one could guess what might catch her fancy, let alone win her love. She even found herself thinking that it would not matter if Gwendolen was not head over heels in love, if only she would accept the right, good husband, spared all the hardship and grief that had marked Mrs Davilow’s own married life. But she dared never whisper any of these daydreams to Gwendolen herself, not after the heartbreak of Rex’s farewell: she knew the moment Gwendolen guessed her family was pushing her toward a man, she would immediately detest him on principle.

The only safe topic was the dress Gwendolen would wear to the Archery Meeting. They had settled on soft white cashmere, accented with a touch of pale green that perfectly set off her fair complexion, and a matching green feather for her hat. Gwendolen was striking a pose before the mirror, pretending to draw her bow, when she suddenly turned to her mother with a glint of wicked, playful glee in her eyes. “I pity every other girl at that archery gathering,” she laughed. “All of them already mooning over Mr Grandcourt. Not a single one of them stands a chance.”

Mrs Davilow froze, caught completely off guard. Gwendolen spun around to face her, grinning wider. “You know they don’t, mamma. You, and uncle, and aunt—all three of you are already planning for him to fall madly in love with me.”

Piqued into a quick, playful defense, Mrs Davilow said, “Oh, my dear, that is not certain at all. Miss Arrowpoint has charms you do not.”

“I know, but her charms demand effort and thought from a man,” Gwendolen laughed. “My arrow will pierce him right through the heart before he has time to think. He will declare himself my absolute slave. I shall send him round the whole world to bring me back the wedding ring of a perfectly happy woman. While he’s gone, all the distant relatives standing between him and his title will die of convenient, separate diseases. He will come back as Lord Grandcourt, but without that ring I asked for. He will throw himself at my feet, and I shall laugh at him. He will rise, stung by my mockery, and I shall laugh harder. He will call for his horse and ride straight to Quetcham, only to find Miss Arrowpoint just eloped with a penniless musician, Mrs Arrowpoint tearing her cap off in a rage, and Mr Arrowpoint standing there gaping. Exit Lord Grandcourt, defeated, who rides back to Diplow and changes his clothes, like that character in the French play, and never bothers me again.”

No young witch ever had half of Gwendolen’s sharp, unreadable intuition. You could sit on your secret and pretend you were innocent, and she would see right away from the tiny twitch of your eye exactly what you were hiding. It was as useless as locking your front door to keep out the rain. Mrs Davilow, half amused, half unsettled, asked, “What sort of man do you imagine him to be, then, Gwendolen?”

Gwendolen pressed one finger to her lip, frowning playfully in thought, then held the finger out sharply. “Short,” she declared. “Just a little taller than my shoulder. He tries to look taller by twirling his mustache and growing his beard long. He wears a monocle in his right eye to look distinguished. He has very strong opinions about the cut of his waistcoat, but is vague and wishy-washy about the weather, which he will try to draw me out on. He will stare at me nonstop the entire time we talk, and that monocle will make him pull terrible, cross-eyed faces every time he tries to smile at me in a flattering way. I shall cast my eyes down demurely, and he will think I am flattered by his attention. That night, I will dream I am looking at the grossly magnified face of some giant insect, and the next morning he will show up at our door to propose to me. And then the rest of my silly little play will follow.”

“That is a portrait of some man you have already met,” Mrs Davilow protested. “Mr Grandcourt may be a perfectly delightful young man, for all you know.”

“Oh, of course he will,” Gwendolen agreed airily, setting down her best hat and turning it thoughtfully in her hands. “I already know he has a stable full of hunters and racehorses, a grand house in London, and two country seats—one with battlements, and one with a wide veranda. I am quite sure, with a little bit of well-timed murder of those inconvenient relatives, he will get his peerage easily enough.”

There was a thread of sharp, genuine irony tangled in that playful speech, and it made poor literal, gentle Mrs Davilow very uncomfortable. “Do not talk like that, child, for heaven’s sake,” she pleaded, her brow creased with distress. “All those novels you read fill your head with such terrible ideas. When your sister and I were your age, we never knew a thing about wickedness. It was far better that way.”

“Why did you not bring me up to be that innocent, then, mamma?” Gwendolen teased—then saw the crushed look on her mother’s face, and the sob rising in her throat, and immediately felt terrible. She tossed her hat aside, knelt at her mother’s feet, and clung to her knees. “Mamma, mamma, I was only joking. I did not mean a single word of it.”

“How could I?” Mrs Davilow sobbed, the tears spilling over as she tried to speak. “Your will was always far too strong for me—even if everything else in our lives had been different.”

Gwendolen understood perfectly. “Dear mamma, I am not complaining about you,” she said, wiping her mother’s tears away gently with her handkerchief. “I love you. Besides, I am far more charming than your generation of girls. You all must have been so dreadfully bored.” That soft, cajoling tone always soothed her mother, as it had a dozen times after similar little clashes. Both of them learned to avoid the topic of Grandcourt entirely after that, for Gwendolen hated that prick of guilt she felt whenever she hurt her mother, and Mrs Davilow hated even the faintest hint that she was pushing Gwendolen down a path her daughter did not want.

Gwendolen had grown more defiant lately, anyway. Anna Gascoigne still could not hide her quiet hurt and resentment over what had happened to Rex, even though neither of them dared speak his name, and that cold distance only made Gwendolen more determined to keep her own freedom. “I see now why all girls are so glad to get married,” she told her mother one day. “It is the only way to escape the endless expectation that you will spend every moment of your life pleasing everyone but yourself.”

Even though thirty square miles of Wessex was full of handsome, eligible young men who loved to sit and chat with lively, easy Gwendolen, not a single one of them had actually proposed to her in the eight months since she arrived at Offendene. For all the local gossip about how charming she was, no man had worked up the courage to formally ask for her hand. But that did not stop the whole neighborhood from assuming that the new, far more eligible Mr Grandcourt would do exactly what all the local young men had not. Likelihood in this world, after all, is nothing more than the reflection of what people badly want to be true. And the Arrowpoints, for their part, did not care at all whether Gwendolen made a brilliant match—they were completely convinced Grandcourt would see no one but their own Catherine the moment he arrived.

_1st Gent._ What defines a worthy woman? Sir, you need only ask the taste of every unmarried man of standing. This planet’s store of iron, cotton, wool, chemicals—every raw substance bent to our craft, takes shape to answer what the market demands. The pulse of public preference rises and falls by a rule as sublime as any natural law. Our daughters must become wives, and wives must be exactly what men choose to value; a man’s taste is the true test of a woman’s worth. You mark that phrase? Is it not fine, its meaning light and sharp, poised perfectly between its t’s and s’s?
_2nd Gent._ Nay, turn it on its head, then—what test do we use for that so-called “fine taste”? Is a perfect menu today what the ancient Roman epicures insisted a gentleman must eat to earn the right to dine well?

---

Brackenshaw Park stretched its gentle green hilltops far over the neighboring valley, all the way to the hazy eastern downs and the broad, sun-drenched sweep of farmland that rose like a soft embroidered curtain to the west. The castle, perched on the highest rise of the clustered hills, was hewn from rough limestone, its walls streaked with dark lichen dust and rain-worn streaks that caught the light in shifting, dappled patterns. Masses of beech and fir sheltered its north face, spilling down the slopes in thick, dark clumps like flocks wandering toward the silver gleam of the lake below.

The archery ground occupied a sun-warmed table-land at the far end of the park, shielded from the blustery south-west by tall elms and a thick wall of hollies that kept the gravel promenade and newly mown turf where the targets stood wrapped in soft, golden afternoon shade. The white Archery Hall, with its pillared arcade out front, glowed like a little classical temple against the lush green backdrop to the north. It could not have made a lovelier frame for the groups of ladies drifting across the grass, bowing and turning their necks with the slow, easy grace of lilies that had suddenly decided to walk. Even after the Wanchester military band fell silent, the air hummed with sweet sound: layered musical laughs, a harmony of light, friendly chatter that rose in peaks of gentle excitement and sank to soft, warm murmurs.

No outdoor amusement could have been freer of the noisy, jostling crowds that spoiled most modern pleasures. This archery meeting was famously exclusive: only pre-vetted friends of club members were granted entry, all to keep numbers limited enough that the dinner and ball to be held that night in the castle would be perfectly comfortable. The only non-gentry guests admitted were Lord Brackenshaw’s tenants and their families, mostly the young mothers and girls, who brought their little ones along to gape. The tenant men passed the time laying silly, low-stakes bets on their favourite archers, but the young farm girls all daydreamed over which of the gaily dressed fine ladies they would choose to be, if fairy fortune granted their wish.

Most of those country girls would likely have picked the plump, pink-cheeked, fashionably fair-haired beauty they saw fluttering her fan across the lawn—but every unmarried man in the crowd immediately and unanimously agreed no girl there outshone Gwendolen Harleth.

Small wonder she was happier that July afternoon than she had ever been in her life. To the soul that craves pre-eminence, even small triumphs taste sweet: the slave proud to be bought first at market, the barn-door rooster preening over his superior strut, each feels that quiet, warm comfort of being the clear favourite. But for perfect joy, the world outside has to match the fire inside. That perfect alignment had come for Gwendolen that day.

Could any weapon on earth be prettier for a woman to hold than a bow and arrow? The poses it inspires are full of grace and quiet, coiled power, the sharp, bright focus of marksmanship stripped of all ugly association with bloodshed. The old British masculine urge to “kill something” no longer attaches to archery; invading armies don’t fight under clouds of arrows any more, poisoned darts survive only in rhetoric or far-off, harmless jungles. Archery has no stench of gunpowder, breaks no shins, breeds no brutish athletes. Its only risk is missing your mark, and the gentle sting of that disappointment is more than enough to make a spirited girl focus all her skill on the shot.

Even the prizes at Brackenshaw were perfectly designed to be honour, not vulgar gain: gold and silver arrows, gold and silver stars, to be pinned to gowns and worn as badges of skill, then later passed on to the next champion. They had all the romance of a victor’s wreath without the inconvenience of a heavy circlet of flowers wilting in the heat of the ballroom later that night. No ridiculous, awkward misstep could possibly mar the day.

The weather cooperated perfectly, too: soft mild warmth, not a breath of wind to ruffle a lady’s hair, tug at her gown, or send an arrow veering off course. Every shot got a fair, unhindered run, and when the whole group trooped out to the targets to pull their arrows free, that slow, laughing promenade of bright, happy young people moving as one toward a shared goal was a sight to make even the most cynical spectator sigh. Gwendolen moved through that crowd like a golden Calypso among her nymphs, every turn of her wrist, every tilt of her head, drawing admiring eyes.
“That girl is like a high-mettled racehorse,” Lord Brackenshaw murmured to young Clintock, a visiting guest, as he watched her.
“First chop, and devilishly pretty on top of that,” the elegant young man replied. “I’ve never seen her look half so good.”

She never had. Her face glowed with unshadowed young pleasure, no spark of discontent marring her mood. Sure of her own success, she felt warm and kind to everyone around her, fully satisfied with the entire world. The fact that she was not the highest-ranked guest there, not a wealthy heiress like Miss Arrowpoint, only made it sweeter that she was effortlessly outshining those advantages. She would not have traded her little family group for any other at the party: her mother looked gentle and dignified enough to pass for a duchess, her uncle and aunt Gascoigne and little cousin Anna made perfectly respectable, pleasant figures, and Gwendolen was too full of bright, unshakable confidence in herself to feel the slightest twinge of jealousy even when she saw that the skilled archeress Miss Arrowpoint was keeping pace with the best of the group.

Even the surprise reappearance of the formidable Herr Klesmer, the famed musician who had once dismissed her singing as trivial, amused her. What great maestro short of Apollo himself could look natural at a countryside archery meet? Gwendolen’s eyes glinted with playful satire as she watched the Arrowpoint party make their entrance, the contrast between Klesmer’s dramatic, foreign air and the staid, uniform reserve of the surrounding English gentry sharp enough to make even a far more serious person smile. The average English gentleman of the class all around them had a easy, unobtrusive way of dressing and moving, that avoided anything that could be labelled “inspired” as a mark of bad taste. They stared at Klesmer: wild thick mane of hair flowing back from his sharp, handsome features, his tall thin frame clad in deliberately dramatic foreign cuts of clothes that looked absurd under a standard stiff English top hat, which sat perched on his head as if he had put it on for a joke. Draped in a loose Renaissance robe and wearing a Florentine beretta, he could have stood beside Leonardo da Vinci himself—but in ill-fitting trousers that drew the wrong kind of eyes, and under that absurd top hat, his fiery, curious glances around the crowd looked more ridiculous than majestic. Many guests only ever saw Klesmer in candlelit concert halls, so this unexpected daylight appearance at an exclusive country party left some guests holding back laughs, and others side-eyeing the Arrowpoints for being so bad at manners as to bring an unorthodox genius into their polite set.
“Good lord, what a ridiculous figure those artistic types always cut!” young Clintock whispered to Gwendolen. “Look at him, bowing with his hand pressed to his heart to Lady Brackenshaw—Mrs. Arrowpoint’s feathered hat only just clears his shoulder.”
“You are one of the profane,” Gwendolen teased back, her eyes dancing. “You are blind to the majesty of genius. Herr Klesmer fills me with awe. I feel crushed in his presence, all my courage oozing right out of me.”
“Ah, right, you’re a patron of his music.”
“Not at all,” she laughed lightly. “It is he who knows all about mine, and thinks it pitiable.” The man’s sharp verdict on her singing had become a private joke to her, ever since he had complimented her rare, striking physical presence instead.
“Glad to hear it. His music isn’t for my ears, either.”
“Oh, you are far too kind. But look how well Miss Arrowpoint looks today! That gold dress would make a perfect allegorical painting.”
“A little too splendid, though, don’t you think?” Gwendolen said. “Too much like the personification of Wealth in a tableau.” It sounded a touch malicious, but it was nothing more than a bubble of playful fun. She had no wish for anyone else to fade into the background—she believed her own good fortune, and her own skill, would carry her right to the top.

That belief only grew stronger as the scoring went on. Her scores were startlingly high for a first-year member, and each new success lit a brighter spark under her. She trod on air, every pleasant possibility seeming within her reach. The day was perfect enough all on its own, she did not have to wonder what would come next to keep her life at that thrilling, sharp pitch.
“Where do the scores stand, do you suppose?” Lady Brackenshaw asked her husband, from her seat as lady paramount, flanked by her two small girls and a stout little boy. “It looks to me as if Miss Harleth is on track to win the gold arrow.”
“Gad, I think she will, if she keeps this pace!” Lord Brackenshaw exclaimed. “She’s running Juliet Fenn right hard. Unheard of for a first year. Catherine Arrowpoint isn’t hitting her usual mark,” he added, turning to the heiress’s mother sitting nearby. “But she won the gold arrow last year. Luck plays a part even in these games of skill, after all. Gives the rest of us a fighting chance.”
“Catherine would be overjoyed to see someone else win,” Mrs. Arrowpoint insisted, all simpering dignity. “She is so magnanimous. It was entirely her idea that we bring Herr Klesmer today instead of Canon Stopley, who was so looking forward to coming. She would have much rather brought the Canon for her own pleasure, but she is always thinking of others. I told her bringing someone so far outside our usual set wasn’t quite proper, but she said ‘Genius itself isn’t proper. It comes into the world to make new rules.’ And one has to admit that, of course.”
“Ay, to be sure,” Lord Brackenshaw said, waving the topic away dismissively. “For my part, I’m not magnanimous at all. I hate losing. But confound it, I never get the chance these days. I’m old and idle. The young ones beat me every time. As old Nestor said, the gods don’t give us everything at once. I was a young fellow once, and now I’m an old and supposedly wise one. At least I’m old, which is a gift everyone gets if they live long enough, so no one need be jealous of me.” He smiled comfortably at his wife.
“Oh, my lord, neighbours who have known each other twenty years don’t talk about age,” Mrs. Arrowpoint protested. “As the Tuscans say, years exist only to let houses be leased. But where is our new neighbour? I thought Mr. Grandcourt was supposed to be here today.”
“Ah, right, he was supposed to come,” Lord Brackenshaw said, glancing at his watch. “He only got to Diplow Manor the other day. He came to dine with us on Tuesday, said he’d been tied up with some tedious business. Might have been dragged off somewhere else. Why, Gascoigne!” he called out, spotting the rector walking across the lawn with Gwendolen on his arm. “You’re a downright traitor. You beat all us old hands at archery yourself, and then you train up your niece to beat every single archeress here.”
“It is quite scandalous of her to outdo all the senior members,” Mr. Gascoigne said, his short upper lip twitching with suppressed pride. “But it’s not my doing. I only wanted her to make a tolerable appearance, not surpass anyone.”
“It’s not my fault either,” Gwendolen put in, with pretty, teasing archness. “If I’m going to aim at a target, I can’t very well help hitting it.”
“Ay, ay, that can be a fatal sort of business for some people,” Lord Brackenshaw laughed good-naturedly. He glanced back at his watch, then addressed Mrs. Arrowpoint again. “You’re right, the time’s getting on. But Grandcourt is always late. I noticed in town he was never on time, and he’s no archer—doesn’t know a thing about the sport. But I told him he simply had to come. He’d get to see the very flower of the county gathered here. He asked about you, by the way. He saw your calling card. I don’t think you met him in town. He’s spent years travelling abroad, most people don’t know him well.”
“No, we’ve never been introduced,” Mrs. Arrowpoint said. “That’s strange, too, since he’s the nephew of Sir Hugo Mallinger, and he and I are such great old friends when we meet.”
“Uncles and nephews don’t haunt each other’s company the way uncles and nieces do, usually,” Lord Brackenshaw smiled, nodding at the rector. “Come with me a minute, will you, Gascoigne? I need a quick word about the upcoming clout-shooting competition.”

Gwendolen drifted back to the group with her mother and aunt Gascoigne while she waited for her next turn to shoot. The possibility that Mr. Grandcourt, the famously eligible, much whispered-about bachelor, might not show up at all had begun to nag at her, a tiny little shadow threatening to spoil the perfect completeness of her day. For all her saucy teasing, for all her mockery of her family’s obvious hope that he might take a fancy to her, she was far from indifferent to the idea of the impression she would make on him. She told herself he would never have the slightest power over her—she had not yet realised that the urge to conquer a man is itself a kind of quiet subjection. She pictured him as just another of the many admiring, assiduous gentlemen she had already encountered, all different coloured beards, all different styles of bearing, fawning over her. The knowledge that everyone around her expected her to think him delightful made her half-determined to find him ridiculous before they even met. But that was no reason she wanted him to stay away. Even the faint, absurd premonition that it would be a bother to reject him did not make her wish he would save her the trouble by never asking. The image of him ignoring her entirely, and shortly becoming engaged to Miss Arrowpoint, was not one that flattered her pride.

She hung on every word Lord Brackenshaw said about his late arrival, and when he finally stepped onto the grounds, no one was more sharply aware of his presence than Gwendolen—though she resolutely refused to turn her head to look for him. Not a single shift of her gaze would betray that the much-discussed Mr. Mallinger Grandcourt mattered to her in the slightest. She threw herself back into her shooting, so deliberately avoiding any curious glance that no one could possibly suspect she knew he was there. And all the while, the quiet, certain knowledge that he was watching her hummed like a bright thread through every other thought in her head.

It only made her shoot better. At last, she sent three arrows in a row dead centre into the gold. The whole crowd erupted in clapping and cheers, a special honour reserved for such a rare feat at the Brackenshaw meet: a extra fine gold star, meant to be pinned to the breast of the winner. The crowd made way for her, clearing a path for her to walk forward to receive it from Lady Brackenshaw’s hands, the long, soft afternoon light gilding the lines of her perfect, graceful figure as she moved. Every eye in the clearing was fixed on her. She was the centre of the beautiful scene, and she would not look anywhere but at Lady Brackenshaw, no matter how much she wanted to dart a quick glance at the new spectator at the edge of the crowd. Part of her pleasure was the thought that even the sharp, unimpressible Herr Klesmer was watching her, music far from his mind, his petty superiority completely overshadowed by the moment. Most of all, she knew Grandcourt was there, seeing her at the very height of her beauty and glory, admiring her unreservedly, no criticism in sight. She did not expect to admire him back, and that was just how she liked it.

She met Lady Brackenshaw’s gracious smile without blushing (she only blushed when she was caught off guard), her face glowing with bright, unforced joy, and bent with easy grace to let the star be fixed to her gown just at the curve of her shoulder. She had drifted off after the ceremony, exchanging playful congratulations with other guests, and was standing at the edge of the group, absently running her thumb over the sharp tip of an arrow, when Lord Brackenshaw strolled up to her, smiling.
“Miss Harleth, here is a gentleman who will not wait one moment longer for an introduction. He prevailed on your mamma to send me over with him. Will you allow me to introduce Mr. Mallinger Grandcourt?”

---

Every new acquaintance, whether with a person or a thing, begins by carving a sharp, clear edge around the vast blank of what we do not yet know.

Gwendolen had braced herself to meet Mr. Grandcourt for weeks, his anticipated arrival no sudden surprise at all—until Lord Brackenshaw stepped aside to make way for the man she’d pictured a dozen different ridiculous, posturing ways, and suddenly she was face to face with him. A hot little shock flooded her cheeks, and her vexation at the flush only made it burn deeper. All her imagined portraits of him were useless, he was nothing like any of them. He stood just a hair taller than her, their eyes meeting almost exactly level: there was not a flicker of a smile on his face as he looked at her, no trace of self-consciousness, no anxious currying of favor. When he lifted his hat, she saw a broad, smooth expanse of baldness ringed only by a thin fringe of reddish-gold hair, and a hand so perfectly formed it might have been carved from polished ivory. His features, unhidden by any full beard, were unapologetically handsome, the line from his brow to his chin almost perfectly straight, his faint side-whiskers trimmed to match that same unyielding perpendicular. No living man could have been freer of grimaces, of fawning little shifts of posture—and no wide-awake, breathing man could have looked less animated. The stiff, correct Englishmen one usually met, drawing themselves rigid after a bow and assenting with a severe nod, always gave off the air of holding back some riotous energy that would burst free the second they were off parade. But Grandcourt bore no trace of that rigid restraint; his posture leaned soft, unhurried, no tension coiled under his skin. His complexion was a faded, fair translucence, like an actress scrubbed clean of stage paint, his long, narrow gray eyes holding nothing but calm, unapologetic indifference.

It is foolish to try to sum a human being up in a single glance: when we first meet someone, we only spot the letters of their alphabet, with no idea of the full story they spell out. All Gwendolen knew for certain, in those first bright minutes under the archery tent, was one sharp, unshakable thought: He is not ridiculous.

Lord Brackenshaw drifted away almost at once, and conversation began, every pause between their lines stretching out longer than the last, Grandcourt’s gaze fixed steadily on her with a faint, unreadable curiosity, no shift of expression to give away what he thought. Gwendolen only dared flick a look back at him now and then, her observation softened a little by her usual coquettish charm.

“I used to think archery was a terrible bore,” Grandcourt said first. His accent was refined, effortless, but marked by a slow, broken drawl, as if some distinguished nobleman had been laid low by a very elegant chest cold.

“Have you converted to it today?” Gwendolen teased.
A long pause followed, in which Gwendolen’s mind raced, running through every possible judgment he might be forming of her.
“Yes. Since I saw you shoot. All you usually see at these things is people missing their targets and simpering to cover it up.”
“I suppose you’re a first-rate shot with a rifle, then?” Another pause, while Gwendolen darted a rapid, assessing look over him, composing a sharp, vivid little sketch of him in her head for some imaginary audience.
“I’ve given up shooting.”
“Oh, that makes you a terribly formidable person. People who master a thing and then toss it aside make the rest of us feel utterly trivial, like we’re still trotting out last year’s out-of-date fashions. I hope you haven’t given up all follies, because I practice quite a great many of them.” She paused again, turning her own last line over in her head, wondering if she’d sounded too bold.
“What do you call a folly?”
“Oh, anything that’s enjoyable, I suppose. But I hear you haven’t given up hunting, at least.” Another lull, and Gwendolen recalled all she’d heard of his vast estates, his fortune, and decided he was the most aristocratic-looking man she had ever laid eyes on.
“One has to do something to pass the time.”
“Do you care for the turf, then? Or is that another thing you’ve left behind?” This pause found her speculating that a man with such a calm, cold, steady bearing might make a far less tiresome husband than most, unlikely to poke his nose into every little whim his wife wanted to indulge.
“I run a horse now and then, but I don’t throw myself into it the way some men do. Are you fond of horses?”
“More than anything. I never feel half so alive as when I’m out on a great gallop, flying across the fields. My mind goes completely empty, I just feel strong, and happy.” She lingered in the silence that followed, wondering if he’d approve of that admission, but stubbornly resolved not to soften her own tastes for anyone’s sake.
“Do you like danger?”
“I don’t know. When I’m on horseback I never think about it. It feels like if I broke every bone in my body I wouldn’t even notice. I’d ride straight at anything that crossed my path.” For a second she let herself daydream through a whole hunting season, two perfect hunters stabled for her to ride whenever she pleased.
“You’d probably love tiger hunting, or pig-sticking. I did a season or two of that out in the East once. All the sport here feels like child’s play after that.”
“So you *are* fond of danger, then?” Gwendolen wondered if the coldest, most unflappable men always turned out to be the most wildly adventurous, and preened a little at her own obvious cleverness for spotting that.
“One has to have something to fill the hours. And you get used to anything, after a while.”
“I think I’m very fortunate, then—everything is still new to me. I never get enough of any of it. The only thing I’m used to is being bored, and I’d like to give that up just the same way you gave up shooting.” It occurred to her then that a man of such distinguished, chilly manners might make a dreadfully dull companion—but then, most people were dull, weren’t they? She’d never seen any of the marriages around her where the husband doubled as a proper entertaining companion, anyway. And besides, she was certainly not going to accept Mr. Grandcourt, no matter how well he looked.
“Why are you bored?”
“This whole neighborhood is dreadful. There’s nothing at all to do here. That’s why I took up archery, for lack of anything better.” She thought ahead, to the years stretching before her as an unmarried woman, with no money of her own, no power to go where she pleased, and the boredom looming larger every day.
“You’ve made yourself its undisputed queen. I’d put good money on you winning the first prize.”
“I wouldn’t be so sure. I have very worthy rivals. Didn’t you see how well Miss Arrowpoint shot?” Gwendolen found herself musing, in the lull that followed, that even the most admired men often picked someone other than the woman everyone assumed they would choose, she’d read that plot a hundred times in novels.
“Miss Arrowpoint? No—wait, yes, I noticed her.”
“Shall we go over to the scoring board now? Everyone’s heading to the other end of the grounds. My uncle’s looking over at me, he probably needs me.”

Gwendolen fled the tête-à-tête with quiet, unacknowledged relief. It wasn’t that talking to him was unpleasant, exactly—but for the whole duration of their conversation, she’d been unable to shake that strange, unwonted flush in her cheeks, the sense that she was less in control of herself than she usually was. This Mr. Grandcourt, who seemed far more wrapped up in his own importance than in hers—an absurdity almost no one else dared inflict on her—must never get the idea that he mattered to her, just because all the local gossips had marked him down as a desirable match, that she would ever dance attendance on his whims. What Grandcourt thought about in all those long silences would make itself clear soon enough.

“You just missed winning the gold arrow, Gwen,” Mr. Gascoigne told her when they reached the board. “Miss Juliet Fenn is eight points ahead of you.”
“I’m very glad to hear it,” Gwendolen laughed easily. “I’d hate to make myself thoroughly disagreeable by taking every prize for myself.” No one could possibly be jealous of Juliet Fenn, a girl as perfectly middling as a midday market stall in every way except her archery skill and her staggering plainness, which outdid even her father’s, with his underhung jaw and receding brow that put one in mind of a particularly clever fish. It was a wonder, Gwendolen thought, that all these “intending bridegrooms” never stopped to look at themselves in the mirror before choosing a pretty wife, as if their own ugly features wouldn’t pop up to surprise them in their daughters no matter how lovely the mother was.

The groups of guests began to mill about more lively then, chatter bouncing from one cluster to the next. Gwendolen, now fully herself again, watched as Grandcourt was introduced to the sharp, energetic composer Klesmer by a strange stout, dark man with fat, nimble hands who seemed on familiar, easy terms with both of them, before leading the pair over to join the Arrowpoints, whose acquaintance he and Grandcourt had already made. She didn’t much care who the stranger was, but she was curious to see how Grandcourt behaved around people other than herself. Exactly the same, it turned out: he barely glanced at Miss Arrowpoint, his gaze resting mostly on Klesmer, who was talking with wild, vivid animation, stretching his long fingers out horizontally, jabbing his forefinger down at the ground, folding his arms and tossing his shaggy dark mane as he hopped from one topic to the next. Grandcourt listened, face impassive, narrow eyes half-lidded, his left forefinger tucked into his waistcoat pocket, his right hand brushing idly at his thin whisker.

I wonder which of those two men Miss Arrowpoint likes best, she thought, a faint mocking smile tugging at her lips. But she refused to gawk like she was curious, turning all her bright charm on the people around her, determined not to care if Mr. Grandcourt ever came near her again.

He came to her a few minutes later, offering to escort Mrs. Davilow to her carriage. “Shall we see each other in the ballroom later?” Gwendolen asked as he lifted his hat to take his leave. His “yes” came out with that same slow drawl, perfectly grave, no extra warmth at all.

“For once you were wrong, Gwen,” Mrs. Davilow said, as their carriage rattled toward the castle where the evening’s ball was to be held. “You were convinced he’d be absurdly ridiculous, and there’s nothing the least bit silly about him.”
“I suppose I could find something ridiculous if I tried hard enough, but I don’t feel like it today,” Gwendolen said, a little pettishly, and her mother thought better of pressing the subject.

It was custom for the ladies and gentlemen to dine apart at these archery gatherings, so both groups could relax without having to perform for each other. The gentlemen all had a stock of old archery ball jokes about how shockingly greedy the ladies were, rumors even flying that some of them had the audacity to ask for the fat off their venison, proof of how far women would sink without strict social rules to keep them in line. Every year Lord Brackenshaw, a devoted gourmet, would lean in conspiratorially and share Byron’s opinion that a woman should never be seen eating at all, as if he was revealing a brand new secret he’d just thought up himself.

In the ladies’ dining room, it was obvious Gwendolen was no universal favorite among her own sex. None of the other girls tried to strike up a close friendship with her, and they all watched what she said far more than they talked back to her freely. She didn’t care much for them, after all, and when she was left alone in their company, it felt like she was performing to a half-empty theater. Mrs. Vulcany once remarked that Miss Harleth was far too fond of gentlemen—but the truth was, Gwendolen wasn’t fond of them at all. She was only fond of their homage, and no woman in the room was offering her that. The only exception was Miss Arrowpoint, who would drift unobtrusively to stand at her side, talking to her with quiet, unforced friendliness. She knows as well as I do that all our parents are ready to claw each other apart to get us a husband, Gwendolen thought, and she’s decided not to join the fight.

“I think Miss Arrowpoint has the most perfect manners I’ve ever seen,” Mrs. Davilow said later, when they were in the dressing room with Mrs. Gascoigne and Anna, far enough away from the other ladies to talk privately.
“I wish I was like her,” Gwendolen said.
“Why? Are you getting discontented with yourself all of a sudden, Gwen?”
“No, I’m discontented with everything else. She looks like she’s perfectly happy with what she has.”
“You ought to be happy today. You clearly loved the archery, I saw how bright you were out there.”
“That’s all over now. I don’t know what’s coming next.” Gwendolen stretched, throwing her bare arms over her head with a soft little moan. It was the fashion at these events to dance in the archery dress, ditching the heavy velvet jacket, and her simple white cashmere gown edged with pale green flattered every line of her figure. A thin gold chain around her neck, and a small gold star pinned to her breast, were her only ornaments. Her soft, smooth hair was piled high in a gleaming crown, framing her brow so perfectly Sir Joshua Reynolds would have begged to paint her portrait. He would have had an easy task of it, too—he only had to capture one perfect, beautiful moment, not capture the messy, messy truth of all the change that was coming for her.
“Dancing is what’s next,” Mrs. Davilow said. “You’re bound to love that.”
“I’m only dancing the quadrilles. I told Mr. Clintock that already. I won’t waltz or polka with anyone.”
“Whatever makes you say that, out of nowhere?”
“I can’t stand having ugly people pressed so close to me.”
“Who do you mean by ugly people?”
“Oh, half the men in that room.”
“Mr. Clintock isn’t ugly, for example.” Mrs. Davilolen didn’t dare name Grandcourt out loud.
“I hate the feeling of woolen cloth rubbing up against me.”
“Would you believe it,” Mrs. Davilow said, drifting over to her sister a moment later, “Gwendolen says she won’t waltz or polka at all tonight?”
“She’s far too given to silly whims, if you ask me,” Mrs. Gascoigne said gravely. “It would be far more proper for her to behave like every other young lady here, especially when she’s paid for first-rate dancing lessons all these years.”
“Why should I dance if I don’t enjoy it, aunt? It’s not written in the catechism somewhere, is it?”
“My *dear*!” Mrs. Gascoigne said, her tone sharp with rebuke, and Anna stared, wide-eyed, at Gwendolen’s audacity. No one pressed the subject further.

Something had shifted in Gwendolen’s mood since that bright, triumphant hour out on the archery range, but she looked more stunning than ever under the chandeliers in the ballroom, the soft gold light spilling over the polished floors, the sweet scent of roses drifting in from the conservatory wrapping around the room like silk. Hardly a single unmarried man there wasn’t desperate to secure her as a partner, and every one of the men she did accept greeted her refusal to waltz or polka with mournful little protests. “Are you under some sort of vow, Miss Harleth?” “Why are you being so cruel to all of us?” “You waltzed with me back in February!” “And you’re so very, very good at it!” Their frustration was far from unpleasant for her. The other young women who were waltzing freely all whispered that Miss Harleth was just making a nuisance of herself to draw extra attention—but when her uncle overheard her refusals, he defended her at once. “Gwendolen always has a good reason for what she does.” He thought refusing the waltzes made her look far more distinguished, and he wanted her to shine. The archery ball was meant to keep a decorous, subdued tone, suitable for clergymen and elders alike, not a wild romp for unruly young people, and he’d always thought the fast, close fashionable dances were far too unseemly anyway.

Mr. Grandcourt, though, never joined the crowd of men begging her for a dance. He stood up for one quadrille with Miss Arrowpoint, and then seemed to decide he didn’t want any other partners at all. Gwendolen spotted him lingering near the Arrowpoints’ small group again and again, never making any move to approach her. Her uncle spoke to him sometimes, but her uncle was everywhere, talking to everyone. She found herself relaxing: he probably didn’t care about her even a little, she decided. He was far too used to every possible pleasure in the world to think of her as anything more than one random pretty local girl who’d been invited to the party. Of course the elders were all fools, gossiping about who he would marry without even speaking to him. He was probably planning to marry Miss Arrowpoint, with all her family’s fortune. No matter what happened, Gwendolen told herself, she would not be disappointed. The whole situation was a joke, no matter how it turned out, because she’d never even let herself silently hope for anything from Mr. Grandcourt. Still, she noticed that every now and then, he would shift his position slowly, subtly, so he could get a clear line of sight to her, watching whenever she danced. If he didn’t admire her, well, that was his loss.

Late in the evening, when Gwendolen had accepted Klesmer as her quadrille partner, the sharp-eyed composer who saw everything all at once leaned over to her as they walked to their places. “Mr. Grandcourt is a man of excellent taste. He likes to watch you dance.”
“Maybe he likes to stare at things he hates, just for a change of pace,” Gwendolen laughed back, feeling perfectly at her ease with him now. “He’s

“O gentlemen, the time of life is short;
To spend that shortness basely were too long,
If life did ride upon a dial’s point,
Still ending at the arrival of an hour.”

Two days after the sun-drenched archery meeting where Gwendolen Harleth’s sharp, bright laugh had outshone every other sound on the green, Henleigh Mallinger Grandcourt lingered over his breakfast at his country estate, his long-time associate Mr. Lush sat across from him.
The morning was soft, golden and honey-thick: jasmine-scented summer air drifted through the wide open sash windows, where half a dozen pampered hounds wandered lazily in from the sun-warmed turf of the lawn beyond. The park rolled out purple-hued in the haze, fading into a thick wall of ancient oak wood at the horizon, and the breakfast room itself hummed with a quiet, old-world elegance that felt almost like a well-bred hush, far removed from the clatter and restlessness of common, newly rich households.
Whether the two men sharing the space were half as agreeable to each other as their surroundings was far less clear. Grandcourt had dragged his carved oak chair out of the table line to face the lawn, left leg propped carelessly over a second empty chair, right elbow braced on the linen tablecloth, smoking a fat, slow-burning cigar while Lush still picked at his poached eggs. The dogs drifted between them, half-playacting hunger, turning up their noses at the fancy table scraps they pretended to beg for — all except Fetch, the sleek liver-colored water spaniel, who sat planted firmly on her forepaws, big soft brown eyes fixed unblinkingly on Grandcourt, loyal to a fault no matter how little attention he paid her.
Perched on Grandcourt’s lap was a tiny Maltese dog with a silver bell on its collar, “Fluff,” whose warm little body he stroked whenever his hand was free of cigar or coffee cup. Fetch was clearly jealous, her drooping ears twitching every time Fluff’s bell tinkled, and when she could bear the neglect no longer she laid her heavy silky paw gently on her master’s knee.
Grandcourt stared at her, face entirely impassive, for a full half minute. Then he set down his cigar, lifted Fluff to nuzzle the tiny creature against his chin, and stroked her slow and deliberate, never taking his eyes off Fetch. The poor spaniel whimpered, trying to hold back the sound, until she finally rested her head against her own outstretched paw, looking up at him with a plea so piteous any dog-lover would have melted. Grandcourt, famous for his menagerie of well-cared-for hounds, understood exactly what she was asking for — but the second Fetch let out a loud, wounded howl of frustration, he pushed her paw off his leg without a word, dumped Fluff carelessly on the table (where the tiny dog’s black nose almost knocked over the salt cellar), and reached for his cigar, only to find it had gone out, and he blamed Fetch for the annoyance.
Fetch, once started wailing, couldn’t seem to stop. Her second howl was louder, the third even sharper.
“Turn that brute out, will you?” Grandcourt said to Lush, not raising his voice, not even glancing over at him — as if he knew full well his companion would jump to obey even the smallest unspoken order.
Lush stood at once, hoisted the heavy spaniel up even though he hated bending, and carried her out of the room, disposing of her somewhere out in the yard before he wandered back a couple minutes later. He lit his own cigar, angled his chair so he could watch Grandcourt’s face without turning his head, and spoke after a lazy drag.
“Ride or drive over to Quetcham today?”
“Not going to Quetcham.”
“You didn’t go yesterday, you know.”
Grandcourt smoked in silence for half a minute, the blue smoke curling slow around his head. “I assume you sent my card and my apologies round there?”
“I went myself at four yesterday,” Lush said. “Told them you were certain to show up shortly. They’ll assume some accident held you back. All the more reason to turn up today, really.”
Silence stretched for another two minutes. Then Grandcourt said, low and drawling, “Which people have you invited to stay here, the ones bringing their wives?”
Lush pulled a little leather notebook out of his pocket. “Captain and Mrs. Torrington arrive next week. Then Mr. Hollis and Lady Flora, the Cushats, and the Gogoffs.”
“Bit of a ragged lot,” Grandcourt said after a long pause. “Why on earth did you ask the Gogoffs? When you send out invites in my name, you will be so good as to show me the list first, instead of dropping a six-foot giantess of a woman on my doorstep unannounced. She makes the whole drawing room look ugly.”
“You invited the Gogoffs yourself, when we ran into them in Paris last spring,” Lush pointed out.
“What does running into them in Paris have to do with anything?” Grandcourt’s voice dropped, no longer the lazy, bored drawl he’d used all morning — now it was a quiet, sharp, unyielding tone, that Lush recognized after fifteen years as the sound of a will that would not be crossed. “I told you to show me the list before you sent any of them out.”
