“Any other couples you’d like me to invite, then?” Lush asked, backtracking fast.
“Think of some decent people. Respectable. Bring a daughter or two with them, not just old bores. And get one of your damn musicians over here. None of the clowns who play comic songs, though.”
“I was thinking Klesmer might agree to come down once he leaves Quetcham,” Lush said, sounding casual, but his eyes never left Grandcourt’s face, watching for a reaction. “Miss Arrowpoint will only tolerate top-tier music, after all.”
Grandcourt turned his head to look at his companion for the first time all morning, slow, deliberate, taking two long, luxurious puffs of his cigar before he spoke, voice lower than ever, edged with clear contempt. “What in God’s name do I have to do with Miss Arrowpoint and her music?”
“Plenty,” Lush laughed, playful, but not joking. “You won’t have to put in much work, probably. But you do have to go through a few formalities if you want to marry a million pounds.”
“Likely enough. But I’m not marrying a million.”
“That’s a damn shame. You’d throw that perfect opportunity away, knock down all the plans we’ve been working on for years?”
*Your* plans, you mean,” Grandcourt corrected him, dry.
“You’ve got stacks of outstanding debts, you know that,” Lush pressed on. “Things could get very awkward for you very fast. The family heirship isn’t 100% guaranteed, no matter what everyone says.”
Grandcourt didn’t answer, so Lush kept going. “It’s such a perfect chance. The Arrowpoints would bend over backwards to welcome you, I saw that the second we met them. Their daughter isn’t a classic beauty, but she’s elegant, sharp, could carry any title you gave her perfectly, no one would ever whisper about her. And there’s no chance she’d turn down a match like yours.”
“Probably not,” Grandcourt said, noncommittal.
“Her father and mother would let you do pretty much anything you wanted once you’re married to her.”
“I don’t want to do anything with them at all,” Grandcourt said, flat.
Lush paused for a beat, then spoke, deep and serious, remonstrating. “Good God, Grandcourt! After everything you’ve done, after all this time you’re going to let some silly little whim ruin the comfortable, settled life you could have?”
“Spare me the speech. I know exactly what I’m doing.”
“What?” Lush set his cigar down, shoved both hands in his trouser pockets, like he was bracing for something that would try his patience, even if he wasn’t going to lose his temper.
“I’m going to marry the other girl.”
Lush’s voice dripped with scorn. “You’ve fallen in love, have you?”
“I’m going to marry her,” Grandcourt repeated, no elaboration.
“Did you already propose to her, then?”
“No.”
“That girl’s got a will of her own, if I’m not mistaken. Gwendolen Harleth? She’s the sort who’ll make a huge fuss if she doesn’t get what she wants. She knows her own mind.”
“She doesn’t like you,” Grandcourt said, the faintest flicker of a smile tugging at the corner of his mouth.
“True enough,” Lush said, sneer still thick in his tone. “But if the two of you are besotted with each other, that’ll be all that matters, I suppose.”
Grandcourt didn’t bother replying. He sipped his black coffee, pushed his chair back, and strolled out onto the lawn, all the remaining dogs trailing after him, bells tinkling and paws pattering on the grass.
Lush watched him go for a moment, then picked his cigar back up and relit it, smoking slow, running his fingers over his neatly trimmed beard, thinking hard, until he finally stroked it in a little smirk of conclusion, muttering soft under his breath. “Check, old boy.”
Lush had known Grandcourt for fifteen years, long enough to learn what sorts of tactics never worked on his stubborn, unreadable patron — though the right tactics to steer him where he needed to go were often very hard to pin down. Early in his career, he’d held a college fellowship, and had nearly taken holy orders to secure a quiet living, but the prospect bored him, so he’d taken a post as traveling companion to a marquess, then later to young Grandcourt, who’d lost his father very young. Grandcourt found Lush so convenient, he’d let him slowly take over as his right-hand man, handling all his messy personal affairs, for fifteen years running.
The habit of all that time had made Grandcourt more and more dependent on Lush’s willingness to fix every mess, and Lush more and more dependent on the lazy, luxury life he’d built for himself by Grandcourt’s side — no work worth mentioning, all the fine things he could ever want. Grandcourt had never respected Lush, not even on day one, and fifteen years hadn’t changed that, but it had cemented his certainty that he could kick Lush out any time he pleased — though of course he’d never stoop to kicking anyone himself. A gentleman got his dogs to do the messy work for him. He only said cruel, dismissive things that would have earned him a black eye if his “confidant” had been a man with any independent pride.
But what son of a poor vicar, who starved his own wife and daughters of new cotton dresses just to scrape together the money to send his son to Oxford, could keep his independent spirit intact once he’d tasted the luxury of fine dining, good thoroughbred horses, and the softest life imaginable, all without ever working a day? Lush had once been considered a brilliant scholar, and still remembered bits of his old learning when he bothered to dig it up, but all those little bachelor arts that soften a man’s manners were perfect preparation for a life of sinecure. His current comfortable situation was the best sinecure imaginable, all it required was a faint whiff of the old scholarship he’d left behind years ago.
He was perfectly aware Grandcourt looked at him as someone he could kick out at any minute, but he wrote that off as just one of his patron’s odd, unaccountable quirks — one whim from Grandcourt was as good as the next, when it came down to it. In his own mind, he’d never done a bad turn in his life, so why would he ever start, just to keep his easy life intact? Lush loved his soft comfort more than anything else, and this morning, even after the surprise of Grandcourt’s reckless new plan, he wandered off to his private sitting room later and played his violoncello for a full happy hour.

“Philistia, be thou glad of me!”

When Grandcourt finally set his mind to marrying Miss Harleth, he proved surprisingly adept at bending every small circumstance to his will. For the next fortnight, scarcely a day passed where he did not contrive an encounter with her, or leave some marked, thoughtful token to prove she was never far from his thoughts. His cousin Mrs. Torrington now presided over Diplow House as his hostess, making it easy to invite Mrs. Davilow and Gwendolen to a grand, crowded party where every guest could see for themselves how the lord of the manor lavished his attention on the penniless local beauty, and spared barely a glance for the nearby wealthy heiress the neighborhood had long presumed he would court.
Every family of standing within visiting distance of Pennicote, including the rector Mr. Gascoigne, felt certain the match was as good as made. The rector had already resolved to do his duty by his niece, and see that her marriage settlements were as generous and ironclad as possible. Even he and anxious Mrs. Davilow found themselves wondering at the delay: so many perfect, private moments had presented themselves for a proposal, yet Grandcourt had not spoken. He too was surprised by his own hesitation. When he had first told Lush of his plan to marry Gwendolen, he had expected the affair to be wrapped up in a week. Every morning he promised himself that this would be the day he gave her the chance to accept him, and every evening he found the unspoken formality still hanging unfulfilled between them. This strange, persistent drag only stoked his determination to see the thing through at last. He had never once entertained the thought that Gwendolen might refuse him — but heaven help us all, we are so often unable to act even on our most unshakable certainties. The very idea of an outcome we cannot bear looms like a ghost between us and the thing we know for sure to be true. We know for a fact a blind worm cannot kill us with a bite, but the thought of being bitten at all is so intolerable, and the creature looks so capable of biting, that we shrink back from reaching to touch it.

He had sent word that he would bring his finest thoroughbred, Criterion, over for Gwendolen to ride, with Mrs. Davilow following behind in the carriage for propriety’s sake, and the three of them would ride to Diplow for luncheon, Grandcourt leading the way. It was a perfect mid-harvest day, mild enough that a five-mile noon ride felt like pure joy. Poppies blazed crimson along the edges of the wheat fields, a soft breeze drifted between the stalks like a gentle, well-mannered guest, and carried the shadow of a slow-drifting cloud across the soft grey downs. Some fields stood thick with uncut sheaves, others had teams of horses straining to haul the last load of grain off the stubble, and fat cattle dozed in the shade of wide-spreading branches. The road wound through rolling dairy farm country that looked exactly as it had for generations, as if peace and permanence had made their home there, far removed from the distant shriek of the railway train streaking across the horizon far away.

That quiet, restful air never managed to seep into poor Mrs. Davilow’s fretful mind, though. Watching Gwendolen and Grandcourt canter ahead, then slow their horses to a walking pace to talk unhurriedly until the carriage caught up to them, was a lovely sight — but it only stoked the warring hopes and fears she carried about her daughter’s future. This was the perfect moment for a lover to speak, to end all uncertainty at last, and she could only wait, trembling, for Gwendolen to make the right choice. Rex’s boyish devotion had always repulsed Gwendolen, after all, and Grandcourt was the exact opposite in every way. The fact that Gwendolen had not cracked a single one of her usual sharp, satirical jokes about him, that she never even spoke of him to her mother at all, was proof enough he had made some entirely new impression on her. “Will she be happy with him?” Mrs. Davilow asked herself over and over, and always tried to soothe the answer out: as happy as any woman could be, probably, or at least as happy as most wives. She could not imagine Gwendolen ever being content to live the small, narrow, modest life most women of their reduced means were forced to settle for.

Grandcourt was thinking much the same thing. He was desperate to be rid of the hanging uncertainty of not having said the words out loud. Any further doubt beyond that, the ridiculous unreasonable kind that hung thick in the air around them, felt like a petty irritation pricking at his wishes, with no logical right to exist at all.

Gwendolen was enjoying the ride, but not in the unguarded, giddy way she had laughed and chattered with Rex on that morning months before. She spoke, she laughed a little, but the sound of her own amusement felt like a distant, faint echo. Some strange weight hung in the air for her too — not that she felt her will bending to Mr. Grandcourt, or to the glittering, splendid future he was clearly about to lay at her feet. No: Gwendolen was determined that everyone, including that tall, dignified gentleman himself, would understand she would do exactly as she pleased, and no one would get to dictate her choices. If she chose to marry him, he would learn very quickly that she had no intention of surrendering her freedom, no intention of doing “what other women did,” as her favourite formula went.

Grandcourt’s few, carefully measured remarks that morning, as always, carried a quiet weight all their own. When a man of his standing speaks in short, deliberate sentences that trail off before he has said all he could, listeners automatically assume there is a formidable, sharp intelligence held back beneath the silence, and it leaves all the space in the world for other people to chatter on at length.
“How do you like Criterion’s paces?” he asked, once they had passed through the park gates and slowed from a canter to a walk.
“He’s divine to ride,” Gwendolen said. “I’d love to take a leap with him, if it wouldn’t frighten mamma. There was a wide, shallow channel we passed five minutes back. I’d love to gallop back and jump it.”
“Let’s do it together,” Grandcourt offered.
“No, thank you — mamma’s so timid, if she saw me she might fall ill.”
“Let me go explain to her. Criterion would clear it without so much as a stumble.”
“No, really, you’re very kind, but it would alarm her far too much. I’ll leap anything when she’s not looking, but I never tell her about it afterwards.”
“We can let the carriage pass on ahead, then set off.”
“No, no, don’t think of it any more,” Gwendolen said, suddenly very reluctant to follow through on her own impulsive suggestion.
“Mrs. Davilow knows I’d never let anything happen to you,” Grandcourt pressed.
“She’d be imagining you having to drag me off the ground with a broken neck,” Gwendolen said, with a light, careless laugh.

There was a long, heavy pause before Grandcourt turned to look directly at her. “I should like to have the right, always, to take care of you.”

Gwendolen did not meet his gaze. For a long, dizzy second she felt the heat of a blush rise in her cheeks, then drain away until she was pale — but to Grandcourt’s measured judgment, her reply came fast enough, light as the note of a flute, as she flicked her reins carelessly. “Oh, I’m not at all sure I want to be taken care of. If I chose to risk breaking my neck, I’d like to be at liberty to do it.”
She pulled her horse to a stop as she spoke, twisting in her saddle to watch the carriage draw nearer. Her eyes brushed over Grandcourt for half a second as she moved, and there was no softness, no warm invitation, in that glance to soften her careless reply. At that very moment she knew she was risking something far more precious than a broken neck: the chance that she might have pushed his advances away for good. And she was not at all pleased by that possibility.

“Damn her,” Grandcourt thought, as he reined in his own horse. He was not a man who wasted words on internal monologues, but that single, explosive phrase summed up all his irritation: he felt as if he was being played with, and he was determined this girl would not make a fool out of him. Did she expect him to throw himself at her feet and declare he was dying of love for her? That was not the gate through which she would enter all the privilege and luxury he could give her. Did she expect a written proposal? That would be just as ridiculous. He would never make his offer in any form that could leave him vulnerable to an outright rejection. And anyway, he reasoned, she had already effectively accepted him by accepting all his marked attentions this whole fortnight. If anything broke it off now, everyone in the neighbourhood would blame her, not him. It was just coquetry, he decided.

By then the carriage had caught up, and there was no chance for another private tête-à-tête before they reached the house, where a crowd of guests waited. Gwendolen, still in her dust-spattered riding dress, hat pushed back from her wind-tousled hair, basked in the quiet, admiring gaze of everyone who watched her — for every one of them already accepted she was the woman Grandcourt had chosen to be his wife. The odious Mr. Lush was nowhere to be seen, and the thrill of all that admiring attention lifted her spirits, chasing away for a little while that uneasy feeling that she was fighting against her own wishes, that she might soon regret her careless reply. There was no way to tell if Grandcourt was offended: his manner was exactly as calm, as unchanging as it had always been. Gwendolen was sharp enough to know by now that his manner gave her no clue at all to what he was thinking — and the fact that nothing had changed in his behaviour only made her more afraid of him.

She had only ever visited Diplow for formal dinners before, so after luncheon, when many of the guests had drifted off, Lady Flora Hollis suggested the remaining party wander the grounds to see the pretty views from the garden windows. Again and again, Grandcourt had a perfect chance to pull Gwendolen aside, to speak to her where no one could hear. But he did not. He barely spoke to anyone else, but what he said to her was no more eager, no more intimate than it had been on the night of their very first meeting. He looked at her as often as he always did, and some of her old defiant spirit rose up in her, so she looked right back at him, unflinching, almost preferring that his handsome, still face gave nothing away at all.

At last, it seemed as if he had settled on a plan. The whole party had gathered by the ornamental pool to laugh at Fetch, the spaniel, who was determined to bring a water lily back to the bank like Cowper’s famous Beau, and refused to give up even after his first attempt ended with him splashing about in the reeds. Grandcourt stood just outside the little crowd with Gwendolen, turned slowly, and fixed his gaze on a small knoll planted full of bright American shrubs, with a winding path leading up to a flat little platform at the top.
“This is tedious,” he said, in his lazy, drawling tone. “Shall we go up there?”
“Oh, of course — we came to explore, after all,” Gwendolen said. She felt a little flicker of pleasure, and a little flicker of fear, all at once.

The path was far too narrow for him to offer her his arm, so they walked up in silence. When they reached the little flat clearing at the top, Grandcourt said: “There’s nothing to see here. It wasn’t worth the climb at all.”
Gwendolen did not laugh. She stood perfectly silent, holding the folds of her riding dress up out of the grass like a marble statue, her hand tightening hard around the handle of her whip, which she had snatched up automatically when the group had set off on their walk.
“What sort of place do you prefer?” Grandcourt asked.
“Different places are nice in their own way,” she said. “On the whole, I like open, cheerful places best. I don’t care for anything dark or sombre.”
“Offendene is far too sombre for you,” he said.
“It is, a little.”
“You won’t stay there much longer, I hope.”
“Oh, I expect I will. Mamma likes being close to her sister, the rector’s wife.”
There was another short stretch of silence.
“Surely you’re not going to live there forever, even if Mrs. Davilow does,” he said.
“I don’t know,” Gwendolen replied, her words tumbling out a little nervously, as she flicked her whip against the leaves of a rhododendron bush in front of her. “We women don’t get to go off adventuring, do we? We can’t go looking for the North-West Passage, or the source of the Nile, or hunt tigers in the East. We have to stay where we grew up, or where other people choose to transplant us. We’re raised just like potted flowers, trained to look as pretty as we can, and be bored without complaining. That’s what I think about plants, anyway. Half of them are dreadfully bored, and that’s why so many of them turn poisonous. What do you think?”
“I quite agree,” Grandcourt said, drawn off his intended train of thought by her odd, whimsical little speech. “Almost everything is a bore.” After a short pause, he continued, in his slow, refined drawl: “But a woman can get married.”
“Some women can,” Gwendolen said.
“And you certainly can, unless you choose to be obstinately cruel,” he said.
“I’m not sure I’m not both cruel and obstinate,” Gwendolen said, suddenly turning her head to look full at him, for she had felt his eyes on her the entire conversation. She wanted to see what effect the look would have on her, not just on him.

He stood perfectly still, half a yard away from her, and for a split second she thought of that lotus-eater’s drowsy stupor that hung around him, and that was now beginning to wrap around her too.
“Are you as uncertain about yourself as you make everyone else feel about you?” he asked.
“I’m completely uncertain about myself,” Gwendolen said. “I don’t know how uncertain anyone else is.”
“And you want everyone to think you don’t care at all?” Grandcourt said, a faint new edge of hardness in his tone.
“I didn’t say that,” Gwendolen said, hesitating, turning her eyes away to whip the rhododendron bush again. She wished she was back on horseback, so she could gallop away as fast as she liked. She could hardly run down the knoll, after all.
“So you do care, then?” Grandcourt said, his tone softening back into that lazy drawl.

“Ah — my whip!” Gwendolen yelped, in a little flutter of distress. Her agitation had made her fumble it, and the gold-handled whip flew out of her grasp, bouncing through the shrubbery to lodge firmly in the branches of an azalea halfway down the slope. She could run down after it now, laughing prettily, and Grandcourt was forced to follow — but she was faster than him, snatched the whip back, and kept walking down to the flat level of the lawn, pausing when she reached it to look back at him, cheeks flushed, eyes bright, as if she had just won some small, secret victory. That bright, excited flush was still visible on her face when the two of them rejoined the rest of the party, and Mrs. Davilow noticed it immediately.
“All just coquetry,” Grandcourt thought to himself. “The next time I beckon, she’ll come.”
He felt sure that final moment would come the very next day, at the archery picnic planned for Cardell Chase, the same event they had first agreed on the night of the ball.

Even in Gwendolen’s own mind, two possible outcomes were circling each other: two different choices she could make, as if she stood on the line between two countries, and she had no idea which side she would tumble over to at any second. This strange feeling that she could not predict her own choices filled her with quiet astonishment and terror. Her favourite rule for life — “do as you like” — seemed to be failing her, and she could not guess what she would want to do a minute from now. The prospect of marrying Grandcourt was far more appealing than she had ever imagined any marriage could be. The dignity, the luxury, the power to do almost every little thing she pleased that was now so close she could almost touch it, wrapped around her like the rich scent of something she had dreamed of for years but never got to smell. And Grandcourt himself? There seemed no flaw at all in him, as far as a lover and husband went. Gwendolen wanted to climb into the gilded chariot of his fortune and drive the plunging, powerful horses herself, with a husband at her side who would simply fold his arms, nod, and let her do as she liked, without looking ridiculous. For all her sharp wits and all the books her mother thought far too daring, Gwendolen had to admit her judgment of Grandcourt was still a little uncertain. He was adorably quiet, completely free of every silly affectation and absurd quirk she hated in other men — he was the perfect husband to stand beside her and make her look her very best. But what else was he? He had travelled everywhere, seen everything. That was very desirable, and it was especially flattering that after seeing all there was to see in the world, he had chosen her above everyone else. He never seemed to enjoy anything very much. That hardly mattered, she thought — and the fewer little passions and petty desires he had of his own, the more freedom his wife would have to follow hers. She was fully confident that once they were married, she would be able to manage him completely.

But why did he make her feel so unusually constrained now? Why was she less playful, less bold with him than she had ever been with any other admirer? That total lack of demonstrativeness she had once found so attractive was a charm in more ways than one — it was slightly numbing, as well. Grandcourt was formidable: a handsome, rare lizard of a species she had never seen before, not the skittish, darting kind. But Gwendolen knew almost nothing about l

I will not clothe myself in wreck—wear gems
Sawed from cramped finger-bones of women drowned;
Feel chilly vaporous hands of ireful ghosts
Clutching my necklace: trick my maiden breast
With orphans’ heritage. Let your dead love
Marry its dead.

The next morning, Gwendolen looked as lovely and brimming with young energy as a tall, newly unfurled white lily. All yesterday’s faint, fleeting self-doubt had melted away into the bright, sharp fizz of youth, no more lasting than a shiver skittering across the smooth surface of a full, sunlit stream. The roving archery match in Cardell Chase glowed before her as a perfect, sun-warmed prospect: she could already picture herself drifting among the gnarled beeches like a wood nymph, surrounded by admiring company, and the imagined scene lent a soft allure to Grandcourt’s slow, deliberate advances. He was no breathless, lyrical Daphnis fit for a wood nymph’s fancies—if anything, his quiet, unreadable languor made him far more interesting. That morning, she was sure he would work his way slowly toward a full declaration, and she would meet it halfway, just as she had resolved with her uncle the night before.

When she wandered down to the breakfast parlor, everyone else had already left the table save her mother, Mrs. Davilow. There was a small stack of letters waiting by her plate, one of which she read with a spreading, delighted smile before passing it across to her mother. Mrs. Davilow smiled back as she handed it over, her own mood lightened by her daughter’s uncharacteristic, unforced good cheer.
“You don’t feel inclined to run off a thousand miles away, then?” Mrs. Davilow teased.
“Not quite that far,” Gwendolen laughed.
“It was such a sad oversight not to write to the Langens sooner. Can’t you dash a note off now, before we leave for the Chase this morning?”
“It’s not nearly that pressing. Tomorrow will do. You saw they leave London today, so I’ll send it to Dover. They’ll be waiting there till Monday.”
“Shall I write it for you, dear, if it feels like a chore?”
Gwendolen paused, sipping her coffee, before answering a little brusquely, “Oh no, don’t trouble. I’ll write it tomorrow.” Then she softened, leaning over to press a playful, tender kiss to her mother’s cheek. “My dear, old, beautiful mamma.”
“Old, am I, child?”
“Don’t say that!” Gwendolen protested. “I only meant you’re my old, dearest darling. You’re barely twenty-five years older than I am. When you talk like that, all my whole future shrivels up right in front of my eyes.”
“Twenty-five years can hold so much happiness, Gwen,” Mrs. Davilow said gently.
“Then I mustn’t waste a single second of mine starting it,” Gwendolen said, giddy. “The sooner I have my palaces and my own coaches, the better.”
“And a good husband who adores you, my sweet girl,” Mrs. Davilow added, encouraging. Gwendolen only pursed her lips in a saucy pout and said nothing.

The only small dampener on her excitement was that the rector, her uncle, had been detained by magistrate’s business and would likely not make it to Cardell Chase at all that day. She cared little that Mrs. Gascoigne and Anna refused to go without him, but her uncle’s steady, reasonable presence would have made the decision she was on the cusp of feel solid, inevitable. Now that she was so close to saying yes to Grandcourt, that promise of a full, unhoped-for life was beginning to feel too sharp, too defined, a cage she had not quite noticed she was stepping into. It is the way of the world: once we begin to crave great things for ourselves, every blessing we grasp soon feels like a new limit, a new exclusion. Still, she clung to the reassuring thought that marriage to Grandcourt would be the very gate to a wider, brighter freedom.

The party was meeting at Green Arbor, a sun-dappled grass hollow walled in by hanging woods that made a soft, sheltered amphitheater. A carriage packed with servants and picnic provisions had been sent ahead to lay out the feast, and the Chase warden had promised to guide the roving archers to keep them within pre-marked bounds strung between the iconic Double Oak, the Whispering Stones, and the High Cross. The plan was to take a short, casual stroll before luncheon, saving the full roving archery adventure for the golden, mellow light of late afternoon. The group gathered quickly, no one left lingering bored waiting, and soon pairs and small groups were drifting through the shifting dappled light, under the close, heavy boughs of beeches and the more spaced, gnarled oaks that dotted the Chase: a scene so picturesque any painter would have lingered to soak it in. This wandering archery was far lovelier than the usual stationary target shoots, but success with moving, random marks was far less predictable than practice at a fixed target, so hits were scattered unevenly across the amateur archers. Between that, and her quiet preoccupation with Grandcourt that made it hard to focus, Gwendolen did not distinguish herself much that first hour—save for the lively, unruffled grace with which she laughed off each miss. She had dressed again in her signature white and green, the same outfit she’d worn the day of the first archery meet where she had first met Grandcourt. He stayed close by her side all morning, yet to an outside observer it would have been almost impossible to tell their dynamic had shifted since that first introduction. Still, every other guest had already decided they were either secretly engaged already, or on the very brink of it—and Gwendolen believed it too.

As the groups drifted back toward Green Arbor, chattering without bothering to nock arrows, the words were finally spoken that felt like the first step toward that long-awaited proposal. “Do you have any idea how long it’s been since I first saw you in this dress?” Grandcourt asked.
“The archery meet was the 25th, and today’s the 13th,” Gwendolen laughed. “I’m terrible at sums, but I’ll hazard it’s nearly three weeks.”
He paused for a beat. “That’s a terrible loss of time.”
“Loss because knowing me has inconvenienced you?” Gwendolen teased. “Don’t be uncomplimentary, I won’t stand for it.”
Another long, quiet pause. “It’s the size of the gain that makes the lost time feel so heavy.”
Gwendolen fell silent then, thinking to herself, He is far more clever than most men. He never says a stupid, clumsy thing. Her unusual stillness read to Grandcourt as the softest, most promising encouragement he could have hoped for, and he leaned a little closer. “The joy of knowing you makes every minute I spend stuck in uncertainty feel wasted. Do you like uncertainty, Miss Harleth?”
“I think I do, actually,” Gwendolen said, beaming at him with a playful, teasing glint in her eye. “There’s far more magic to it than certainty.”
Grandcourt held her laughing gaze with a slow, steady, unblinking look, as if he could see straight through all her pretty performances to the girl beneath. “Do you mean more torment for me?”
Something about that quiet look shook Gwendolen out of all her usual poise, and she blushed hot, darting her eyes away. “No. That would make me very sorry.”
He would have pressed on, then, to make his full declaration—her soft, flustered answer had made her acceptance all but sure—when he realized they were in full view of the rest of the party, halfway down the steep slope leading into Green Arbor, an ill-chosen spot where the ground fell away sharp and slippery. He offered her his hand to steady her, and she took it, the two of them descending in full, obvious silence, watched by everyone already gathered below—including Mrs. Arrowpoint, who stood beside Mrs. Davilow. Mrs. Arrowpoint had already decided Grandcourt was far too languid, far too unremarkable a man for her own daughter Catherine, who had turned down no less a suitor than Lord Slogan; she watched him now with completely dispassionate eyes.
“Mr. Grandcourt is not half the man his uncle, Sir Hugo Mallinger, is,” she said, rather too loudly. “For all he’s decades younger, I wouldn’t be at all surprised if Sir Hugo outlives him. It’s never wise to count on inheritances.”
“Indeed it is not,” Mrs. Davilow agreed serenely, so pleased with the turn things were taking that her usual quiet melancholy had completely vanished.

There is no need to dwell on the spread of cold meats and champagne laid out in that green forest clearing, or the tales of the vast rolling forest stretching beyond the hollow. The world outside their small circle barely registered to the party that afternoon, all their attention wrapped up in this tiny, glittering social drama as small and self-contained as a puppet show. The food and wine were of the finest quality, the laughter and banter the easy, unforced kind only high society can produce, where no one overplays their privilege, and all the advantages of the world are taken with that casual, well-bred nonchalance that comes of having never wanted for anything. Some of the gentlemen drifted off to smoke cigars, leaving a pleasant lull before the party was due to set off again at four o’clock. Oddly enough, Grandcourt was among them—but not Mr. Lush, who was behaving with uncharacteristic, over-eager helpfulness all afternoon, ordering every detail of the picnic for the guests, fussing over every small need: a presence that made Gwendolen’s skin crawl, though he carefully kept his distance, never daring to look at her directly. When the party began to gather their things to leave, all the archers’ bows had been left in the care of Lord Brackenshaw’s valet, and Lush had offered to fetch them all from the carriage for the ladies. Gwendolen, fearing he might be the one to hand her her own bow, hurried to get it herself. The valet saw her coming, met her halfway with the bow in hand—and slipped a sealed letter into her other palm. She did not question him, saw at a glance the soft, delicate, old-fashioned feminine handwriting on the envelope, and stepped away from the carriage holding her bow, just as Lush rounded the corner to collect the rest of the weapons. To avoid him, she turned her back to the carriages and stepped behind a tree to tear open the note. It read:
> If Miss Harleth is in doubt whether she should accept Mr. Grandcourt, let her break from her party after they have passed the Whispering Stones and return to that spot. She will then hear something to decide her; but she can only hear it by keeping this letter a strict secret from every one. If she does not act according to this letter, she will repent, as the woman who writes it has repented. The secrecy Miss Harleth will feel herself bound in honor to guard.

Gwendolen felt a sharp jolt of shock run through her—but her first, strange thought was It has come just in time. Youth made her leap immediately to the idea of a long-held, important revelation, no flicker of suspicion that the note might be a trick. She resolved instantly that she would slip away unseen to the Whispering Stones, tucked the note deep in her pocket, and turned back to the party with a strange, bracing thrill of having a secret, that quiet sense of hidden purpose that always made her feel sharper, more in control of herself.

Everyone was surprised when it was time to leave and Grandcourt was nowhere to be found, the other smokers already back and ready to go. “We’ll run into him soon enough,” Lord Brackenshaw laughed. “He can’t have gone far.” No one bothered to wait for him: the group joked that he was so lost in daydreams about Gwendolen he had forgotten they were supposed to meet, and the good-natured earl even tossed Gwendolen a playful little quip to that effect, which she accepted with quiet, appropriate demureness. But her own thought was darker: Is he running away from this decision, too? It was not a pleasant thought—but it was near the truth. Grandcourt, wrapped up in a lazy, hazy fit of stubborn numbness, had lit a second large cigar, lingering well away from the group for no reason he could even name, simply because the idea of reaching a definite end made him want to drag his feet, to defy everyone’s expectations just for the petty thrill of exerting his own will. He would have sent any mortal fool who dared interrupt him straight to the devil, and no one dared interrupt him that afternoon.

The rovers set off without him, their spirits unflagging, leaving behind only the less energetic ladies, including Mrs. Davilow, who preferred a quiet, unhurried stroll without the pressure to keep pace. The afternoon light softened and deepened, and the archery grew more spirited as the party moved through changing groves and sunlit glades, every shadow longer and lovelier than the last. They joked that they were living out their own improvised version of *As You Like It*, and when someone complimented Gwendolen that she was the perfect Rosalind for their little forest play, she leaned into the part, glowing brighter than ever. It was no hard work: the thrill of adventure thrumming in her veins, the quiet planning to slip away unseen, made her more radiant than usual.

It was nearly an hour of wandering before they reached the Whispering Stones: two tall, conical gray boulders leaning toward each other like two giant, cloaked figures. The group paused to laugh that they would make perfect ghosts on a starlit night, then moved on, unaware that Gwendolen had lingered a step behind. The stones sat right beside a grove of tall beeches full of perfect archery marks.
“About how far are we from Green Arbor, warden?” Gwendolen asked, falling into step beside him.
“Only half a mile if you take that avenue over there, but we’re going the long, scenic way ‘round, two miles out past the High Cross.”
She drifted back to the rest of the group, and just as she had hoped, Mr. Lush hurried the whole party forward in a rush, and Gwendolen slipped unnoticed away from the line, turning back toward the Whispering Stones. She did not run, but it felt like she flew over the grass, counting the seconds until she rounded the right-hand boulder—where a pair of large, dark, unflinching eyes met hers, only a foot away. Even prepared, Gwendolen flinched back, taking in the full figure of the woman in front of her: unmistakably a lady, one who must have been breathtakingly beautiful in her youth. A few yards behind her, two small children sat playing in the grass.
“Miss Harleth?” the woman said.
“Yes.” Gwendolen could do nothing but stare in wonder.
“Have you accepted Mr. Grandcourt?”
“No.”
“I promised to tell you something. And you must promise me to keep my secret. No matter what you choose to do next, you will not tell Mr. Grandcourt, or any other living soul, that you have seen me today?”
“I promise.”
“My name is Lydia Glasher. Mr. Grandcourt ought never to marry anyone but me. Nine years ago, I left my husband and my child for him. Those two little ones behind me are his, and we have two older girls besides. My husband is dead now. Mr. Grandcourt promised he would marry me, that he would make that little boy my son his heir.” She nodded toward the boy as she spoke, and Gwendolen’s eyes followed hers. The small, handsome little fellow was puffing out his cheeks trying to make a tiny trumpet make a sound, his hat hanging off the back of his head by a string, his soft brown curls catching the last of the sun like gold. He looked like a cherub.
The two women’s eyes met again, and Gwendolen spoke, her voice sharp and proud even as she began to shiver, her lips going pale. “I will not interfere with your wishes.”
“You are very beautiful, Miss Harleth,” Lydia said, her tone sharp with old, bitter grief, no hint of histrionics in her steady voice. “But when he first knew me, I was just as young. Since then, my life has been broken, embittered, torn to pieces. It is not fair that he should get to be happy, while I am miserable, and my own son is hidden away and replaced by a new wife’s children.”
Gwendolen stared at her as she spoke, a cold, crawling terror seeping into her bones, as if a ghastly dream figure had stepped out of the dark and said, I am what a woman’s life becomes when a man breaks it.
“Have you anything more to say to me?” Gwendolen asked, her voice low but still cold, still proud. The sharp, sudden revulsion churning in her chest did not soften her, it made every person around her feel hateful, untrustworthy.
“Nothing. You know everything I needed you to know. You can look me up if you like, my husband was Colonel Glasher.”
“Then I will take my leave,” Gwendolen said, giving a stiff, ceremonious little bow, which Lydia returned with equal quiet grace.

Within minutes, Gwendolen was back in the beech grove, but the rest of her party had vanished entirely, and no one had noticed she was gone enough to send anyone looking. She hurried down the short avenue the warden had mentioned, and was back at Green Arbor in no time, walking fast as if she could outrun the whirling, screaming thoughts in her head that threatened to break her calm. She already knew exactly what she was going to do.
Mrs. Davilow was shocked to see her return alone, a quiet anxiety pricking at her that she could not show with all the other ladies present. When she asked what had happened, Gwendolen laughed it off. “Oh, I was being silly. I stopped to stare at the Whispering Stones for a minute, and everyone else hurried on after something in the trees, so I lost sight of them. I thought it made far more sense to come back the short way the warden told me about. I’m not sorry, my feet are tired of walking anyway.”
“I don’t suppose your group ran into Mr. Grandcourt, then?” Mrs. Arrowpoint asked, pointedly.
“No,” Gwendolen said, with a flash of defiance

*“Festina lente—let celerity be tempered by care.”* — Sir Thomas Browne

For weeks abroad, Gwendolen Harleth had drunk deep of the giddy new thrill of the gambling tables, half-convinced she was born an empress of luck. The sharp upheaval that had wrecked her family’s fortune at the hands of the shadowy, ruthless lenders known only as Grapnell & Co. had left her with a reckless, faint belief that nothing in the world mattered so long as you could amuse yourself: that all the old rules of prudence were made for people too dull to bend fate to their will. But try as she might to ignore it, a heavy little secret clung to her when she sailed back to England: the pearl necklace she’d pawned in her lowest moment, which some unknown stranger had redeemed and returned to her, against every wish of hers.

While she was making her way home, the man who’d come close to laying a marriage proposal at her feet was journeying to find her — but in his own endless, unhurried fashion. Mr. Grandcourt had not rushed post-haste from his estate at Diplow straight to Leubronn, where he’d been told she was staying. The presence of a few Russian acquaintances at Baden-Baden had made him linger for days, making and breaking plans with them on a lazy whim, his desire to find Gwendolen only pricking sharp enough at last to drag him away from their company. Grandcourt’s passions never blazed hot; they flickered low, unhurried, as unshowy as the careful way he tied his cravats, or the empty expected speeches he made toasting royal dignitaries at dinner. A man could glide through the highest tiers of society, be hailed as the perfect specimen of an English gentleman, full of quiet wit and repressed strong opinion, all on barely a spark of vital energy — and still be stubborn, still hold a quiet, inexplicable pull that made women turn to look twice at him.

He had not been even the least bit annoyed that Gwendolen had run off without a word the day before he planned to call on her to formally ask for her hand. The act had piqued him. He liked to imagine she’d fled out of sharp, proud resentment at his cold, careless behavior to her in Cardell Chase, when he’d drawn her so close to soft, tender feelings for him, then turned and walked away with no further care for winning her. A girl with spirit, that, he thought — just the kind of wife worth the work of taming. He was sure she meant for him to chase her, and chase her he would… eventually. For a whole week, he never so much as asked anyone where she’d gone.

Mr. Lush, his right-hand man, seethed with a glee half-mixed with distrust. Grandcourt had not breathed a single word about Gwendolen to him, his face as blank and unreadable as an alligator’s. But the longer he delayed a decision, the more of his scattered, low-burning energy wasted away, and Lush could not have been happier.

The guests staying at Diplow, though, were bubbling over with curiosity. How was no one hearing word of Miss Harleth? Was it possible she’d actually refused the great, wealthy Mr. Grandcourt? Lady Flora Hollis, a lively, middle-aged woman with an endless appetite for gossip, set off to pay calls all across the neighborhood: stopping first at the rectory, then Offendene, then Quetcham, and gossiped over tea with the Arrowpoints till she’d twice confirmed the same story — Gwendolen had run off to Leubronn with old family friends the Baron and Baroness von Langen. Mrs. Davilow and the Gascoignes, still raw from the shock of her flight, had decided there was no use pretending her trip was some secret, odd disgrace, and the rector had even convinced himself the whole thing was just a classic coquette’s trick. Amaryllis flees the one she loves so he will chase her, he told himself, quoting his old familiar verses. Gwendolen, he thought, was a clever, daring Amaryllis — the only question was if she’d dared too far.

Lady Flora returned to Diplow brimming with news, and did not hesitate to drop it right in front of Grandcourt over dinner, teasing a little that rumor was saying he was a disappointed suitor. He heard it all with perfect quiet, no flicker of surprise on his face, and the very next day told Lush to find a polite excuse to break up the house party at the end of the week — he was off yachting to the Baltic, or somewhere far away, he said, he could not bear to stay cooped up at Diplow like a man on parole with a set of guests he’d never invited in the first place. Lush did not need to be told twice: he knew exactly Grandcourt was heading for Leubronn, and he made himself indispensable enough that he was allowed to tag along. Gwendolen’s sharp, open hatred of him only amused his patron, who liked having Lush close at hand no matter where he went.

When Grandcourt stepped through the doors of the Hotel Czarina at Leubronn, it was five days after Gwendolen had already left. And waiting there, by total coincidence, was his uncle Sir Hugo Mallinger, his wife, their three daughters — and Daniel Deronda. The heir presumptive and the reigning squire of the Mallinger estates were never pleased to stumble across each other by accident. Sir Hugo was an easy-tempered man, but he had carried a lifelong bitter grievance: after three daughters born in quick succession, his wife had borne him no more children at all, and the family will had locked every acre of the Mallinger lands, including his beloved Diplow, in a settlement that meant he only held a life interest. Grandcourt, the cold, tedious nephew he had never liked, stood to inherit everything when he died. For years, Sir Hugo had dreamed of striking a deal: pay Grandcourt a huge sum of ready cash, and buy the Diplow estate from him, so his wife and daughters would have a proper home to retire to after he was gone. He had saved and invested shrewdly for years, hoarding the funds for just that moment, and he was determined to stay on perfectly polite terms with Grandcourt, no matter how much he bored him.

Grandcourt, for his part, thought his uncle a tedious bore he would be glad to see the back of — but he knew full well Sir Hugo wanted Diplow, and the power to refuse what his uncle craved fed his sense of his own importance perfectly. The two men had never been more than perfectly civil to each other, neither willing to risk a falling-out that would spoil their own quiet plans.

On the first evening of Grandcourt’s arrival, all four men — Sir Hugo, Grandcourt, Deronda, and Lush — wandered out to the terrace after the table d’hote dinner, then drifted inside to saunter through the glittering, smoke-heavy gambling rooms.
“Did you gamble much in Baden?” Sir Hugo asked Grandcourt.
“Hardly touched it,” he drawled, flicking a finger against his perfectly curled whisker. “Just watched, bet a little with some Russians. Boring strain, playing these days.”
“What did you win again, Lush?”
“About two hundred pounds,” his man answered.
“Ah, then you’re not here for the tables?” Sir Hugo said. “I never cared for gambling myself. Wraps your brain up in knots, gives me a headache. The air in these rooms is poison, I never stay ten minutes. Deronda, where is that pretty gambling beauty of yours? The one who was playing here a few days back — have you seen her lately?”
“She’s gone,” Deronda said, quiet and short.
“Uncommonly fine girl, a perfect Diana,” Sir Hugo went on, turning back to Grandcourt. “Worth straining your neck to look at. She won a fortune one day, took it as cool as if she’d known every fall of the card beforehand. The day Deronda saw her lose every penny, she didn’t flinch once, carried it like a queen. Must have decided it was time to head home. How do you know she’s left, Deronda?”
“Checked the visitor list,” Deronda said, with a faint, almost unnoticeable shrug. “Vandernoodt told me her name was Harleth, staying with the Von Langens. I saw her name was crossed off the new list.”

Lush already knew Gwendolen was gone, but he had no intention of telling Grandcourt until he asked — but Grandcourt had heard every single word. He paused a beat, then turned to Deronda. “You know those people, the Langens?”
“Talked to them a little after she left. Never met them before.”
“Where has she gone home to?”
“Offendene, near Winchester,” Deronda answered. His voice was cool, like he did not want to say another word on the subject — then he turned, and fixed Grandcourt with that sharp, steady, intense gaze of his, the kind that made strangers stop short, half-convinced he had already said something to them they had not heard. “Not far from your estate at Diplow.”
Grandcourt felt a flicker of sharp, unshown irritation at the way Deronda had turned to stare at him unprompted. He did not let it show, save for a tiny twitch of his eyelid, and drawled, “Yes, I know her,” before turning his shoulder to Deronda to watch a croupier rake in a pile of gold.

Sir Hugo pulled Lush to the side a little, as they wandered out to the open promenade for fresh air. “What’s the story with that girl Harleth? Old Blenny lived at Offendene after the dowager died, didn’t he?”
“Far too much of her,” Lush murmured, low and meaningful. He was happy to let Sir Hugo in on the secret. “He was on the brink of marrying her, a week or two back. Hope it’s off now. She’s the rector’s niece, no fortune at all, living with her widowed mother and a whole brood of younger sisters. Dangerous as gunpowder. The silliest match he could possibly make. She took a freak against him, ran off here without a single warning the day he was meant to call on her. Fact is, he’s come all this way after her — but he was never in any great hurry. Between his caprice and hers, they’ll probably never get back together. That means he’s lost his chance to marry the rich Miss Arrowpoint, too.”
Grandcourt wandered back to them a minute later. “Ghastly hole this place is. Worse than Baden. I’m heading back to the hotel.”

When Sir Hugo and Deronda were left alone, the baronet let out a low chuckle. “Quite a little story. That girl has fire in her. Plenty of unexpected spark. If this romance goes anywhere — or even if it doesn’t, and Grandcourt wastes a year chasing her all over the continent — it makes my chance of buying Diplow all the better.”
“I hope no such foolish marriage ever happens,” Deronda said, his tone sharp with quiet disgust.
“Are you a little smitten yourself?” Sir Hugo teased, lifting his glasses to squint at the younger man. “Wouldn’t surprise me. You could easily out-charm Grandcourt. A girl with that much spirit would pick you over him, easy.”
“On the contrary,” Deronda said, cold. “I should far rather run far away from her.”
“Fine thoroughbred beats a fancy pedigree every time, my boy,” Sir Hugo laughed. “Napoleon said he was his own ancestor, remember? You could be that for her.”
“I’m not sure I have any desire to be anyone’s ancestor,” Deronda said. “That hardly seems like the rarest kind of new beginning.”
“So you won’t be chasing the pretty gambler, then?” Sir Hugo said, setting his glasses back down.
“Decidedly not.”

The answer was perfectly truthful. But for a split second, Deronda had pictured what might have been, if he were a free man — the wild, proud girl he’d watched across the gambling tables, winning and losing with equal grace, the strange pull she had on his curiosity. But his own unspoken, tangled history left him no freedom to chase any romantic adventure, no matter how bright the spark. He had no right to step into this story at all.

Every man, like the planets wheeling overhead, carries two lives: the one the world can trace, laid out in public acts and spoken words, and the hidden, winding history of feeling and thought no other soul can ever fully map. The astronomer chases dark gaps in a star’s orbit with strict, patient deduction; a storyteller who did their work half so thoroughly would trace every quiet, unspoken path that leads a soul to its sharpest choices, and its sharpest suffering—anguish so vivid it burns brighter than the open sky and the sea, the cry of a chained Prometheus defying the god who bound him.

Daniel Deronda’s life had been stitched from exception from the very start. One searing, sun-drenched memory burned into the core of him would become the turning point of all his years: a July afternoon, great blushing pink roses shedding soft petals across short grass of a walled courtyard, shut in on three sides by the weathered stone arches of a Gothic cloister. There lay thirteen-year-old Daniel, sprawled prone in the shade, his curly dark head propped on his forearms as he pored over Sismondi’s *History of the Italian Republics*, while his tutor, the sharp young Scotsman Mr. Fraser, sat on a camp stool a few feet away, half lost in his own volume of political economy. The boy was ravenous for history, hungry to know every scrap of how the world had turned since the great flood, how people had lived through all the dull, unchronicled stretches of the past.
Without warning, he dropped his left arm to the grass and lifted his head to stare at his tutor, voice clear and unselfconscious in its pure boyish lilt: “Mr. Fraser, how did the popes and cardinals always end up with so many nephews?”
Mr. Fraser roused from his book, a little irritated at the interruption, and his broad Scotch accent carried the blunt truth with the crisp weight of a well-honed blade: “Their own children were the ones they called nephews, lad. They couldn’t marry, you see, as priests, and the children were born out of wedlock. They hid the truth behind the name, for decency’s sake.”
He had already dropped his eyes back to his book before the words fully landed. But Daniel felt as if a wasp had stung him sharp, right through the thin fabric of his shirt. He jolted upright, his back turned fully to his tutor, his face burning so hot his ears turned scarlet.

He had always called Sir Hugo Mallinger his uncle. Every time he’d asked after his parents as a small child, the baronet had smiled gently and told him he’d lost both of them in a fever when he was tiny, which was why Sir Hugo had taken him in to raise as his own. Daniel’s only fragment of that far-off early life was a soft, blurry memory: the scent of lavender linen, someone kissing his cheeks over and over, till his small fingers caught on a sharp hard brooch that pricked him and he’d started to wail. Every other memory he owned was rooted in the world of Monk’s Topping: the grand old abbey estate that had been in the Mallinger family since Henry VIII had seized it from the monks, its halls lined with generations of portraits from armored medieval knights to ladies painted in Lely’s signature soft rosebud mouths, all looking down on the “nephew” Daniel as he wandered the cloisters. None of those faces looked anything like him, though he was fairer, softer, more achingly beautiful than any of the stiff painted ancestors, the kind of boy a painter would beg to use as a model for the perfect young saint.

But that afternoon, the world he’d thought was bright and whole split open at the seams. A new idea bloomed sharp and hot in his mind: the man he’d worshipped as the kindest, most perfect uncle alive was not his uncle at all. He was his father. And the pretty name “nephew” had been just as much of a lie for Daniel as it had been for those hidden papal children in his history book. He sat stock still, his back to his tutor, his mind racing so fast he could barely breathe. Who was his mother? What had become of her? Why had he been taken away from her? The thought of ever saying the words out loud, of asking anyone about it, felt like swallowing hot coals. Great, slow, heavy tears started rolling down his cheeks before he even realized he was crying, hidden from his tutor’s eyes till Mr. Fraser finally spoke and made him jump: “Daniel, lad, you’re sitting right on the bent pages of your book.”

He snatched the book up without turning around, stumbled to his feet, and walked fast out into the open park till he could wipe his tears unseen. For days after, the secret hung over him, soft and heavy, like a veil draped over every corner of his life. He noticed every offhand comment, every wink between the servants, every pause in conversation when he walked into a room, and a quiet, gentle reserve grew in him that no one had seen before.

A month later, that quiet hurt came boiling to the surface. Daniel had a voice like no other, rich and warm, the kind that could make grown men stop talking just to listen, and Sir Hugo loved to make him sing for guests. One rainy morning, after he’d sung “Sweet Echo” for a small party of gentlemen stuck inside by the downpour, Sir Hugo called him over, grinning: “Come here, Dan! What do you say to being a great world-famous singer? You could have crowds adoring you, taking the opera house by storm, just like Mario and Tamberlik!”
Daniel’s face flushed bright red, and before he could stop himself he blurted out, angry and sharp: “No. I would hate it.”
He turned and ran out of the room before anyone could soothe him, locking himself in his favorite window nook where he could look out over the rain-slicked park, the great gnarled old oaks standing under streaks of sunlight breaking through the clouds. The thought made his chest ache so bad he could barely stand it: Sir Hugo, the man he’d thought would never want anything but the best gentleman’s life for him, was thinking of parading him onstage like a pretty toy for strangers to gawk at. That could only mean his birth made him unsuitable for the quiet, dignified life he’d always taken for granted. He thought back to old Banks the bailiff, years ago, winking at his wife and saying “He features the mother, eh?” when he’d been drinking a mug of warm whey at the farmhouse. Back then he’d thought it was just the silly way farm hands talked. Now it burned in his brain, a clue to a secret he dared not dig for. He wanted to sneak down to the library and pry open the old cabinet that held the Mallinger family tree, to see if his name was written anywhere on that yellow parchment—but his shame was too strong, and he never went.

But days later, Sir Hugo called him into his study, and patted the stool beside his armchair, his hand warm and gentle on Daniel’s dark curls. “I wasn’t serious about the singing, lad. I’ve got other plans for you. You’re going to Eton. To get the full education of an English gentleman, before you go up to Cambridge, just like I did.”
Daniel’s heart soared. All at once, the heavy secret in his chest felt a little lighter. He would get to be a gentleman, after all. He would stay. The joy flooded back into him fast, the bright boyish spirit that no quiet hurt could fully quench: he danced through the halls making little parting gifts for the old servants, fussed over his black pony for hours to make sure the groom would take perfect care of him while he was away at school, and left off brooding on his secrets for a while.
At his first new tutor’s house, he met a boy who was eager to make a fast friend, who chattered endlessly about his own home and his parents, and asked Daniel to tell him all about his own family. Before he even thought about it, Daniel drew back, quiet and reserved, and never spoke a word of his hidden past to the other boy. Everyone who knew him thought him a sweet, kind, unassuming lad, a little quiet, a little reserved—but no one guessed the secret pain he carried under his soft, beautiful smile, the hidden history that would shape every choice he ever made for the rest of his life.

“This is true the poet sings, That a sorrow’s crown of sorrow Is remembering happier things.”
—TENNYSON, *In Memoriam*

The last honey-gold days of July draped soft, fading light over the Thames the evening Daniel Deronda’s quiet, unmoored life shifted on its axis.

It had been more than a year since he’d returned to England, his formal education complete, expected to slip easily into the gilded routine Sir Hugo Mallinger had laid out for him. At the baronet’s gentle urging, he’d begun reading law just to fend off the lazy, aimless idleness that threatened to swallow him whole—but the half-hearted decision only tangled his existing indecision deeper. What was he meant for, exactly? The old, unspoken question hummed under his skin day and night: the secret of his unknown parent, the face he’d spent half his life trying to picture, the vague, heavy sense that he was permanently out of step with every other privileged young man about town.

Rowing was his only true escape. He kept a small, sleek boat moored at Putney, and whenever Sir Hugo didn’t need him, he’d push off at dusk and row until the sun sank low and the first stars pricked the purple sky, the river wrapping around him like a quiet cloak no one else could pierce. That evening he wore a loose dark blue shirt and a close-fitting skull-cap, his curls cropped short, a soft wave of beard framing his mouth—no trace left of the seraphic little boy who’d once trailed clouds of glory, but the rare, gentle gravity of his gaze was still there: the exact look Gwendolen Harleth had once complained was “dreadful” for how softly, unflinchingly it saw right through you. His hands were long, strong, flexible, the kind of hands that could pull an oar for hours without tiring, that could hold a falling person steady, no trace of the foppish, dainty grace affected by other rich young men.

He’d been half-humming under his breath the whole way upriver, the mournful gondolier’s song from *Otello* that set Dante’s lines to music: no greater sorrow than to remember happy times in the depths of misery. He didn’t even realize he was singing out loud when he pulled aside to make way for a heavy barge near Kew Bridge, resting his oars an arm’s length from the riverbank.

The first notes of the final pianissimo line, nella miseria, drifted over the water.

Most of the people meandering on the towing path barely noticed him. But one girl standing a few yards down the bank froze. She couldn’t have been more than eighteen, small and slim as a wren, a delicate little pale face peeking out from under a large black hat, a heavy long wool cloak wrapped around her shoulders even on that warm summer night. Her hands were clasped tight in front of her, her eyes fixed dead on the surface of the river with a look of statue-perfect despair, so still and unblinking she might have been carved from marble.

Deronda stopped singing mid-phrase. The sudden silence jolted her. She turned, their eyes locked, and for a long, stretched beat of time the whole rest of the world fell away. Her gaze was soft, skittish, like a fawn about to bolt into the undergrowth—no flush, no sharp alarm, just a quiet timidity that made her hold his gaze a fraction too long before she turned and walked slowly to a weathered wooden bench tucked under a tree.

His chest ached. He had no right to linger, no right to intrude on a stranger’s private grief, so he picked up his oars and rowed on. But he couldn’t shake the image: the dark folds of her cloak, the pale cameo of her face, the long dark lashes fanned over her cheeks. He knew there were a thousand lost, lonely girls slipping through the cracks of the world, their tragedies unremarked, unwritten, like small wounded birds dragging broken wings to die hidden in the hedgerows. But that evening, that quiet sorrow tangled with all his own old doubts—what right did he have to slip into the easy, unthinking routine of society, to apologize for all its wrongs just to get ahead? He let his oars fall slack, let the tide carry him slowly back downstream as the sun sank lower, painting the sky and the river in double streaks of rose and amber.

He tucked his boat into the shadow of a line of willows on the bank opposite Kew Gardens, propping his head on his hands against the cushions to watch the sun set, to wait for the stars to prick through the dusk, that soft sacred hour he loved more than any other. He’d almost forgotten the girl entirely, half lost in his own soft reverie, when he saw movement on the bank across the water.

It was her.

A cold jolt of dread shot through him. He didn’t move, didn’t dare startle her. She glanced around to make sure no one else was near, pushed her hat deep into the willow bushes, then shrugged off her heavy wool cloak. She lowered it slowly into the river, held it under the surface until it was heavy and saturated, until Deronda understood exactly what she planned to do: wrap that water-logged cloak around herself, sink into the dark, and never come up.

He didn’t hesitate. He grabbed his oar and drove the boat across the stretch of water in two sharp strokes.

She looked up, terrified, and sank down onto the muddy bank, clutching the half-soaked cloak to her chest, covering her face like she could wish him away if she tried hard enough. But he steadied the boat right at the edge, and spoke so softly his voice was barely louder than the lapping of the water.

“Don’t be afraid. You’re unhappy. Please trust me. Tell me what I can do to help you.”

She dropped her hands and looked up. His face was lit by the last of the sunset, and she knew him. The man from the bridge, the man who’d been singing that song that had wormed its way straight into her empty, hurting chest. For a long moment they just looked at each other again.

“I saw you before,” she said, her voice low and sweet, a faint lilt to it that felt almost foreign, but not quite. Then, dreamily, like the words had fallen out of her before she could stop them: “Nella miseria.”

It all clicked. “You heard me singing Nessun maggior dolore,” he said, and the sound of the words on her tongue made the melody hum in his own ears. “You mustn’t stay here, you’ll catch your death. Let me take you somewhere safe. Let me take that cloak.”

She clutched it tighter for a second, her eyes searching his face, wide and wondering. “You look good. Perhaps this is God’s command.”

“Trust me,” he said, leaning forward, his voice low and fierce with earnestness. “I will die before I let any harm come to you.”

She stood then, let the heavy saturated cloak slip from her hands onto the wet grass, her small chilled hands clasped tight over her waist, stepping so close to the boat her toes nearly touched the edge of the hull. The words Great God! slipped out of him unbidden, a quiet prayer, and the old, old thought flashed through his mind—maybe the mother he’d never known, the mother he’d spent years wondering about, had once stood just like this, alone and desolate, no one to reach out a hand to her.

She let him wrap his hand around hers, his big, warm one closing soft around her tiny cold palm. Then she hesitated, drawing back a little. “I have nowhere to go. No one belonging to me in all this land.”

“I’ll take you to a kind lady who has daughters,” Deronda said immediately. He wouldn’t bring her to the grand, gilded formal house in Park Lane, not to face staring footmen and sharp suspicious maids, not to jolt her fragile, frayed nerves. He’d take her to the warm little cottage in Chelsea, to Mrs. Meyrick, the Quaker-soft widow who’d been a second mother to his friend Hans, and Hans’s three sweet, romantic daughters who still thought all the drama in the world was out of history books and old plays, who’d treat this lost girl like Rebecca from *Ivanhoe*, who’d take her in no questions asked.

“Are you part of the theatre?” she asked, timidly.

“Not at all,” he said, smiling, holding out his coat to her. She was shivering now, in the cool dusk, and she wrapped it around her shoulders, the fabric soft and warm from his body. She wouldn’t take his biscuits, said she’d bought bread that morning with the last of her money, and that she couldn’t eat a bite anyway.

He rowed steadily downstream, the twilight deepening all around them, the stars pricking out one by one. She sat quiet on the cushions, watching the dip of his oar in the water, the tension slowly draining out of her small frame.

“If you hadn’t come,” she said, after a long silence, “I’d be dead now.”

“Never speak of that,” he said, his throat tight. “I hope you’ll never be sorry I found you.”

“I don’t know how I’ll ever be glad to live,” she whispered. “The sorrow has lasted far longer than the happy times. I thought death couldn’t be wrong—that before God, life and death are the same, that I could keep my soul pure. But now… I’m commanded to live. I don’t know how.”

“You’ll find friends,” he promised her. “I’ll find them for you.”

She shook her head, tears glistening on her cheeks. “Not my mother and brother. I ran away from far across the sea, to find them. I thought God would guide me. This morning I woke up and all I could hear was never, never. But now…” She paused, looking right at him, straining to see his face in the faint moonlight. “You want to know if I’m English? I was born here. But I’m a Jewess.”

Deronda went still, his chest flooding with a strange, soft, thunderous recognition. All the years he’d spent wondering about his own hidden heritage, all the half-formed questions he’d never dared to ask—suddenly they had a face, a story, a life right in front of him.

“Do you despise me for it?” she whispered, like a small hurt animal.

“Of course not,” he said, gentle and firm. “Only a fool would despise anyone for that. There are good and bad people of every faith.”

The tears burst out of her then, great heaving sobs, and she buried her face in her knees. Deronda said nothing, just rowed on, carrying her away from the river that had almost claimed her, toward the little warm house in Chelsea, toward the rest of their lives. That evening, he felt, he had grown years older in a single hour. For the first time in all his adrift, unmoored life, he had found a purpose: not to fight some grand abstract battle of the world, but to keep this small, fragile lost soul safe. It felt less like a burden, and more like a miracle.

Life is a mother of endless faces: one day she drapes herself in plumes and gemstones, climbs marble staircases with her head held high, never glancing back at the footmen trailing in her wake; the next, she tramps grim and shabby down dark alleyways, breathes the fumes of hot gin, screams in the raw chaos of a pauper’s riot. But for the Meyrick women, she arrived as a frugal, brisk, cheerful matron, sharp-eyed at making joy stretch thin, who found whole worlds of warmth in the smallest, shabbiest slice of foggy London.

Their narrow, unassuming house hugged the river on one side, and sun-dappled back gardens on the other, so on that mild autumn evening, all the windows stood open to freshen the air of the double front parlor lit by a single lamp and two beeswax candles. One candle sat at a separate small table for Kate, hunched over pen and ink, drawing book illustrations for a local publisher. The lamp lit Mrs. Meyrick as she read aloud from a tattered French novel, and cast soft golden light over Amy and Mab, who stitched satin cushions embroidered with wild poppies and roses for wealthy clients in “the great world.” From the street outside, the glow through their holland blind showed off the heavy, old-fashioned window frames, making the place look like just one more grim, cramped home carved out of London’s overcrowded rows. But few outsiders would guess how much gentle, unvulgar richness lived inside its walls: poverty had stripped away any urge to perform grandeur, left the Meyricks free to love beauty for its own sake, no petty rivalry for fine displays to taint it.

Every inch of their parlor held beloved, familiar things: the old engravings Mr. Meyrick had collected before his death, which the children had memorized the faces and stories of before they could read full sentences, the scuffed piano in the corner, the frayed wool rug stitched by Mrs. Meyrick as a new bride. Their furniture was worn, no secondhand broker would have wasted time haggling over it save for the prints and the piano, but it held more space for joy in music, poetry, and painting than many a grand townhouse stuffed with gaudy new possessions. Even in their hardest years, before Kate’s illustration work brought in steady pay, when they had no servant to light their fires or sweep the floors, they remained fastidious and kind, refusing to believe the high society tales they read in novels were anything but cruel exaggeration of how ladies ought to behave. They were a little eccentric, these four Meyricks, their minds like medieval houses full of hidden nooks, sudden staircases, and unexpected bright outlooks—bound tight by three unbreakable ties: fierce family love, a shared devotion to the finest art and kindest deeds, and a habit of gentle, uncomplaining industry. When their son Hans, the painter, had tried to spend his first big paycheck buying them fancy new furniture and a full staff, they’d all turned him down flat, happy instead to save up for a single grand treat: a trip to the opera, sitting in the cheap gallery seats, whenever he came home to visit.

Looking at them that evening, no one could have wished their life to be any different. They were all small in stature, perfectly suited to their miniature rooms: Mrs. Meyrick, barely five feet tall, half French half Scotch, lively and quick-witted, with rippling silver hair tucked under a plain Quaker cap, bright brown eyes, and a black buttoned dress cut almost like a priest’s cassock that suited her neat frame perfectly. Her three daughters matched her size: Amy soft and round, Kate sharp and focused behind her drawing, and Mab with Hans’ pale sun-streaked hair and faint, quirky little facial tics that reminded the whole family of him. All of them wore plain grey skirts, cut unapologetically against the current fashion that would have required four layers of crinoline to swallow every spare inch of their small parlor, their dark hair coiled tight at the nape of their necks in a simple Chinese knot. Even the largest thing in the room was perfectly mild: Hafiz, their fluffy Persian cat, balanced on the back of the oldest leather chair, opening his great golden eyes every few minutes to make sure no one forgot to give him a scratch.

Mrs. Meyrick had just finished reading the last page of Erckmann-Chatrian’s *Histoire d’un Conscrit* when Mab dropped her embroidery on the floor, leaning forward so far she nearly tipped off her chair.
“That is the finest story in the whole world,” she declared, breathless.
“Of course it is,” Amy teased, grinning. “It’s the last one you heard. Everything that makes you giddy is automatically the best thing that’s ever been written.”
“It’s barely even a story,” Kate put in, leaning back to squint at her half-finished illustration. “It’s a piece of history pulled right up to our eyes with a telescope. We can see the soldiers’ faces, hear their boots squelching through mud—we can almost feel their hearts beating.”
“I don’t care what you call it!” Mab swatted her thimble across the table, giddy. “Call it a chapter out of Revelation. It makes me want to do something grand, something kind. I feel like Schiller—like I could wrap the whole world in my arms and kiss it. I’ll kiss you instead, little mother!” She threw herself across the room to tuck her arms around Mrs. Meyrick’s neck.
“Whenever you get this heroic, your work goes straight to the floor,” Amy laughed. “It would be a very good deed to finish that cushion you’re stitching without getting ink all over it.”
“Oh no,” Mab groaned, bending to grab her discarded hoop. “I wish I had three wounded conscripts right here to nurse back to health, instead of satin poppies.”
“You’d spill all their beef tea while you were telling them how brave they are,” Amy said.
“Poor thing, don’t tease her,” Mrs. Meyrick said, holding out a hand for the embroidery hoop. “You keep your grand, heroic thoughts, Mab. I’ll stitch the pink poppies for you.”
“Mama, I think you’re more teasing than Amy,” Kate said, still squinting at her drawing.
Mab hopped to her feet, stretching her arms over her head. “I wish something wonderful would happen right now. I feel like the flood—all the windows of heaven are open! I’m going to play scales on the piano.”

She was halfway to the piano keys when a cab pulled up sharp outside the narrow house, followed by a quick, urgent rap on the knocker.
“Dear me,” Mrs. Meyrick said, jumping to her feet. “It’s past ten, and Phoebe the maid already went up to bed.” She hurried out of the parlor, leaving the door propped open.
The three girls heard her gasp, clear as day from the hall: “Mr. Deronda!”
Mab clasped her hands to her mouth, whispering so loud it was almost a shout: “There! Something wonderful *did* happen!” Kate and Amy dropped their work in surprise, but Deronda’s voice was so low, gentle and harried, that they couldn’t make out a single word he said, and a moment later Mrs. Meyrick closed the parlor door tight behind her.
“I know I’m asking for an extraordinary favor, trusting you like this,” Deronda was saying, the faint shake in his voice obvious even to her. “You can’t imagine how helpless I felt when I pulled her out of the river, that poor young thing, just minutes from drowning. I couldn’t drag her off to a house full of strangers, not in that fragile, terrified state. I trusted you would be the only ones who wouldn’t turn her away. I hope you don’t think my act is unwarrantable.”
“On the contrary—you honor me by trusting us,” Mrs. Meyrick said at once. “I see exactly how hard this must be for you. Bring her right in. I’ll go prepare the girls.”

While Deronda stepped back out to the waiting cab, Mrs. Meyrick slipped back into the parlor to face her three wide-eyed daughters.
“Mab, you got your wish for someone to take care of instead of wounded conscripts,” she said softly. “A poor girl who was about to drown herself in despair. Mr. Deronda pulled her out of the river just in time, brought her here in his boat, and knew no one else but us would be gentle enough to receive her.
