She’s a Jewess, he says, refined—speaks Italian, plays music.” The three girls crowded close, their faces soft with sudden compassion, Mab looking half awe-struck, as if the wild wish she’d breathed out two minutes before had been answered by magic.

Outside, Deronda leaned down to the cab window, where a pale, dark-haired face was peeking out, bright with confused, new hope.
“I’ve brought you to some of the kindest people in the world,” he told her, holding out his hand. “They have daughters just about your age. This is a happy, safe home. Will you let me take you to meet them?”
She stepped out of the cab without hesitation, her small cold hand slipping into his, her hat slipping forgotten off the back of her head. When he led her into the full warm light of the parlor, where the four small Meyrick women waited for her, the sight of her would have stirred even the coldest heart. Her dark curls were still damp at the edges from the river, her fine pale gown spattered with dark water stains, her great dark eyes dazed from the shock of the bright parlor lamps. Deronda felt a quiet rush of relief that all the Meyricks were so small, none of them towering over her, so she would never feel overwhelmed.
“You must be so weary, poor child,” Mrs. Meyrick said softly, taking her other hand.
“We will take care of you! We will comfort you! We will love you!” Mab burst out, unable to hold herself back, wrapping both her hands around the girl’s small one.
That gentle warmth seeped into the strange girl’s bewildered, tight chest. She hung back a little, looking each of the four Meyricks in the face in turn, the tense, terrified line of her shoulders softening, no smile coming to her lips but a quiet, peaceful relief settling over her features. For a second she looked up at Deronda, as if all this unexpected kindness was a gift directly from him, then turned back to Mrs. Meyrick, her sweet soft voice steady for the first time since he’d met her on the riverbank.
“I am a stranger. I am a Jewess. You might have thought I was wicked.”
“No! We know you are good!” Mab burst out.
“We think no evil of you at all, poor little one,” Mrs. Meyrick soothed. “You are safe here now. Come sit down. We’ll get you a cup of hot tea, and then you can rest.”
The girl looked back up at Deronda. “I would not fear here. I could rest. You are all ministering angels, I think.”
“Tell us your name, dear,” Kate said, pulling a soft armchair forward for her.
Hafiz, who had been watching the scene restlessly, padded over to rub his fluffy side against her ankles.
“My name is Mirah Lapidoth,” she said, as Amy hurried out to bring the tea tray. “I traveled all the way from Prague alone. I escaped from terrible people who kept me trapped. I came to London to find my mother and my little brother—they took me from her when I was small, and I thought I could find her again. But all the old neighborhoods are gone. I lost all trace of them. I ran out of money, and I could not see any other way.”
“Our mama will look after you just like your own,” Mab said.
Deronda stirred at last, knowing it was time to leave. “Will you let me come back tomorrow, around five o’clock, to check on her?” he asked Mrs. Meyrick.
“Of course. We’ll all know each other a little better by then.”
He turned to Mirah, holding out his hand to say goodbye. She stood to take it, and the moment sent a sharp, thrumming jolt through both of them, bringing back the second she had first reached up to take his outstretched hand on the cold riverbank. She lifted her great dark eyes to his, her voice thick with reverence.
“The God of our fathers bless you, and deliver you from all evil, as you have delivered me. I never thought there was a man so good. No one before ever thought I was worthy of the best. You found me poor and miserable, yet you gave me kindness no one else ever dreamed of offering.”
Deronda could not find words to reply. He gave the Meyricks a quiet, wordless nod of farewell, and hurried out the door, his chest tight with a new, unnameable feeling that he would never be able to shake.

*“I pity the man who can travel from Dan to Beersheba, and say, ‘’Tis all barren’: and so it is: and so is all the world to him who will not cultivate the fruits it offers.”*
—STERNE, *Sentimental Journey*

To call Daniel Deronda a flighty, whimsical romantic would be to misread him entirely. Beneath his calm, carefully restrained exterior, however, burned a quiet, steady fervor that let him pluck threads of poetry and quiet magic out of the most unremarkable ordinary moments. Romance and wonder never faded from the world, he thought—they only vanished for the cold, sluggish souls who would write off any age as dull, who dismiss tenderness as a foolish, mistaken fancy. They sit easy beside a microscope on a laboratory table, they hum in the clatter of a railway carriage: what chases them away is the empty, unfeeling vacuum in the chest of people who pass through the world never stopping to feel awe, never reaching for the soft thrum of kinship that ties a person to the stranger down the street, to the stars above the farthest firmament, to the faint, warm memory of their mother’s arms holding them when they were small.

For Deronda, the afternoon he found Mirah lingering on the river’s edge was as heart-stirring, as full of high stakes, as any mythic quest Orestes or Rinaldo ever embarked on. He sat up half the night replaying every second of the encounter, the details growing sharper, not dimmer, each time he revisited them: the damp hem of her dress, the way her fingers twisted in the frayed edge of her shawl, the quiet catch in her voice when she spoke of the family she’d lost. When he picked up a book to try to quiet the loud, bright rush of memory crowding his head, the printed words blurred to no more than a thin, transparent net—he could still see and hear every moment of those two hours as clearly as if they were unfolding right in front of his eyes. He saw not just the events that had actually happened, but all the endless possibilities of what might have been, what still could be, fed warm and beating with the blood of his own passionate, tangled hope and fear.

Something deep in his own hidden experience made Mirah’s search for her mother and brother cling to his imagination with a peculiar, unshakable grip. His first, unthinking impulse was to help her search: if two lost people were somewhere in the sprawling, crowded expanse of London, there were ways to track them down as precise and meticulous as a careful scientific experiment, if you set the right tools and the right care to the work. But the tangled, conflicting feelings that came with his own unspoken secret soon seeped into every part of his anxiety on Mirah’s behalf.

The quiet, burning desire to find his own mother, or to learn any truth of her, was always tangled up in sharp, cold dread. And when he let himself imagine what Mirah might face when she found the family she’d been separated from as a small child, it struck him fast that that long-awaited reunion could easily turn out to be a catastrophe. She had told him in the boat that her mother and brother were good—but that goodness might only have existed in the soft, unworldly innocence of her childhood memory, in the aching yearning that had clung to them for a dozen years. And ten or twelve years apart was more than enough time for even the kindest people to warp and harden beyond recognition.

He had always been quick to side with the targets of unfair prejudice, to defend anyone the world had written off as worthless. But his sympathy had never before been drawn to real, living Jewish people in any personal, practical way, and the facts he had absorbed about them—whether they walked the streets in fine, conspicuous clothing or lurked in the shadowed alleyways off the main roads—were mostly the ugly, unkind sorts of details that filled him with distaste. He had long assumed that any educated, accomplished Jewish people he knew had shed their faith entirely, eager to erase every trace of their heritage to merge quietly into the population of the country they lived in. Any slur flung at a Jew simply for being Jewish would have roused all his fiery, defensive sympathy for the inherited griefs of that marginalized people—but blind, indiscriminate scorn for an entire race often lands on a person who has earned every bit of that disdain on his own, who deserves to be called out as a rascally son of Adam no matter what his background. Deronda could not avoid knowing the ugly, unfair anecdotes floating through London about supposed Jewish traits and occupations, but even as one of the loudest voices protesting the way people severed the past from the present to justify their cruelty, he had never bothered to dig deeper for nuance, only settling for the vague, lazy conclusion that the Jewish people carried the same mix of virtues and flaws as any long-oppressed group.

But now that Mirah’s longing forced his mind to turn over the details, very unpleasant images pressed unbidden to the front of his mind: what if they tracked down this middle-aged Jewish mother and her son, and they were nothing like the soft, loving people Mirah remembered? Of course, Mirah’s own exquisite gentleness and charm made a strong case that her immediate family might carry the same grace—but he could not be sure, not yet. He thought he might glean more hints of her history, slowly, through Mrs. Meyrick, from Mirah’s own lips. Her soft voice, her gentle accent, her whole presence wrapped in a sweet, untainted purity like a consecrated garment—all of it made him shrink back, both in his imagination and in reality, from ever tying her to anything hateful, anything that could sully that goodness in her.

His thoughts did not move in the fine, polished phrases we use to soften and cloud unpleasant facts. They raced forward in quick, vivid images of what might come: he saw himself led by some official guide down a dingy, grim side street, stepping through a dim, shadowed doorway to find a sharp, hawk-eyed woman with unkempt hair and dirty hands, haggling over a hungry young girl’s last scrap of fine, well-loved finery. Or in a slightly less squalid, more respectable neighborhood, he found himself breathing the stale, thick air of a room full of a brash, overly familiar young Jewish man, chattering away about his supposed familiarity with the tastes of wealthy gentlemen, willing to agree to any shady transaction they might propose.

It was no insult, no lazy stereotype on his part—Deronda was not prone to spontaneous unkindness, or to the cheap wit that tried to reduce all Jewish people to nothing more than penny adverts for pawn shops. He was simply governed by a sharp, unshakable dread in that moment, and if Mirah’s parents had been Christian, the only difference would have been that his forebodings would have been fed by a wider range of personal experience. It was a long-held habit of his mind to tie that sharp, cold dread to unknown parentage, and in this case, just as in his own unspoken secret, there was more than enough reason for that connection to feel justified.

But first, what was he to do with Mirah? She needed shelter, full and complete protection, and every chivalrous instinct in his chest rose up to insist that the sooner he could draw the care and interest of other trusted people around her, not just his own, the better he could honor the claim she had on his kindness. He had no right to provide for her entirely on his own, even if he had the means to do so; the very depth of the impression she had left on him made him desperate for her to know she was completely independent of him, no strings attached. Vague, half-formed visions of a future he tried to dismiss as silly and impossible still lingered, leaving him with a sharp, unnameable anxiety stronger than any logical reason he could name: he wanted everyone who saw his daily actions closely to know the full, true story of his connection to Mirah from the very first day. He had grown to hate secrets about the most precious ties and obligations in his life—hated them all the more because a tangled, heavy web of unspoken loyalties had long stopped him from breaking the one secret that hung over his own life. Deronda had sworn a private vow to himself that, since not every disgrace that befalls a mortal is one they have brought on their own head, he would never be the one to turn an innocent truth into a source of shame for another person. He was not free of the quiet terror that he might break that vow someday, falling prey to the lazy, apologetic philosophy that twists the world around until nothing seems better or more noble than one’s own small, selfish conduct.

For one long moment, he resolved to tell the whole story of his strange, fateful encounter to Sir Hugo and Lady Mallinger over their breakfast the next morning. But the thought that some new, unexpected detail might reveal itself when he next visited Mrs. Meyrick’s house held him back, and he finally drifted off to sleep on the quiet resolution to wait until he had made that visit, and heard more of Mirah’s own story first.

It would be folly to argue that even in this hard, unsteady world, we do not sometimes cross paths with people whose very bearing, the quiet rhythm of all their days, carries such an unmistakeable stamp of gentle goodness that we trust them not out of careful calculation, but on instinct, at a single glance. — Alexander Knox, quoted in Southey’s *Life of Wesley*

Mirah said she had slept deeper and sweeter than she could remember in years. When she stepped down to the parlor that morning, clad in Mab’s neatly mended black dress, her dark hair sprung in soft new curls as it finished drying from the long, warm bath, she looked like a flower that had bent under weeks of storm, finally lifting its petal to the sun. The blue half-circles of exhaustion under her pale cheeks were fading at last, the haggard tightness around her mouth gone.
It was Mab who led her down, carrying her tray of tea and bread, practically bouncing with quiet pride over the pair of tiny felt slippers she had run out before dawn to buy. No shoes in the Meyrick household were small enough for Mirah’s narrow feet, and the borrowed dress stopped just above her ankles, showing the cheap soft fabric that clung to her skin like the sheath of a new bud. The little farthing buckles on the slippers glinted like the finest jewelry to Mab’s eyes.
“Oh, Mamma, look!” she cried, clasping her hands and bending to gesture at Mirah’s feet the second they crossed the parlor threshold. “See how perfectly they fit! I swear she’s exactly Queen Budoor from the Arabian tales—‘two delicate feet, the work of the kind and all-rewarding Creator, bear her up, and I wonder how they ever carried the weight of all her sorrow.’”
Mirah looked down at her own feet with a soft, childlike curiosity, then smiled shyly at Mrs. Meyrick. The widow had been thinking to herself, You would never guess this creature could hold an unkind thought in her head. Any sensible person would warn me to be wary of strangers. But she smiled back warm and open. “I fear those little feet have had to bear far too heavy a burden lately. But today you will rest, and keep me company.”
“And she’ll tell you all sorts of stories and I’ll miss every word,” Mab grumbled playfully, already grabbing her bag for the day’s music pupils. She felt like she was halfway through the first volume of the most delightful romance ever written, and furious at being forced to skip chapters for work.
Kate had already left to sketch along the river, Amy was out running errands for the house. Mrs. Meyrick had made sure of that—she wanted to be alone with this quiet stranger, whose story she already knew must be steeped in grief, but which they both needed to say out loud.
That tiny front parlor felt like a sacred, soft little temple that morning. Sunlight spilled across the glinting river outside, and cool fresh air drifted through the open window. The walls, lined with cheap black-and-white prints, bloomed with a cloud of silent, beloved witnesses: the Virgin ascending flanked by cherubs; grand, solemn Melancholia holding her whole quiet universe in her hands; the Prophets and Sibyls, the School of Athens, the Last Supper, mystic groupings from centuries past that felt alive and present all at once. There were grave Holbein and Rembrandt portraits, the Tragic Muse, soft drawings of 18th century children lost in thought or at play, portraits of Italian poets. No clutter, no noise. Mrs. Meyrick, who had weathered so many troubles and still carried a cheerful, gentle face, sat sorting skeins of colored wool for her embroidery. Their old tabby cat Hafiz purred on the window ledge, the mantel clock ticked slow and unhurried, and the faint rumble of passing carriages felt like it was miles outside the thick, soft hush wrapped around the room.
Mrs. Meyrick decided not to break the quiet first—she thought it would make Mirah feel safer to speak in her own time. Mirah sat across from her, hands folded in her lap, ankles crossed, her eyes drifting slowly over all the art on the walls before finally settling on Mrs. Meyrick with a quiet, reverent softness. At last she spoke, her voice low and soft as the breeze through the window.
“I can remember my mother’s face clearer than anything else in the world, even though I wasn’t seven when I was taken away. I’m nineteen now.”
“That makes perfect sense to me,” Mrs. Meyrick said. “The very first things you learn to love stick with you longer than anything else.”
“Oh, yes. My whole life feels like it started the day I woke up and loved my mother’s face first. She was so close, her arms wrapped all around me, singing. One hymn she sang over and over, until I learned to sing it with her. They were all Hebrew hymns, and I never knew what the words meant—so they never held anything but our love, all our happiness. When I lay in my little white bed, she would lean over me, her face between me and the pale ceiling, and sing in that sweet low voice. I can step right back into that moment even when I’m awake, and it comes to me in dreams all the time. My hand is tiny, I lift it to her face, and she kisses it. Sometimes I wake up trembling, thinking we’re both dead, and my hand lies limp on the sheets, and for a second I don’t even recognize myself. But if I saw her again, I would know her face instantly.”
“Twelve years will change a person, dear,” Mrs. Meyrick said gently. “Look at my grey hair—ten years ago it was bright chestnut. The days and months fly over us like small restless birds, their little feet leaving marks all over us. And when the days are heavy with grief, they tread much harder.”
“I know her heart must have been so heavy, missing me. But to think of the joy on her face if we found each other again! To make her know how much I love her, to give her that comfort after all the years of mourning. If that could happen, I would never mind any of the trouble I lived through. I despaired, you know. The whole world felt cruel and wicked, no one would help me, I couldn’t bear the way strangers looked at me, the things they said. I was sure my mother was dead, that the only way to get to her was to drown. But yesterday, right when I was longing for the river to close over me, when I thought death was the only mercy left, living kindness found me. And it’s strange, but that second I started hoping she was still alive too. Now I’m here, with you, this morning—peace and hope have flooded into me like something I never dared dream of. I want for nothing. I can wait. I hope, and I believe, and I am so grateful—so grateful! You never thought ill of me. You never looked at me like I was something to despise.”
Mirah spoke with low, burning fervor, sitting perfectly still as if she was a painted saint on a chapel wall.
“Plenty of other people would have felt exactly the way we do, my dear,” Mrs. Meyrick said, feeling a warm mist blur her eyes over her half-sorted wools.
“But I never met any of them. They never found me.”
“How did you end up taken away from your mother, then?”
“Ah, that’s the hard part. Dreadful to say out loud, but I must tell you everything. It was my father who took me. I thought we were just going on a little day trip, I was excited. I had a little box packed with all my toys and ribbons. But we kept walking, got on a ship, and the land drifted further and further away. I got so seasick I thought the pain would never end. That was the first bit of misery, and it felt endless. When I finally got my feet on land again, my father comforted me, said we would go home to my mother soon. We were in America, and it was years before we came back to Europe.
“At first I asked every day when we could go home. I tried to learn to write as fast as I could, so I could send her a letter. But one day my father caught me scribbling to her, pulled me onto his knee, and told me my mother and my little brother were dead. That was why we could never go back home. I barely remembered my brother, just that he used to hoist me up on his shoulders once, but he was out working most days. I believed him. I pictured them buried under the earth, their eyes closed forever. I never thought to doubt him, and I cried every night for months. But then my mother started appearing to me in my dreams so often, I thought she must still be alive somewhere close by, even if I couldn’t see her. That comforted me so much I was never afraid of the dark. I’d shut my eyes in the middle of the day, bury my face, and try to see her, hear her singing. After a while I could do it without even closing my eyes.”
Mirah paused, a soft, sweet smile spreading over her face, as if she was staring right at that happy memory, looking out at the glinting river through the window.
“I hope your father wasn’t cruel to you?” Mrs. Meyrick asked, after a quiet minute, gently drawing her back to the present.
“He petted me, spent a lot of time teaching me things. He was an actor, you know—later I figured out the ‘Coburg’ theatre he kept talking about back home was where he used to work. But he did more than act. He’d been a teacher once, spoke half a dozen languages. His acting wasn’t very good, but he managed stage productions, wrote and translated plays. There was an Italian opera singer, Signora, who lived with us for years. They both taught me to sing, I had another master too who made me recite whole parts from memory. I worked so hard even as a tiny girl, but I wasn’t nine when I first stepped onto the stage. I learned lines easily, I was never shy in front of an audience, but I hated every second of that life.
“My father had money back then, we had all sorts of flashy finery scattered around the house in a mess, strangers coming and going at all hours—loud laughter, shouting arguments, people strutting and jeering, faces I didn’t want to look at, even if most of them fussed over me and stroked my hair. But I still held my mother close in my head. Even when I was too little to understand much, I shrank away from all that noise, and lived in my own private world of thoughts I’d picked out as beautiful. I read every book I could get my hands on—plays, poetry, Shakespeare and Schiller—and I learned to tell good from bad very fast. My father decided my voice was going to make us rich, that I’d be a world-famous star. He hired the best teachers money could buy, but it made my skin crawl the way he’d brag about me, make me sing for random guests at a moment’s notice like I was nothing more than a fancy music box.
“When I was nine, I played a little lost girl in a play who didn’t know she’d been abandoned, singing to herself while she picked flowers on the stage. I did it perfectly without even trying, but all the clapping and the roar of the audience made me sick. I never liked any praise I got, because none of it felt soft, none of it had the love I was born back home with. I built my own little world in my head, separate from all the chaos around me, picking out every beautiful line of poetry and every kind character from the plays I read to fill it. It was like a sharp knife nicking at me all the time, the way the two lives never fit: actresses on the stage playing soft, gentle women, speaking kind lines like they meant them, and the second the curtain fell they were yelling crude insults at each other behind the scenes. My father noticed I always shrank away from everyone, and Signora said once after a rehearsal, ‘She’ll never make a proper artist. She only knows how to be herself. That’s cute now, but soon she’ll be as empty of performance as a little singing bird.’ They fought over it, and I sat alone crying, because what she said felt like she was unrolling a long, miserable future right in front of my eyes. I never wanted to be an artist, but that was all my father had ever planned for me.
“After Signora left, a governess came to give me lessons, because my father was scared I’d strain my voice too much. I still acted sometimes, but the more I grew, the more I hated that life, the more I ached to run away. But I had nowhere to go, I was terrified of the world. And I thought it would be wrong to leave my father. I was so scared I’d turn wicked, that I’d end up hating myself the same way I hated all the cruel, loud strangers around me. I’d spent so long never feeling safe in the real world, I knew if I turned wicked I’d lose the little happy world I’d made where my mother still lived with me. That was the childish thought that got me through all those years. Oh, they felt so long.”
Mirah fell quiet, lost in thought.
“Did no one ever teach you what to believe, about right and wrong?” Mrs. Meyrick asked, careful not to say the word religion, not sure what Mirah’s Hebrew upbringing might have been.
“Only that I had to do whatever my father wanted. He didn’t practice our faith in New York, he never wanted me to learn much about it. But I could still remember my mother taking me to the synagogue, sitting on her lap, looking through the railing at the men chanting and singing. I ached to go. One day I snuck out when I was small, trying to find the nearest synagogue, and got completely lost for hours until a peddler found me and walked me home. My father had been frantic with worry, and he was so angry at me I didn’t dare sneak out again for years. But after Signora left, our landlady was a Jewish woman who kept all the traditions. She let me come to synagogue with her, I read her prayer books and her Bible, and when I got a little bit of money of my own I bought my own copies. Those books felt like they brought me closer to my mother—I knew she had read those exact same words, said those exact same prayers. That’s how I learned a little of our faith, and the history of our people, on top of all the bits and pieces I picked up from plays and other books about Jews. I was sure my mother followed all the traditions faithfully.
“I stopped asking my father about her, after a while. It’s dreadful to say, but I started to not believe him. I’d caught him lying so many times, promising things he never meant to do, and I started to suspect my mother and brother were still alive. When I thought back to that day he tricked us onto the ship, I realized my mother had been completely deceived, expecting us to come back after a short little trip. The day he sat me on his knee and told me they were dead? It was just a performance, to make me stop asking questions. That lie was so cruel, it sank right into my bones, and I started hating every untruth because of it. I wrote her a secret letter, addressed to our old street in Colman Street, I remembered our original last name was Cohen, before my father changed it to Lapidoth, saying it was the name of his Polish ancestors. I sent the letter hidden, no one found out—but no answer ever came. I thought all hope was gone.
“We didn’t stay in America much longer. My father packed all our things one day and told me we were sailing for Hamburg. I was glad, I hoped we’d meet kinder people there. I spoke German almost fluently by then, I knew whole plays by heart. I was thirteen, and I felt so old—I knew so much, but nothing at all. I used to wish I’d drowned on that first ship ride away from my mother. But I told myself to be good, to be patient. What else could I do?
“One day on the voyage, a new terrible thought came to me. I wasn’t seasick that trip, I spent most days up on deck. My father sang and joked and did little acts to amuse all the other passengers, and I heard people whispering about him. Once I was staring out at the sea, no one was paying any attention to me, and I heard a gentleman say, ‘Oh, he’s one of those clever Jews—probably a rascal. There’s no race like them for cunning in the men and beauty in the women. Wonder what market he’s planning to sell that daughter of his to.’
“That thought pierced me right through. I realized all the unhappiness in my life was because I was a Jewess, that for the rest of my days the whole world would look down on me, judge me for my name before they even knew me. But then it comforted me, to think my pain was part of the long, shared affliction of my people, my little verse added to the centuries-old song of mourning they’ve sung through every generation. Even if some of my people were wicked and laughed at their own sins, that pain was still the burden the good ones had to carry, despised for the wrongs of people they’d never even met. But you—you never rejected me.”
Mirah’s tone softened, suddenly remembering she was not speaking of grief now, but of the kindness that had saved her.
“We will make sure no one judges you unjustly ever again, my poor child,” Mrs. Meyrick said, putting her half-sorted wools down completely, folding her hands in her lap, her face as earnest as Mab’s ever was when she dove into a new romance novel. “Go on, dear. Tell me everything.”
“After that we lived in a string of different cities—Hamburg, then Vienna for the longest stretch. I started taking singing lessons again, my father always found work around the theatres. I think he’d brought a big sum of money over from America, I never knew exactly why we left New York. For a long time he was so excited about my singing, making me rehearse parts over and over, he was counting down to my grand opera debut. But as time went on, it turned out my voice never got strong enough. It never lived up to the promise it had when I was little. My singing master in Vienna told him, ‘Don’t make her strain it any further. It’s pure gold, but it’s only a thread of gold dust. It will never fill an opera house.’
“My father was bitterly disappointed. We got much

We all repeat the old adage that Knowledge is power—but who ever stops to reckon with the far greater, far crueler power of Ignorance? Knowledge spends patient, frugal centuries building up, documenting, refining, making life bright and layered with every small, hard-won discovery. Ignorance? It will burn every last page of that recorded progress in an hour, just to roast its single dinner, to flavor its one small pleasure with the toil and lost dreams of a hundred generations. Knowledge is reined in by scruple, by a quiet sense of what ought to be. Ignorance is a blind, roving giant: unshackle it, and it will gleefully rip out the pillars holding up all the hard-won good of human life, smother every joy under darkness as thick as the dust of buried Babylon. That same ruinous force works on a single life, too. How many of us, blind to the slow, fragile chain of cause and effect, chase a foolish fantasy a single reckless step too far, and send our whole carefully curated world crashing down?

It was half past ten on a crisp, blustery autumn morning when Gwendolen Harleth stepped off the train fresh from her gloomy, sleepless journey from Leubronn, at the sort of country station that never seemed built to be near anywhere at all, just equally far from every neighboring village. No carriage was waiting for her: she’d told her mother in her Dover telegram to expect her on a later train, and her own impatience to escape the chaos of London’s terminals had made her rush off without picturing how miserable it would be to arrive unannounced, alone, with all her trunks, thirty minutes’ drive from the home she’d always ruled over like a little queen.

The waiting room was a grubby, paint-peeling hovel, the water in the glass decanter stale and flat, the walls plastered with loud, screaming religious texts that felt like a personal taunt. Every detail felt like a preview of the life of humiliation that had just opened up before her. She fled out to the platform, but even the sunbeams over the fields felt thin and sad, the wind tangling the autumnal leaves, flaring the feathers of a scruffy old rooster and two croaking hens pecking aimlessly at the grass, left behind by their grown chicks with nothing to do all day. The station master was a new man, didn’t know her, and stared at her with his cross-eyed, innocent curiosity like he was wondering what sort of unimportant stray she was. The dirty, dented old barouche hired from the nearby railway inn trundled up at last, and as the old farm laborer strapped her overstuffed trunks to the seats, Gwendolen felt the last of her old bright, defiant spirit fraying at the edges. She was used to being admired, waited on, never having to beg anyone for anything—and now here she was, dumped at a hick station, broke, with no one to run to. What was the point of being charming, clever, sharp, if all the world had to offer her was ugliness, petty indignity, and no money? This was only the start of coming home.

She stewed all through the slow, jolting drive down the rutted lanes, one great trunk squishing the meek old driver next to him. She’d clung to the hope all the way from the continent that the family would just move abroad, settle in some quiet cheap coastal town, avoid all the pitying stares of their old neighbors. She’d seen plenty of poor English expats scraping by in France and Italy, and she’d dreaded the thought of wasting away there, watching her little half-sisters Alice, Bertha, Fanny and Isabel grow up dull and tedious, while she crept closer and closer to thirty, no prospects, nothing to make her stand out. But even that miserable future was better than the one she was driving back to, she soon realized. She’d lost almost two hundred pounds at the roulette table in Leubronn that last night—if only her mother had told her the bad news earlier, that money could have kept them afloat for months. And the man who’d stepped between her and the big win she’d chased? The stranger Deronda? She found herself seething at Grandcourt, too, the wealthy suitor who’d been almost hers, whose dark secret had stopped her from marrying into the fortune she’d thought was hers, all of them robbing her of the life she was owed.

The barouche rumbled up the long avenue to Offendene, and she spotted her mother waiting on the portico, that beautiful soft face lined with new grief, and all her petty anger dissolved for a second. She sprang out of the carriage and threw her arms around her mother’s neck, and for a minute she only felt sorrow for the woman she loved, not for herself. The four little girls crowded behind, their faces bright with relief—wherever Gwendolen was, something interesting always happened. Even poor meek Miss Merry the governess scurried around, paying the driver and seeing to the trunks, not expecting a greeting. Gwendolen dragged her mother up to their shared favorite bedroom, the black and yellow one with the soft velvet curtains, and kissed the tears off her cheeks. “Never mind, mamma,” she whispered. “I’m here now. I’ll do something. I’ll make things right.” For a few bright minutes, she meant it completely, brimming over with generous, fierce resolve.

But the hope ebbed as the day wore on, when the full weight of their ruin settled over her. Her mother broke the news slow, gentle as she could: they had to move out of Offendene at once, there was no money to pay for the servants or the fuel to heat the big drafty house. They couldn’t afford to travel abroad at all. The only cheap cottage they could find, the only place they could possibly live? Sawyer’s Cottage. The tiny two-parlor, four-bedroom hovel that had once been home to the village exciseman.

Gwendolen went white with rage. “That’s impossible. Uncle mustn’t let that happen. I won’t submit to that.” Her mother tried to explain: every last penny of their money was gone, lost in a reckless mine speculation, they had nothing at all except the small charity her uncle and aunt were willing to give them. The only prospects anyone had thought up for her? Governess jobs, one in a bishop’s household with three awkward daughters, another at a fancy girls’ school, paying a paltry hundred pounds a year.

“Be a governess?” Gwendolen laughed, sharp and bitter. “Shut up tutoring three girls as dull as Alice, getting looked down on by every rich family that thinks they’re doing me a favor? I’d rather emigrate than do that.”

She was already reaching for the desk by the window, her mind flying back to the brilliant, sharp-witted musician Herr Klesmer, who was staying with the wealthy Arrowpoint family at their house in Quetcham. She knew he’d seen her sing once, had told her she had a rare, wonderful gift for performance. Maybe that gift could be her ticket, not just out of Sawyer’s Cottage, but to fame, to a life worthy of her. She scribbled a quick, urgent note: begging him to call on her the very next day, to give her advice on a matter of life or death, that her family’s ruin had forced her to make her way in the world using her talent, and she trusted no one but his great judgment to guide her.

She sent the note off with a farm boy from nearby Leek’s, told him to wait for a reply. As she paced the big quiet drawing room, her old confidence coming back, she told herself that if anyone could help her make a name for herself, escape the petty drudgery everyone else was pushing her toward, it was Klesmer. For the first time since the ruin broke, she dared to hope that she was still that remarkable girl, that her charm and her wit and her talent would not let her be dragged down into the small, miserable life everyone else was planning for her.

---
We spin daydreams of bright, untested new paths for our lives, all the while not noticing that the loom of reality is already hard at work: stoking passion passes its shuttle back and forth, weaving the pattern of what we will do before we have even decided to do it.

Gwendolen’s note arrived for Klesmer perfectly timed, neither too early nor too late, pressed right into his hand the exact moment he was stepping out of Quetcham Hall’s grand front doors. To honor her urgent appeal, he rearranged his whole schedule at no small inconvenience to himself, stopping for the night at a small inn in Wanchester rather than heading straight for his next booking. There was no question of him staying another hour at Quetcham.

The glittering, overpriced mansion, fitted out to the highest possible standard of wealth, had grown far too hot for him. Its owners, like bumbling politicians stunned by an unforeseen uprising, had just been blindsided by a rebellion they never saw brewing under their very noses.

As usual, the house was full of weekend guests, and among them was a new, highly approved suitor for the Arrowpoints’ only daughter and heiress Catherine: a rising politician of noble stock who was all but guaranteed a peerage, and had openly declared he needed a large fortune to properly support the title when it came. Heiresses, the ton agreed, were simple: they were just an accessory to their money, bound to marry whoever society said would make the best use of their fortune. But nature, as it often did, had refused to follow the script. Catherine Arrowpoint had a sharp, independent mind and a will of iron, and she had already spent months turning down every suitable, fortune-hunting suitor her parents paraded in front of her. The Arrowpoints had never once considered the danger of her falling in love with Klesmer, their world-famous live-in music tutor — the kind of blind spot almost every parent suffers. We are all always shocked when the outcome we never bothered to stop comes to pass, and bewildered when the outcome we begged for never arrives. The Arrowpoints’ hour of shock was right on its way.

For a proud, genius musician and a stubborn, kind-hearted heiress, it is far too easy to fall into deep, quiet love before either dares name it. For a whole year, Klesmer had come to Quetcham for lessons, and the pair had spent hour after hour side by side at the piano, debating art and poetry and politics, sharing a perfect, easy sympathy that neither had ever found in another soul. The Arrowpoints had thought paying Klesmer a handsome, generous check would make him as harmless as a footman — how could a man so far below their daughter’s station ever dare fall in love with her? They had forgotten that the most powerful beauty of all is not the flashy, obvious sort of a perfect face or figure. It is the beauty that grows on you: the glint of understanding in Klesmer’s eyes when Catherine made a half-formed musical observation, the quiet warmth of Catherine’s smile when Klesmer ranted about a new piece of music that had moved him to tears. It is the magic of two people who see every part of each other, and like what they see. Klesmer was brilliant, versatile, the kind of man whose mind ran as fast and wild as his compositions, and Catherine was steady, sharp, full of quiet depths that made every conversation feel like a new discovery. For months, they had both been happy just to steal that time together, too proud and too afraid to name their feelings, certain the gap between their stations could never be crossed. Klesmer had only just begun to make plans to stop coming to Quetcham forever, to spare himself the pain of watching her marry a man who would never see a fraction of what made her special, when Mr. Bult — the future peer, the boring, solid man who cared far more about Niger trade routes than counterpoint or poetry — arrived as a guest.

The breaking point came after dinner, when Klesmer, fired up by Bult’s flippant comment that art was no more than a frivolous pastime for rich ladies, let loose a fiery, passionate rant about how great artists shape nations and influence the world just as much as any politician. Later that evening, Bult came over to the piano where Klesmer and Catherine were sitting, and blithely commented that he had no idea the "mere musician" was such a clever, political man. Klesmer bit back his rage at the insult, but he was seething.

The next day in the music room, Catherine pulled him aside to chide him for his anger, telling him there was no need to fight with a man who meant no harm. The tension boiled over. Klesmer snapped that he would not tolerate anyone calling his life’s work, the art he worshipped, "mere" — and then, before he could stop himself, he confessed he was leaving, for good, to take a long-term post in St. Petersburg. He could not bear to stay another day, torturing himself in a house where he would have to watch her marry a man as thick and unfeeling as a stone. He sat down at the piano and played a soft, distant, echo-like rendition of an old Heine melody: *I have loved you, and I love you still*.

He told her he had no right to make any demand on her — he knew how impossible a life together would be, how cruel the gossips of the ton would be. But he wanted her to know, at least, that he had stayed for an entire year not for the pay, not for the prestige, but for her. She was the most extraordinary woman he had ever known, the one he carried like a knight’s standard, tucked between his heart and his armor. He would not ask her to sacrifice a life of ease for him. He would just pack his bags and leave.

But Catherine stopped him before he could reach the door. Her hands were shaking so hard the stack of sheet music she was holding nearly slipped out of her grip. Why, she asked him, should she marry a man who cared nothing for who she was, only for her fortune, when the man who loved her, who saw every part of her, was standing right in front of her? She would not let social custom rob her of the only happiness she would ever have. She would marry him, no matter what anyone said.

The choice was made. Catherine marched straight to the library where her parents were sitting, and told them plainly that she had promised herself to Klesmer.

The Arrowpoints’ shock was indescribable. Mrs. Arrowpoint, who had once written grand literary essays about the tragic romance of Tasso and his noble patron, could not fathom that her own daughter would turn that fiction into real life, right in her own drawing room. She raged that Klesmer was nobody — a wandering Jewish musician, no family, no name, a man paid to be in their house, that the whole ton would laugh at them, that Catherine was throwing every last bit of her social standing away for nothing. Her father, puffing on his cigar, gruffly agreed that Klesmer was not a "proper gentleman" fit to run their great estates, that he was too foreign, too impractical.

But Catherine stood firm. She reminded them that their own family fortune had been built by trade, not noble blood, that the silly arbitrary social rules they were clinging to were nothing compared to the chance to spend the rest of her life with the man she loved, who loved her for herself, not for her money. She would not give him up.

Of all the gifts Art offers her devotees, none are handed freely on a silver platter. Every heir to that promised land must fight tooth and nail to claim their patch of it; the grand prophecies of success we whisper to ourselves in the dark are no legal deed, no firm title to possession. Tucking a map of an unowned, unvisited estate into your coat pocket is no proof you will ever walk a single acre of it, no matter how vividly you daydream of casting your shoe over Eden’s gates to mark the land as yours.

The sturdiest, most unshakable beliefs we hold about ourselves are rarely rooted in fact, anyway. They spring from that constant, warm, spontaneous thrum of self-satisfaction beating in our chests — a tiny, hidden seed of wild, unproven madness, the quiet confidence that we can move the entire world without ever bothering to find a solid foothold or a proper lever.

“Go on to church this morning, mamma, please,” Gwendolen said over breakfast the next day. “I’d prefer to see Herr Klesmer alone.” He’d written back to her note the evening before to say he would call at eleven sharp.

“I hardly think that’s quite proper, dear,” Mrs. Davilow fretted, her brows drawn tight with anxiety.

“Our affairs are far too serious to waste time on silly little social rules,” Gwendolen said, dismissive, her tone sharp with contempt. “Those rules aren’t just ridiculous — they’re insulting, frankly.”

“Would you mind if Isabel sat with you? She’d just tuck herself in the corner with a book, she wouldn’t interrupt.”

“No, she won’t,” Gwendolen said, shaking her head. “She’ll pick at her nails and stare the whole time. It will drive me mad. Trust my judgment, mamma. I have to be alone. Take all the rest of them to church with you.”

Gwendolen, of course, got her way. Only Miss Merry and two of the younger girls stayed home, lingering by the dining room windows to make the house look lived-in rather than completely empty of guests.

It was a perfect, soft autumn Sunday. The pale, fading golden sun of late autumn spilled over the half-strewn grass outside, slanting gently through the windows in broad bands of light that gilded every old piece of furniture, the mirrored panel that reflected it all back, the tapestry-draped chairs strung with faded silk flower garlands, the dark, mysterious old oil paintings on the walls, the rickety old organ where Gwendolen had loved to pretend she was Saint Cecilia on the first bright, happy day they’d moved into the house. She could even see, through the open door to the antechamber, the shelves of pale, dust-furred knickknacks that had stood witness to the day she’d dressed up in her flowing Greek costume to play Hermione, posing for their amateur charades. That memory was looping through her head on repeat — hadn’t Klesmer been there that night, hadn’t he stopped short, struck breathless by the grace of her pose and the fire in her expression? Every word he’d said then, every little look she’d imagined him giving her, hummed with sharp, burning interest right now. For the first time in her life, she felt no unshakable, easy confidence. She was inwardly trembling, consciously, deliberately waiting for the verdict of another person. A new flutter of uncertainty had stirred in her chest, a new, careful self-doubt that had never touched her before, back when her self-esteem had been nothing but a blissful, unchallenged gift of intuition. Even so, she kept whispering to herself that Klesmer had only ever seen snippets of her life, only glimpsed tiny fractions of what she could do. Any unfair judgment he made would be based on far too little evidence. She really, truly felt clever enough to take on anything.

To pass the time before he arrived, she dragged out all her sheet music and her dog-eared volumes of plays, stacked them on top of the piano, and set about sorting through them. When she caught sight of her own reflection moving in the mirrored panel, she drifted over to stare at it. Dressed all in black, not a single jewel or ribbon to adorn her, the warm pale glow of her skin framed perfectly between her crown of light brown hair and the square cut of her bodice, she looked like a statue carved from blocks of black, white, and tawny marble, the kind old Roman artisans used to make for their temples. Watching her own image drift toward her, she thought, quiet and sure, I am beautiful. Not exultant, just grave and certain. Her beauty was the one thing she couldn’t prove through hard work, the one thing she needed the world to confirm for her. If anyone dared criticize the curve of her nose, or the line of her neck and jaw, she knew she could not magically practice those things into perfection.

There was no time to linger over her reflection much longer. The crunch of carriage wheels on the gravel drive, the loud, sharp ring of the doorbell, the sound of the footman drawing open the front door — all told her she would not be disappointed. A little flutter of panic leaped up in her chest. For all her usual swagger and self-assurance, she was still a little afraid of Klesmer. He was part of that unmanageable world that did not bend to her whims, something sharp as vitriolic acid that would burn just as hot no matter if she smiled at it or stamped her foot in rage. Poor girl! This moment was a higher turning point in her fate than the day she’d first been proposed to by Grandcourt. Back then, the only question she’d had to answer was whether to take a specific man as her husband. Now, the secret, thrumming heart of her question was whether she had to take any husband at all — whether she could carve out a life for herself on her own terms, sate her hungry ambition without ever bending to the bondage of marriage for security.

Klesmer bowed low, very deliberately, in the wide antechamber doorway, every inch the respected master: his fine grey kerseymere trousers pressed to perfection, his kid gloves immaculate, proof that even the greatest of the world’s artists are still very human about their presentation. Gwendolen stepped forward to meet him, her face unusually grave, and held out her hand. “It is so kind of you to come, Herr Klesmer. I hope you didn’t think me horribly presumptuous to send for you.”

“I took your request as a command that did me great honor,” Klesmer said, his tone just as grave as hers. He had rearranged his entire schedule for the day to give her his full, undivided attention, but his temper was still strung tight from the events of the day before, making every word he spoke sharper, more cutting than he might have intended.

Gwendolen was so strung tight with nerves that she forgot all the usual little formalities. She stayed standing near the piano, and Klesmer positioned himself at the other end, his back to the window light, his sharp, omniscient eyes fixed unblinkingly on her. There was no point in putting on airs, no room for her usual coquetry. She spoke at once, no preamble.

“I need your advice, Herr Klesmer. We’ve lost all our money. We have nothing left. I have to earn my own bread, and I want to make enough to support my mother, so she never has to want for anything or face any hardship. The only path I can think of — the only one I’d ever want to take — is to be an actress, to go on the stage. But of course I want to rise to the top, make a name for myself, and I thought… if you thought I had it in me… it would be even better if I studied singing too, so I could do both.”

Klesmer set his hat down carefully on the piano top, folded his arms across his chest, and settled into a still, focused stance.

“I know my singing method is very bad,” Gwendolen rushed on, flushing bright pink then draining pale in the space of a breath. “I had terrible teachers as a girl. I could learn properly, I could study hard. You understand what I want, don’t you? To sing and act both, like Grisi, that’s the highest possible rank. Naturally I want to climb as high as I possibly can. I trust your judgment completely. I know you’ll tell me the absolute truth.”

Somehow, she was fully convinced that now she’d laid this desperate, serious appeal at his feet, the truth would be kind. It would be on her side.

Still Klesmer said nothing. He pulled off his gloves quickly, tossed them into his hat, rested his hands on his hips, and paced to the far end of the room. His heart ached for this girl, and he was trying to choose his words carefully, to be gentle. When he turned back to her, there was a soft, troubled frown on his face, and he spoke quickly, but gently. “You’ve never spent any time around working artists, have you? Not musicians, not actors, not anyone who makes their living that way?”

“Oh no, of course not,” Gwendolen said, unruffled. It was obvious she’d grown up a wealthy, sheltered young lady, after all.

“Forgive me for asking,” Klesmer said, pausing again near the piano. “When you make a decision this big, every little detail counts. You’re twenty, I’d guess?”

“I’m twenty-one,” Gwendolen said, a flicker of fear pricking at her chest. “Do you think I’m too old?”

Klesmer pursed his lower lip, shook his long, thin fingers upward in a gesture that was completely unreadable.

“Plenty of people start later than that, you know,” Gwendolen jumped in, falling back on her usual habit of being sure she had the facts right.

Klesmer ignored the comment, speaking even more softly than before. “You never thought about taking this path before all this trouble hit you, did you? You never longed to be an actress, never daydreamed about it, until the day your fortune was gone.”

“Not exactly — but I’ve always loved acting. I’ve done it, you saw me, don’t you remember? You saw us do those charades right here, you saw me play Hermione,” Gwendolen said, suddenly terrified he’d forgotten that night entirely.

“Yes, yes,” he said quickly. “I remember. I remember every bit of it.” He paced back across the room again, a habit he couldn’t break when he was working through an argument out loud or inside his own head.

Gwendolen could feel him weighing her in the balance, judging her. The wait was agonizing. But she still couldn’t fathom that the scale might tip the wrong way against her. “Thank you so much for taking the trouble to advise me, whatever you end up saying,” she added, as gracefully as she could.

“Miss Harleth,” Klesmer said, turning to face her, his accent thickening a little with the gravity of what he had to say next. “I won’t lie to you, not an inch. I would count myself a wicked man if I painted this life brighter than it is, or darker than it is. The gods curse any man who intentionally sends another person down the wrong road. If I misled a girl as young, as beautiful as you — who deserves to find happiness on the right path — I would be no better than a villain.” The last word came out as a low, sharp whisper.

Gwendolen’s heart sank like a stone in her chest under that unexpected solemnity. She stared at him, transfixed, as he went on.

“You are a beautiful young lady. You’ve grown up in comfort, you’ve done exactly what you wanted your whole life. You’ve never once had to say to yourself, ‘I must know this perfectly,’ ‘I must understand this exactly,’ ‘I must do this flawlessly.’” As he spoke those three terrible, unyielding musts, he held up three long fingers one after another. “All your life, you’ve never had to be anything other than a charming young lady, the sort of girl it would be rude to find fault with.”

He paused for a beat, then set his hands back on his hips, thrusting out his strong, square jaw.

“Well then. With that preparation, you want to try the life of an artist. A life of grueling, unceasing toil, of praise that never comes easy, that has to be earned the same way your bread does — slow, scant, and often never coming at all.”

That discouraging tone, which Klesmer had hoped would be gentle enough to spare her feelings, sparked a hot little flare of defiance in Gwendolen. She turned her head away, cheeks pinking with pique. “I thought as an artist yourself, you’d think the life was one of the most honorable, delightful paths a person could take. And what if I can’t do anything better than that? I’m sure I can brave the same risks every other working person braves.”

“Do nothing better?” Klesmer said, his voice rising a little with fire. “No, my dear Miss Harleth, you could do nothing better — no man or woman on earth could do anything better — if you could produce work of the highest, truest quality. I’m not belittling the life of a real artist. I’m exalting it. It’s a life only open to those with the rarest constitutions: natures made to love perfection more than ease, to labor for it, ready like all true lovers to endure, to wait, to say ‘I am not yet worthy, but my mistress Art is worthy, and I will live to earn her.’ It is an honorable life, yes. But that honor comes from the secret, burning vocation in your bones and the hard-won achievements you bleed for. There is no honor in putting on that life like it’s a fancy livery coat, just because it looks grand.”

Some leftover fire from the day before had seized Klesmer, pulling him away from his original gentle purpose. He’d only wanted to delicately make her see how unfit she was for this perilous, brutal path, but he had never been able to stand the pretensions of people who thought they could skip all the work and claim the rewards of art anyway. He caught himself, paused abruptly, but Gwendolen only heard how deeply he revered art — it was a glamour he threw over everything he loved, she thought. All she cared to make clear was that she wasn’t afraid of a little early hardship. The unshakable belief that if she stepped on a public stage, the same charm that had always won her admiration in private would work its magic, was so much a part of her, she could no more rip it out of her chest than she could tear out her own flesh. “I’m fully prepared to bear hardships at first,” she said, insistent. “Of course no one becomes famous overnight. And not every actress or singer has to be a first-rate genius. If you’d only tell me what steps to take, I’ll take them. I’m not afraid of climbing uphill. It’s so much better than the dead, dull life of a governess. I’ll do anything you recommend.”

Klesmer knew then he had no choice but to speak plainly.

“I will tell you the steps that will not be recommended by anyone, but will be forced on you if you insist on going through with this. No matter what your goal is — excellence, fame, second-rate work, third-rate work — it’s all the same. You’ll have to go to London under the care of your mother. You’ll have to put yourself through full training: musical, dramatic, theatrical. Every single thing you want to do, you will have to learn from scratch.” Gwendolen opened her mouth to protest, but Klesmer lifted a hand, stopping her. “I know. You’ve ‘practiced’ your talents — you recite, you sing, in drawing rooms, for your friends. My dear young lady, you have to unlearn all of that. You don’t even understand what real excellence is yet. You have to unlearn all your silly, mistaken little ideas of what good performance is. You have to learn exactly what you are striving for, then bend every part of your mind and your body to unbroken, unforgiving discipline. Your mind first, mind you. You cannot think about fame. Blow that candle out of your eyes, and only look at the work, at perfection. You will not earn a penny, not get a single professional engagement, for years. You will need money to support yourself and your whole family. But that,” Klesmer frowned, waving his fingers as if swatting that trivial difficulty away, “that money could probably be found, if it came to it.”

Gwendolen flushed bright pink then went pale as death through that whole speech. Her pride had been sliced open by a sharp knife, and that last line about finding money made the sting burn even worse. She could feel how upset she looked, so she hurried to sit down on a nearby chair, then gestured for Klesmer to take the one across from her. He didn’t sit. He turned a little, leaning back against the piano to face her. At that exact moment, she wished she’d never sent for him. This was the first time in her whole life anyone had ever treated her as something other than a pretty, privileged young lady to be cosseted and flattered, and it tasted bitter on her tongue. Klesmer, focused entirely on the seriousness of what he had to tell her, kept speaking in the same steady tone.

“Now. What reasonable outcome could you possibly expect after all that years of self-denial? You will want to know. It is only fair you know the full truth. The outcome is uncertain — and the most likely outcome is that it will not be worth all the pain you’ve endured.”

At those ruthless words, Klesmer pushed his lower lip out, staring through his spectacles like some unyielding monster completely untouched by her beauty.

Gwendolen’s eyes began to burn with unshed tears, but the terror of looking weak made her clamp down on her emotions, holding them fast. She forced herself to speak in a hard, cold tone. “You think I don’t have the talent, or that I’m too old to start.”

Klesmer hummed a low note, then said emphatically: “Yes! The desire to do this, the training, should have started seven years ago — or much, much earlier. A mountebank’s child who helps her father earn pennies on the street at six years old, a child who inherits a singing throat from generations of choristers and learns to sing the same way she learns to talk — that child has a head start far better than yours. Any great achievement in acting or music grows with you, as you grow. When an artist

Gwendolen’s shoulders slumped with a quiet, unnameable relief when she walked away from Klesmer’s studio—she had weathered his brutal, unvarnished verdict on her actress ambitions before she had to face her aunt and uncle, and the new, sharp-edged clarity of her circumstances had settled over her like a lead shawl. She had steeled herself to dogged, unflinching calm now; every path stretched before her was lined with small, unavoidable humiliations, and she would not break, no matter what burdens they tried to lay at her feet.

The meeting with the Gascoignes was delayed until Monday, when Gwendolen walked to the rectory arm-in-arm with her mother. They detoured first to Sawyer’s Cottage, the drafty little home the rest of her family would soon be moving into, and wandered every narrow inch of its cramped rooms under a harsh midday sun, unsoftened by blinds or curtains that had not yet been salvaged from the rectory’s old stores. The bare walls, scuffed floorboards, tiny garden dotted with wilted cabbage stumps, and yew arbor stuffed full of dust and cobwebs made the space feel more like a discarded closet than a home.

“How will you ever bear it, mamma?” Gwendolen burst out once they were out on the lane again, her voice tight with the frustration she had swallowed the whole time they toured the cottage. “You and the four girls all crammed into that one tiny sitting room, with that garish green-and-yellow wallpaper pressing on your eyes? And I won’t even be there to help you manage it.”

“All that matters is that you won’t have to suffer it too, dear heart,” her mother soothed.

“If I didn’t need to earn money to send home to you,” Gwendolen said sharply, “I would rather squeeze into that cottage with all of you than go be a governess.”

“Don’t set your heart against it before you’ve even tried, Gwendolen,” her mother chided gently. “They say the Bishop’s household is a palace of luxury compared to falling up and down those rickety cottage stairs, listening to crockery rattle at every turn, and the girls chattering over mending. You’ve always loved nice things—this won’t feel half so harsh as you think.”

“It’s all a terrible, wrong dream,” Gwendolen burst out, hot with sudden anger. “I can’t believe Uncle Henry would let you all be shunted off to a hovel like that. He should have found some other way to fix things.”

“Don’t be unreasonable, darling. What else could he possibly have done?” her mother sighed.

“That was his job to figure out!” Gwendolen snapped. To her, who had always lived in a world where her future was pre-written to be bright, admired, and full of ease, the idea that her entire family could sink so far, so fast, felt like a cruel trick played on her personally. Her words were sharper than her heart was, though—she had always cared far more about sounding clever than sounding virtuous, a flaw that softened even her own awareness of how selfish her frustration sounded.

But when her uncle and aunt greeted her with a warmer, softer kindness than they had ever shown her in all the years she’d lived with them, the hot edge of her anger melted into quiet guilt. She watched, humbled, as they spoke of the coming household economies with steady, uncomplaining cheer: Mr. Gascoigne’s quiet goodness, once a little hidden by the small vanities of a comfortable rectory life, shone bright now that fortune had stripped them bare. He had already given up his carriage, sorted through his worn old suits to mend what he had, cut meat from his breakfast, cancelled all the expensive periodicals, pulled his younger sons out of boarding school to teach them himself, and trimmed every unnecessary expense down to the bone. Mrs. Gascoigne and Anna, who had always taken the rector as their model, did not even mourn the small luxuries they had lost; to them, the worst part of their misfortune was how hard the fall had been for Gwendolen’s mother and her sisters.

Anna even set aside her old quiet resentment on Rex’s behalf, her heart softening entirely for the cousin she had once adored. Neither she nor Mrs. Gascoigne had any urge to lecture Gwendolen on the value of hardship; instead, they spent their days rummaging through old linen cupboards for spare curtains and blinds for the cottage, careful not to rub the family’s misfortune in her face, and starting the visit by asking her about her travels, and how good it was for her mother to have her home again.

There was nothing for Gwendolen to reasonably rebel against here, and she found herself sitting straighter, as if she had been called to line up with the rest of the household to face the hard days ahead, when her uncle leaned forward and spoke to her in his steady, kind voice about the work he had done to secure her a position. He had thought of Grandcourt, of course, but a vague, uncertain hope of a wealthy suitor was not something a practical, responsible man like the rector could hang all his niece’s future on. He would do the best he could for her with the hand life had dealt them right now.

“I knew we could not waste a single day, Gwendolen,” he told her. “A good position, where you will be treated with proper respect, does not fall into one’s lap. After looking everywhere, I found you a spot that could not be better for you in Bishop Mompert’s household. I have known the bishop and his wife for years, and that reference will serve you well. Our correspondence has gone very smoothly, though Mrs. Mompert has asked to meet you in person before she makes the offer official. She will be passing through Wanchester on her way to London soon, and has arranged for you to meet her there. I know it will feel like a trying interview, my dear, but you have a little time to prepare.”

“Does she have a specific reason she needs to meet me, uncle?” Gwendolen asked, her mind already racing through a dozen unpleasant possibilities, all of them casting her as a specimen to be inspected and graded before she was deemed acceptable to care for three privileged girls.

The rector smiled reassuringly. “No need to be nervous, love. She simply wants a clearer sense of who you are than my letter could give her. Any mother would be careful about who she hires as a companion for her daughters. I told her you are very young, but she supervises her girls’ education herself, so your age is no problem for her. She’s a woman of great taste and strict principle, and prefers not to hire a French governess for the girls. I am certain your manners and accomplishments will far exceed what she is looking for. The bishop and his wife will oversee all the religious and moral elements of the girls’ education themselves, too.”

Gwendolen could not find the words to reply, but the effort of swallowing down her sharp, immediate hatred of the whole prospect sent a deep, hot flush creeping up her neck and across her face. Anna squeezed her cousin’s hand tight, full of gentle sympathy, and the rector, seeing how hard the blow had landed, hurried to paint a brighter, easier picture of the position.

“I think so highly of this spot that I would have tried to secure it for Anna, if it suited her at all,” he said easily. “It is a proper home, not a grueling job. The word ‘governess’ makes it sound far worse than it is. The bishop’s views are a bit more strictly Low Church than my own—he is a close friend of Lord Grampian’s, you know—but he is never narrow or unkind in public. He has always been a good friend to me, ever since he was a local incumbent here and we had a silly little disagreement about the Bible Society years ago.”

To the rector, his words were a list of all the good, respectable perks of the position—but to Gwendolen, they felt like a handful of snakes being loosed in the room. “Strictly Low Church”… close supervision… moral oversight… the whole prospect loomed before her like a penitentiary, not a home. Panicked, she scrambled for any other option.

“Mamma mentioned there was another position, at a school?” she said, forcing herself to sound calm and reasonable.

“Yes,” the rector said, his tone turning a little apologetic, “but that is nowhere near as good a fit for you. The work is far harder, the hours far longer, and there is no guarantee you would get the spot at all. You might not even have a bedroom of your own there.”

Gwendolen’s memories of strict, crowded schoolrooms made that “alternative” feel no less of a nightmare. “When is Mrs. Mompert expected to send for me?” she asked, as if she had already accepted the plan.

“Any time in the next fortnight, most likely. She promised me she will not consider any other candidate before she has met you. She has been very kind, and fully understands your family’s situation,” the rector said cheerfully, standing to leave. “I’m off now, to rent out a plot of the glebe land for a very good price.” He left the room fully convinced that Gwendolen, sensible girl that she was, would adapt gracefully to her new circumstances.

“Henry is such a rock for all of us,” Mrs. Gascoigne sighed once he was gone.

“He truly is,” Gwendolen’s mother agreed warmly. “Cheerfulness is a fortune all its own. I only wish I had half of his.”

“And Rex is just like him,” Mrs. Gascoigne went on, pulling a letter out of her pocket. “We got a letter from him the other day, and it has been such a comfort. Let me read you a bit of it—” Anna paled, nervous, for they had all avoided speaking of Rex in front of Gwendolen since their broken engagement. But Mrs. Gascoigne scanned the page and tucked it back into her pocket before she could read any of the lines that referenced the pain of what had happened between Rex and Gwendolen. “He says our family’s troubles have turned him into a man. He’s determined to win a fellowship, take on private pupils, get his little brothers set up in life, make something remarkable of himself. He jokes, ‘Tell mother she advertised for a hard-working, reliable son just in time to stop me running off to sea, and I’m applying for the job.’ We haven’t seen Henry that moved since the day Rex was born. It feels like such a gift to balance out all we’ve lost.”

That letter was the very reason the Gascoignes had been so gentle and forgiving to Gwendolen lately, and she smiled at Anna, pinching her cousin’s chin playfully to signal that there was no hard feelings left between them. She had never taken any pleasure in hurting people who cared for her; she only hated it when other people tried to make her life miserable.

But when the talk turned to arranging old bits of furniture for the cottage, Gwendolen could not summon even a flicker of interest. She thought she had already done more than enough that morning, holding the raging, terrified war inside her quiet. The recoil from the only future laid out for her was far sharper than she had ever imagined it would be. The idea of standing before Mrs. Mompert to be judged, accepted or rejected, felt like pressing on a fresh, throbbing bruise. Even after she forced herself to accept the strict bishop and his wife, they still got to decide if she was good enough. Every moment of every day would be spent under inspection, expected to perform lessons she did not care about, piety she did not feel, for three girls she had no desire to “edify.” Only six weeks prior, she had been the belle of every local party, her quick, playful wit making everyone laugh, the very last person anyone would have pitied. Now the life ahead of her felt like a prison.

Wild, giddy thoughts of running away to be an actress still darted through her head, luring her with the promise of freedom—but Klesmer’s harsh, cutting words still hung heavy on her soul, scaring her pride and her sense of herself as a lady. She could not bear the thought of being thrown among vulgar strangers, men who would leer and make rude, familiar remarks, no polite social barrier between her and their rudeness. Even her daydreams of winning big at the gaming table had always assumed that everyone would still treat her with the deference a lady deserved. The strict, stuffy life at the bishop’s house, where at least she would be treated with the basic respect due to a gentlewoman, was less terrifying than that uncertain, unprotected freedom.

Her rebellion curdled into a heavy, world-weary nausea. She was the one person in the whole world who had been built, all her life, to shine, to be admired, to be the center of attention—and now she was expected to fade into the background, to take orders, to be patronized. She could not bring herself to believe things would ever get better. Even the cheap solace of a governess romance, the kind she had read about in novels where the quiet, pretty teacher catches the eye of a wealthy heir, could not cheer her. There was no space for hope, no space for dreams. She saw no reason at all to want to go on living.

The feeling sank into her bones, making her numb. She could not bring herself to do a single small chore. The slightest demand, even that she sit down to eat a meal, felt like an irritation. She avoided the rectory entirely, because she could not bear to pretend to be compliant and grateful. She would not even feign interest in arranging furniture for the horrid little cottage. Miss Merry had come to stay to help, and Jocosa and the other girls were happy to sort through linens, so her mother had to make excuses for her absence even when Anna came to call. The steeled calm she had promised herself had melted into a listless, heavy apathy. She thought to herself, *I’ll pretend soon enough. Why bother now?*

Her mother watched her with silent, tender distress, and fell back into the old habit of indulging her every wish, desperate to do anything at all to ease her daughter’s pain. One afternoon, while they were alone in the black and yellow bedroom, her mother lingering under the pretense of sorting through Gwendolen’s old dresses, Gwendolen suddenly roused herself to drag out the little casket that held all her old trinkets and ornaments.

“Mamma,” she said, rummaging through the top tray, “I forgot all about these. Why didn’t you remind me? Take them to be sold. You don’t mind parting with them, you gave them all to me years ago.” She lifted the top tray to get at the layer beneath, and her eyes fell on the torn handkerchief she had stuffed in next to her turquoise necklace, the one that had been the only thing to save her necklace from being tossed into the river by Deronda back at the casino.

“Darling, if we don’t need the money that badly, I would rather keep them for you,” her mother said, relieved that Gwendolen was finally talking about something. “Why, how did that old torn handkerchief end up in here?”

“It just got tossed in with the necklace, I was in a hurry,” Gwendolen said, pulling the handkerchief out and tucking it into her pocket. “Don’t sell the turquoise necklace, mamma,” she added, a strange, soft new feeling rising in her chest for that small, fateful souvenir she had once resented so bitterly.

“Of course I won’t, dear. It was made from your dear father’s old watch chain. I would never sell that. None of these trinkets are worth much anyway—all my own best jewels were taken from me years ago, by your stepfather.” Mrs. Davilow colored, avoiding Gwendolen’s eye, for she never liked to speak of the old shame of her late husband stealing and selling off all her jewelry. “These haven’t been budgeted for any expenses. You can take them with you, to the Momperts’.”

“That would be useless,” Gwendolen said coldly. “Governesses don’t wear pretty ornaments. You might as well buy me a gray frieze uniform and a poke bonnet like the ones the charity children at the rectory school wear.”

“Don’t look at it that way, dear,” her mother said, distressed. “I’m sure the Momperts will love to see you graceful and elegant.”

“I have no idea what they will want me to be. All I know is I am expected to be exactly what they like,” Gwendolen said bitterly.

“If there is any other path you would rather take—anything at all that would be less hard than going to the bishop’s house—tell me, Gwendolen,” her mother begged, taking her hand. “Tell me what is in your heart. I will do anything I possibly can to make it happen. Don’t shut me out. Let’s bear this together.”

“There’s nothing to tell,” Gwendolen said, not looking at her mother, twisting the turquoise necklace between her fingers. “There is no better option for me. I’m lucky if they even agree to hire me.
