All I have to think about is sending money home to you. I won’t spend a single penny of the eighty pounds a year they pay me. You can have all of it, to help with the housekeeping. You won’t have to stitch your fingers raw, or cry your eyes out over mending late at night.” She did not lean in to kiss her mother, the way she used to, her eyes fixed on the glint of the turquoise stones.

“Bless you, my sweet, good child,” her mother said, tears running down her cheeks. “Don’t despair now, just because there are clouds. You are so young. There is great happiness still waiting for you somewhere, I just know it.”

“I see no reason to count on that,” Gwendolen said, her voice hard. Her mother fell quiet, wondering, as she had so many times before, what exactly had passed between Gwendolen and Mr. Grandcourt that day he had come to visit.

“I will keep this necklace, mamma,” Gwendolen said, tucking it to one side before closing the casket. “But sell all the other trinkets, even if they don’t fetch much. Ask uncle what to do with them. I will never wear any of them again. I’m as good as taking the veil. I wonder if every other poor soul who ever did that felt the same hollow, dead feeling I do right now.”

“Don’t make the hardships worse than they are, dear.”

“How can anyone say I’m exaggerating? No one else can feel what I

How do you untangle the messy motives of a mind reduced to the barrenness of fastidious egoism? A mind where every sharp, direct desire has dulled into a vague, vacillating expectation of wanting something eventually, a patch of weeds where a single unruly impulse might sprout tall and conspicuous out of nowhere? That is the exact fate that befalls a life lived entirely unshackled by obligation, untethered to any duty that might shape its drift. The old adage holds true: total freedom is a terrible compass.

Potentates state their intentions and shift entire markets with only a handful of words. So when Henleigh Grandcourt, fresh off learning that Gwendolen Harleth had left Leubronn, casually dismissed the glittering resort as a disgusting dump far worse than Baden, Mr. Lush knew exactly what that meant: his patron intended to head straight back to his Diplow estate.

Grandcourt dragged his feet the next full day, deliberately avoiding giving any clear order to depart, just to amuse himself by making Lush wait. He lingered for hours over his toilet, descending at last with that perfectly faded, effortless air of high refinement that made every rosy-cheeked, blooded newcomer around him look crude and vulgar by comparison. He loitered on the terrace, in the gambling rooms, in the quiet reading nook, devoting all his energy to being utterly indifferent to every person and every trivial amusement around him. The only exception was when he ran into Lady Mallinger: he tipped his hat, paused, and let his languid seriousness play off her innocent chatter about the supposed health benefits of the resort springs, dryly noting “I heard someone say it’s awfully convenient that gambling towns always happen to have mineral springs.” Lady Mallinger, missing the joke entirely, trotted off to tell Sir Hugo, who laughed and said “Don’t you be fooled, my dear. That man is no fool. He plays his cards as well as any of us.” Privately, Lady Mallinger still felt a prickle of discomfort around Grandcourt—he was nothing less to her than a living reminder of what she saw as her own failure as a wife: her inability to give Sir Hugo a son of their own to inherit his title.

Daniel Deronda received a brief, deliberate nod from Grandcourt that afternoon, and he did not feel grateful for it, though he returned the greeting with perfect civility. No logic about the arbitrary rules of inheritance could erase the deep, old sting of the knowledge that but for the circumstances of his birth, all of Grandcourt’s fortune and status could have been his. Proud as he was, he refused to ever let a man like Grandcourt see even a flicker of bitterness, even the faintest hint of petty envy. He would not sink into that ugly pit of unreasoning rancor, clinging to the kind, generous parts of his nature that he valued far more than any estate. But there was no guarding against Grandcourt’s assumptions: he was fully convinced the “poor devil” of his cousin secretly ached at their unequal positions, and that made Deronda’s company far more pleasant than it would otherwise have been. The fantasy that others envied his good fortune was the favorite shadow that followed his egoism everywhere; he liked to feel he had power over people enough to make them jealous, not just his prized hunting hounds. So he even stopped to chat with Deronda on the terrace about the upcoming hunting season around Diplow, and casually invited him over to join in a few runs when the season opened.

Lush, for his part, was perfectly happy with the delay in their departure. He gossiped easily with Sir Hugo, answering all the baronet’s questions about Grandcourt’s tangled financial and personal affairs, no longer holding back on sunny autumn afternoons when there was no finer pleasure on the promenade than to speak freely of his tyrannical patron behind his back. Sir Hugo loved this kind of light, juicy gossip he called *traits de mœurs*, and never passed snippets on to anyone who might take them too seriously. He never breathed a word of Grandcourt’s secrets to Deronda, who wrote the man off as a pale, listless mortal and had no desire to hear the sordid details of how he’d ended up the way he was.

“Let me know the moment things shift,” Sir Hugo told Lush. “If that marriage looks like it might go through after all, or if any trouble pops up that makes him need cash fast. My plan for him to sell off his partial interest in Diplow will be far better for him than burdening his Ryelands estate with endless debt.” Lush agreed, but added that you could never push Grandcourt directly on what was good for him—especially if he suspected it was also good for someone else. “I’ve stuck by him for fifteen years now,” Lush added, with a put-upon loyal air. “He’d never find anyone else to put up with his peculiar ways. He could be the most charming man alive if he chose to, but he’s grown far more stubborn these past years. I do my best to steer him right.” Sir Hugo did not bother to offer sympathy or agreement; as a member of Parliament, he was well used to the convenient little stories hanger-ons wove to justify their comfortable positions.

But Lush’s stretch of easy procrastination was cut short the very next morning, when Grandcourt greeted him with a cold, sharp question: “Have you made all the arrangements for us to take the Paris train today?” Lush, not as surprised as he let on, said he had not realized they were leaving so soon. “You should have known,” Grandcourt murmured, low and disgusted as he stared at the smoldering end of his cigar. “See to everything at once. Make sure no stranger or strange brute gets into the same carriage as us, and leave my calling card for the Mallingers to say goodbye.”

They reached Paris the next day, but Grandcourt immediately sent Lush straight on ahead to Diplow to make sure the house and stables were perfectly prepared, lingering in Paris himself with his valet for three more days. When Lush finally got the telegram ordering the Diplow carriage to meet Grandcourt at Wanchester station, he had spent every spare minute digging up every scrap of gossip from the local townsfolk about Gwendolen Harleth and her family. He knew her family had been ruined utterly by a rascally bank failure, left penniless, forced to move out of Offendene into a tiny cottage little better than a laborer’s hovel. Lush could not predict how Grandcourt’s flighty obstinacy would react to the news. On one hand, Gwendolen’s poverty meant she would almost certainly accept a proposal from him now, no more risk of the humiliating rejection that had been holding Grandcourt back for months. On the other, the certainty that she would say yes was exactly the kind of boring, guaranteed outcome that might make Grandcourt lose interest entirely, drifting away like a moth that had already gotten too close to the flame.

Lush did not want this marriage to happen at all. He would have been perfectly happy if Grandcourt married a wealthy heiress, or even married his long-time mistress Mrs. Glasher: either outcome would keep Lush in his comfortable, privileged position as the man the grand household relied on. But Gwendolen? She already openly despised him. She would boot him out of the estate before the wedding bells stopped ringing, and he would lose every last bit of the easy, indulgent life he had earned after fifteen years of pandering to Grandcourt’s whims. He was determined to do everything in his power to stop the match, even if that meant throwing a few well-placed spanners in the works.

When Grandcourt arrived at Diplow, he spent the entire first day fussing over his stables, and even ordered a side-saddle strapped to his finest hunter Criterion to test the horse’s paces—a clear, unmistakeable sign of his intentions. Lush spent the whole night turning the situation over in his head, nearly resolved that he would reveal Gwendolen’s ruined circumstances the moment Grandcourt was in a talkative mood, to see how he reacted. But Grandcourt was dead silent all the next morning, answering no small talk, giving only short, sharp orders for the household. Lush got up to leave the room when Grandcourt dismissed him, only for the man to call after him, quiet and languid: “Oh, shut the door, will you? I can’t shout into the corridor.”

Lush shut the door, came back, and sat down. After a long drag on his cigar, Grandcourt said flatly, “Is Miss Harleth at Offendene?”

Lush tried to play it casual. “Well, I hardly know. Her whole family is done for. They and the Gascoignes lost every last penny in that bank fraud. The poor mother hasn’t a sou to her name. They’re being kicked out of Offendene any day now, moving into that tiny cottage on the edge of the village.”

Grandcourt’s voice dropped so low it was almost a purr of menace. “Don’t lie to me, if you please. It’s not amusing, and it serves no purpose.”

Lush was stung out of his usual careful calm. “That’s no lie. Bazley, Brackenshaw’s man, told me himself they’re already searching for a new tenant for Offendene.”

“I’m not talking about the house. Is Miss Harleth there, or not?” Grandcourt repeated.

“Upon my soul, I can’t tell,” Lush said, sullen. “She might have left yesterday. I heard she’d taken a post as a governess, for all I know she’s already gone to it. Though I suppose her mother would send for her back if you wanted to see her.”

Grandcourt did not rise to the sneer. He only said, “Send Hutchins to their house to ask if she will be there tomorrow.”

Lush did not move. All the arguments he had rehearsed for weeks came tumbling out, sharp with irritation. “You might want to think this through. This isn’t the casual half-hearted flirting you were doing before, where nothing has to mean anything. You have to decide right now if you want to be accepted—and more than that, how you’ll take it if she turns you down. You can’t hang around philandering after her for six more weeks.”

Grandcourt said nothing, only flattened the newspaper on his knees and lit a new cigar. Lush took that as a sign he was listening, and pushed harder. “Marrying her will mean you have to provide for her entire penniless family. It’ll pin you down tighter than you’ve ever been pinned before, and you don’t have that much extra cash to spare to go around supporting three generations of Harleths. Why burden your estate over a whim you’ll regret in a year?”

Grandcourt plucked the cigar from his mouth, adjusted the moist leaf between his delicate fingertips, and said, “I knew before that you objected to my marrying Miss Harleth. But I never considered that a reason not to.”

“It wasn’t my objection I was pointing out,” Lush said, dry now. “It’s the absurdity of it. You couldn’t make up your mind before, so it’s impossible you can care that deeply about her now. And you know how headstrong she is—she ran away from Leubronn for a reason, remember? She’s not the kind of girl you can predict.” Then he dropped the bomb he had been hoarding for months. “She saw Lydia Glasher that day in Cardell Chase. She heard every last word of the whole story.”

Grandcourt did not flinch. He did not move, did not change his expression, only kept smoking. Lush waited, pacing to the window, expecting him to blow up, to call the whole thing off, to realize how impossible it would be to court a girl who already knew his darkest secret. But at last Grandcourt looked up at him, narrow eyes steady, and said, perfectly cool: “What follows?”

Lush realized he had been outplayed. There was no more argument to make. He shrugged one shoulder, ready to storm out, when Grandcourt nodded at the desk. “Oblige me by pushing that pen and paper over here, will you?” The quiet, unshakable authority he carried was impossible to resist. Lush passed the stationery over, and Grandcourt scrawled a short, brief note in seconds, addressed it to Gwendolen Harleth, Offendene. “Send Hutchins off with this right away,” he said, pushing the letter across the desk.

Lush left the room seething, when his anger cooled he knew two things for certain. There was a permanent mark against him now, and Grandcourt would make him pay for that little reveal sooner or later. And worst of all? The secret he had spilled to scare Grandcourt off had not put him off at all. It had only hardened his resolve, making him more determined than ever to claim Gwendolen Harleth as his own.

He brings white asses laden with the freight
Of Tyrian vessels, purple, gold and balm,
To bribe my will: I’ll bid them chase him forth,
Nor let him breathe the taint of his surmise
On my secure resolve. Ay, ’tis secure:
And therefore let him come to spread his freight.
For firmness hath its appetite and craves
The stronger lure, more strongly to resist;
Would know the touch of gold to fling it off;
Scent wine to feel its lip the soberer;
Behold soft byssus, ivory, and plumes
To say, “They’re fair, but I will none of them,”
And flout Enticement in the very face.

The day Mr. Gascoigne rode up to Offendene’s overgrown iron gates, he carried what he believed was the kindest possible news for his niece: Mrs. Mompert had fixed the following Tuesday as the date for Gwendolen to travel to Wanchester and take up her new post as governess to her three unruly, wilful daughters. He said nothing of the stray bit of local gossip he’d overheard, that Mr. Grandcourt had returned to his Diplow estate—he knew no more than Gwendolen herself that the wealthy, mysterious bachelor had trailed her all the way to Leubronn on the Continent, and he saw no mercy in stirring up the ghost of the glittering, once-within-reach prospect of marrying him now that their family fortunes had crashed so thoroughly. Privately the rector regretted his niece’s inexplicable rejection of Grandcourt, but he excused her: the man himself had acted so strangely, vanishing mid-courtship just when he was on the cusp of making his intentions perfectly clear, that no one could blame her for taking offence. His only duty now, he was certain, was to urge Gwendolen to face the hard new path laid out before her, for there was no sign any miracle would come to save her from it.

“You will find a new richness in varied experience, my dear,” he told her gently, “and I have no doubt you will grow into a far more capable, admirable woman for the part you are being called to play now.”

“I can’t pretend I will enjoy it,” Gwendolen snapped, sharp with a rare petulance she had never directed at her uncle before. “But I know full well I have no choice but to bear it.” She remembered, not long ago, when he had urged her to look forward to that very different, very glamorous prospect that had been Grandcourt’s courtship, and the bitter contrast stung.

“Your good sense will guide you to behave as you ought,” Mr. Gascoigne said, a little more sternly. “I am sure Mrs. Mompert will take a great liking to you. You will know exactly how to comport yourself around a woman who is, in every sense, your superior. This hardship has fallen on you young, which makes it lighter in many ways, and there is profit in all chastisement if we only bend our minds to accept it.”

That was the very last thing Gwendolen could bring herself to do. The moment her uncle left, the slow, hot tears she had refused to shed through weeks of worsening misfortune began to roll down her cheeks as she sat alone in the empty drawing room. Her heart railed against the idea that her suffering was easier just because she was young. When was she supposed to have any joy at all, if not in the bright bloom of her youth? She no longer harbored the old childish fantasies that she could pluck every prize of life without ever tasting a bitter core, but that new disenchantment—with the world, and even with herself, now that she knew she was not nearly extraordinary enough to command fate to bow to her will—only made her lonelier. Ahead of her stretched a barren, endless stretch of dull duty, and she had no strength to set foot on the path. She was in that first, fiery young rebellion against the absence of joy, that blinding, furious grief of disappointment that hits you the very first morning you wake up and realize life will not hand you the happy ending you always assumed you were owed. Who among us, softened by years of accumulated hardship, does not forget how sharp that sting was, when you first reeled at the unfairness that it was you, and not some stranger, who had been dealt the blow? Even those who later went on to brave pain in silence for the people they loved, to build goodness out of their own wounds, started there—raging at the tiny, burning wasp sting of a denied wish, as if the whole universe had conspired to hurt them alone.

That was exactly Gwendolen’s state now. Who cared if hundreds of other girls had fallen from the same height of comfort and privilege to become governesses? All that mattered to her was that it was *her* fate. It was the weight of Klesmer’s brutal truth that she was not talented enough to outrun circumstance, the dread of Mrs. Mompert’s constant exacting eye, the drudgery of forcing cheer for three sullen Miss Momperts day in and day out, the small humiliation of having to swallow every part of her proud, sparkling self to please strangers. To be dethroned from a crown you never asked to wear is nothing compared to falling from the illusion that you are special, that you can do no wrong, that all eyes will always turn to admire you. There she was, the spoiled child with the lovely full lips, the bright bold eyes, the tall, regal bearing that had once turned every head at every party, and all her old magic was gone.

She got up from the low ottoman where she had been sitting motionless, pacing the drawing room, one elbow propped in her palm, her cheek resting heavy on her hand, another slow tear dripping down. “Ever since I was little I knew mamma was never happy,” she thought, “and now I’ll be even unhappier than she ever was.” She pictured herself fading away, her youth slipping by, no longer caring what she did from one day to the next—but the vision quickly filled with the face of her mother. “Poor mamma. This will be worse for her, too. At least I’ll be able to send her a little money. That’s the only thing that matters now.” For the first time, she imagined her mother growing old, her hair turning white, while she too faded, no longer young, no longer bright, the two of them holding onto each other through all their loss, and she broke into soft, heaving sobs, not from anger, but from a tender, aching misery.

She looked up and saw her mother walk through the door, her eyes so heavy with tears she barely registered the movement. She fumbled for her handkerchief, pressing it hard to her eyes, but before she could dry her face she felt her mother’s arms wrap around her, and she sobbed all the harder, no longer able to hide her pain. Mrs. Davilow was pale and trembling, clutching a letter in her hand that had already set her heart racing, and she held her daughter tight until Gwendolen’s sobs softened.

“It’s nothing, mamma,” Gwendolen said, breathing deep as she pulled back, dabbing at her wet cheeks. “It’s all over now.”

But she saw the letter in her mother’s hand, and a sharp, bitter jab of fear hit her. “What is that? More bad news?”

“I don’t know what you’ll think of it, my dear,” Mrs. Davilow said, holding the note out carefully. “You’ll never guess who it’s from.”

“Please don’t make me guess anything,” Gwendolen snapped, as if someone was pressing on an old bruise.

“It’s addressed to you. It came from Diplow.”

Gwendolen recognized Grandcourt’s faint, slanted handwriting instantly, and her face flushed bright red—but by the time she finished reading the short, formal note, all the color had drained away, even her lips pale. She held it out to her mother, her hands shaking a little:
*Mr. Grandcourt presents his compliments to Miss Harleth, and begs to know whether he may be permitted to call at Offendene to-morrow after two and to see her alone. Mr. Grandcourt has just returned from Leubronn, where he had hoped to find Miss Harleth.*

Mrs. Davilow read it, then looked at her daughter, bewildered. Gwendolen let the note slip from her fingers to the floor, turning away.

“You have to answer it, darling,” Mrs. Davilow said softly. “The messenger is waiting downstairs.”

Gwendolen sank onto the settee, clasping her hands tight in her lap, staring straight ahead at nothing. A few minutes before, she had been trapped on an inescapable path of dull, hateful drudgery, no choice at all—and now, suddenly, a choice had fallen right into her lap. She felt a spark of triumph, hot and bright: even at her lowest, stripped of all her fortune, the man everyone in the county envied was still chasing her, had followed her all the way to Leubronn to find her. She was not invisible, after all. But the triumph curdled into terror fast. All the fragments of that old memory rushed back at her: the pretty dark-haired woman, the little boy clinging to her skirts, the unspoken promise she had made to herself that she would never marry Grandcourt, the bitter disillusionment that had settled over her after that awful confrontation. What did she want, now? Nothing had changed. But a new, quiet thought curled in the back of her mind: I wish I never got this letter. She would give anything to not have to make this choice.

It felt like an eternity to Mrs. Davilow before she spoke again, gentle as ever. “You need to write back, dear. Or would you like me to draft a reply for you, and you can tell me what to say?”

“No, mamma,” Gwendolen said, taking a long, steadying breath. “Just lay out the pen and paper for me.”

That bought her time. She could turn him away, shut the door, go back to the fate she had already accepted, no risk, no mess. But the young, wild, restless part of her that had always craved excitement, craved to feel powerful again, was thrumming under her skin. The day ahead no longer stretched before her empty and dead.

“There’s no reason to rush, you know,” Gwendolen said, a little sharp, as her mother laid the writing supplies on the table in front of her. “Servants are paid to wait. There’s no need for me to dash off a note instantly.”

“Of course not, dear,” Mrs. Davilow said, picking up a bit of needlework from the side table. “He can wait another quarter hour, no trouble at all.”

The quiet, gentle acceptance of it made Gwendolen *anxious* to hurry, to avoid sitting and picking apart every last pro and con of this choice until she drove herself mad. She stood up, smoothing back the dark curls that had fallen loose around her face. “I don’t mean he has to wait long enough for you to finish that horrible needlework,” she said, walking over to the writing desk and sinking into the chair. Her mind was racing, half of her thinking of how quickly she could send the note off, half of her wondering what harm there could be in letting him come. He wasn’t forcing her to do anything. He had chased her all the way to Leubronn, he was clearly going to propose—why shouldn’t she let him show up, just so she could reject him, and prove to herself she still had that power?

“Mr. Grandcourt only just got back from Leubronn,” Mrs. Davilow said softly. “I wonder if he’s heard what happened to our fortune.”

“A man with his money wouldn’t care about something like that,” Gwendolen said, a little scornful.

“Most men would,” Mrs. Davilow persisted. “They wouldn’t want to marry a girl whose family is nearly beggared, you know. Imagine if he found us living crammed in that tiny cottage at Sawyer’s Lane. Most men hate the thought of being bored or burdened by their wife’s relations. If he *does* know, and he’s still writing to you? That’s very strong proof of how much he cares for you.”

The words hit Gwendolen like a jolt. A whole new world of possibilities opened up before her: Grandcourt was rich, he could make sure her mother never had to want for anything ever again, never had to scrape by on charity, never had to live in a tiny cramped cottage. The thought made her fingers move fast, before she could stop and think any more, and she scribbled the note in a hurry:

“Ring the bell, please mamma,” she said, leaning back in her chair, breathless, after she sealed it. She had barely even thought about what she was agreeing to. It was only after the footman had taken the letter away that she stretched, pressing a hand to her forehead, a low half-moan that was half relief, half tension, escaping her.

“What did you write, Gwen?” Mrs. Davilow asked, softly.

“I wrote that I would be at home tomorrow,” Gwendolen said, lifting her chin, proud. “But don’t go getting any ideas that something is going to happen, mamma. Just because he’s coming to call doesn’t mean I’m going to say yes.”

“I’m not expecting anything, dear. I only want you to follow your own heart. You never did tell me exactly how you felt about him.”

“What’s the point of talking about it?” Gwendolen said, stung. “If I had something nice to tell you, you’d be the first to hear it.”

“But Mr. Grandcourt will take that note as an acceptance of his suit, won’t he?” Mrs. Davilow said. “His note says plain as day he’s coming to propose.”

“Good. Then I get to enjoy the pleasure of turning him down.” Gwendolen stood up, shoving the pen and paper away from her, the air in the drawing room suddenly feeling thick and stifling. “Put down that awful needlework, will you? Let’s walk in the avenue. I can’t breathe in here.”

Desire trims the sails, and Circumstance brings no more than the breeze to fill them. That soft sun-dappled afternoon, Grandcourt rode his glossy black thoroughbred Yarico at a leisurely canter, his groom trailing a short distance behind on the mare Criterion, every hoofbeat eating up the lane between Diplow and Offendene, every line of his bearing radiating the quiet, unshakable assurance of a man who expects to get exactly what he wants. Upstairs in her bedroom, Gwendolen sat before the gilded mirror as her mother brushed out the full, heavy length of her pale light-brown hair, the strands glinting like spun honey in the sunlight.
“Only twist it up into a simple coil, mamma,” Gwendolen said, flicking a stray lock away from her forehead.
When the hair was arranged neat and unadorned, Mrs. Davilow’s gaze drifted to their shared reflection, and she gasped softly. The haggard, shadowed lines that had carved their way into Gwendolen’s face in the weeks since Klesmer crushed her performing dreams were gone, her eyes bright with a spark she had not seen for months. “Let me bring you your pearl drop earrings, Gwen,” she said, her voice light with unspoken hope.
Gwendolen shook her head, rising to slip off her silk dressing gown to reveal the plain, untrimmed black silk beneath. “No ornaments. Black is the only proper wear when you are going to turn a man down,” she said, flashing her old sharp, playful smile at her mother.
“Suppose he never even makes the offer at all?” Mrs. Davilow teased, a faint glint of mischief in her eyes.
“Then it will come to exactly the same thing,” Gwendolen said, tossing her head proudly. “I will have refused him before the words ever leave his mouth.”
She walked down the stairs moments later, the heavy black silk swishing around her ankles, her head held high, the spring back in her step that had vanished the day she met Mrs. Glasher at the Whispering Stones. Mrs. Davilow watched her from the landing, her chest tight with confused joy. She’s quite herself again, she thought. That can only mean she is happy he is coming. Can she really be set on refusing him?
If Gwendolen had heard that thought, she would have snapped back sharp. But the truth was, she had not known what she truly wanted for almost twenty hours, her mind flipping back and forth between opposing impulses so fast her resolve to refuse Grandcourt had turned hollow, no more alive than a “God’s will be done” murmured by someone who is leaning over a fence to watch a race, eager for a winner. She had never forgotten Mrs. Glasher’s desperate face, had never forgotten her promise not to interfere with the woman’s wishes for her boys, but over and over again she pictured the grim little cottage at Sawyer’s Lane, the sharp-eyed Mrs. Mompert drilling her to be a governess to squalling small children, her mother’s gentle, worn face lined with constant worry over their lost fortune. Everyone knows, she told herself, that society turns its cold shoulder to the illegitimate children, not the rich gentleman who sired them. What business is it of mine to throw away a whole life of ease, of being the one who calls all the shots, for the sake of a stranger’s secret? Even the old hot revulsion, the anger that Grandcourt would dare offer her a life built on top of hidden old mistakes, had dulled to a faint memory. She would not accept him, she told herself, she would not—right up until she heard the sound of his horse’s hooves crunching on the gravel drive.
Her heart lurched so hard she almost stumbled. All that steady preparation, all that resolve, melted away like snow in the sun. She had to clamp down on every trembling nerve to walk into the drawing-room slow and graceful, offer him her hand, her voice soft and low, matching his familiar lazy drawl as he said he hoped she was well. They settled into two of the wreath-painted chairs across from each other, Grandcourt leaning one arm over the back of his seat, his hat loose in his left hand, the faint, sweet scent of attar of roses drifting across the space between them. Any onlooker would have seen two people suspended in the tender hush of a new courtship, and Gwendolen could feel herself being wooed, wrapped in the quiet, complete focus of his attention that she had craved for months.
“I was gutted not to find you at Leubronn,” Grandcourt began, his drawl thick with soft, amorous languor. “The whole place was a filthy kennel without you. Don’t you agree?”
“I can’t speak for what it was without me,” Gwendolen said, a flicker of her old mischief returning as she lifted her eyes to his. “With me, I liked it well enough to stay longer if I could. But I had to come home for family troubles.”
“It was very cruel of you to run off like that,” Grandcourt said, brushing right past her mention of the troubles, as if they could not possibly matter. “You knew you were the heart and soul of every gathering there. Are you really so reckless with my feelings?”
Gwendolen blushed hot all the way down her neck, dropping her eyelids again. She could not say yes, she could not say no.
“Is there some other man?” he pressed, his voice low. “Some engagement that you owe it to me to tell me about? Is anyone standing between us?”
Her first thought was, No—but there is a woman. But the words would not come, stuck fast in her throat. She could not break her promise to Mrs. Glasher, could not bring herself to air that sordid secret out loud to Grandcourt, and suddenly the thought of him riding away for good, leaving her stuck with the governess post, the tiny cottage, no more horses, no more pretty gowns, made her chest ache with sharp, panicky dread.
“I fear you do not know what has befallen us,” she said, her voice steady with new-found dignity as she lifted her eyes to meet his. “My mother has lost all her fortune. We are leaving Offendene soon. That is why my mind has been so preoccupied.”
Grandcourt’s narrow, unreadable dark eyes held hers, and for a long, quiet moment neither of them spoke. Then he said, slow and lazy, as if it were the most trivial, obvious thing in the world, “I hope you will agree that Mrs. Davilow’s loss need never trouble either of you again. Trust me to make sure that weight never touches her. Give me the right to provide for you both.”
The words sank into her like a sip of heady, warm wine. All the scruples, all the old revulsion, all the faint fear of doing wrong faded to something distant, like a pain she had suffered months ago. She could see it all spread out before her: no more Sawyer’s Cottage, no more Mrs. Mompert looking over her shoulder, her mother happy and relaxed, herself galloping across the fields on Criterion every morning, answering to no one.
“You are very generous,” she said, her voice soft.
“You accept that future?” Grandcourt asked, no sharp eagerness in his tone, only quiet certainty. “Will you be my wife?”
Gwendolen went pale, rising abruptly to step a few feet away, her hands folded tight in front of her as she stood silent. Grandcourt stood too, his hat still loosely in his grip, that small flash of hesitation from the destitute, usually unflappable girl making his interest burn sharper than it had in years.
“Do you command me to leave?” he said.
No words could have been more perfectly chosen.
“No,” Gwendolen said. That single word was a leash, holding him fast. She could not let him go.
“You accept my devotion?” he asked, stepping a little closer, their eyes locked.
Wait as long as she might, there was no way to take that no back, no way to send him away. “Yes,” she said, as solemnly as if she were swearing an oath before a judge in a courtroom.
He accepted the word gravely, no messy, over-eager kiss, no clumsy clinging. He stepped forward and pressed a soft, respectful kiss to the back of her hand, then released it. Gwendolen thought his behavior was perfect, more charming than any suitor she had ever read of in novels.
“Will you not come meet mamma?” she said, a playful curl of her lips tugging at her mouth. “I will fetch her.”
“Let us wait a little while,” he said, standing close beside her, his left thumb and finger tucked into his waistcoat pocket as he stroked his whisker with his other hand. “I have one more thing to ask. How soon could we be married?”
“Not today,” Gwendolen said, tilting her chin saucily.
“Then tomorrow. Think on it before I come back. A fortnight, three weeks—no longer than we have to wait.”
“Ah, you think you will grow tired of my company once we are wed,” she teased, laughing bright and warm.
“You shall have whatever you like,” Grandcourt said.
“Then promise me I will never have to be around Mr. Lush,” she said, half playful, half earnest. “I do not like his company at all.”
“You shall not have to,” he said, his tone utterly indifferent. “I will get rid of him immediately. He is nothing but a poor, coarse brute who has trailed after me since I was a boy. I never could stand him.”
Gwendolen laughed, light and happy, sure that she had stumbled into the perfect life, the perfect husband. She ran up to her mother’s bedroom moments later, kissing her on both cheeks, her eyes sparkling.
“Come downstairs, mamma,” she whispered. “I am engaged to Mr. Grandcourt. You will never have to move to Sawyer’s Cottage. I will never have to be inspected by Mrs. Mompert. Everything will be exactly as I like.”
Mrs. Davilow stared, quiet, solemn surprise sweeping over her face, and Gwendolen tugged her by the hand toward the landing, giddy with the bright, unburdened relief of knowing she had escaped every grim fate the last weeks had stacked up against her.
The new chapter of her life, she thought, was already beginning.

An hour after Henleigh Grandcourt had ridden away from Offendene, the news of Gwendolen’s engagement had traveled all the way to the rectory, and by sunset Mr. and Mrs. Gascoigne and quiet Anna had walked over to crowd the little drawing room, their faces bright with the kind of eager celebration that clings to a favorite cousin’s triumph.

“My dear girl, let me be the first to congratulate you on having inspired such a deep, steady attachment,” the rector said, patting her shoulder gently. “You look serious, and no wonder: a life-long union is no trivial promise. But from all Mr. Grandcourt has said and done, we can already see that our recent hard luck has turned to good. It gave him the chance to show you how delicate, how generously he means to care for all the people you love.”

He was referring, of course, to Grandcourt’s quiet assurance that he would make ample provision for Mrs. Davilow—every word of which Gwendolen had repeated perfectly to her mother earlier that day.

“I’ve no doubt he would have offered just as handsomely even if you’d never run off to Germany,” Mrs. Gascoigne put in, her tone firm with the weight of well-meaning auntly duty. “You could have been engaged to him more than a month ago, after all. But there’s no room for silly caprice now, and I’m sure you’ve no taste for it. A woman owes a great debt of gratitude to a man who persists in laying a fortune and his whole heart at her feet. I’m certain you feel exactly how much this means.”

“I’m not at all sure I do, aunt,” Gwendolen replied, her tone light with saucy gravity. “No one ever sat me down to list all the things a proper engaged lady is supposed to feel.”

The rector laughed, and patted her shoulder again, writing it off as harmless, endearing naughtiness. Anna leaned in to kiss her, her eyes already glistening, and whispered “I hope you’ll be so, so happy” before she slipped back to the edge of the group, pressing a handkerchief to her eyes to hide the tears. For weeks she’d been nurturing a soft, quiet little romance of her own: if Rex’s heart still ached for Gwendolen, surely his old crush might soften her cousin enough that in time, when their fortunes improved, the two of them might marry. Now all that daydream was dust, and she was fighting to find the grace to rejoice for Gwendolen instead of grieving for her brother.

Even Miss Merry and the four little girls—Alice with the high, proud shoulders, Bertha and Fanny who always whispered in corners, quiet little Isabel who listened to every word—were clustered around the fringes of the room, all of them agreeing that real life was turning out far more thrilling than the pages of *Sir Charles Grandison*. The rest of the evening passed in the rector’s calm, decisive remarks, as the two elder ladies speculated over what their new life would look like. The rector insisted there was no need to fuss over legal settlements yet: everything would be left safely, generously, in Grandcourt’s hands.

“I do wish I knew exactly what Ryelands and Gadsmere are like,” Mrs. Davilow said, her voice soft with wonder.

“Gadsmere’s just a secondary, smaller estate,” Mr. Gascoigne told her. “But Ryelands? It’s one of the finest country seats in the whole county. Thousands of acres of parkland, woods so valuable they’re worth a fortune all on their own. The house was built by Inigo Jones, ceilings painted in the Italian style, just like the royal palaces. They say the estate brings in twelve thousand a year, and the Grandcourts have the gift of two church livings, one a rectory, to give away. There might be small old debts on the land, but Grandcourt was an only child—there’s no one else to claim a share.”

“Imagine if he inherits the Lord Stannery peerage on top of all that!” Mrs. Gascoigne gushed, ticking the advantages off on her fingers. “The Grandcourt estate, the Mallinger estate, the baronetcy, and a seat in the House of Lords—only they say the peerage doesn’t come with extra land.” She frowned, finding no fifth finger left to mark another grand prize, and sighed at the small disappointment.

“The peerage is still a long shot,” the rector said judiciously. “There are two cousins between the current lord and Grandcourt, after all. It’s quite remarkable how chance deaths can pile whole fortunes onto one man’s shoulders. But a man with that much wealth and station, with the name Sir Mallinger Grandcourt Mallinger, has a gift more than enough for any man to manage well. Let’s hope he uses it kindly.”

“And what a position for you, Gwendolen!” Mrs. Gascoigne turned to her, eyes sparkling. “It’s a great honor, and a great responsibility. Henry, you must write to Mrs. Mompert at once—your excuse of an engagement will save you from the governess post you were dreading. She’s a very proud, high-tempered woman, and we don’t want her offended.”

Gwendolen almost smiled. The name Mompert had come to feel like some bogeyman’s name to her, and now that horror was gone forever. She barely spoke the rest of the evening, and that night she could not sleep a wink in her little white bed. It was almost unheard of for her, strong, bright, unshakable as she’d always been, to lie wide awake at midnight. She took extra care not to wake her mother, not to let anyone see her restlessness: she who had always been so sure of herself, so ready to twist everyone around her finger, had just taken a step she’d once sworn she would never, ever take. There was no turning back now. She loved almost everything she could see stretching out before her: the fine houses, the horses, the freedom from shabby genteel poverty, the thrill of being the lady of a great estate. But the shadow of that old, firm, gut-deep refusal of Grandcourt hung over her still.

Staring at the pitch black around her curtains, the kind of darkness the blind can “see” all too clearly, she felt a cold terror creep over her. She was about to do the very thing she’d run away from in Leubronn, the thing she’d recoiled from with all her might. For the first time in her life, a question of right and wrong was spurring real fear in her: before, every small misstep she’d made had been fixed with a soft apology or a pretty new present. Now a new, sharp consciousness was waking in her chest. She was on the edge of making that bitter, reckless line she’d spat out in her despair months ago her whole life’s rule: nothing matters, just amuse yourself as best you can. That lawlessness, that throwing away of every standard of right, terrified her. Behind it loomed all the vague, awful calamities she’d once thought were only words, all the half-remembered lines from old sermons she’d never paid attention to, wrapping her in a vague, heavy sense that some avenging power was watching her. The brilliant future she’d dreamed of, the freedom she was sure she would carve out for herself in marriage, the escape from the dull smallness of her girlhood—all of it was right there, within reach, but it felt like food that had been touched by sacrilege, something she’d have to snatch at while her hands shook with fear.

In the dark quiet, her usual boldness couldn’t fight off that first wave of dread. The face of that unhappy woman Mrs. Glasher, and her little children, kept replaying behind her eyes, clinging to her memory like a spot of disgrace, until every other thought faded away, leaving only the knowledge that she was letting those messy, ugly secrets become part of her own life. Her long wakefulness felt like a half-delirium, until a thin, pale streak of light seeped past the curtains, and the room grew chill. She could not bear it another second.

“Mamma!” she called, her voice small.

“Yes, my love,” Mrs. Davilow answered immediately, already awake.

“Let me come to you.”

She stumbled across the hall, and was asleep in minutes, curled up on her mother’s shoulder, and slept late until she dreamed she was waltzing in a glittering ballroom, and opened her eyes to see her mother standing by the bed, holding a small wrapped package.

“I’m sorry to wake you, darling, but I thought you should have this at once. Grandcourt’s groom brought Criterion—he rode a second horse, and says he’s to stay here for you.”

Gwendolen sat up, tearing open the paper. Inside lay a delicate enameled casket, holding a brilliant diamond betrothal ring, and a short note folded around a slip of colored paper:
*Wear this ring when I come at noon today, as a sign we are promised to each other. I’ve enclosed a check made out to Mr. Gascoigne to cover all your immediate expenses. Of course Mrs. Davilow will stay on at Offendene, at least for the foreseeable future. I hope when I arrive you will have picked an early date for us to marry, so you may begin to command me from a much shorter distance.
Yours, devotedly,
H. M. Grandcourt*

The check was made out for five hundred pounds. Gwendolen held it out to her mother alongside the letter.

“How very kind, how thoughtful,” Mrs. Davilow said, her voice warm with feeling. “But I truly would rather not be dependent on a son-in-law. I and the girls could get along very comfortably on our own.”

“Mamma, if you say that one more time, I won’t marry him,” Gwendolen snapped, her eyes flashing.

“My dear child, I do hope you aren’t only marrying for my sake,” Mrs. Davilow said, soft and apologetic.

Gwendolen tossed her head away on the pillow, leaving the ring lying on the sheets. She was furious that her mother was trying to take her reason for this marriage away from her, and deep down, she knew she wasn’t only marrying to save her family from poverty: she was pulled to this marriage by all sorts of urges far stronger than that, urges no small protest from her mother could stop. She woke to the full, bright light of day, and knew there was no going back. All the ugly visions, all the midnight alarms, would look far weaker when the sun was high.

“All I want is your happiness, my love,” Mrs. Davilow said gently. “I won’t say anything to vex you. Won’t you put the ring on?”

Gwendolen was quiet for a few minutes, turning the thought over in her head. Finally she sat up, the same fierce determination she used to spur herself on when she mounted a skittish horse, no matter what wild fancies were running through her head.

“I always thought the lover was supposed to slip the betrothal ring on himself,” she said, laughing, as she slid the brilliant onto her finger and tilted her head to admire the sparkle. “I know why he sent it ahead,” she added, nodding at her mother, conspiratorial.

“Why?”

“He’d rather I put it on myself than beg me for permission to do it. Aha! He’s very proud. But so am I. We’ll match each other perfectly. I’d hate a man who groveled on his knees, fawning all over me. He really isn’t disgusting at all.”

“That’s very faint praise for your future husband, Gwen.”

“No, it’s very high praise for a man,” Gwendolen said gaily. “Now I’m getting up to dress. Come do my hair for me, mamma dear,” she went on, pulling her mother’s face down to kiss her cheeks, “and stop being so silly about living in poverty. You have to let me make you comfortable, even if you don’t like it. Mr. Grandcourt is behaving perfectly, isn’t he?”

“Certainly he is,” Mrs. Davilow said, encouraged, fully convinced now that Gwendolen was truly fond of her betrothed. She’d always thought a suitor had to be judged by his station and his standing in polite society; it was so hard to know much else about a man, after all. All her anxiety was for Gwendolen’s mood, not Grandcourt’s character.

That mood shifted fast once she was awake and moving around. Halfway through doing her riding habit up, she was pulling every scrap of common sense she had together to justify her choice. The thing she kept coming back to, over and over, was the firm promise to herself: once she was Grandcourt’s wife, she would make him be as generous as possible to Mrs. Glasher’s children.

“What good would it do her if I refused to marry him? He could have married her years ago if he’d wanted to. He didn’t. Maybe she’s the one to blame for that. There’s so much about her I don’t know. He must have been good to her, in his way, otherwise she’d never have wanted to marry him.”

But that argument felt flimsy almost as soon as she thought it. Of course Mrs. Glasher didn’t want another woman’s children standing between her own son and the inheritance. That made sense. So Gwendolen found another way to make it all fair: “Maybe Grandcourt and I will never have children. I hope we never do. Then he can leave the whole estate to her pretty little boy. My uncle said Mr. Grandcourt can do whatever he likes with the land anyway, and once Sir Hugo Mallinger dies, there’ll be more than enough money for everyone.”

Suddenly Mrs. Glasher seemed unreasonable for demanding her son be the sole heir. Gwendolen Harleth, with all her sharp pride and her good intentions, would never wrong her. Gwendolen had gone her whole life thinking she was almost blameless, that all the fault in the world belonged to other people.

It was remarkable how fast her midnight revulsion at Grandcourt’s past faded, how small that old repugnance had become next to her urgent need to tell herself she was doing no harm at all. The midnight terror that she was crossing some invisible line into wickedness had dulled all her outrage at his choices. She thought of him, no matter what secrets he held, as a man she would soon hold nearly unlimited power over. Loving him had never been part of her plan, so any charm he had was just a nice bonus. Gwendolen had no idea that marriage could hold forces no one could manage, no matter how clever you were: she saw it as a game she would easily win, a role where she got to reign. She even found herself making excuses for him, telling herself he was no worse than any other man of his class, and making little plans to learn just what sort of behavior was considered normal and acceptable for gentlemen of his station.

Her hair was soon pinned perfectly for riding, and she stepped out in her habit, eager to get on horseback. She wanted the wind whipping through her hair, the thrill of a hard gallop, the old reckless daring she’d always carried with her back. The weight on her chest felt lighter already: in the bright daylight, moving fast, doubting her choice was far less oppressive than the alternative, that shabby, small life of servitude and insignificance she’d been trapped in before.

“Go back up and make yourself look like a duchess, mamma,” she said, turning on the stairs suddenly. “Put your best point-lace shawl over your head. I won’t have you looking meek and humble, not when you’re the mother of the lady of Ryelands.”

When Grandcourt arrived, he lifted her left hand gently, his eyes fixed on the diamond on her finger. “It was so good of you to think of everything and send that packet,” Gwendolen said, her tone soft and serious.

“Will you tell me if there’s anything I ever forget?” he said, holding her hand softly in his. “I will do absolutely anything you wish.”

“But I’m very unreasonable when it comes to what I want,” Gwendolen smiled.

“Yes. I expect that. All women are.”

“Then I won’t be unreasonable at all,” she said, pulling her hand away and tossing her head saucily. “I won’t be lumped in with every other woman on earth.”

“I didn’t say that,” Grandcourt replied, his usual quiet gravity softening a little as he looked at her. “You are no other woman. You are the only one who matters.”

“And what is that supposed to mean, exactly?” Gwendolen asked, stepping a few feet away from him, a playful little challenging glint in her eye.

Grandcourt paused, for a long, deliberate beat, before he spoke. “You are the woman I love.”

“Oh, what lovely, smooth speeches!” Gwendolen laughed. The sharp, odd twinge she’d felt before, the thought that he’d once said those very same words to another woman under far stranger circumstances, was already fading, growing familiar.

“Give me a nice speech back,” he said. “Tell me when we’re to be married.”

“Not yet. Not until we’ve had a full gallop over the downs. I’m so thirsty for that ride, I can’t think of anything else. I wish the hunting season would start. Sunday the twentieth, twenty-seventh, Monday, Tuesday…” Gwendolen counted off the days on her fingers, nodding prettily as she looked up at him, before sweeping one palm over the other triumphantly. “It starts in ten days!”

“Then let’s be married in ten days,” Grandcourt said. “Then we won’t have to fuss over the stables, or the hunt schedules, at all.”

“What do all women say when a man asks them that?” Gwendolen teased, mischievous.

“They say yes,” Grandcourt replied, caught a little off guard.

“Then I say no,” Gwendolen laughed, pulling her gauntlets on, her eyes bright with fun.

The moment was perfect for both of them. A cruder, more overeager lover would have ruined it all, grabbing for clumsy, unwanted kisses, spoiling the quiet, delicious tension of their little game. But Grandcourt loved the drama of it, the dance, and Gwendolen was free to play at being his queen, her spirits rising higher by the minute. Even Klesmer, the harsh, unimpressed music master, might have thought she had far better luck at her new life if he’d seen her like this, acting her natural bright self instead of hamming up some

“Surely whoever speaks to me in the right voice, him or her I shall follow. As the water follows the moon, silently, with fluid steps anywhere around the globe.” —WALT WHITMAN.

“Diplow’s full of my cousins right now,” Grandcourt said, lazy and low from his perch on the Offendene drawing-room sofa, one elbow slung over the back, the other hand tucked between his crossed knees like he had all the time in the world just to watch her. “Will you go there tomorrow? The carriage can come for Mrs. Davilow. Tell me exactly what you want moved or fixed in the rooms here—we need to get everything in decent order before we leave for Ryelands. And tomorrow’s the only free day we’ve got.”

Gwendolen had never tolerated needlework in her life, but ever since their engagement she’d taken to white embroidery with a ferocious, performative zeal that would have looked entirely unconvincing to anyone who bothered to count the messy, knotted false stitches scattered across her work. The last eight or nine days had mostly been spent galloping side by side on horseback, but they’d always carved out long stretches of this slower, trickier companionship—one Gwendolen had surprised herself by finding entirely pleasant. She thought she had the measure of Grandcourt perfectly. His drawling answers to all her bright, curious questions about his travels and his past life suited her down to the ground. From their very first meeting she’d noticed he always knew exactly what to say, and he radiated that quiet, unshakable certainty that all folly belonged to everyone else, not him. A man who has always gotten every good thing he ever wanted wields that soft, lazy disdain for other people’s choices like a crown.

His lovemaking, too, had never crossed the line into anything cloying or demanding—nothing more than a soft, wafted perfume of rose-scented admiration that fed every part of her vanity. Only once had he gone too far, leaning in to press a kiss just below her ear on the curve of her neck. Gwendolen had jumped up so fast the floorboards creaked, her agitation so obvious he’d scrambled to his feet right after her. “I beg your pardon—did I annoy you?” “Oh, it was nothing,” she’d laughed, breathless, suddenly terrified of her own reaction, “only I can’t bear being kissed under my ear.” She’d sat back down, her heart hammering with a strange, unnameable fear, because she could no longer snap and flout him the way she’d cast poor Rex aside. Grandcourt had taken that small show of reserve as a compliment, and never dared cross that line again.

That day a light, persistent rain had spoiled their ride, but the consolation had been a heavy package delivered from London, stuffed full of the beautiful, expensive gifts Grandcourt had ordered for her, spread across every drawing-room table for her and her mother to admire. Mrs. Davilow had just stepped out of the room after gushing over every pearl and silk and lace piece, and Gwendolen let her embroidery fall into her lap, tipping her head with that familiar, playful perversity that always made Grandcourt lean in closer. “Why is tomorrow the only day we can go to Diplow?”

“Because the next day’s the first meet with the hounds,” he said.

“And after that?”

“After that I’ve got to nip away for a couple of days. It’s a bore, but I’ll go one day and come back the next.” He caught the flicker of unhappiness that crossed her face, pulled his hand out from under his knee, and laid it over hers. “You don’t want me to go?”

“It’s no use objecting,” Gwendolen said, cold, fighting so hard to hold back the words bubbling up in her throat—the words that would say she suspected exactly who he was running off to see, the secret woman and children she’d only heard whispers of from far away.

“Of course it’s use,” Grandcourt said, wrapping his fingers tight around hers. “I’ll put the whole trip off. I can travel through the night, so I’m only gone for a single day.” He thought he understood what had sparked this little bit of pique, and this small, willful show of temper made her feel more desirable to him than ever.

“Don’t put it off, then,” Gwendolen said, enjoying the small, sharp thrill of knowing she could command him, using that peremptory edge to let out the irritation she couldn’t name. “Travel at night instead.”

“So you’ll come to Diplow tomorrow?”

“Oh, yes, if you want it,” she said, her voice lifted in bright, careless assent, so wrapped up in her own tangled thoughts she didn’t even notice he was still holding her hand.

“How you treat us poor devils of men,” Grandcourt murmured, his tone dropping low and warm. “We always end up getting the worse of the bargain with you.”

“Are you?” Gwendolen asked, looking at him softer, more naive than she ever had before. She ached to believe that light, teasing line was the whole truth—if she was the one calling all the shots, then she didn’t have to feel guilty for anything. If she knew every secret, Mrs. Glasher and her children would look far more to blame than Grandcourt ever could. “Do you always get the worst of it?”

“Always. Are you half as kind to me as I am to you?” Grandcourt asked, his narrow, steady gaze locked on her eyes.

Gwendolen felt like she’d been struck still. She was suddenly, overwhelmingly conscious of all she’d already taken from him, and that bright sense of command melted away to nothing. She looked around her, and realized there was no path back. It was as if she’d already climbed up into a chariot, and someone else held the reins—proud as she was, she couldn’t throw herself off in full view of the whole watching world. She hadn’t said yes to him in ignorance, and any complaint she made now would only be a confession that she’d known exactly what she was getting into from the start. She had no right left to demand explanations. All she could do now was adjust, so the sharp spikes of guilt pricking at her wouldn’t tear her apart. She shivered a little inside, then deliberately shifted her whole frame of mind, the small pause stretching out before she broke into a bright, teasing smile. “If I was as kind to you as you are to me, I’d ruin all your generosity. It wouldn’t feel half as grand as it does now, when I let you spoil me.”

“So I don’t get even one kiss?” Grandcourt asked, perfectly happy to pay whatever price she named for this new, soft kind of courtship that made marriage feel like something bright and new, not a weight to carry.

“Not one?” Gwendolen laughed, saucy, nodding at him in playful defiance.

He lifted her small left hand to his lips, then released it, gentle and respectful. Calling him not disgusting was far too faint praise—he was almost charming. For a wild, dizzy second she thought she would never have loved any other man better than this one. His quiet, unhurried reticence wrapped around her like something warm, inexplicable and delicious.

“Speaking of Diplow,” she said, picking her embroidery back up, “is anyone there besides Captain and Mrs. Torrington? You’re not leaving them all alone tête-à-tête, are you? I expect he only talks to her while he smokes his cigars, and she only answers by adjusting her chignon.”

“She’s got her sister staying with her,” Grandcourt said, the faint ghost of a smile playing across his face, “and two other men besides. One of them you already know, I think.”

“Ah, then I already think poorly of him,” Gwendolen said, shaking her head.

“You saw him at Leubronn. Young Deronda, the fellow who’s with the Mallingers.”

Gwendolen’s heart did a sudden, wild little flip, and her fingers went cold around her embroidery hoop, though she fought to keep her hands steady.

“I never spoke to him,” she said, dreading that he’d see how rattled she was. “Isn’t he disagreeable?”

“No, not particularly,” Grandcourt drawled, in his laziest, most dismissive tone. “Thinks a little too highly of himself. I thought someone must have introduced you two.”

“No. Someone told me his name the night before I left, that’s all. What is he, exactly?”

“Some sort of ward of Sir Hugo Mallinger’s. Nothing of any real consequence.”

“Oh, poor creature,” Gwendolen said, the words slipping out unthinkingly, no trace of sarcasm in them. “How unpleasant that must be for him.” She stood up quickly, walking to the window. “I wonder if the rain’s stopped.”

The next day was bright and dry, to her relief, and Gwendolen rode to Diplow on her horse Criterion, the same way she had that day she’d come back from Leubronn in the carriage with her mother. She always felt bolder, sharper, more confident in her riding dress—certain she looked her absolute best, a comfort for any meeting that felt even a little bit intimidating. All her old anger at Deronda had curdled into a strange, superstitious dread, born of the way he’d wormed his way into her thoughts when she’d least expected it. She couldn’t shake the feeling that this man who’d corrected her at the roulette table was bound to leave some mark on her future sooner or later. Superstition is always born of that sharp, intense focus on the self that makes an evening star feel like a threat, and a beggar’s blessing feel like a promise of luck—they carry enough weight on their own to make their own forebodings come true.

The time before lunch slipped past as she wandered through Diplow’s rooms with Mrs. Torrington and her mother, and she told herself firmly that if she saw Deronda at all, they’d only exchange a single, distant bow. She intended to pay him as little mind as possible.

But the second they were both in the same room, an inward compulsion too strong for her pride took over. Everything else around her faded to automatic, habitual small talk, and all she could do was notice him.

When he sat down at the lunch table, Grandcourt spoke up. “Deronda, Miss Harleth tells me you two never got introduced properly at Leubronn?”

“Miss Harleth hardly remembers me, I imagine,” Deronda said, looking at her simply, straightforward, as they bowed to each other. “She was far too busy to pay me any mind the times I saw her.”

Did he really think she’d forgotten the man who’d slipped her the money to redeem her lost necklace?

“On the contrary,” Gwendolen said, her voice a little unsteady even as she forced herself to meet his gaze, “I remember you very well. You didn’t approve of me playing roulette.”

“What gave you that idea?” Deronda asked, grave and quiet.

“Oh, you cast an evil eye on my game,” Gwendolen laughed, tilting her head. “I started losing the second you came over to watch. I’d been winning nonstop right up until then.”

“Roulette in a hovel like Leubronn is a total bore,” Grandcourt put in.

“I only found it a bore once I started losing,” Gwendolen said, smiling over at Grandcourt as she spoke, but darting a quick sidelong glance at Deronda. She caught his eyes fixed on her, that gaze so gentle and penetrating it cut sharper than Klesmer’s brutal, unvarnished judgment of her singing. She snapped her head around fast, pretending to listen to the rest of the table’s conversation, while every part of her was still fixed on Deronda. There was something about his face, his soft, quiet intensity, that made her feel like all her own little rules and standards were somehow out of alignment. His deep voice, which she was hearing for the first time, felt like the low, thrumming note of a violoncello, compared to Grandcourt’s toneless daily drawl—like the sound of real music next to the lazy cluck of farmyard fowl in drowsy afternoon sunshine. Grandcourt was probably right, she told herself, that Deronda thought too highly of himself: it was the easiest, most common way to explain a superiority that made you feel small.

The conversation drifted on to rinderpest and Jamaica. Grandcourt declared the Jamaican negroes were a brutish, baptist race of Calibans; Deronda said he’d always felt a deep sympathy for Caliban, who had his own perfectly valid point of view, and could probably sing a much better song than the men who enslaved him. Mrs. Davilow mentioned her father had once owned an estate in Barbadoes, though she’d never set foot in the West Indies herself; Mrs. Torrington insisted she’d never sleep a wink in her bed if she lived surrounded by Black people; her husband corrected her, saying half-breeds were the only real trouble; and Deronda pointed out plainly that the white colonists had only themselves to blame for the half-breed population that bothered them.

Gwendolen poked idly at her jelly while the rest of them bickered, glancing at every speaker in turn so she could feel justified in letting her eyes rest on Deronda whenever she wanted.

What did he really think of her, she wondered. He must have cared about her, at least a little, or he never would have gone out of his way to return her necklace. What did he think of her upcoming marriage? What strange, soft convictions did he hold that made him so grave about everything? Why had he come to Diplow at all? The questions looped over and over in her head, a restless, hungry longing to be looked at by Deronda and seen as entirely, unreservedly admirable— a longing that had taken root the very first time he’d fixed that critical, steady gaze on her at the roulette table. Why did she care so much about the opinion of a man who was “nothing of any consequence”? She didn’t have time to unpack the answer. She was far too busy caring.

Back in the drawing room later, when Grandcourt was called away on some errand, Gwendolen walked over to Deronda entirely on impulse, no plan in her head, finding him standing at a side table turning over a book of prints. “Are you going to hunt tomorrow, Mr. Deronda?”

“Yes, I believe I am.”

“So you don’t object to hunting, then?”

“I make excuses for it,” he said, a faint smile touching his lips. “It’s a vice I’m happy to indulge in, whenever I can’t get out boating or playing cricket.”

“Do you object to *me* hunting?” Gwendolen asked, lifting her chin in that saucy, defiant little way of hers.

“I have no right to object to anything you choose to do.”

“But you thought you had a right to object to my gambling,” Gwendolen pressed on.

“I was sorry to see you losing at it. I never said a word to you about any objection I had,” Deronda said, his gaze steady and direct, that wide-eyed gravity holding no trace of judgment or malice. His eyes had that rare, dark, soft intensity that seemed to shine with a particular, gentle interest in every single person he looked at— the kind of look that made people who were lonely, or troubled, reach out to him for things he never even knew he could offer. It was wrapping around Gwendolen right now, warm and unshakable.

“You stopped me from gambling forever,” she said. No sooner had the words left her mouth than she blushed bright red all over her face and neck, and Deronda blushed too, both of them suddenly conscious of that quiet, secret, generous act with the necklace that had been a bold, almost improper liberty for a stranger to take.

There was nothing more they could say to each other. Gwendolen turned on her heel and walked to the window, half mortified that she’d blurted out something she never meant to say, and half deliriously happy that they now shared this quiet, unspoken secret between them. Deronda didn’t mind it at all. Gwendolen seemed far more vivid, far more desirable than she had back at Leubronn: the quiet, tangled fight she’d been waging with her own choices for weeks had woken something new in her, a soft new soul that held both brighter possibilities and sharper pains than her old, arrogant, untested self-confidence had ever known.

That night, back at the rented house they were staying in, Mrs. Davilow turned to her, curious. “Was it really true, or just a silly joke, that Mr. Deronda spoiled your run of luck at the roulette table, Gwen?”

“Oh, it was just a coincidence that he was standing there right when I started losing,” Gwendolen said, offhand. “I noticed him, that’s all.”

“I’m not surprised,” Mrs. Davilow said. “He’s such a striking young man. He reminds me of those old Italian Renaissance paintings. You can tell without anyone saying a word that there’s foreign blood in his veins.”

“Is there?” Gwendolen asked.

“Mrs. Torrington told me. I asked after him specifically, and she said his mother was some foreign noblewoman, very high rank.”

“His mother?” Gwendolen asked, sharp and sudden. “Then who was his father?”

“Well, everyone says he’s Sir Hugo Mallinger’s son, even though he’s officially just his ward. They say if Sir Hugo could have done whatever he liked with his estates, he would have left all of it to Mr. Deronda, since he has no legitimate sons of his own.”

Gwendolen went completely silent, and her mother saw the look that crossed her face, and immediately regretted repeating the gossip. She hated to fill her daughter’s head with all this ugly knowledge of the world, and wished she’d never said a word.

Gwendolen could see the unknown mother perfectly in her mind’s eye—dark-eyed, almost certainly sad, the kind of woman society writes off entirely. The crayon portrait of Sir Hugo hanging at Diplow looked nothing like Deronda, and that dark-eyed, forgotten woman settled into Gwendolen’s thoughts like a ghost.

Later that night, curled up in her small bed with only a dim candle burning by the nightstand, she spoke out to her mother in the dark. “Mamma, do men usually have children before they get married?”

“No, dear, no,” Mrs. Davilow said, startled. “Why ever would you ask that?”

“If that’s the sort of thing that happens, I ought to know about it,” Gwendolen said, sharp with sudden indignation.

“You’re thinking of that story I told you about Mr. Deronda

No penitence and no confessional, No priest ordains it, yet they’re forced to sit Amid deep ashes of their vanished years.

Picture a rambling, patched old house, its finest walls hewn from soft gray stone and capped with weathered red tile, a round turret jabbing out from one corner, its dark conical roof crowned with a whirling weathercock that glows warm in summer sunlight or stands sharp against the heavy, snow-heavy winter clouds. Broad spreading trees cast its grounds in dappled shade: one great gnarled oak towers at the side, a stand of bare-branched Scotch firs clinging to a broken bank behind it with roots exposed like knuckled fingers, and beyond them, a rookery chattering at dusk. The other edge of the house overlooks a dark pool fringed with brambles, where wild fowl flutter and shriek, and all around stretches a wide meadow so large it passes for a park, ringed with old planted woodland and guarded by squat stone lodges that look like tiny, barred prisons.

Outside the iron gates, the once soft, green rural countryside has turned black, blighted by coal mines, the roads caked in coal dust, the spoil heaps rising like dark funeral mounds draped in perpetual mourning. The district’s streets are filled with colliers, each with a candle stuck in the brim of his hat, their faces smudged dark as devils’—a sight that filled the four beautiful Glasher children, who had lived secluded at Gadsmere for three years, with quiet, childish suspicion. It was mid-November now, the flowerbeds bare, the trees stripped of every leaf, the pool shivering black under gray skies, and the whole place would have felt unbearably somber if not for the four small children chasing their dogs across the gravel drive, their high laughter cutting through the hush.

But Lydia Glasher liked Gadsmere as well as she might like any home these days. The total seclusion, guaranteed by the ugly, uninviting mining country surrounding them, was exactly what she craved. When she drove her two ponies harnessed to a waggonet full of children, she never ran into fine ladies in carriages, only sharp-faced businessmen in gigs. At church, there were no eyes she feared: the curate and his wife either knew nothing of her past or chose to ignore it, seeing her simply as a quiet widowed lady, the tenant of Gadsmere. The grand name of Grandcourt meant nothing here, far less than the names of Fletcher and Gawcome, the men who leased the local collieries.

Ten years had passed since the scandal of the Irish officer’s beautiful young wife eloping with the heir Grandcourt, followed by a farcical duel where the two men’s bullets never came close to grazing anyone. Most folks who remembered the affair long ago assumed Grandcourt had finally freed himself from the disreputable connection, that the foolish woman who’d run off with him had sunk even lower in the world, after abandoning her little son to leave with her husband. No one spoke of Lydia Glasher now any more than they spoke of the forgotten victim of a ten-year-old manslaughter trial. She was a lost ship no one bothered to launch a rescue mission for, while Grandcourt was still seen sailing proudly into harbor, all flags flying, pronounced perfectly seaworthy.

But Grandcourt had never untangled himself from Lydia. His passion for her had been the strongest, longest-lasting he’d ever known, and even now, when that passion was as dead as the music from a cracked, discarded flute, it left a slow, dull lingering fondness. When her husband died three years before, he’d half-heartedly toyed with the idea of marrying her, just as they’d often promised each other back in the wild early days of their affair. Back then, he’d have happily paid to arrange a divorce, but Lydia’s husband had refused to cooperate: he had no interest in remarrying, and even less interest in parading his own terrible domestic habits in open court.

Lydia’s feelings, meanwhile, had shifted entirely in the opposite direction. At the start of their elopement, she’d barely cared about the possibility of marriage. She’d escaped five years of brutal, rude treatment at the hands of her first husband, and the sheer bliss of being adored by a young, handsome, wealthy lover who’d seen every corner of the world was enough for her. She was passionate, vivacious, starved for affection, and the thrill of freedom had quieted every other anxiety. An ambiguous, unmarried position hadn’t bothered her back then; she’d never envied the hollow honors of a ignored, dull wife. The only crack in her perfect new world was the memory of the three-year-old little boy she’d left behind, who died two years later—his first soft “mamma” sounding nothing like the voices of the children she’d had after running away.

But years had changed everything, eroding the soft curves of her cheeks and throat, and now marrying Grandcourt was her entire, all-consuming desire. The once irrelevant ambiguous position was now harming her children, whom she loved with a devotion stoked hot by the burning need to atone for her past. She had no other penitence. If Grandcourt married her, her children would suffer no stigma. They would see their mother stand in a dignified, respected position, and no one in the world could look down on them. Her son could be named his father’s rightful heir. That yearning was the only thing that mattered to her now; her love for Grandcourt had long since softened into sharp, constant anxiety that he would give her the permanent, unimpeachable status of his wife. She expected no other happiness from marriage than the satisfaction of her maternal love and pride, the pride of holding her head high in front of her own children. She had schooled herself for years to never badger him with passionate pleas, never make a scene, because she’d learned early that Grandcourt turned hard, unyielding, the more anyone begged him. All those withheld sharp words, all that smothered rage, had been slowly curdling into venom. She was completely dependent on him; though he’d always been generous with their expenses, every penny he spent was a voluntary favor, never a right. Even if he never married her, she’d told herself, he might never have a legitimate son, and their boy could still inherit the bulk of his vast estates. Even Grandcourt’s quiet right-hand man Lush had once said he’d bet on Lydia marrying his master as soon as any other possible outcome. But when Gwendolen Harleth had appeared out of nowhere, derailing his plans to court Miss Arrowpoint, Lydia had leaped eagerly into Lush’s scheme to warn the new girl away from Grandcourt, letting a little of that stored-up venom finally loose.

After that, Lush had written to say Gwendolen had left town, and the threat was gone for good. No one had written to tell her the threat had come back, and was now a certainty. She’d written to Grandcourt as usual, but he’d taken far longer than normal to reply. She’d dared to hope, as only desperate, strong wishing can make you, that the sting of that failed earlier courtship would push him easily back into the old promise of marrying her.

Grandcourt had two unshakable purposes driving him on his journey to Gadsmere that day. First, he would break the news of his impending marriage in person, to make this first, messy hurdle final. Second, he would take back the diamonds he’d long ago given Lydia to wear, diamonds he’d decided belonged on the neck of his new wife. They weren’t some legendary mountain of light, just a set of modest earrings, necklace, and hair jewels worth several thousand pounds, but Grandcourt was determined to have them back. The last time he’d asked her to return them, claiming they’d be safer locked in a bank vault, Lydia had refused calmly, absolutely, and finally told him, “If you ever marry another woman, I will give them up to her. Are you going to marry another woman?” Back then, he’d had no reason to push the issue. He’d always held back from throwing his weight around to hurt or cow Lydia, the last lingering trace of the power she’d once held over him, the last faint ghost of that old passion that had once made life feel bright. But new novelty, the thrill of chasing Gwendolen, had woken him up now, and it pushed him to cast that old softness aside.

Lydia was seated in her favorite morning room, the four children clustered around her.
