The square bay window looked out over broad gravel and grass sloping down to a tiny brook that fed the black pool. The top of a low black oak cabinet, the old dining table, the scuffed tawny leather chairs were all littered with toys, storybooks, and the children’s messy garden coats, while a pastel portrait of a smiling, indulgent maternal lady looked down on the chaos from the wall. The three little girls, barely older than toddlers to nine years old, were exact miniature copies of Lydia: dark-eyed, delicate-featured brunettes with rich rose bloom on their cheeks, their small noses and eyebrows so perfectly formed they looked like tiny grown women. Their little boy Henleigh sat cross-legged on the carpet some distance away, his blonde head bent over a set of Noah’s ark animals, lecturing each one in a stern, commanding voice, occasionally licking the spotted toy beasts to check if their paint would smudge. The eldest girl Josephine was having her French lesson, while the other two sat demurely with their dolls in their laps, as still as Madonna statues.

Lydia had dressed with careful, deliberate effort that morning, as she did every day now, telling herself Grandcourt might walk through the door at any moment. Her fine profile, the crisp curves of her dark hair, her sharp, distinct eyebrows still made her head look striking, rising above her bronze silk and velvet gown and the old gold necklace Grandcourt had clasped around her neck all those years ago. It wasn’t vanity that made her fuss over her appearance; when she looked in the mirror, all she saw was how much she had changed, how worn she’d grown. But she would hold onto every scrap of the beauty she had left. If her greatest wish came true, she knew she would bloom again, growing into the dignified, handsome matron fit to stand at his side. Her children’s faces, exact copies of her own younger self, reminded her of the soft, blooming curves she’d lost, but the children kissed her pale sunken cheeks all the same, and never thought them anything but perfect. Their love was the whole point of her life now.

Suddenly, Lydia pulled her gaze away from Josephine’s lesson book and tilted her head, listening. “Hush, dear! I think someone’s coming.”

Henleigh jumped up immediately. “Mamma! Is it the miller bringing my new donkey?”

He got no answer, and tugged insistently at her knee to repeat his question, just as the door swung open, and the servant announced Mr. Grandcourt. Lydia rose to her feet, trembling with agitation. Henleigh scowled, furious that it was not the miller, and the three little girls lifted their dark eyes to Grandcourt shyly, none of them caring much for this strange man who visited their mother. When Grandcourt took Lydia’s hand, then leaned down to rest his other hand on Henleigh’s head, the little boy began beating his arm away with small, furious fists. The girls submitted bashfully to a light pat under the chin and a quick kiss, but it was clear the best course was to send them all out to the garden, where they were soon dancing and chattering with the dogs on the gravel.

“How far have you ridden?” Lydia asked, as Grandcourt set down his hat and overcoat.

“From Diplow,” he drawled slowly, seating himself across from her and looking at her with that detached, unnoticing gaze that made her skin prickle.

“Then you must be tired.”

“Not at all. I rested at the junction—hideous little hole. Railway journeys are always a confounded bore, but I had coffee and smoked.” He pulled out his handkerchief, rubbed his face, then stared down at his crossed knee and spotless polished boot, as if a total stranger sat across from him, not the woman quivering with suspense, every word and look of his tipping the scale between her greatest hope and worst dread. The difference between them was stark: Lydia, worn thin by years of sharp, desperate dependence, and Grandcourt, dulled into a thick, unyielding stubbornness.

“I expected you,” she said, her voice quick and sharp, the tone matching her fine features like the tight strings of a violin match its carved wood. “It’s been so long since I heard from you. The weeks drag so much slower at Gadsmere than they do at Diplow.”

“Quite,” he said, plucking idly at his whisker, not looking at her at first. “But you got the money I paid into your bank account, didn’t you?”

“Oh, yes,” she said shortly, tingling with impatience. He had never been this distant with her before, or at least she had never noticed it so sharply.

“Things have moved at quite a rattling pace for me lately, when usually they drag by slow as molasses. A lot has happened, as you know—” he finally lifted his eyes to meet hers.

“What do I know?” she snapped.

He paused a beat, his tone not shifting an inch. “That I was thinking of marrying. You saw Miss Harleth, didn’t you?”

“Did she tell you I spoke to her?” The pale skin of her cheeks went even whiter, lit from within by the fierce bright fire in her eyes.

“No. Lush told me,” he answered slowly. It felt as if the screws of a torture device were being set down, creeping, within sight of the waiting victim.

“Good God—just say it out, then. You’re going to marry her,” she burst out passionately, her knees shaking, her hands clenching tight until her knuckles went white.

“Of course something like this had to happen sooner or later, Lydia,” he said, now that the metaphorical screw was fully in place, not wanting to make the pain any sharper than he had to.

“You didn’t always think it was necessary.”

“Maybe not. I see it now.”

Those few low, calm words hit her like a slamming iron door, her thin fingers pushing uselessly against its solid surface. She knew exactly how helpless she was, and she shrank from making a fool of herself, begging to a man whose ears were already dead to her pleas, clinging to his dead knees only to stare at his unmoving, cold face. She didn’t weep, she didn’t speak, stunned by the sudden certainty that carried all the sick chill of a physical blow. For a long, breathless moment, the defeated, useless clutch of her dying hope made her feel like she was suffocating. Finally, she made a spasmodic effort to stand, and unconscious of everything but her own wretchedness, pressed her forehead hard against the cold glass of the window. The children playing on the gravel saw her, thought she was calling them, and ran up to press their sweet faces to the glass, staring up at her expectantly. It jolted her back to her senses. She shook her head at them, waved them away, and the effort was so painful she sank back into the nearest chair, gasping.

Grandcourt had stood up too. He was doubly annoyed: annoyed by the messy, emotional scene playing out in front of him, and annoyed that even all his imperious willpower couldn’t make it go away. But the task had to be finished, and he had to arrange everything so there would be as little future annoyance as possible. He leaned against the corner of the fireplace. Lydia looked up at him, her voice sharp with bitterness.

“All of this means nothing to you. Me and the children are just burdensome, importunate creatures. You can’t wait to get back to Miss Harleth.”

“Don’t make this more disagreeable than it has to be, Lydia. There’s no use harping on things that can’t be changed. It’s deucedly unpleasant for me to see you making yourself miserable. I came all this way to tell you what you have to make up your mind to. You and the children will be provided for exactly as before, and that’s an end to it.”

Silence hung heavy in the room. She dared not speak. This woman with the bright, hungry eyes had the iron of a mother’s anguish driven deep into her soul, and it gave her a harder, stonier repression than any screaming or struggle could manage. Underneath that still surface, though, rage and vindictiveness lashed out. She wished this new marriage would make two people as wretched as she was.

After a minute, Grandcourt spoke again. “It will be better for you in the long run. You can stay right here at Gadsmere. Later on, I’ll settle a very large sum on you and the children, so you’ll be able to live wherever you like. You’ll never have anything to complain of. You’ll be completely secure, no matter what happens. Nothing could be arranged beforehand, everything has happened very quickly.”

He finished his slow, flat speech. He didn’t expect her to thank him, but he thought it was reasonable for her to be at least somewhat contented, as much as Lydia could ever be contented. She showed no sign of shifting, and after another minute he added, “You never had any reason to fear I’d be illiberal. I don’t care a curse about the money.”

“If you did care about it, I suppose you wouldn’t give any of it to us,” Lydia said. The sarcasm slipped out before she could stop it.

“That’s a devilishly unfair thing to say,” Grandcourt replied, his voice dropping low. “I’d advise you not to say that sort of thing ever again.”

“Are you going to punish me by letting the children starve in beggary?” The little crack in her self-control had let another spurt of venom out.

“There’s no question of that,” Grandcourt said, still in that low, dangerous tone. “I advise you not to say things you’ll regret later.”

“I’m used to repenting,” she said, bitterly. “Maybe *you* will repent. You already repented of loving me.”

“All this is only going to make it uncommonly hard for us to ever meet again. What other friend do you have besides me?”

“Quite true.” The words came out as a soft, ragged moan. At that exact moment, a thought flashed through her head: after Grandcourt had promised himself a happiness better than anything he’d ever had with her, he would be struck by such terrible loneliness and misery that he would come crawling back to her, chasing the memory of the days when he had been young, glad, and hopeful. But no. He would walk away scatheless. She was the one who had to suffer.

The scorching, sharp words

The day Gwendolen Harleth became Mrs. Grandcourt dawned soft and gilded, the low sun kissing a thin, sharp frost that crisped the edges of every leaf along the Pennicote lanes. Half the village had turned out to line the winding path to the church, craning their necks to catch a glimpse of the bridal party, and even the air hummed with the thrill of a match far grander than any the little parish had seen in a generation. The rector’s old friend had traveled in to perform the ceremony, while the rector himself walked Gwendolen down the aisle, stepping in for the father she had never known.

Every onlooker remarked that only two faces in the procession bore any trace of tears: Mrs. Davilow’s, her delicate lids pink and puffy as if she had sobbed half the night long, and quiet Anna’s, fidgeting with the lace of her bridesmaid’s gown. No one found the mother’s grief odd — to lose the daughter who had been the bright, unchallenged center of her whole world, especially after all the recent hardship of their near ruin, was a parting that would wring any heart. But no one could quite puzzle out why Anna looked so pale, when Gwendolen’s good fortune was lifting all their families’ worries at a stroke.

Everyone else glowed in the light of the occasion, none more so than the bride herself. The village agreed unanimously that her tall, proud carriage was fit for a lady of title, even if her cheeks lacked the soft rose bloom most folks expected of a wedding day. But the groom himself was pale, too — as the miller’s wife, Mrs. Girdle, pointed out to her 14-year-old daughter, he had the same sallow, quiet complexion as her own husband, so their match was all the more perfect. “He must be daft about her,” the village whispered. “Only hope he never flings it in her face how close she came to going off to work as a governess, and her ma moving into that tiny hovel at Sawyer’s Cottage — that’s a story we’ve all gossiped over for months.”

Her wide-eyed daughter protested that no high-born gentleman would ever treat his wife badly, but Mrs. Girdle huffed and set her straight. “Oh, child, men’s men, gentle or simple, they’re all much of a muchness. My own mother was lady’s maid up to Squire Pelton’s, and she saw him drag hounds and a long whip into his wife’s chambers just to frighten her half out of her wits, back in her day.”
“That’s ill luck to talk of on a wedding day, Mrs. Girdle,” the tailor cut in. “Quarrels might end with the whip, but they start with the tongue, and we all know the women have the lion’s share of that.”
“Lord gave it to us to use, I reckon,” Mrs. Girdle sniffed. “Never meant the men to have it all their own way.”
“Word from the groom’s groom up at Offendene,” the tailor went on, “is that Mr. Grandcourt barely speaks a word. Everything gets done just the way he wants it without him ever having to order a soul.”
“Then all the more whip, mark my words,” Mrs. Girdle grunted. “Our Gwendolen’s got plenty of tongue, that one. Look — there they are, coming out the church steps together!”

Gwendolen’s long brown eyes glowed that day with a strange, electric lustre, her step lighter, her bearing more erect than any of the villagers had ever seen it. It was the same sharp, heightened thrill she had felt that night at the gambling table, when Daniel Deronda had stood watching her and she had begun to lose, every nerve strung tight to a point where even uneasiness felt like a kind of euphoria. The quiet pricks of her conscience, the faint, nagging memory of the promise she had broken, she brushed them off as one might swat at a gnat mid-triumph — nothing was going to stand in the way of the ambition she had nurtured through all those humiliated weeks when she had been forced to hunt for work to feed her family.
She could not, that morning, have said she regretted marrying Grandcourt. No hazy fear of the future could dim the bright, glowing fact that she was the center of every eye, the object of every envy, about to step into a life of luxury and power she had dreamed of since she was a small girl. She did not want for quiet, half-suppressed dread: the knowledge she was doing something wrong, the certainty that the woman whose life she had upended was right now burning with resentment against her, the sharp intuition that Deronda — who had seen right through her greed at the roulette table — must despise her now for selling herself to this man, the creeping terror that the tie she had once held loosely in her hand was now being slung around her neck like a noose. All that roiled in her chest like yeasty, frothing wine, but she crushed it down under her boot with a giddy, exultant defiance. She was playing for the highest stakes at the game of life, and even if she lost, she would lose with grandeur, not the small, shabby, penurious fate that had been waiting for her if she had said no. She was not the tearful, trembling bride of storybooks — she was a girl drunk on the heady fumes of her own ego, walking blind through a garden of illusions, with only a faint, unnameable under-tug of awareness that she was a little unsteady, a little too intoxicated to be walking straight.

“Thank God you bear it so well, my darling,” Mrs. Davilow whispered, trembling all over, once she had helped Gwendolen step out of her white bridal gown and into her soft traveling dress. All the mother’s strength was spent on holding back her sobs, which only made Gwendolen lean into the performance of triumph harder.
“Why, you’d have fussed just as much if I was leaving to go be governess to Mrs. Mompert’s brats, you dear, sad old thing,” Gwendolen laughed, cupping her mother’s pale cheeks in her hands, then stepping back to spread out her arms and show off her new outfit. “Look at me! I’m Mrs. Grandcourt now! What else could I be but everything you ever wanted me to be? You were half mad with vexation a few months back, convinced I’d throw this all away, remember?”
“Hush, hush, child, for heaven’s sake,” Mrs. Davilow hissed, clutching her wrists. “It just tears me apart to part with you. But I will bear anything, anything at all, if only you are happy.”
“Not gladly, mamma,” Gwendolen teased, shaking her head with a bright, wobbly smile. “Sorrow’s your favorite sauce — you can’t take any good thing without a dash of it. But you’ll sit at home and gush over all my grand things, you’ll see: the big houses, the horses, the diamonds, the trips to court, all the titles we’ll climb to. And I’ll love you more than anyone else in the whole wide world, always.”
“My sweet girl — I won’t even be jealous if you love your husband more than me. He ought to be first in your life, now.”
Gwendolen made a playful, pouty grimace. “That’s a pretty ridiculous expectation. But I won’t treat him badly, not unless he deserves it.”
They clung to each other then, and for a second Gwendolen couldn’t hold back a stray tear, her voice cracking as she whispered, “I wish you were coming with me, mamma.” The faint damp on her long lashes only made her look more glowing when she stepped out to take Grandcourt’s hand and climb into the waiting carriage.

The rector stopped by to give her a final, solemn blessing, then turned back to Mrs. Davilow and said, half reassuring, half reverent: “Count your blessings, Fanny. She’s in a position far better than any of us ever dared hope for. Few women are ever chosen so wholly for their own sake. You are a happy mother.”

The train carried the newlyweds fifty miles west, and the bright morning sky had clouded over completely by the time they pulled into the little station near Ryelands. It was nearly twilight when their carriage turned through the park gates, and Gwendolen, leaning out the window, drank in every inch of the grand estate she now called hers: the long, winding drive lined with dark evergreens, backed by gnarled, ancient grey oak trunks, wide rolling grassy hills dotted with dark clumps of trees, until at last she spotted the grand white house, set against a wooded hill, the line of its terrace balustrade curving gently in the dim light.
She had chattered nonstop the whole train ride, bright and vivacious as a frisky kitten, ignoring every unspoken shift in their dynamic, turning every soft attempt Grandcourt made to take her hand into a playful, two-handed squeeze, too wired to sit still. Now, as they neared the house, her chatter died away unbidden, her heart thudding hard against her ribs. She could almost see the girl she had been at 14, daydreaming about living in a place like this, walking down long, grand corridors under painted ceilings, with servants who bowed and did exactly what she told them to, no one ever daring to point out that she wasn’t born a great lady. Was that all really hers now? The thrill tangled with a nameless, cold prickle of dread, the kind that comes when you’re about to open a door you’ve spent years dreaming of, and you can’t shake the feeling something terrible is waiting on the other side.
“Here we are, home at last,” Grandcourt said, and leaned in to kiss her softly on the lips. She hardly noticed, numb with the flood of new sensations, after the long, overexcited day, a soft, foggy numbness settling over her.

The hall blazed with lamplight, warm and full of thick carpets, full length portraits, marble statues, quiet attentive servants. Gwendolen’s new maid was led off by the housekeeper, and Grandcourt led her down a corridor scented with jasmine wax, to a little sitting room glowing with soft firelight.
“These are our private dens,” he told her. “Rest here a little while before dinner. We’ll dine early tonight.” He pressed her hand to his lips and left her, more hopelessly, tenderly in love than he had ever imagined he could be.

Gwendolen tossed off her hat and mantle, sank into a chair by the hearth, and saw her own reflection multiplied in the glass panels lining the walls, against soft pale green satin upholstery. The housekeeper hovered in the doorway for a moment, and Gwendolen, impatient to be alone, told her to send her maid Hudson in when she’d laid out her dinner gown, and that she needn’t wait around.
“Madam, there is a sealed packet here, Mr. Grandcourt’s orders that no one’s hands but yours should touch it, when you were alone,” the housekeeper said, stepping forward to set a small wrapped parcel on her lap. “The man who brought it said it was a wedding gift he’d arranged for you to get as a surprise, and that he wasn’t to know it had arrived till you wore it for dinner. I hope you’ll forgive me for lingering, madam, I wanted to make sure I handed it to you properly.”

The doors clicked shut behind her, and Gwendolen’s fingers curled around the packet. Of course — it was the diamonds Grandcourt had promised her, the set he had said was locked away for their wedding day. She’d been half hoping he’d give them to her, a little treat to play with, a bright, glittering diversion to break the strange, soft languor stealing over her.
She tore off the layers of paper, the velvet jewel case inside, and flipped it open. The diamonds blazed up at her, catching the firelight — but there was a letter, tucked right on top of the gems, in a handwriting she recognized instantly.
It was like an adder had risen up out of the velvet to strike her. Her heart gave a wild, hard leap that left her breathless, her hands shaking so hard the thin paper rattled as she unfolded it. The words burned into her eyes, sharp as if they had been printed in ink of fire:
*These diamonds, once given with all my young love to Lydia Glasher, are passed on now to you. You broke your word to me, to steal the life that was mine. You think you will be happy now, as I once was, think you will bear his beautiful children to push mine out of his sight. But God is too just for that. The heart of the man you married died a long time ago. His only real young love was mine. You cannot take that from me, even if you have stolen everything else. I am the grave where your chance of happiness is buried, right next to mine. You had your warning, and you chose to throw it all away to hurt me, to hurt my children. He was going to marry me, at last. He would have, if you had not broken your promise. You will get your punishment. I wish for it with every part of my soul. Will you run to show him this letter, to turn him against me, to ruin my children and me further? Will you stand before him tonight with these diamonds glinting on your neck, and carry my words in both your hearts? Do you think he will let you complain when he makes you miserable? You took him with your eyes wide open. The wrong you chose to do to me will be your curse.*

Gwendolen read the words over and over, as if trapped in a penance spell, until a wave of wild, terrified panic crashed over her. She lunged forward, hurling the letter straight into the roaring hearth. It caught fire instantly, curling up into ash before she could think twice, but the movement sent the jewel case slipping from her lap, the diamonds rolling across the rug. She didn’t even glance at them. She fell back into her chair, limp, helpless, her lips and hands trembling so hard she could not move. She sat there for what felt like hours, the words of the letter repeating in her head, over and over, the diamonds glinting like shards of broken ice scattered at her feet. The gems she had dreamed of wearing were poisoned, and the poison had already seeped deep into her bones.

A soft tap at the door cut through the silence, and Grandcourt stepped in, dressed for dinner. The sight of him jolted every frayed nerve in her body past breaking point. Gwendolen opened her mouth and screamed, again and again, high, sharp, hysterical sobs tearing out of her throat. He had expected to find her laughing, all dressed and ready for dinner. Instead he saw her, deathly pale, screaming as if a monster stood right before her, the scattered diamonds glinting all over the floor at her feet.
Somewhere, the Furies had followed them across the threshold, and stepped straight over the line of their new home.

Every generation has clung to the favorite old truth: a love fierce enough to rearrange your whole world is no rational choice, but a kind of fated isolation that makes all your long-held opinions and steady resolutions feel like strangers to you. Daphnis raving with passion would never have been cured by mastering Heraclitus’s philosophies; Tristan, drugged by that magic love philtre, would have had all his logic fray to threads no matter how deep his study of Duns Scotus; and Romeo, falling for Juliet the second he saw her, would not have paused to argue over Ptolemaic astronomy while he stood beneath her balcony. Yet not all powerful love burns that blind, unconnected flame. It can weave itself into every last corner of the soul, tie itself to hopes you never knew you held, and echo the grand, ancient orbits of all that has been and all that will be.

When Deronda returned to London, he could assure Sir Hugo that he had left Grandcourt in no doubt: he could walk away from his distant, far-from-certain inheritance for a guaranteed fifty thousand pounds. There was no clear sign yet that Grandcourt would take the offer, but it was obvious he wanted to keep their dealings friendly.
“And what did you make of the future bride, seeing her up close?” Sir Hugo asked, half teasing.
“I liked her far better than I did in Leubronn,” Deronda answered. “Roulette was never the right setting for her — it pulled out something sharp, almost demon-like, under that cool self-possession. At Diplow she seemed far softer, warmer, less guarded. There was a whole different light in her eyes, a different tenderness to the line of her mouth.”
“Don’t you go flirting with her too much, Dan,” Sir Hugo laughed, playful as ever. “If you rile Grandcourt up when they come to the Abbey for Christmas, it’ll muck up all my plans.”
“I can stay in town for the holidays, sir,” Deronda said evenly.
“No, no — Lady Mallinger and the children couldn’t bear Christmas without you. Just don’t stir up trouble — unless you can engineer a duel and shoot Grandcourt, in which case a little inconvenience would be well worth it.”
“I don’t think you’ve ever seen me flirt,” Deronda said, not amused.
“Oh, haven’t I though?” Sir Hugo prodded, delighting in his annoyance. “You look at every woman so soft-eyed, talk to them in that gentle, persuasive Jesuit way. You’re a dangerous young man — the sort of Lovelace who makes all the Clarissas chase you instead of you chasing them.”
It was useless to be furious at a tasteless joke, of course — but the anger always hits before you remember how little sense your irritation makes. Few friendly remarks sting worse than being told you always look like you’re doing something you never once intended to do. Sir Hugo’s definition of flirting had to be wildly peculiar, Deronda told himself; he knew for a fact he had never flirted in his life. He was only relieved the baronet knew nothing about him buying back Gwendolen’s lost necklace, which would have given him endless ammunition for this sort of teasing.
He resolved to be far more careful going forward — especially on his first visit to Mrs. Meyrick’s, now he was back from the continent. Mirah was the kind of creature it was nearly impossible to greet without letting tender interest show in every look and every word.
Mrs. Meyrick had written to keep Deronda updated on Mirah’s wellbeing at their home. “We fall more in love with her every single day,” her letter ran. “At breakfast we all turn to the door, waiting for her to walk in, and we watch and listen to her like she’s a visitor from some brand new, undiscovered country. I haven’t heard a single word from her that makes me doubt a thing about her. She’s perfectly content, and brimming over with gratitude. My girls are learning so much from her, and they’re trying to find her more pupils — she’s determined not to eat idle bread, to work for her living just like the rest of us. Mab says our whole life feels like a fairy tale now, and her only fear is that Mirah will turn back into a nightingale and fly away. Her voice is perfect: not loud or harsh, but searching, melting, like the memory of all the best old times. That’s the kind of voice us old folks melt for.”
She didn’t write to mention the quiet awkwardness Amy and Mab had felt when they’d accompanied Mirah to the synagogue. Their favorite Jewish heroine was Scott’s Rebecca, and seeing Mirah’s real faith didn’t sit quite so easy with them — but they held their tongues out of respect, for Mirah’s religion was such a tender, fragile thing to her no one would dare poke at it carelessly. After a few days, though, practical, reformer Amy couldn’t hold the question in any longer.
“Forgive me for asking, Mirah,” she said gently, “but does it feel right to you, that the women have to sit separated behind a railing in the gallery?”
Mirah blinked, soft and surprised. “I never thought anything else of it. It’s all I’ve ever known.”
“And — do you really like seeing all the men keep their hats on during service?” Mab asked, testing the smallest possible point of difference.
“Oh yes,” Mirah said. “I love everything I’ve always seen in that place. It brings back all my oldest, most precious feelings — feelings I wouldn’t trade for anything in the whole world.”
After that, not a single one of the generous Meyrick girls would dream of criticizing any part of her faith. It was stitched straight into the fibre of her heart, never a set of dry rules or propositions to her.
“She says she’s a very bad Jewess, and barely knows half of her people’s beliefs,” Amy whispered later, when Mirah had gone to bed. “Maybe it’ll just fade away slowly, and she’ll become Christian like the rest of us, if she grows to love us enough, and never finds her mother. It feels so strange, to follow the Jewish faith in this day and age.”
“Oh no no no!” Mab wailed. “I wish I wasn’t such a clumsy, terrible Christian! How could an ugly, fumbling Christian who’s always dropping her sewing ever convert a perfect, beautiful Jewess who doesn’t have a single fault?”
“I know it’s wicked,” sharp little Kate said, “but I can’t help hoping her mother is never found. There might be something awful waiting for her out there.”
“Nonsense, my dear,” Mrs. Meyrick said, shooting her girls a playful, mischievous glance. “I’d wager Mirah is exactly the spitting image of her mother. What a joy it would be for that woman to get her lost daughter back! I suppose none of you think a mother’s feelings are worth counting for anything — a dead mother is so much more convenient than a live one, isn’t it?”
“Fair point, little mother,” Kate laughed. “We’d far rather value you cheap and keep you alive forever.”
Neither the Meyrick girls — who’d scraped together all their scattered knowledge through odd books and eager, independent foraging, as clever young women so often had to do — nor Deronda himself, with all his fine, privileged education, had ever stopped to realize how little they knew of modern Judaism, of the living, beating inner history of the Jewish people. The Chosen People had always been treated like a group selected for everyone else’s story, their thoughts a thing that should have been entirely something else. Deronda, like every other gentleman he knew, had always written Judaism off as some eccentric, fossilized old form of faith that no cultivated man needed to bother studying, leaving it all to specialists. But Mirah — running terrified from one cruel parent, and aching with every fibre of her to find the other — had flashed on him the long-forgotten truth: Judaism was something alive, throbbing in real human lives, the very framework that made the whole world make sense for them. He had caught that new curiosity while wandering on the lazy holiday he’d taken with Sir Hugo before heading home, stopping every time he saw a synagogue to peer through its doors, hunting down books about Jewish history to bring back with him. That awakening, that shift from smugly assuming you already know everything about a subject you never cared about, to suddenly caring deeply, to realizing all your old opinions were just ignorance, was the best cure for boredom there could ever be — the kind no doctor could write you a prescription for. It made those weeks of aimless lounging feel full of purpose.
It was on that journey that he first walked into a synagogue, in Frankfurt, when their party stopped there on a Friday. He’d seen the Juden-gasse, the old Jewish quarter, years before, remembered its picturesque twisty old houses — but now he didn’t stare at the architecture. He stared at the people, his thoughts linking every face to all the centuries of their race’s long history, stirring that deep historical sympathy in him that had always shaped his quiet, gentle character. When a young man is handsome, well-mannered, well-educated, and comfortably off, no one ever bothers asking what he actually thinks, right? They’ll happily write him off as a pleasant, clever young fellow without ever digging into his deeper convictions. Only when he gets older, a little slovenly, a little stout, do people start noticing his peculiarities, and count themselves lucky if those peculiarities aren’t offensive. But anyone who wants to understand how future events shape Deronda has to know who he really was at twenty-five, far beneath the surface of polite small talk.
His vivid, sharp impressions of the world had always made him seem like an enigma to his friends, his feelings feeling vague and unmoored to them. His early-woken sensitivity had bloomed into a wide, all-encompassing sympathy that threatened to stop him from ever committing to a firm, clear path. The second he tried to take a firm stance against anything, his imagination filled with the other side’s humanity — he felt like the Sabine warriors of old, about to throw his spear at nothing but the flesh of his own flesh, people he could find something to love in. He was so used to seeing the world the way it must look to every other person that strong, blind partisanship felt like a lie to him. His soft, flexible sympathy blended with his overactive reflective mind to dilute that sharp indignation against wrongdoing that moral force depends on. He was one of the least vice-touched young men alive, but he hated vice gently, seeing it not as some abstract evil, but as a flaw in a messy, individual human life that deserved understanding and pity. He was fiercely democratic in his care for ordinary working people, but intensely conservative too, tied by affection and memory to old long-sanctioned traditions no logic could ever make him let go of. He devoured every book on government and religion he could find, but shrank from letting go of those old familiar forms full of happy memories. He couldn’t help loving the world’s losing causes, he realized, and shrank from the bitter triumph of people who chased success at any cost, turning their backs on everyone who lost. That over-wide, over-reflective sympathy was in danger of paralyzing him entirely, robbing him of the ability to stand firm against cruelty, to choose a clear circle of fellowship to stand beside. In the last few years, he’d become so sharply aware of this flaw that all he ached for was some external event, some sudden flash of insight, that would pull him out of his drift, compress all his wandering, scattered energy into a single clear path. He no longer cared about hoarding knowledge, or taking up a random respectable career, unless both could flow alongside his deepest emotions. He dreaded that hollow, dead sort of intellectualism, the kind that knows every single trivial fact about a violet’s chemistry except what a violet actually smells like. He refused to live his life without making some small, real difference for the better — but he couldn’t find the road. He blamed his upbringing, the strange, unmoored circumstances of his birth that had laid no special demands on him, given him no fixed certainties to build his life around. He was drifting into a meditative numbness, he knew, slipping further and further away from that life of passionate, practical good he’d always dreamed of as the only life worth living. He wanted to hold onto those soft, sacred feelings that make life taste worth living, even when his overactive mind threatened to grind everything down into meaningless dust. That quiet undercurrent hummed beneath everything he did, as he pretended to study law, or make polite dinner conversation.
As he wandered the warm Frankfurt evening, heading for the old orthodox synagogue, that imaginative rush of connection to all that long-forgotten history drowned out the small, ugly little annoyances he met on the way. He’d stopped at an old bookshop to ask the time of the service, and a precocious, friendly Jewish boy had enthusiastically directed him to the old traditional Rabbinical school synagogue, not the fancy new Reform temple — before charming him out of an extra thaler for a very common book, insisting it was “very rare and hard to find.” Across the shop counter, two grizzled, shabbily dressed peddlers in long coats and wide low hats yelled back and forth to each other in Yiddish. Deronda laughed at himself for his silly earlier alarm that Mirah’s unknown relatives would all be somehow crass or unpleasant; no group of people had a monopoly on guile or rough edges. He paid his overpriced bill, turned his back on the bookshop, and walked into the old synagogue just as the sun set, where a full congregation of men had gathered for the service.
He happened to sit a little distance away from an elderly man with a full white beard, whose fine sharp profile could have easily been Italian as it was Hebrew. The man glanced at him again and again until their eyes met; Deronda looked away, not wanting to intrude, only to find an open prayer book gently pushed across the bench toward him. He bowed his thanks. The congregation settled, the reader climbed the raised platform, and the service began. Deronda flipped through the German translation of the Hebrew text, saw they were chanting psalms and old scripture passages, and gave himself over to the raw, overwhelming emotion of the chanted liturgy — the same feeling you get listening to Allegri’s Miserere or Palestrina’s Magnificat, that sears through even if you don’t understand a single word. It was that wordless yearning to escape the limits of your own small weakness, to invite all goodness into you and let it stay — that selfless lifting of joy that cries Gloria in excelsis that such goodness exists. That feeling drew all its power from the knowledge that every note he heard had been sung by generations of struggling people before him. The deep rolling voice of the chazan, the high sweet notes of the little boys’ choir, all the men swaying back and forth in prayer, the plain shabbiness of the small building where a faith that had shaped half the world’s religion still echoed in a quiet forgotten corner — all of it blended for Deronda into one single expression of a shared history, tragic and glorious at once. He was shocked by the strength of his own feeling, it felt like a divine presence filling the dark room before he even had words to explain it.
When the last note of the chant died away, and he looked around at all the indifferent, ordinary faces, a cold thought darted through him: he was probably the only person in the whole congregation who had felt that deep surge of feeling, everyone else had just been going through dull, mindless routine. He had just bowed to his kind neighbor and started to move toward the door when he felt a firm hand on his arm. He turned, that sharp unpleasant jolt of being grabbed by a stranger running through him, and saw the white-bearded old man leaning in. “Excuse me, young gentleman,” he said in soft German. “Allow me — what is your parentage? What is your mother’s family name, her maiden name?”
Deronda flinched, pulling his arm sharply away from the man’s touch. “I am English,” he said coldly.
The stranger stared at him doubtfully for a long moment, then just lifted his hat and turned away. Deronda walked back to his hotel, telling himself he could not have acted any differently. How could he possibly admit to a total stranger that he did not even know his own mother’s maiden name? The man had been wildly out of line, asking such a personal question unprompted, no doubt just struck by some random passing resemblance that meant nothing at all. The incident was trivial, he told himself — but his sharp, instinctive dislike of the question meant he never mentioned it to the Mallingers, alongside his usual habit of keeping his private enthusiasms to himself. Sir Hugo was one of the kindest men alive, especially to women, doing deeds others would call wildly romantic — but he never saw those deeds as romantic, and always laughed at grand, far-off motives or convictions that flew far beyond polite everyday life. That was the biggest difference between him and Deronda, who could never get through a breakfast at the table without letting his mind wander off to grand, quiet thoughts that had nothing to do with the casual polite conversation around him.
Those Frankfurt memories were still bright and humming in his mind, stoked by his determination to do right by Mirah. That stranger’s question about his parentage, which he had so quickly shaken off as trivial, left him newly resolved to be extra careful when tracking down Mirah’s missing family. If he uncovered something cruel and ugly, was he obligated to tell her, and destroy the peaceful, happy new life she had built with the Meyricks?
He arrived at their house at four o’clock, the time he had written to announce, and found Mirah sitting at her work near the open piano, only Mrs. Meyrick and Mab in the room, surrounded by their walls of old beautiful engravings. Her hair was neat and soft, her dress spotless and simple, her face glowing with a quiet, radiant happiness that a painter could have copied straight to put in the middle of a nativity scene, right next to the angels singing of peace on earth. Mirah immediately thought of how different she looked from the desperate girl Deronda had found on the river bank, and spoke first the second they greeted each other.
“See how different I am from that miserable creature by the water? All because you found me, and brought me to the very best people alive.”
“It was only luck that I was the one who found you,” Deronda said gently. “Any other good man would have done exactly what I did.”
“That’s not the right way to think about it,” Mirah said, shaking her head with soft, unshakable gravity. “I don’t think about what any other man might have done. I think about what actually happened. It was you. No one else.”

A Rabbi once put it as an indisputable, unarguable truth: no man could ever carve his father and mother’s bones into spoons. Every hearer understood the quiet, stubborn sentiment behind the rule, even if no one could name a logical, practical reason to forbid the trade. The market for spoons would never expand far enough to make anyone ask, “Why not?” and claim human progress depended on turning beloved family remains into cutlery. The only check is sentiment—and those who do not believe sentiment is the richest, most precious wealth in the world will never be coerced by it at all.

All the while, Daniel Deronda had given up his fashionable daily rides in Rotten Row for a far quieter, less celebrated form of wandering. He spent hours drifting through the East End streets where London’s common Jewish families made their homes, slipping into synagogues during services, peering through shop windows, studying every face he passed, chasing the faint, soft promise of the clues Mirah had shared with him: a mother named Cohen, a brother named Ezra, both lost to her since she was a small, lonely child. It hardly seemed like a method that would turn up answers. He knew he could go straight to a prominent rabbi or a respected leader in the Jewish community for help, and he told himself he would do it—after Christmas.

But the truth was, for all his love of the poetry in ordinary, everyday lives, Deronda could not escape the heavy, unyielding weight of the hard, unpolished Actual when his heart was tangled up in a personal quest. Enthusiasm flows easy among grand, distant ideas: you can look back at medieval Jewish communities breathing garlic in candlelit synagogues, or imagine heroic martyrs defying crusaders at the height of the Hep! Hep! Hep! violence, and find nothing shabby about the scene. But when that ideal presses up against you as a living, breathing person you care for—when Mirah, with her soft voice and her tears for the family she lost, hangs over every one of your wandering steps—suddenly the dingy shop fronts and unremarkable faces of modern London feel far harder to face than any grand historical tragedy. Deronda could dream of sailing down the Rhine in the 11th century, hunting for Mirah’s kin among the persecuted Jews of Cordova, and never flinch at the grit. But seeing that same beloved idea of Mirah’s family made flesh in the harsh midday light of Whitechapel, with no dramatic martyrdom to gild it, only commonplace, half-repulsive ordinary life, that was the test. He knew his usual self would have scorned anyone too fastidious to see the beauty in broad, unvarnished human life—but with Mirah’s fragile hope wrapped up in every possible discovery, he could not shake the quiet fear that the family she had imagined as a sacred, gentle haven would turn out to be something she could never have dreamed of, something that would break her. Worse, a quiet, suppressed voice deep inside him whispered he might be running toward that same collision for his own hidden, unknown parentage too. He had no real reason to expect Mirah’s family to be wealthy, among the fancy Jewish circles that outdid the English lords at sport, so those distant people did not occupy his thoughts at all. He wandered those streets half hoping to prepare his own mind for whatever he might find, no more than that—like if Mirah had been related to Welsh miners, he might have walked their villages just to understand their lives, so he could explain their world to her gently if the time ever came.

He did not *want* to find anyone. Every time he glanced at the name painted over a shop door, he was relieved to see it did not spell *Ezra Cohen*. He particularly, selfishly hoped Ezra Cohen did not run a shop at all. Wishes are notoriously ominous, after all—the world seems to arrange itself so that if you find the idea of a squint unforgivably distasteful, your child will end up with one, and if you crave a squint, you will never get it. That despondent view of probability was the one Deronda leaned into, unconsciously, one grey morning when he turned down a small side street off the din and congestion of Holborn, tired of wandering, and paused to hail an approaching hansom cab.

A flash of chased silver in the shop window at his right hand caught his eye: a pair of exquisite old missal clasps, their details worn soft with centuries of use. He thought at once of Lady Mallinger, who had a great fondness for turning old Catholic religious relics into pretty Protestant trinkets, and imagined these would make a lovely bracelet. His gaze drifted over the rest of the window, and he saw it was a pawnbroker’s shop, one that dressed up its trade in bric-a-brac, displaying jewelry, lace, and fine trinkets on every shelf. A placard in the corner read *Watches and Jewelry Exchanged and Repaired*.

His survey had not gone unnoticed. A young man stepped out the shop door, around thirty years old, unmistakably Jewish, with a warm, encouraging smile. “Good day, sir,” he called. Deronda winced, sure the man was about to launch into a persuasive sales pitch, returned the greeting, crossed to the other side of the street, and waved the cab over. Then he looked back at the sign painted over the shop front, and his chest lurched. It read *Ezra Cohen*.

There might be a hundred Ezra Cohens running shops in London, but Deronda had never seen a single one. This young man, hovering to greet a possible customer, was almost exactly the age Mirah’s brother would be, old enough to be a grown man while she was still a tiny child. As he rode home in the cab, he tried his hardest to convince himself there was no reason at all to presume this man was Mirah’s brother. Even if by some impossible chance he *was*, and the investigation found Mirah’s mother was dead, Deronda told himself, it was not his duty to break the news to her. But the thought would not settle: he knew Mirah’s deepest, most devout religious wish was to know if her mother was gone, and if her brother still lived. How dare he shape another person’s life according to his own personal comfort? Was that not exactly the secret, cruel way his own life had been ordered, all his childhood hidden from him, no open daylight on his own family ties, no clear path to follow his most basic duties, like every other man?

The immediate relief was that he had made no real discovery at all. He would go back to the shop, he decided, as soon as he could conveniently, buy the silver clasps for Lady Mallinger, and learn enough facts to confirm there was no reason for him to make any choice at all. But Sir Hugo kept him busy for days on end: the older man was preparing an after-dinner speech on a fiery political topic, and wanted Deronda to dig up all the legal references for him, then argued with him for hours every day, their debates always ending in a friendly draw. “Confound it, Dan!” Sir Hugo would exclaim, half annoyed, half proud, when Deronda laid out his point clearly. “Why don’t you get up and say these things in public? You’re wrong, you know, the heavy mass of the country is against you—but that’s the perfect stage for a young man to make a name for himself. When I was your age, I’d have leapt at the chance. You’d be in Parliament before you know it, and that’s the very thing that would make me happy.”

“I’m sorry I can’t do what would please you, sir,” Deronda said. “But I can’t bring myself to treat politics as a profession.”

“Why not?” Sir Hugo pressed. “A man who isn’t born into public life has to work for it. The country’s business has to get done, as the old Duke said—you can’t wait around for a divine call from heaven or from your constituents.”

“I don’t want to make a living out of opinions,” Deronda told him. “Especially out of borrowed ones. I don’t judge other men for it—many better men than I don’t mind stepping up on a platform to praise their party’s work, but that life isn’t for me.”

“Any man who fights every little bit of humbug in the world is just a three-cornered, impossible fellow,” Sir Hugo laughed. “There’s good humbug too, the kind that oils the wheels and makes progress possible. If you want to lead people, you have to meet them where their ideas are. You don’t issue an Order in Council to wipe out public shallowness, you meet them halfway. A little acting is necessary for any action at all.”

“I’ll concede you sometimes have to make a temporary compromise,” Deronda said. “But there’s a world of difference between putting on a foolscap hat to grin for a specific occasion when you have no choice, and carrying a foolscap hat in your pocket, practicing grinning every day. I can’t treat my own success as proof that what I’m doing is right for the public.”

That conversation had left Deronda feeling jarred and unsettled when he finally set out for his long-planned second visit to Ezra Cohen’s shop. He entered the street from the end opposite the Holborn entrance, and a heavy reluctance slowed his steps, his thoughts circling back from the public compromises he and Sir Hugo had argued over to his own private dilemma: how far was it right to hide the truth about Mirah’s family, just to spare her pain? Most people would call that an over-refinement, but Deronda had come to hate secrets above all things, knowing how the hidden truths around his own birth had poisoned his whole youth. The idea of being forced to keep another unnecessary secret grated on his every nerve. He paused in front of every plausible shop window on the street, half deciding he would not learn any more about this modern Ezra Cohen, even though his own logic told him the odds were astronomically low that this man was Mirah’s lost brother.

One of the windows he paused at belonged to a second-hand bookshop, where a small table outside held a jumbled, charming mix of literature from Homer’s verse to cheap railway novels. Deronda spotted a copy of Solomon Maimon’s famous autobiography, the life of the wandering Polish Jew he had long wanted to read, slipped it into his pocket, and stepped inside to pay for it, expecting the usual morose, unenthusiastic secondhand bookseller who never tries to talk you into buying anything.

But the figure he saw behind the shelves was nothing like that. A thin man in threadbare clothing, his skin a pale, yellowed flatness like old carved ivory, sat on a stool leaning against the shelves, reading yesterday’s *Times*. When he lifted the paper and looked up at Deronda, Deronda thought he had never seen a face more like the portrait of an ancient Hebrew prophet from the Babylonian exile, or a medieval Jewish poet. It was a sharp, clearly cut face, worn thin with a lifetime of strenuous, eager searching that had never given him easy, immediate joy, the dark eyes far-off and intense, as if he was perpetually waiting for a messenger to arrive, bringing either salvation or terrible news. “That book has no price mark on it,” he said, not rising from his seat. “Mr. Ram is out at dinner, I’m minding the shop. What will you give for it?”

Deronda asked if he knew its market value, and the man leaned forward, voice deepening with sudden, electric eagerness. “Have you read it? Are you a scholar, interested in Jewish history?”

“I am interested in Jewish history,” Deronda said, curiosity overriding his surprise at the strange, intense inspection.

Then the stranger rose, reached out, and gripped Deronda’s arm tight with his thin, bony hand. “Are you of our people?” he whispered, hoarse with excitement, barely loud enough to hear.

Deronda flushed deeply, shook his head, and said “No.” The grip fell away instantly. All the light and excitement drained out of the man’s face, melting into flat, distant melancholy, as if the spirit that had leapt up in him at the sight of Deronda had crawled back to hide in the deepest recesses of his bones. “Half a crown will do, sir,” he said, holding out the book with cold, polite distance. The rejection left Deronda oddly humiliated, as if some high dignitary had looked him over and dismissed him unfit. He paid his half crown, bade him good morning, and left the shop carrying the book, still confused and a little annoyed that their strange, charged conversation had ended so abruptly.

He turned and stepped through the door of Ezra Cohen’s pawn shop seconds later. Ezra himself was leaning over the counter, his round, plump face glistening, haggling over two plated stoppers and three silver teaspoons with a customer on the other side of the glass. He looked up, grinned, and called “Mother! Mother!” over his shoulder, before nodding warmly at Deronda. “Coming, sir—be with you in a second.”

Deronda’s heart sank a little when a robust, vigorous woman in her fifties came out from the back of the shop to serve him. She was not nearly coarse or unkind enough to rule out the terrible possibility that she was Mirah’s mother. Deronda found himself trying to wipe away the fat that had softened the outlines of her youth from her face, trying to see what she had looked like at twenty. The line of her eyebrows was almost identical to Mirah’s. It was not impossible, he thought, that this woman could have once been the mother of a gentle, beautiful girl like Mirah. “How can I serve you, sir?” she said, in a soft, guttural tone.

Deronda asked for the old silver clasps in the window. Ezra leapt forward before his mother could stretch to reach them, laughing and handing them to Deronda. “My mother likes to do everything herself,” he explained cheerfully. “She’d hurt herself stretching, so when we get a gentleman customer, I always call her out to make sure she gets to greet them proper.” His mother laughed a soft, amiable gurgle, clearly proud of her adoring son.

“They’re only three guineas, sir,” she said, encouragingly.

“First-rate workmanship, worth twice that,” Ezra put in. “I got them cheap, all the way from Cologne.”

Two new customers stepped into the shop just then, and Ezra called “Addy!”—and a whole swarm of Cohen family members emerged from the back. A bright young woman with black ringlets and a huge coral necklace, Addy, carried a tiny black-eyed baby, set it down on the counter, followed by a sturdy six-year-old boy and a little girl barely four, all of them dark-eyed and vividly, unmistakably Jewish. Deronda patted the eldest boy’s head, amused, and asked his name.

“Jacob Alexander Cohen,” the little boy said, perfectly clear and unshakably confident, hands stuffed in the pockets of his tiny knickerbockers.

“Named after your grandfather?” Deronda asked.

“Grandpa sells knives and razors and scissors,” Jacob announced, pulling a tiny pocketknife out of his pocket, flipping open two blades and a corkscrew in one quick, practiced motion. “He gave it to me.”

Deronda turned to the grandmother. “Isn’t that a little dangerous for him to play with?”

“Bless you, he’ll never hurt himself!” she said, beaming at him.

Jacob stepped even closer, his small voice hoarse and sharp, like an old, experienced merchant. “Do you have a knife?” he asked.

Deronda smiled, pulled his small penknife out of his waistcoat pocket, and held it out for the little boy to see.

*“His name is Israel. A witch’s spell once turned him into a dog… But every Friday at dusk, the magic breaks, and the dog is human once more.”*
—Heine, *Princess Sabbath*

When Daniel Deronda arrived at five o’clock sharp, the pawnshop’s front shutter was bolted tight, and the gentile servant girl opened the door just a crack to usher him in. She led him through to the back parlor, and he stopped short, surprised by how soft and warm the scene was, nothing like the harsh, clattering daylight of the shop floor he’d visited days before.

The old house stretched deep behind the storefront, and the large back room would no doubt have felt dim and gloomy at midday—but now a fine seven-branched brass oil lamp hung low over a table spread with snow-white linen, gilding every corner of the space. The smoky, dark-stained ceiling and walls threw the family gathered there into sharp, glowing relief, their dark eyes and warm complexions lit to a rich Venetian amber hue. The grandmother wore a gown of soft yellow-brown, a heavy thick gold chain strung around her throat in place of a necklace, and her lined face, framed by neat coiled silver hair, looked as stately as any old matriarch’s portrait under the lamplight. Young Mrs. Cohen was dressed in bold red and black, strands of oversized faux pearls looped again and again around her neck, the baby fast asleep in its cradle swaddled under a bright scarlet counterpane. Little Adelaide Rebekah was tricked out in braided amber, and Jacob Alexander darted around in black velveteen and bright scarlet stockings. All four pairs of dark eyes turned to him with unfeigned, glittering welcome, and Deronda felt almost ashamed of the supercilious, distant dislike he’d carried for this loud, prosperous family when he’d last called in the glare of day. Their hospitality was so unforced, so cordial, that both the younger and elder Mrs. Cohen seemed to carry a quiet, unshakable dignity on their own hearth, as they fussed to make him comfortable.

He glanced around at the mismatched old furniture as he talked: the heavy oaken bureau, the tall scuffed side table, the great blue-and-yellow glazed platter perched on it flanked by two tarnished old silver goblets, and the big dark vellum tome with its deep ribbed spine propped in front of them. A door at the far end of the room stood open to a lit inner chamber, and Deronda stored those little details away in the back of his mind while he pulled the new knife he’d bought for Jacob out of his pocket.

“Is this the sort you were asking for?” he said, offering it to the little boy.

Jacob subjected it to a rigorous, critical scrutiny, popping open every blade and the curved hook, then fished out his own old knife with the corkscrew attachment to hold them side by side for comparison.
“Why do you like the hook better than the corkscrew?” Deronda asked him, half smiling.
“’Caush I can get hold of things with a hook. Corkscrew won’t go into nothing but corks. But it’s better for you, you can draw corks,” Jacob lisped, perfectly serious.
“Then we’ve got a swap?” Deronda said, glancing over to see the grandmother watching them, delighted.
“What else have you got in your pockets?” Jacob asked, squinting up at him deliberatively.
“Hush, little love,” the grandmother chided softly, but Deronda, not wanting to spoil the game, shook his head gently.
“I think that’s our secret, isn’t it? Our deal was only about the knives.”
Jacob stared up into his face for a long, careful moment, as if weighing whether Deronda could be trusted, then solemnly held out his old corkscrew knife. Deronda pocketed it with equal gravity, and the small dark-haired boy darted off into the next room chattering a mile a minute, before skittering back just as his father stepped through the door. He snatched a tiny velveteen cap off a chair and pulled it onto his head to approach Cohen, who kept his own hat firmly on and gave his new visitor no immediate attention. He stood still as the two children ran to clasp his knees, laid a hand on each of their heads in turn, and murmured a soft Hebrew benediction, before Mrs. Cohen lifted the sleeping baby out of its cradle and held it up under his outstretched palms, so it might be blessed even in its rest. For a long quiet second, Deronda forgot this was a pawnbroker whose shop was lined with other people’s forfeited trinkets—there was nothing prosaic about that small, sacred scene.

“Hope you see what a welcome you’ve got in my house, sir,” Cohen said at last, setting his own hat down and slipping back into his familiar brisk, jovial self. “And you’re punctual. Nothing like a little pressure to sharpen the wits, eh?” He tapped his side pocket lightly. “It’s good for all of us in turns. I’ve had my share of scrambling to make payments, back when I first started mending every last kind of box you could name. Bracing for the mind. Now then, let’s have a look at that ring you mentioned.”

Deronda slipped the diamond ring off his finger. “I believe it cost me a hundred pounds. It’s surely enough collateral for fifty, and I’ll almost certainly redeem it in a month or so.”

Cohen’s glistening dark eyes drew a little closer together as he took the ring and turned it over in his fingers, clearly amused that this naive young gentleman actually thought pawnbrokers were eager to see people come back for their pledges. He handed it back, his face perfectly neutral. “Good, good. We’ll talk numbers after we’ve eaten. You’ll join us for our Sabbath meal, if you’ve no objection? My wife, my mother, we’d all be honored.”

The invitation was warmly echoed by the whole family, and Deronda accepted gladly. Everyone moved to gather around the table, where the only visible dish was one covered with a starched linen napkin. Mrs. Cohen set a china bowl of water near her husband so he might wash his hands—then Cohen pulled his hat back on, and called out in a loud, clear voice: “Mordecai!”

Deronda assumed it was some part of the Sabbath ritual, until a soft “Yes” drifted from the inner room, and he turned to look at the open door. He froze. Standing in the doorway was the exact enigmatic Jewish man he’d met that very morning in the old bookshop, the one whose face had lit up with such desperate hunger when he’d spotted the rare volume of Judaica Deronda had bought. Their eyes locked, both equally shocked, neither giving the smallest sign that they’d met before. When Mordecai took his seat at the far end of the table, he only bent his head to Deronda in a cold, distant nod, as if the sharp disappointment of that morning still hung sour between them.

Cohen washed his hands, reciting Hebrew blessings under his breath, then pulled the napkin off the dish to reveal two long, flat seed-dusted loaves: the Sabbath challah, the legacy of the manna that had fed the Israelites wandering in the desert. He broke off small pieces for everyone gathered around the table, even little Adelaide Rebekah, who stood balanced on her chair, her amber dress spread out, her small face solemn as she tried to look suitably reverent. Cohen spoke another blessing, the men removed their hats, and everyone sat down to eat. Deronda barely noticed what he was eating, too preoccupied by the quiet exchange of half-furtive, fascinated glances passing between him and Mordecai. The man no longer wore the threadbare rusty black coat he’d had on at the bookshop, but a faded light drab overcoat that had once been a fine loose paletot, now shrunk thin from years of washing—and the contrast made his sharp, eager dark face look even more striking, like the prophet Ezekiel pulled straight out of an old, forgotten scroll. The family passed him the tenderest fried fish tails, the best bits of the meal, as if tending to a fragile, cherished poor relation.

Cohen kept the conversation bright and lively, gushing about the Queen and the royal family, the Emperor and Empress of France—topics the whole family threw themselves into with gusto. Young Mrs. Cohen could recite every distinguished royal birthday off by heart, and her mother-in-law chimed in with stories of when the Emperor and Empress had visited London ten years prior.
“Our baby’s named Eugenie Esther,” she told Deronda brightly.
“Would you believe the Emperor looks exactly like a cousin of mine?” the grandmother put in. “I saw him through the crowd and it hit me like lightning. Couldn’t believe my eyes.”
“Me and my mother went to see them at the Crystal Palace,” Cohen laughed, stroking his mother’s shoulder affectionately. “I had a job keeping her from getting squeezed flat. Told myself even if I had a hundred mothers, I’d never drag one to a royal public viewing again—especially not when you’ve only got the one.”

Deronda seized his chance to speak. “Your mother’s been a widow a long while, I imagine? That must have made caring for her all the more important when you were young.”
“Ay, a good many yahrzeits have passed since I had to manage for the both of us,” Cohen said, nodding quickly. “I started young. Makes you a sharp knife, as they say.”
“Makes what a sharp knife, father?” Jacob lisped, his cheek stuffed full of sweet cake.
Cohen winked at Deronda. “Having your nose put to the grindstone, lad.”
Jacob slipped off his chair, cake still in hand, and trotted over to Mordecai, who had not spoken a single word all meal. “What does that mean? Putting my nose to the grindstone?”
“It means you bear being hurt without making a noise,” Mordecai said, his soft voice gentle as he turned his kind dark eyes to the small boy. Jacob held out his cake for Mordecai to take a bite, and the thin man smiled and obliged, making the little boy glow with pride. Deronda took the chance to turn to him directly.
“I suspect you’re the one with all the book learning in this house, aren’t you?” he said. “You have studied a great deal, I think.”
“I have studied,” Mordecai said quietly, looking back at him, the old spark of interest from the bookshop waking in his eyes, as if his earlier disappointment was melting away. “And you? You read German, from the book you were buying this morning.”
“Yes, I studied in Germany. Do you work in Mr. Ram’s bookshop full time?” Deronda asked.
“No, I only look after it for him while he goes to eat his midday meal,” Mordecai said, leaning forward a little. “Do you know Hebrew?”
“I’m sorry to say I don’t, not at all.”
Mordecai’s face fell. He dropped his gaze to his folded hands in his lap, and said no more. Deronda noticed for the first time the faint, laborious catch in his breath, the quiet sign that consumption was slowly wasting him away.

“I never had time for that sort of book learning,” Cohen put in, tapping Deronda’s ring lightly. “I had to learn about things that make a living, like precious stones. I’m not afraid to take that ring of yours at my own valuation. So—what sum were you looking for?”
“Fifty or sixty pounds,” Deronda said, a little carelessly.
Cohen pushed his hands into his pockets, stared at him with those glistening bright eyes, and shook his head. “Can’t do that. Happy to oblige, but forty pounds is the highest I can go. Forty, take it or leave it.”
Deronda glanced up to see Mordecai looking at him again, his curious gaze fixed on his face. “Very well,” he said. “I’ll redeem it in a month or so.”
“Good. I’ll write the ticket out later,” Cohen said casually, holding up a finger to signal silence. All the men pulled their hats back on, and Cohen opened the Sabbath grace, the rest of the family chiming in with responses, until Mordecai lifted his chin, his thin hands clasped loosely before him, and chanted the final, long solemn portion of the thanksgiving in a low, ringing tone. There could be no starker contrast between the loud practical pawnbroker at the head of the table and this otherworldly, emaciated man singing the old prayers as if he spoke directly to heaven. The moment the last note faded, Mordecai gave Deronda a small, slight nod, rose quietly, walked back into his inner room, and shut the door behind him.

“He’s a very remarkable man, that Mordecai,” Deronda said, turning to Cohen. Cohen immediately tapped the side of his temple, sticking his tongue out just a little to make it clear he thought Mordecai was a little touched, not quite right in the head.
“Is he part of your family?” Deronda asked.
The whole family smiled, as if the idea was absurd. “No, no, we took him in out of charity,” Cohen said. “He used to work for me, got weaker and weaker, so we gave him a roof. He’s a bit of a burden, but he brings blessings down on the house, and he teaches the boy his letters, plus he mends all the watches and fine jewelry that comes through the shop.”

Deronda could not press for more details. Cohen immediately steered the conversation back to their business, writing out the pawn ticket, counting out forty pounds in notes and gold, and handing both over to Deronda in exchange for the diamond ring. It would have felt rude to linger any longer than necessary, so Deronda took his leave, the pawn ticket tucked safely in his breast pocket. He knew then that he would come back, the very second he returned to town after Christmas. There was some strange, unnameable thread of fate tying him to that quiet, burning-eyed man in the back room—some secret history he had to uncover, some purpose waiting to be revealed between them.

If the weight of a guilty conscience were measured strictly by the harm a person has done, human history would read very differently: the architects of greedy wars and the ravenous moguls of the money markets would march in the same self-lacerating line of penitents as the petty thief, the pickpocket, and the desperate murderer who kills for a paltry handful of coin. Wickedness doles out its glittering prizes, no doubt—but to claim the highest reward of unshaken, serene cruelty, a soul must be far baser, colder, and more brutal than most human beings born into this soft, messy world can manage. The vast majority of us carry some faint spark of conscience: a fear that is justice’s long shadow, a flicker of pity that is love’s own echo, enough to bar us from that icy peak of perfect, unfeeling vice.

On the twenty-ninth of December, Deronda knew the Grandcourts had arrived at the Abbey—but he had not caught a single glimpse of them before he headed up to change for dinner. A heavy, glorious snowfall had blanketed the grounds that day, granting the children rare hours of loud, giddy joy: snowball fights that left their mittens soaked through, lopsided snow forts capped with sprigs of holly. The Mallinger girls would suffer no amusement at all unless their beloved “cousin” joined in and oversaw it, and after all that outdoor exertion he had drifted straight to the billiards room, his mind so full of small, loud, childish demands that he barely spared a thought for the new couple’s arrival. Even so, the prospect of seeing Gwendolen again hummed quietly at the edge of his attention, and when he slipped into his room before the half-hour dinner bell rang, a little flushed and tired from hours of playing at amusement, he found himself wondering how her marriage to Grandcourt had changed her. He knew there would be new shades to her manner, just as there had been between their first meeting at the Leubronn gaming tables and their later encounter at Diplow.

“Some natures you could watch shift, grow, or erode a little every single day, if you paid close enough attention,” he thought to himself. “I’m certain she holds tight, deep inside, to every impression that ever marked her. That little business of the necklace, the quiet knowledge that someone thought her gambling was wrong—you could tell it had bitten right into her. That kind of deep impressibility cuts two ways, though: it can push a person to desperate, reckless choices just as easily as it can lead them toward something good. And no matter what small charms Grandcourt might hold for capricious tastes, good heavens—who could imagine him drawing out soft, tender affection in the quiet, unforgiving hours of daily life? I could almost want to horsewhip him, just to force some flicker of real passion into that blank, perfect face and steady, unreadable voice. I’m afraid she married him out of pure ambition, to escape the grinding poverty that loomed over her family. But why did she run from him at first? The poverty came later, of course. Poor thing—she was almost certainly pressured into it. How could anyone feel anything but pity for a creature as bright as that, full of unused, unspent life, reckously rash, hanging all her blind, glowing hopes for the future on that empty, polished remnant of a man?”

His harsh judgment of Grandcourt came all the easier for the soft pity he wrapped around Gwendolen in his thoughts. His description of the bridegroom as a “remnant” was not rooted in any secret knowledge, merely the impression that every polite conversation with him left: Grandcourt had long since worn out every scrap of natural, healthy curiosity about the world around him.

It is a near-universal rule that when a prominent couple marries, male acquaintances will almost always pity the bride, female acquaintances the groom—each side convinced their beloved friend could have done far better. Especially when the bride is as brilliant and charming as Gwendolen, every young gentleman in the room is apt to assume she could never care for a man as uninteresting to them as her husband, and must have married him for status or money alone. Who ever pities the groom, in that situation? Even his female friends tend to think his position is retribution, that he ought to have chosen someone else. Deronda can hardly be blamed, then, for feeling no shred of pity for Grandcourt, who had never struck a single acquaintance as the sort of man who suffered more than he inflicted. For Gwendolen, though—young, headstrong, adored by flattery that had long fed her belief that she was born to rule—how quickly could life curdle from bright expectation to the bitter, suffocating realization that a choice could never be undone? After all he had seen of her, Deronda would have had to be made of stone not to look forward to her entrance with quiet, intense interest. With the honeymoon three weeks behind her, and Gwendolen already established first at Ryelands, then at Diplow, he expected she would have mastered the art of composing her face to hide whatever feelings churned beneath it—she was never the sort to give gawking onlookers the satisfaction of seeing her raw and unguarded.

The drawing room was filled with a crowd invited expressly to meet the newlyweds: old aristocracy in Lord and Lady Pentreath, old gentry in the young Mr. and Mrs. Fitzadam of the Worcestershire branch, a member of Parliament for West Orchards named Mr. Fenn who cared for nothing but cider, accompanied by his two daughters, Lady Mallinger’s brother Mr. Raymond and his wife, the eminent lawyer Mr. Sinker, and Mr. Vandernoodt, the Dutch-born bachelor Sir Hugo had found so pleasant at Leubronn that he had invited him to the Abbey.

Everyone had gathered in the drawing room before the new couple appeared. The guests passed the lingering minutes before dinner cooing over the children—Lady Mallinger’s three girls, and the horde of little Raymond nephews and nieces, all allowed to stay up for this pre-dinner hour. The room was soft and glowing: full-length portraits set deep into the cedar paneling, a ceiling crammed with bright, richly colored coats of arms, light pouring more warmly from the crackling oak-bough fire on the hearth than from the pale beeswax candles dotted across the mantel. The deep piled carpet swallowed sound, and the quiet, well-bred tone of the gathering softened every voice, while the mix of ages—from white-haired Lord and Lady Pentreath down to four-year-old Edgar Raymond—lent every group of guests a soft, varied charm. Lady Mallinger drifted through the room in her black velvet gown, a tiny white lapdog tucked against her arm like a little finishing detail to her costume, the children clustered around the ladies while most of the gentlemen stood apart, chatting with the very moderate, unhurried vivacity common to drawing rooms in the minutes before a dinner bell. Deronda found himself cornered by Mr. Vandernoodt, that useful fixture of society who knew every prominent person alive but cared nothing for their actual work: pale-eyed, nonchalant, neatly clipped, a perfect, unassuming foil to Deronda’s vivid gravity.

Vandernoodt was gossiping about the bride and groom they were all waiting for, gleaning every small personal detail he could: “Men who’ve lived as much life as Grandcourt don’t usually pick wives this well, you know. He’s got quite a history, deep into every sort of pleasure, lazy as he seems. But of course you know all about him, being Sir Hugo’s ward.”
“No, really,” Deronda replied, keeping his tone deliberately indifferent. “I know little more than that he is Sir Hugo’s nephew.”

Just then the drawing room door swung open, cutting off Vandernoodt’s eagerness to share all his collected secrets.
The room was perfectly set to show off a striking new arrival, and no one in the gathering could deny that Mr. and Mrs. Grandcourt cut a perfectly distinguished figure. The bridegroom was exactly as he had always been: his costume faultlessly tailored, his face as impassible and unreadable as a statue’s, as if he would never settle for anything less than absolute perfection in his outward possessions, his wife included. The bride was exactly the sort of jewel he would have chosen. “By George, she’s even handsomer than she was at Leubronn,” Vandernoodt breathed. Deronda agreed, though he said nothing. She wore white silk, and diamonds glinted on her neck, her ears, tucked into the curls of her hair.
