That new, imposing sharpness to her beauty struck him immediately, more unquestionable than it had been the first time he saw her leaning over the roulette table. Back then, he had found a soft, tender, womanly charm in her when they met at Diplow—but he could not spot it now. She moved through the greetings with a cold, proud quietude, a thin, superficial smile on her lips, and he thought he could see that same sharp, almost feral force in her that had gripped her the day she had fixed him with that resolute glance, then turned away to walk off as a loser at the tables. There was no time to parse his impressions further, no time even to go over and greet her, before the gong sounded for dinner.

He sat not far opposite her at the table, and could overhear bits of her conversation with Sir Hugo, who was in especially high spirits chatting to the new bride. Though he looked toward her again and again, hoping to catch her eye and bow, she pointedly avoided his gaze for long minutes. At last Sir Hugo, who assumed they had already spoken, called across the table: “Deronda, you’ll like to hear what Mrs. Grandcourt has to say about your favorite Klesmer.”
Gwendolen’s lashes had been lowered to her plate, and Deronda, already watching her, saw a faint, involuntary quiver run through her eyelids as she was forced to lift them. She met his unembarrassed, gentle bow and smile, but her own smile never reached past her lips, a thin, formal curve. It lasted only an instant, before Sir Hugo was talking again, unhurried: “The Arrowpoints have forgiven the marriage, you know. He’s spending Christmas with his new bride at Quetcham.”
“I expect he’s glad of it for his wife’s sake,” Deronda said. “Else I don’t imagine he’d have minded keeping far away.”
“It’s a proper troubadour story,” rumbled Lady Pentreath, the deep-voiced old dowager. “I’m glad there’s still a little romance left in the world. These young people nowadays are all far too worldly-wise for their own good.”
“Still, it shows good sense on the Arrowpoints’ part, taking the pair back after all that fuss in the papers,” Sir Hugo laughed. “Disowning your own child over a mésalliance is like disowning one of your eyes: everyone knows it’s yours, and you’ve no other to make a show with.”
“As for mésalliance, there’s no grand blood on either side,” Lady Pentreath scoffed. “Old Admiral Arrowpoint was one of Nelson’s men, a doctor’s son, for heaven’s sake. And we all know where his wife’s money came from.”
“If there was ever a mésalliance in this case,” Deronda put in, “I’d say it was Klesmer marrying up, not down.”
“Ah, you think the immortal genius married the mortal girl! What do you think of that, Mrs. Grandcourt?” Sir Hugo asked, turning to her.
“I’ve no doubt Herr Klesmer considers himself completely immortal,” Gwendolen said, her composure fully recovered now. “And I’ve no doubt his wife will burn as much incense before him as he could ever require.”
“Don’t you approve of a wife burning incense before her husband?” Sir Hugo teased, playful.
“Oh, yes,” Gwendolen replied. “If only to make everyone else believe in him. When Klesmer is too busy admiring his own genius to notice how absurd he sounds, it takes the edge off if his wife says Amen.”
“Klesmer’s no favorite of yours, I see,” Sir Hugo said.
“On the contrary, I think very highly of him,” she corrected quickly, suddenly serious, the sharp edge of her unspoken bitterness over his rejection of her singing career pricking through. “His genius is far above my poor judgment, and I know for a fact he is exceedingly generous.”

Deronda found himself wondering what he would have thought of her if he had met her for the first time that night: he would have guessed she was putting on a thin coating of hardness and defiance to hide some sharp, unspoken pain. But why was she so deliberately unfriendly to him?

To shift the subject, Sir Hugo gestured around the dining hall. “Isn’t this a beautiful room? It used to be the refectory of the old Abbey. Those pillars and three arches used to split the space in two, half as big again as it is now. Long rows of Benedictine monks used to sit right where we’re sitting. Imagine if the lights suddenly dimmed, and the ghosts of all those old monks rose up right behind every chair!”
“Please don’t say that!” Gwendolen laughed, with a playful little shudder. “It’s all very nice to come after ancestors and monks, but they ought to know their place and stay underground. I’d be terrified to wander this house all alone. I expect all those old generations are furious with us for altering their home so much.”
“Oh, I expect the ghosts are all political sorts,” Sir Hugo chuckled. “The ones who spent their whole lives wanting to change things and never got their way would certainly be on our side. But if you don’t want to tour the house alone, you’ll be happy to go in company, I hope? You and Grandcourt ought to see every inch of it. We’ll ask Deronda to lead the tour—he knows more about the place’s history than I do.”

Gwendolen stole a quick, furtive glance at Deronda, who was looking her way as he helped himself to the entree on his plate, his face as impassible as a painted portrait. The thought of him showing her and her husband the estate that, she had heard with a sharp, painful twist, might well have been his if circumstances had been different, sent a rush of tangled, unnameable thoughts through her head, and she knew her glance had come out awkward, furtive, obvious. To cover her betrayal, she laughed lightly. “You don’t know how afraid I am of Mr. Deronda. Ever since that first night at Leubronn. He came over to the roulette table to watch me play, and I started losing immediately. He put a curse on my luck! He didn’t approve of gambling, he told me so himself. Now whatever I do in front of him, I’m sure he’s judging me.”
“Gad, I’m a little afraid of him myself when he disapproves,” Sir Hugo said, grinning over at Deronda, then leaning in to add softly to Gwendolen: “I don’t think most ladies object to having his eyes on them, though.” The old baronet’s usual facetiousness stung Gwendolen, every bit as much as it often annoyed Deronda.
“I object to any eyes that are critical,” she replied coolly, lifting her chin. “Are there many old rooms left in the rest of the Abbey?”
“Not that many. There’s a beautiful cloistered courtyard, with a long gallery running over the top of it. The finest bit of the old place was turned into stables, though—part of the original medieval church. I restored every other part of the house as well as I could, but the stables were far too big a project, so the horses get the run of the old Benedictine choir. You absolutely must go see it.”
“I’d love to see the horses as much as the old building,” Gwendolen said.
“Oh, I’ve hardly any stud to speak of—Grandcourt will look at my horses with utter contempt, I’m sure,” Sir Hugo waved off. “I’ve given up hunting, gone at a slow, jog-trot pace that suits an old fellow with three daughters. We all lived out at Diplow for two years while the Abbey renovations were going on, you know. Do you like Diplow?”
“Not particularly,” Gwendolen said, with deliberate, effortless indifference, as if she had half a dozen family estates of her own to choose from.
“Ah, of course it can’t compare to Ryelands,” Sir Hugo said, delighted. “Grandcourt only took it for the hunting, of course. But he found something far better there,” he added, lowering his voice, “so he could well prefer it to any other house in the whole world.”
“It has one attraction for me,” Gwendolen said, brushing off the compliment with a thin, cold smile. “It’s close to Offendene.”
“Of course you miss your family,” Sir Hugo said, letting the subject drop.

Deronda had not heard most of that exchange, his attention turned elsewhere, but the glimpses he had caught of Gwendolen that night deepened his impression that every part of her manner was newly, deliberately artificial.

Later, back in the drawing room, Deronda sat down at the piano at someone’s request, and sang. When Mrs. Raymond took his place at the keys, he glanced over and saw that Gwendolen had left her seat, drifting to his end of the room as if to listen closer, now standing with her back turned to the whole party, staring at a beautiful carved ivory cowled monk’s head hung over a small side table. He longed to walk over and speak to her. Why not? He would have done the exact same for any other lady in the room. He hesitated for several long seconds, watching the soft graceful lines of her back, before his feet carried him forward.
It is a terrible mistake to stare long at the back of a woman you have an unspoken wish to speak to: the urge to see what face she hides only grows stronger. Deronda rounded the small table, stepping to stand at her side, and before he could utter a single word, she turned to him. There was no sharp, false smile on her lips, no teasing flicker in her eyes—only a raw, pleading look of deep, unguarded sadness, so different from the chill formality of her greeting at the dinner table that the words died in his throat. For a long, suspended beat, the rest of the drawing room faded away, and they looked only at each other: she sinking into the quiet, wordless rest of someone who no longer has to pretend, he answering her with a depth of soft sympathy that erased every other feeling from his mind.
“Will you not join in the music?” he said at last, to break the hush.
The tiny, perceptible shake that ran through her, the quick shift of her features back to calm, showed

*“Nothing weighs so heavy as a secret: the carrying of it far beyond reach is a hardship few ladies can bear, and for that matter, I know full well a great many men who turn as soft and yielding as women in the face of it.” — LA FONTAINE*

Deronda had been tugged away from the others by Mr. Vandernoodt, who craved a brisker walk, a lit cigar, and the kind of unguarded gossip no one could say to a man whose secrets they might accidentally brush against. When they were far enough from the house party’s clusters of guests, Vandernoodt huffed out a laugh around his cigar.
“Grandcourt’s the palest, most washed-out scrap of cambric I’ve ever laid eyes on. If he’s a favorite of yours, I’ll take the remark back at once.”
“Not the slightest,” Deronda assured him.
“I thought not. One can’t help wondering how he worked himself up to a great passion all over again—he must have felt something fierce, to marry a girl out of the blue like that. Though Lush, his old college chum, hints he did it purely out of stubborn obstinacy. By George, that obstinacy is far more accountable than people think. No man would need anyone egging him on to decide he wanted to marry her. But he must have drained his purse pretty dry, eh?”
“I know nothing of his affairs,” Deronda said evenly.
“What! Not about his other establishment?”
“Diplow? Of course. He took that of Sir Hugo’s, but only for the year.”
“No, no—Gadsmere. Sir Hugo knows all about it, I’d stake my life on that.”
Deronda said nothing. He was already curious, and knew full well Vandernoodt would spill the whole story without him stooping to ask.
“Lush won’t admit it outright, of course—he’s Grandcourt’s go-between, his confidant. But I’ve it on the best authority. There’s a lady living at Gadsmere with four children. She’s had Grandcourt under her thumb for ten years and more. Left her own husband for him, traveled everywhere at his side, and her husband’s dead now. I met a man who served in the same regiment as him, who knew this Mrs. Glasher back before she ran off—fiery, dark-eyed, a famous beauty in her day. He thought she was long dead. They say she still has him wrapped around her finger. It’s a wonder he never married her, what with a fine young son to carry his name, and full control over his estates to do whatever he pleases. Lush told me as much himself.”
“What right had he to marry this girl?” Deronda said, sharp with disgust.
Vandernoodt puffed his cigar, shrugged, and pursed his lips.
“She can know nothing of it,” Deronda said firmly—then immediately asked himself, quiet and cold: Could she have known?
“It’s a delightfully piquant little scene,” Vandernoodt went on. “Grandcourt caught between two fiery women. Depend on it, that fair-haired new bride of his has got plenty of devil in her. I thought as much back at Leubronn. It’s a Medea and Creusa sort of story. Fancy the two of them meeting! Grandcourt’s a new kind of Jason, and I can’t wait to see what fool’s part he stumbles into. I can almost hear Ristori wailing out ‘Jasone! Jasone!’ These fierce, brilliant women always get hold of a stick to beat a man like that with.”
“Grandcourt can bite, though. He’s no stick,” Deronda said.
“No, no, I meant Jason. I can’t quite puzzle Grandcourt out, but he’s sharp as a tack, well-built too. If he comes into all that property, the estates will stretch far enough for both households. This girl, whose family was down to begging, I hear, should think herself lucky to land a man like him. I don’t mean to be hard on a man tangled in an old affair of that sort—but he might make himself a little more agreeable. I was telling him a wonderful story last night, and he just got up and walked away mid-sentence. I nearly kicked him. D’you think that’s inattention, or pure insolence?”
“Both, likely. He goes through the motions of politeness, but he never really listens,” Deronda said. He paused a beat, then added, “I’d wager there’s some exaggeration in that story about the lady at Gadsmere.”
“Not a bit, depend on it. It’s all lain quiet for years, people forgot all about it. But the nest is there, and the birds are all settled inside. Grandcourt drives out there regularly, I’ve got solid proof of it. Still, it’s nobody’s business but his own. The whole affair sank under the surface long ago.”
“I wonder how you dug up so much of it,” Deronda said, a little drily.
“Oh, there are dozens of people who know every detail. But old gossip gets packed away like forgotten letters, doesn’t it? I love that stuff. I don’t care for the dusty old scandals of Semiramis or Nitocris that all the stuffy historians dig up, the ones poets write a thousand verses about. What do I care about the faux pas of mummies? You’re the one who likes all that old history, aren’t you—more interested in a lady when she’s got rags for a face and skeleton toes peeking out? Flatter your imagination, does it?”
“Well, if she suffered over love, at least you can take comfort in knowing she’s well out of it now,” Deronda said.
“Ah, you’re thinking of Medea, I see.”
Deronda turned to point out a stand of gnarled, bare giant oaks worth stopping to look at. He could not remember the last time he had turned his thoughts so fiercely to unpacking the hidden truths of a stranger’s private life—not since he was a boy, picking apart every scrap of rumor he could find about the secret of his own birth. Now his mind raced through every possible thread of Gwendolen’s marriage. Could she have learned of Mrs. Glasher and the children, before the wedding? Could that be the reason she shrank from the match, until poverty and desperation pushed her over the edge? He could recall every single word she had ever said to him, and in half of them, he now saw the glimmer of a confession: that she knew she had done something wrong, inflicted some unspoken harm. His own lifelong, quiet experience of being one of the forgotten, unacknowledged children stung sharp in his chest. Was Gwendolen, beneath that bright, proud mask of satisfaction, being eaten alive by a three-headed grief: remorse, disappointment, and the gnaw of jealousy? He had no more patience for teasing himself that she was just a pretty flirt laying traps for him. From that moment on, he decided, he would never evade a chance to speak to her again.

He shook off Vandernoodt soon after, wandering into a dim, empty corner as twilight fell, and spent half an hour turning all these possibilities over in his head. When he resolved to seek her out, he remembered she would be in the drawing room for tea with the other ladies. He was right: Gwendolen had locked herself in her room after lunch, swearing she would not come down for four hours, but by the end of one, she was wild with restlessness, furious to be wasting the last two days of her visit shut away from all chance of seeing or hearing something that might distract her. She straightened her gown, set her face in its usual mask of easy self-possession, and went down to make herself agreeable to the other ladies. Lady Pentreath was in the middle of describing a Regency drawing room, the ridiculous figures all the lords and ladies cut in 1819, the year she was presented, when Deronda walked in.
“Am I intruding?” he asked. “I ought to go back and find the rest of the gentlemen—they’re probably in the billiard room.”
“No, no, stay,” Lady Pentreath waved him in. “They were all getting sick of hearing me talk. Let’s hear what you have to say for yourself.”
“That’s an embarrassing order to fill,” Deronda laughed, pulling a chair up next to Lady Mallinger at the tea table. “I came to mention our songstress, actually—unless you’ve already told everyone about her.”
“Oh, the little Jewess!” Lady Mallinger said. “No, it never even crossed my mind anyone here wanted singing lessons.”
“Every lady knows at least one other person who does,” Deronda said, turning to Lady Pentreath. “I stumbled on an exquisite singer, recently. She’s staying with some friends of mine, the mother and sisters of an old Cambridge chum of mine. She was on the stage in Vienna, but she wants to leave that life and make her living teaching.”
“Swaths of girls do that, don’t they?” the old lady said. “Are her lessons absurdly cheap or absurdly expensive? Those are the only two lures I know of.”
“There’s a third lure for anyone who hears her sing,” Deronda said. “Her voice is exceptional. She’s had perfect training, but it sounds as if every note comes straight from her nature, no effort at all.”
“Why would she leave the stage then?” Lady Pentreath asked. “I’m too old to believe people with first-rate chances ever give them up for nothing.”
“Her voice is too slight for the opera house. It’s a perfect, delicious sound for a drawing room. You who put up with my terrible Schubert singing would be enchanted by hers,” he added, smiling at Mrs. Raymond. “She’d also be happy to sing at small private parties or charity concerts, her voice is more than strong enough for that.”
“I’ve invited her to my drawing room when we go up to London,” Lady Mallinger put in. “You’ll all hear her then. I haven’t heard her sing myself, but I trust Daniel’s recommendation. My girls are going to take lessons from her.”
“Is this a charity project?” Lady Pentreath frowned. “I can’t stand charity music, all thin and wobbly and full of pathos.”
Lady Mallinger, who had promised not to tell anyone Mirah’s sad story, smiled awkwardly and glanced over at Deronda for help.
“It’s a kindness to everyone who wants to hear what good, pure singing sounds like,” Deronda said smoothly. “Everyone with ears would benefit from hearing something better than the overwrought style most people sing in these days. If you heard Miss Lapidoth,” he turned to Gwendolen then, “I think you might even revoke that resolution you made to give up singing forever.”
“I’m far more likely to decide I was right to stop,” Gwendolen said. “I don’t take comfort in the idea of enjoying my own mediocrity, I’m afraid.”
“For my part,” Deronda said, “anyone who does something beautifully makes me want to try, too. Not because I think I can do it half as well, but because they make the thing itself seem worth doing. I don’t mind if my own music isn’t much good—what a dismal world it would be if I thought music itself wasn’t worth caring about. Excellence makes you feel hopeful about life, you know? It shows you how much spiritual wealth the world holds.”
“But if we can never reach that level, it just makes our own small lives feel all the tamer, doesn’t it?” Gwendolen said, sharp, resenting the idea that she was supposed to be happy being second-best.
“That depends entirely on how you look at it,” Deronda said softly. “We’d have a miserable time if we could only take pleasure in the things we do ourselves. A little fumbling, private effort to copy something beautiful is a kind of quiet devotion to it. Most of us should only practice art as a way to learn how to understand and enjoy the things the rare few can do for us. And Miss Lapidoth is definitely one of the rare few.”
“Then she must be perfectly happy, isn’t she?” Gwendolen said, a faint, sharp edge of sarcasm in her tone as she turned to Mrs. Raymond.
“I wouldn’t say that until I know more of her,” Mrs. Raymond said, steady.
“It must have been a terrible disappointment to her, to lose the stage career she’d worked for,” Juliet Fenn said sympathetically.
“I suppose she’s past her prime, though,” Lady Pentreath rumbled in her deep voice.
“On the contrary, she’s barely twenty, and hasn’t even reached it yet,” Deronda said.
“And she’s very pretty, too,” Lady Mallinger added kindly, eager to back Deronda up. “Perfect manners. It’s a pity she’s a strict Jewess, of course, but that doesn’t matter at all when it comes to singing.”
“Well, since her voice isn’t strong enough to scream across an opera house, I’ll send her to my nine granddaughters,” Lady Pentreath declared. “I hope she convinces eight of them they’ve got no business singing anywhere but in church. My theory is half the girls these days need lessons on how not to sing.”
“I’ve already had that lesson, as it happens,” Gwendolen said, looking straight at Deronda. “You see Lady Pentreath is on my side.”
Just then Sir Hugo walked in with the rest of the gentlemen, Grandcourt among them. He leaned against the edge of the tea table, smiling good-humoredly. “What trickery is Deronda pulling on you all, slipping in here alone without the rest of us?”
“Trying to pass off some unknown girl as better than any famous opera singer,” Lady Pentreath said. “A pretty Jewish songstress who’s supposed to wow all these young people. You and I, who heard Catalani in her prime, aren’t so easily dazzled.”
Sir Hugo took the cup of tea his wife handed him, still smiling. “Well, a liberal man has to believe there have been good singers since Catalani’s time, you know.”
“Ah, you’re younger than me. I expect you were one of the men chasing after Alcharisi. Then she went and married and left all of you high and dry.”
“Shameful, that sort of thing—great singers silenced by marriage before their voices even crack. Her husband’s a public thief, that’s what he is. I remember Leroux saying ‘A man might as well pull down a whole peal of church bells and cart them off to the steppes,’” Sir Hugo laughed, setting down his cup and wandering off. Deronda, who had moved to make room for the new arrivals, sat apart from the group for a minute, until he noticed Gwendolen slipping away from Vandernoodt’s attempts at small talk, and walking over to the piano, where she stood looking down at the sheet music stacked on the stand.
No one could blame Deronda for guessing she wanted him to join her. He had noticed before that her first impulse was always to snap and say something sharp, then immediately regret it—and he thought she might be trying to make amends for her cold, prickly tone when he’d spoken of Mirah earlier. He walked to her side.
“Are you relenting, and looking for something to play or sing?”
“I’m not looking for anything at all—but I am relenting,” Gwendolen said, her voice soft, submissive, nothing like her usual bold self.
“May I ask why?”
“I want to hear Miss Lapidoth, and take lessons from her—since you admire her so much. That is, when we go to London. Lessons on how to rejoice in how brilliant she is, and how mediocre I am,” she added, turning to him with a sweet, open smile, the kind he remembered from their first meeting at Leubronn.
“I will be genuinely glad for you to meet her,” Deronda said, smiling back.
“Is she as perfect in every other way as she is in her singing?”
“I can’t swear to that, not quite. I haven’t known her long. But I’ve seen nothing in her that I would change. She’s had a terrible life, troubles starting when she was a tiny child, growing up around nothing but pain. But I think you’ll agree no amount of privilege could have given her more grace, or truer refinement.”
“I wonder what sort of troubles she suffered through?”
“I don’t know all the exact details. But I do know she was on the very edge of drowning herself, in despair.”
“What stopped her?” Gwendolen asked quickly, her wide eyes fixed on his face.
“Some small ray of hope, that made her feel she ought to go on living—that living was worth doing,” he said quietly. “She’s full of piety, and she’ll submit to anything, as long as it feels like duty.”
“People like that don’t deserve pity,” Gwendolen said, impatient. “I’ve no sympathy for women who never do anything wrong. I don’t believe they ever really suffer.” Her fingers twitched, moving fast over the edges of the sheet music at her feet.
“That’s true,” Deronda said. “The weight of knowing you have done wrong cuts deeper, tastes more bitter. We flawed, stumbling creatures will always feel more for the people bruised and scraped raw from fighting their own mistakes than we ever will for the irreproachable ones. It’s the oldest story there is—the lost sheep—but it feels new every single day.”
“That’s just talk, though. No one acts like that, not really,” Gwendolen said, bitter. “You admire Miss Lapidoth because you think she’s blameless, perfect. And you’d despise a woman who’d done something you thought was very wrong.”
“That would depend entirely on how she herself felt about what she’d done,” Deronda said.
“I suppose you’d be satisfied if she was utterly miserable, then,” Gwendolen burst out, impetuous.
“No—never satisfied. I’d be full of sorrow for her. That wasn’t just empty talk. I didn’t mean that a pure, uncomplicated nature isn’t lovely, but the people who would never have caught your interest before can become far more worthy of sympathy once they’ve done something that wakes in them a sharp, burning remorse. Lives get opened up in all kinds of ways. I daresay plenty of people would never have their eyes opened at all, if they didn’t get a hard, violent shock from the consequences of their own actions. And when they’re suffering like that, you care far more for them than you ever do for the people who are smug, comfortable, and perfectly pleased with themselves.” Deronda forgot everything else in that moment, the whole room full of guests, every unspoken rule of politeness, because he could see

[Epigraph, from the old play:
*Aspern.* Pardon, my lord—I speak for Sigismund.
*Fronsberg.* For him? Oh, ay—for him I always hold
A pardon safe in bank, sure he will draw
Sooner or later on me. What his need?
Mad project broken? fine mechanic wings
That would not fly? durance, assault on watch,
Bill for Epernay, not a crust to eat?
*Aspern.* Oh, none of these, my lord; he has escaped
From Circe’s herd, and seeks to win the love
Of your fair ward Cecilia: but would win
First your consent. You frown.
*Fronsberg.* Distinguish words.
I said I held a pardon, not consent.]

Daniel Deronda had been burning to return to London for weeks, his quiet anxiety for the vulnerable young Jewish singer Mirah tangled tight with his burning curiosity to learn more of the enigmatic, learned Mordecai who had spoken of shared heritage and unfulfilled purpose. He failed to beat Sir Hugo there, though—his guardian had hurried ahead of the rest of the family to be ready for the parliamentary opening on the sixth of February, so Deronda took temporary lodgings in Park Lane, fully expecting his old bachelor chambers would be overrun by his old Cambridge friend Hans Meyrick. That much he had prepared for. What he had not braced for was the tangled, unnameable tangle of feeling that awaited him there.

Most of us have seen that old Retzsch sketch: destiny as Mephistopheles, leaning over a chessboard, playing man for his soul, making fake, unthreatening moves to lure his opponent into moving all his defenses away from the real point of attack. The fiend loves to mock careful preparation, luring us to leave our waterproofs at home when he knows full well the skies are about to pour, just to make us stubbornly distrust all weather forecasts later. It is a cruel test of a man’s spirit, when he has carefully mapped out all his plans, steeled his heart to meet one set of hardships, only to find every bit of his precaution slightly misaligned, his good intentions no better than perfectly carved dovetails cut from the wrong starting point. His magnanimity has readied itself to meet cruelty, only to be called to a completely different kind of trial. That was exactly the situation Deronda walked into.

His first thought was bright, uncomplicated amusement. His familiar sitting room had been turned entirely into an artist’s atelier: canvases propped against every wall, loose sketches scattered across every surface, two uncrated chests shipped all the way from Rome spilling over with plaster casts and small antiquities, the lower half of the windows draped in baize to soften the harsh daylight for painting. Blonde, wild-haired Hans was there at the center of the mess, longer locks falling into his eyes, his face crinkling with that familiar impish charm, his high, quick voice rising higher the more excited he got. Their friendship had stayed warm and steady since their Cambridge days, stitched together by long letters and little shared adventures across England and the continent, that old dynamic of Hans spilling every last feeling and secret, and Deronda offering quiet, unjudging indulgence, deepening the longer they knew each other.

“I knew you’d be dying to see my Roman casts and trinkets,” Hans said, after the first hearty clap on the shoulder and round of questions about each other’s travels, “so I didn’t even feel bad dumping all my stuff here. But I’ve found two perfect little rooms in Chelsea, not five minutes’ walk from my mother and sisters. I’ll be out of your hair as soon as they scrape the walls and put in new skylights for me. That’s all I’m waiting for. But you can’t stop great art, can you? The seed of immortality already sprouted in my bones.”

“More like a fungoid growth,” Deronda teased, the way an older brother teases his favorite sibling. “I’d lay odds it’s a growing disease in your lungs.” He wandered over to the sketches propped on his bookcase ledge: five quick charcoal studies of the exact same face, each capturing a different turn of the head, a different soft expression. He stopped a little ways back to study them, and said nothing. Hans went quiet too, picking up his palette to dab idly at the half-finished canvas on his easel.

“Well? What do you think?” Hans finally prompted.

“The full face is a little too massive, the proportions off,” Deronda said, colder than he ever meant to sound. “Otherwise the likenesses are perfect.”

“No, the proportions are right,” Hans said, decisive. “People are just always surprised when they move from a profile to a full face. But I’m going to make her grander, bigger, for my Berenice series. Look at the rest of the sketches over there—this is going to be my masterpiece, and you know what? You’re exactly the model I need for Agrippa.” He moved to Deronda’s side, palette still in hand, then winced like he’d said something stupid. “Wait no, no—forgot you hate sitting for portraits, curse you. Don’t pout. I already found a perfect bloke to pose for Titus. There’s five paintings total, for the series. First one’s Berenice on her knees, clinging to Gessius Florus’s robes, begging him to spare her Jewish people. That’s the one on the easel right now. The second’s her standing on the Xystus with Agrippa, pleading with her people not to get themselves slaughtered for a hopeless rebellion.”

“Agrippa’s legs look ridiculous,” Deronda said.

“They’re perfectly realistic,” Hans said, his face crinkling into a silly grin. “Public men are always wobbly on their pins, aren’t they? ‘Their legs, the emblem of their various thought,’ as some fellow wrote in The Rehearsal.”

“They’re as impossible as the legs on Raphael’s Alcibiades,” Deronda countered.

“Which makes them ideal,” Hans shot back. “Art doesn’t copy life, it intensifies it. Agrippa probably did have terrible, wobbly legs. I just make them impossibly bad, so no one can miss it. But forget the stupid legs for a minute. Third painting: Berenice glowing with joy, when the news arrives that Vespasian is named Emperor, her lover Titus is his heir, and she’s about to be Empress of Rome.”

“You’d better give her a little scroll tucked in her mouth or something, no one’s going to guess that backstory from just a canvas,” Deronda said.

“Good. Let them feel ignorant. It’s an excellent aesthetic effect. Fourth painting: Titus forcing Berenice to leave Rome, even after she shared his palace for ten years. Neither of them wants to part, both heartbroken—invitus invitam, as Suetonius wrote, the unwilling man sending away the unwilling woman. I already found the perfect brutal Roman centurion to pose for Titus.”

“Won’t Berenice look fifty then? She’d have been that old by then.”

“Nah. Just a few mature lines, to show the years have passed. Dark-eyed Jewish beauty wears like solid gold, especially hers. Fifth and last painting: Berenice sitting all alone on the ruins of Jerusalem. Total pure imagination, that’s what should have happened, even if no history book wrote it down. There’s no sixth painting. Nobody knows what happened to her after that, right? The story just cuts off, ragged edge and all, dissolving into nothing—le néant. Sublime, isn’t it? The common crowd would want to see her funeral, her will read, her linen distributed to her servants. Boring. Come look at the easel piece, I’ve made loads of progress.”

“That beseeching pose is really lovely,” Deronda said after a long moment of quiet study. “You’ve been remarkably productive these Christmas holidays, I take it, picking this whole project up when you got back to London.” Neither of them had said Mirah’s name out loud, not yet.

“Nah,” Hans said, dabbing more paint onto the canvas. “I dreamed the whole thing up way earlier. I ran into this spectacular woman in Trastevere, you know—half the grandest, most striking women in Rome are half Jewish. That made me hunt for the perfect story of a Jewish woman in ancient Rome. Like all great learned men, I took the best story that was right there under my nose. I’ll show you the sketch I did of that Trastevere woman as soon as I can find it.”

“Surely she’d make a better model for Berenice than anyone else,” Deronda said, fumbling to put his strange, growing unease into words.

“Not a bit. The model should be the most beautiful Jewess in the whole world. And I found her.”

“Have you even stopped to ask if she’d want to be cast in that role? I can’t imagine any Jewish woman would be happy to be portrayed as the queen who abandoned her people for a Roman conqueror. Does she even know what the whole series is about?”

“Of course she does. I had her throw herself right into that begging pose. My own little mother Mrs. Meyrick sat for Gessius Florus, and Mirah knelt right there, clinging to her knees.” Hans stepped back a little to judge the effect of his latest brushstroke.

“I’m sure she doesn’t even know Berenice’s real history,” Deronda said, a hot, unnameable indignation rising in him that he couldn’t quite justify.

“Oh she does, she read the soft ladies’ history of the Roman emperors. She thinks Berenice was a fiery patriot who got tricked by love and ambition into loving her people’s worst enemy, the Nemesis following her back to the smoldering ruins of Jerusalem all alone. That was her own exact phrase. I couldn’t bear to tell her I made up that whole ending, the part where she sits grieving on the ruins.”

“Show me that Trastevere sketch,” Deronda said, just to give himself something to do before he said something rash.

“Would you mind flipping through that big folio over there?” Hans said. “All my head studies are in there. They’re a total mess, though. You’ll probably find her sketch right next to a drawing of some crop-eared Cambridge undergraduate.”

Deronda flipped through the pages for a minute, then frowned. “These are all portraits of Cambridge blokes, and little landscapes of the countryside. I’ll start from the other end.”

“Nah, she’s somewhere in the middle. I emptied one folio into the other a week ago.”

“Is this one of your undergraduates?” Deronda asked, holding up a soft portrait of a young man’s kind, open face. “It’s a very lovely face.”

“That? That’s Rex Gascoigne. Great guy. I tutored him before he won his scholarship. Should’ve gotten top honors last Easter, but he got sick, so he’s got to stay at Cambridge another year. I’ve got to look him up soon, see how he’s getting on.”

“Here’s the Trastevere woman, I assume,” Deronda said, holding up the dark, vivid sketch of the Roman woman.

“Pfft,” Hans said, glancing at it dismissively. “Too coarse. I was a total unregenerate idiot back then.”

Deronda closed the folio, leaving the Roman sketch sitting out on the table. He hooked a finger under the edge of his coat collar, turned to face Hans, and said, “My scruples might be stupid, Meyrick, but I need to ask you to do me a favor and drop this whole Berenice project with Mirah as the model.”

Hans threw himself into an over-the-top tragic pose, voice rising to a dramatic scream. “What? My series? My immortal, world-famous Berenice masterpiece? Think about what you’re saying, man! You’re not just killing a life, you’re killing an immortality, as Milton would say. Wait one second, let me set down my paints so I can tear my own hair out properly.”

He laid down his palette and brush, flopped backward into a big overstuffed armchair, hung half over the edge, let his long hair fall over his face, hooked his fingers on either side of his head, and stared up at Deronda with comic wide-eyed terror. Deronda couldn’t help but smile.

“Paint as many Berenices as you want,” he said, “but I wish you’d understand—you will, once you think on it—you need to pick a different model.”

“Why?” Hans said, standing up, all his silly playfulness fading into seriousness.

“Because Mirah’s about to step out into the world as a professional singer. Mrs. Meyrick and I have been working so hard to help her build that reputation, make her independent, give her a safe public standing. She’s already got one very important introduction lined up, and I’m planning to speak to Klesmer soon, the great musician, to help launch her career. Soon her face will be everywhere, known by thousands. And… well, I can’t explain all of it, unless you feel what I feel. I’m convinced if Mirah knew all the details, she’d hate the idea of being portrayed this way, as a queen who betrayed her own people for a Roman lover.”

Hans stood listening, thumbs hooked in the belt of his paint-splattered blouse, his face first showing surprise, then amusement, until a huge laugh almost burst out of him. But he saw Deronda’s grave, offended expression, and reined it in. “I’m so sorry for laughing, Deronda. You’ve never given me such a perfect, sweet little advantage over you before. If you’d been talking about anything other than my own paintings, I’d have believed every single word you said, just because you said it. Do you seriously think my five sad little canvases are ever going to get hung on the line at the Royal Academy, all the public lining up to stare at them? C’mon, man! Cider and wild conceit never gave me a dream half that pretty. My work’s going to stay as private as you could possibly wish, no one but my family and a couple of artist friends will ever see it.”

He turned back to his easel to fill the awkward silence. Deronda stood perfectly still, accepting he’d been stupid about the public attention angle—but his core repugnance hadn’t faded one bit. He was cross with himself, cross with Hans, but his practiced calm carried him through the awkward moment. Hans revered his friend too much not to feel a little shy about Deronda being in the wrong for once, but he was never one to back down easily from a whim, and he kept talking while he painted.

“Even if I did have a public mad for my work, lining up to buy every painting like they’re cheap prints of railway scenes with nurses and babies and bonnet boxes, I still don’t see what’s unfair about what I’m doing. Every great painter worth remembering painted the face he admired more than any other, over and over again. He pours his soul right out into those canvases. He paints the people he hates as caricatures. He paints the people he adores as something sacred, something heroic. If a man could paint the woman he loves a thousand times as the Stella Maris, the star of the sea, to give courage to a thousand sailors on a thousand different ships, that would be the greatest honor he could possibly do her. Isn’t that a thousand times better than painting some leering, immodest nude and calling it by some holy, worshipped name?”

“You can weasel out of any objection with big, broad generalities,” Deronda said, a little sharp, “but no question of personal conduct ever gets settled right that way. I can agree with every last grand thing you just said, and still be right that you shouldn’t make Mirah’s face the public face of Berenice. Fine, I admit I was being unreasonable about the public attention.” He hesitated for a long moment. “Even if it’s all just private, though, there are very good reasons you shouldn’t spend all your time painting her through that specific narrative. You know Mirah’s situation is incredibly delicate right now. Until she’s fully independent, with her career solid under her feet, she’s as fragile as a piece of thin Venetian glass—we can’t risk jarring her out of the safe, happy little home she’s found. Are you sure you can be discreet? Forgive me, Hans. I found her half-dead on the river bank, I promised her I’d look after her. You understand that, right?”

“Perfectly,” Hans said, breaking into a warm, good-humored grin. “You think I’m exactly the kind of clumsy oaf who’d shatter every piece of glass I walk near, and break my own skull in the process. Fair enough. Every single thing I’ve loved my whole life has landed me in some scrape, sooner or later. My painting’s the latest scrape, and I’ll be spending the rest of my life clawing my way out of it. You think I’ll mess things up at home with Mirah? No. I’m regenerate. You think I’m head over heels in love with her, and I’ll make a fool of myself, scream and plunge and ruin everything. You’re half right, I am completely head over heels. But you’re wrong about the rest. I’ve been baptized by awe, man. I won’t mess this up. Ask my mother.”

“So you don’t think that love is a hopeless scrape for you, then,” Deronda said, his voice dropping a little deeper, unbidden.

“I don’t call it hopeless,” Hans said, infuriatingly cool, laying down his brushes, shoving his thumbs in his belt, stepping back to squint at his half-finished painting.

“My dear fellow, you’re only setting yourself up for heartbreak,” Deronda said, firm. “Mirah would never marry a Christian, even if she loved him. You’ve heard her talk about her people, her faith, right?”

“That won’t last,” Hans shrugged. “She’ll never meet a tolerable Jewish man. All of them are insupportably advancing, their noses leading the way.”

“She’s desperate to find her lost family, her mother and brother. They’re almost certainly strict, observant Jews.”

“I’ll convert, if that’s what she wants,” Hans laughed.

“Don’t be stupid,” Deronda said, getting heated. “You said this was a serious love.”

“It is serious. You think it’s desperate, but I don’t.”

“I don’t know anything for certain, I can’t say what’s going to happen. But I can’t imagine any surprise bigger than finding out Mirah has feelings for you that you could build that kind of romantic hope on.” Deronda realized a second later he’d

There are those who say the truer, deeper tragedy of Prometheus is not the pain of being chained after he has stolen celestial fire for mankind—but the agony of being thrust into a desolation of ignored, scoffed-at ideas by the unyielding force of unthinking custom, long before he ever manages to tuck that first spark into his fennel stalk to carry to the mortal world. Helpless, throbbing, trapped between the slow drag of poverty and the creep of fatal disease, he finds himself passed by on every side by people who never once bother to glance his way.

People call this kind of unaccountable, foreshadowing intuition “second sight,” and mock the ground it stands on. But anyone who has felt their own deepest yearnings take shape as vivid, living visions knows the truth of it: the deed you are meant to do rises before you whole and complete, a quiet, unshakable blueprint; the future you hunger for (or dread) grows like a seed fed by every small, half-unnoticed impression you absorb through the days. These are not flighty, addled fools, these people—they are no less logical, no less grounded than the sharpest, most practical traders haggling in the market squares. Often their natures are stitched through with a hundred openings, like the hundred-gated Thebes, where far more life and light can pour in than ever could through one narrow, doorman-guarded portal. Of course there are small, pathetic specimens of the visionary, the same way there are pygmy mammals tiny enough to fit inside the finger of a glove. That tiny distant cousin to the elephant does no harm to anyone, but what great, noble kind of person is entirely free of small, ugly, useless specimens that drag the whole genus down? It is a terrifying thing to think of all the different kinds of people the label “patriot” has been applied to; or the crush of elbowing crowds we will find on judgment day when every man who ever called himself an author shows up hauling armfuls of books, or rolling stacks of them on carts.

This long, gentle preamble is only here to lead us to Mordecai. That gaunt, quiet Jewish workman had settled somewhere soft and persistent in Daniel Deronda’s mind after their first meeting, a new, unanswered question he found himself turning over in quiet moments, curious to find out more. Still, that interest was no more than vague, gentle suspense: the consumptive, fervent young scholar who eked out a living mending trinkets at his workbench much the same way Spinoza polished lenses, fit none of the paths Deronda had expected his life to follow.

But that meeting had struck Mordecai like a lightning bolt. For years now, he had felt his physical life ebbing away, even as the vast, quiet loneliness of his spiritual world kept stretching wider. Every last thread of his passionate desire had wound itself tight into one single, all-consuming yearning: to find a young, willing ear he could pour his whole mind into, as a final, living testament. To find a soul kindred enough to take every precious, hard-won truth his short, painful life had birthed, and carry it forward as a sacred mission he was meant to fulfill. Remarkably, that bright, hopeful delusion that so many consumptive patients carry, the fantasy of one day waking well again, had never settled on the thought of his own body healing. All that hopeful energy had been diverted full force into this single, burning hunger for transmission. That yearning, which had first panted up from the dark under the weight of a hundred overwhelming discouragements, had grown into a hope—and that hope into a unshakable, devout belief. Far from being doused by the clear, sharp knowledge that his own body was wasting away fast, that belief only burned brighter, the kind of fierce, expectant faith a person has in a prophecy that only has a handful of days left to come true.

Years back, he had begun to look at every man he passed with a sharp, searching glance, hunting for a possibility that grew clearer and more defined with every month. At first he shaped that ideal man by contrast: he wanted someone who was nothing like him. Tracing all the ways his own weaknesses and disadvantages had brought him rebuffs and barriers, he dreamed of a man who held every trait he needed to feel seen and understood, but wrapped in a life nothing like his own. This man must be Jewish, intellectually sharp, morally fervid—every part of him ready to be filled with all the sacred truths Mordecai carried. But his face must be beautiful, his frame strong, he must have known all the soft refinements of a gentle life, his speech flowing easy and full, his hands free from the sordid grind of scraping for bread. He must be the kind of man who could make the world see how bright and noble the possibilities of his people were, not a wasted, breathless figure like Mordecai, stamped all over with the marks of poverty and fading life.

Mordecai was sensitive to every line of a face, every line of a body, and abroad, then in England, he stared at paintings as often as he stared at strangers, lingering for hours in the National Gallery when he had the time, hunting for grave, noble faces that might feed his hope, faces that could easily belong to a man of his own people. But he always left disappointed. There are barely a handful of paintings across all the galleries of Europe where even the greatest masters managed to capture a face young, grand, and beautiful, where the quiet melancholy there is no weak, passive sorrow, but a foreshadowing of great, brave heroism yet to come. Any visitor to the gallery might remember his gaunt, hollow-cheeked figure, dark eyes sunk deep in his sockets, standing motionless in front of a single canvas, lost in meditation. He always wore a cloth cap edged with black fur, and no painter would ever have asked him to take it off. The other visitors would glance at him and dismiss him as a odd-looking Jewish man who probably made his money somehow scamming tourists out of their cash near the artworks, and Mordecai always knew exactly what they thought of him.

Experience had taught him, painfully, how much a man’s poverty and physical frailty cheapen every idea he speaks out loud—unless he is a Peter the Hermit, thundering from a pulpit that whips the rabble into a frenzy. But he was too sane, too generous, to blame all his spiritual exile on other people’s ignorant prejudices. He knew his own limitations had earned him that sentence of exclusion. And that was why his imagination had built that other man: not just a “second soul” the Kabbalah speaks of, sent to help carry the weight of an insufficient first life, but a blooming, full, vigorous human existence, ready to weave all the most precious parts of Mordecai’s life that was burning away fast into its own, so they might live on together. His deep, inner need for this expanded, prolonged version of himself felt to him like an outward necessity. The thoughts of his heart (that old soft phrase is the only one that fits the truth) felt too precious, too tightly woven into the growing good of the world, to vanish into oblivion. And as that stronger, brighter, more capable version of his ideal companion took shape in his mind, he loved him in advance, with an affection half merged into his own identity, half soft, reverent, and grateful, like the love a man has for his long-awaited beloved.

Mordecai’s mind worked constantly in vivid images, so his trains of thought felt like the meaningful, sacred dreams that waking people in their most creative moments imagine for sleepers—even, sometimes, like real dreams, the kind that blur the line between the world you know and the world you are still reaching for. For years, he pictured the Being he ached for as a figure standing far off, facing away from him, dark and clear against a blazing golden sky. That golden sky was tied to one of Mordecai’s favorite small habits. He saw the quiet poetry hidden in the chaos of London, and whenever his strength and free time allowed, he would walk out to the bridges over the Thames, most often at sunrise or sunset. Even when he was hunched over tiny watch wheels and trinkets at his workbench, or sitting in his tiny garret room staring out at grimy bricks and cracked window panes, his imagination would carry him off to a place with a wide, unbroken view, and his thoughts would unfurl in wide, open spaces. As often as he could, he sought out the real thing, the wide sky, the open air. Leaning on the parapet of Blackfriars Bridge, he would meditate on the wide, calm stretch of the river, its long vista half hazy, half glowing, the grand, dim shapes of the tall warehouses that marked the endless hum of world trade, the barges drifting toward him out of the distant haze, slowly coming into sound and color. All of that blended seamlessly into his thoughts, the way a fine symphony you barely are even aware of listening to holds you up, its music carrying your spiritual wings. That was why the figure of his longing always stood dark against that flood of golden sunset light.

Slowly, as his imagination filled in more and more detail, the figure stopped facing away from him. He began to walk forward, and a face grew clear: the words youth, beauty, refinement, Jewish birth, noble gravity, all resolved into a form that was not just one specific man, but the perfect type, drawn from his memories of the faces of Jews he had seen in Holland and Bohemia, and the old paintings that brought those memories back to life. It is no exaggeration to say that this mature, deep spiritual hunger was akin to the soft daydreams of a young boy or girl picturing the lover they have not yet met—but the faint stirrings of that young desire are nothing next to the fierce, rushing current of ideal life straining to find flesh and blood, made sharper by the constant, imminent threat of dissolution. That visionary form became his constant companion and audience, not just in his waking imagination, but in those soft, light half-slumbers where the truest saying is “I sleep, but my heart waketh”—where the trivial small events of the day before are suddenly charged with all the impassioned purpose of years.

Lately, the pressure of time running out, marked by the way his breath grew shorter and his chest tighter every week, had turned his quiet trust into a restless, watchful waiting for the fulfillment that could not be far off. Was the bell about to toll? Was the sentence he had waited for about to be carried out? The deliverer’s footsteps must be near—this deliverer who would rescue all of Mordecai’s spiritual labor from oblivion, and give it a permanent home in the most precious heritage of his people. Many would have called this yearning an insane, overinflated belief in his own worth, even if his ideas were as true and precious as Columbus’s or Newton’s. They would say it is nobler to shrug and say “If not I, then another” and care nothing for the meaning of your own individual life. But the fullest, most alive natures crave to be the one who acts, the one who creates, not just the one who watches from the sidelines. Strong love hungers to give blessing, not just to see it given to someone else. And as long as there is warmth enough in the sun to feed an energetic, living heart, there will be men who feel, “I am the master of this moment’s turn, and I will fill it with my whole soul.”

Yet for all that certainty in the friend who was coming, Mordecai could not make himself wait passively. He tried a hundred small, pathetically humble little tricks, whatever was within his limited reach, to plant small pieces of himself in the world. It had been two years now since he moved into the Cohen household, where everyone treated him with great good will, a strange mix of skilled workman, tutor, recipient of their charity, pious man, inspired fool, and (if anyone ever dug too deep) dangerous heretic. In that time, little Jacob had grown old enough for knickerbockers, and had developed that sharp, quick eye for trades and bargains that everyone who knew the family had already seen. He had also grown very fond of Mordecai, though he considered him a slightly inferior person, a sentiment that only made him fonder. He took Mordecai’s clever help with his toys and lessons the same way he would take the services of an enslaved, all-powerful genie. Mordecai had given Jacob his first lessons, and his natural soft tenderness had easily turned into the gentle, fatherly affection of a teacher. He was fully aware of the vast spiritual gulf between him and the Cohens, and he would never have dreamed of trying to share his private, sacred world with them—but little Jacob moved him, that soft idealizing love that sees past any small flaws in a child to the bright glow of childhood itself, the endless potential of a long, unwritten future. That feeling had led him, first without planning, then with quiet purpose, to pour bits of his own poems and ideas into the boy’s ear, rambling recitations that would have sounded wildly absurd to any practical businessman who happened to overhear.

No one ever did overhear, though. Jacob would climb up to Mordecai’s garret on a slow workday, or after all the trinkets for the day were mended, and after a quick lesson in English reading or numbers, he would hang around leaning against his tutor’s knees, or clamber astride them, no matter how the wasted, thin limbs ached under his weight. The lure was usually Mordecai’s knack for fixing broken toys, or some tiny clever mechanical trick his years of working with tiny parts had perfected. With the boy thus tethered, Mordecai would begin to repeat a Hebrew poem he had written years before, where he had poured all his youthful first ardor for his sacred, beloved vision of his people’s past and future, telling Jacob to repeat the words after him. “The boy will have these words engraved deep inside him,” Mordecai thought. “It is a kind of printing, a way of planting seeds.” Jacob was happy to play the game, parroting the strange, unintelligible sounds with no complaints, and if no other distraction popped up he would keep at it as long as Mordecai’s breath held out. Mordecai poured all the fervor of a sacred ritual into every repetition. Jacob barely fidgeted, only occasionally pausing to rummage through the treasures in his pockets, or pull at his cheeks to make his eyes look big and scary, or reach out to test the size of his own nose next to Mordecai’s, curious as a small monkey. Mordecai never paused, happy as long as the boy’s young vocal cords kept repeating the words. But more often than not, some sudden impulse would send Jacob leaping away to chase some wild, energetic game, and he would only repeat the last few words he had heard back to himself in a silly singsong rhythm, bouncing around the room, mangling the verse Mordecai had poured so much of his limited strength into writing. But Mordecai waited with the patience of a prophet, undiscouraged, and tried his strange little printing game again the next day, telling himself, “One day these words will rule him. Their meaning will flash bright in his mind. That is how it works for a whole nation, after many, many days.”

Meanwhile, Jacob had a whole new store of magical, unintelligible sounds he used to make his baby sister giggle, chase the big house cat into a dark corner, or threaten to terrify any Christian boy his own age he met on the street. One week he saw a street mountebank performing tricks, and all his new muscle memory went completely off track, away from the beautiful new Hebrew poetry he was supposed to be learning, modeled after the great Yehuda Halevi. Mordecai had just gotten to a new, impassioned section of his poem, the consumptive man’s strong high baritone made hoarse by a haze of cough, his breath catching every few moments, rising almost into a chant as he recited verses that meant:
“Away from me the garment of forgetfulness,
That withers the heart.
The oil and wine from the presses of strangers,
Poisoned through and through with scorn.
Solitude hangs over the slopes of Mount Nebo,
A tomb is hidden in its heart.
There lie the buried ark and golden cherubim,
Shining with hidden light.
There the solemn, unchanging gaze waits,
Their wings spread unbroken.
Shut beneath that silent, awful speech,
The Law lies carved in stone.
Solitude and darkness wrap me around,
My own heart is a tomb.
Strike and shatter it, O Gabriel!
Shatter it like the soft clay of the caster,
Wrapped around the golden image.”

Lost in the wild enthusiasm of that final invocation, Mordecai did not notice Jacob had climbed off his knees and run off. He paused, and suddenly saw the boy was on his hands, feet in the air, doing a perfect mountebank handstand, trying to pick up a shiny farthing he kept among his favorite pocket treasures with his lips. It was a silly trick, one Mordecai was used to—but at that exact moment, it felt like a horrifying, mocking grin from Satan himself, right at the prayer he had just poured his whole soul into. “Child! Child!” he cried out, in a sharp, strange voice that made Jacob jump to his feet in terror. Then he sank backward, shuddering, and closed his eyes. “What?” Jacob yelped. When he got no answer, he shook Mordecai’s knees roughly to rouse him. Mordecai opened his eyes, a fierce light burning in their dark sunken depths, leaned forward, and grasped the little boy’s shoulders, whispering hoarse and fast, “A curse is on your generation, child. They will break open the mountain, drag the golden cherubim wings out, and melt them down to make coins, and they will break those solemn, holy faces into tiny trinkets, earrings for vain, wanton women! They will take new, empty names for themselves, but the angel of shame, with his fiery brand, will know every last one of them. Their hearts will become tombs of dead, hollow desires that rot their lives away from the inside out.”

Mordecai’s face, his voice, his thin fingers digging gently into Jacob’s shoulders were so new, so mysterious, so full of vague, overwhelming threat, it felt like the soft, kind tutor he had always known had turned into some terrible, unknown monster. The little boy shook all over with awe, certain the whole house was going to tumble down around them, that they would never get to eat dinner ever again. But when the terrifying speech ended, and the fingers on his shoulders loosened their grip, the shock dissolved into tears. Jacob lifted his round, little face and wailed at the top of his lungs. The sound of that childish grief immediately

*"Stand before the wise, you see,
Safe you are in certainty!
Though you long have grieved and striven,
He will spot where fault is given.
You may hope for praise sincere,
For he knows where you hit near."*
—GOETHE: *West-östlicher Divan*

The very evening of that quiet visit to the Chelsea cottage, where the Meyronds had debated what public stage name Mirah might use, Daniel Deronda carried home news that would shift the whole shape of the young singer’s future. But for the warm, ragtag Meyrick household, the most life-changing ripple from that conversation landed two days later, at exactly four o’clock. A pair of hansom cab wheels crunched to a stop outside their small front door, followed by a grand, echoing knock and ring so loud it outstripped the usual most exciting sound in their quiet neighborhood: the cheerful tinkle of the muffin man’s bell.

All five women were at home that afternoon, the two small parlours thrown open to make space for Kate’s drawing board and a great sprawling length of shared embroidery that had replaced their old satin cushion projects, a sort of household group project that any free clever hand could pitch in on. Mrs Meyrick bent over one corner of the stretched fabric, Mab in the middle, Amy at the far end. Mirah, whose sewing skills were no more refined than a tailor-bird’s haphazard nest stitch, her childhood education in needlework long neglected, perched light as a sparrow on a camp stool reading to the group. She also served unknowingly as Kate’s model for a vignette title page: a soft, rapt young woman, the very symbol of an audience lost to a book on the family tea table. She was reading Charles Lamb’s *The Praise of Chimney-Sweeps*, her voice clear and honeyed, all of them grinning over the line about “innocent blackness,” when that grand knock dragged their thoughts far from chimney sweeps and cozy essays.

“Goodness!” Mrs Meyrick said, blinking. “Could that be Lady Mallinger? Do you see a grand carriage out front, Amy?”
“Only a hansom cab,” Amy reported, peeking out the window. “It’s a gentleman.”
“Prime Minister, obviously,” Kate said dryly. “Hans says the greatest man in London doesn’t think twice about hopping in a hansom.”
“Oh! Oh what if it’s Lord Russell!” Mab squeaked, bouncing a little on her stool.

All five bright faces were crinkled in amusement when their scatterbrained old maid servant wandered in holding a calling card, leaving the parlour door propped open behind her. Standing on the threshold was no respected gray-haired premier: tall, broad-shouldered, massive-jawed, his bright flamboyant hair tucked neatly back, gold spectacles glinting, his fine kid gloves and kerseymere coat lending him an air of grandeur that made their whole cottage shrink to the size of a dollhouse. The card read *Julius Klesmer*.

Quick as always to read a room, the “little mother” Meyrick felt a surge of quiet relief that the greatest music critic in the city had come calling on Mirah, rather than demanding the orphan girl travel across London to call on him. Even Mab, staring wide-eyed, thought their tiny cottage piano suddenly looked like a silly tin toy, their whole small, happy household no bigger than a family of mice nesting in the Tuileries. But for all his grand, imposing air, Klesmer was no distant snob. He’d grown up in a cottage no bigger than this on the edge of a Bohemian village, and he knew every thread of the quiet romance and hardship that came with scraping by on a small income in the art world.

“I hope I have not overstepped,” he said, bowing to Mrs Meyrick with perfect deference. “I was in the neighborhood, and Deronda told me we might have the honor of meeting the young lady he speaks of so highly—Miss Lapidoth.” He had spotted Mirah the very second he walked through the door, but with gentle politeness he glanced around at all three Meyrick sisters first, as if uncertain which of them was the singer.
“These are my daughters,” Mrs Meyrick said, waving gently toward Mirah. “This is Miss Lapidoth.”
“Ah,” Klesmer breathed, a warm, radiant smile spreading across his face as he bowed low to her. “You will not mind beginning our acquaintance by singing for me now, will you? No use wasting time on small talk.”
Mirah’s face lit up with quiet, unflurried pleasure. She already liked him: she could tell he was the kind of great musician who would tell her the truth, sharp but kind, like her old Vienna teacher. “I would be glad,” she said, moving to the piano. “Shall I accompany myself?”
“By all means,” he said, seating himself as invited, with a clear view of her face. The little mother would never admit it out loud, but she thought to herself, *Of course he’ll like her singing better if he can see how lovely she is*.

Every heart in the room except Mirah’s was thudding with anxiety as he sat there, a slight listening frown on his face, the sisters only daring to glance at him out of the corners of their eyes. They clung to the little comfort that even the grandest prince who had heard every famous singer in Europe thought Mirah’s voice was the sweetest he’d ever heard. Mirah herself was entirely at ease, choosing the setting of Leopardi’s grand ode to Italy that her old teacher Leo had written for her, the recitative soft and burning, the mournful, rhythmic plaint of the “But I see no glory” line winding into a triumphant, devout climax. When the last note faded, Klesmer sat quiet for a long beat.
“That is old Leo’s work,” he said finally.
“Yes,” Mirah said, a soft sad smile on her face. “He was my last teacher in Vienna. Fierce as a storm, and kinder than anyone I ever knew. He said my voice was never meant for the opera stage. He was right.”
“Sing more, please,” Klesmer said, pursing his lips, shaking out his long fingers, muttering half-voiced notes no one else in the room could understand. The three girls all secretly loathed him a little for not uttering a single word of praise. Mrs Meyrick’s chest grew tight with alarm. Mirah, simply eager to give him exactly what he asked for, moved straight into Gretchen’s songs from *Faust*, no pauses, no asking permission, letting the melodies spill out unbroken.

When the last note died, Klesmer stood, paced the full length of their tiny parlour, then walked back to the piano where Mirah had risen, her little hands folded meekly in front of her, waiting for his judgment. All at once his frown melted away, his eyes bright, he held out his hand abruptly. “Let us shake hands,” he said. “You are a musician.”

Mab had to press a hand to her mouth to stop herself from sobbing. All three of the girls instantly thought Klesmer was the most wonderful man alive. Mrs Meyrick let out a long, long breath she had not realized she was holding.
Then the familiar sharp seriousness settled back on his face, his hand turning palm-down to tap lightly at the back of Mirah’s knuckles. “But not for great stages,” he said. “No high vast opera roofs. You are no skylark, flying to the rafters. We must be modest.” He paused, and Mab’s adoration faded fast—how dare he assume Mirah was the slightest bit conceited? Mirah said nothing, quiet, waiting for the full weight of his advice. “I would never advise you to sing in any space larger than a private drawing room. But there, you will shine. In London, that is one of the kindest, most rewarding careers a singer can have. You will give lessons, you will be invited to perform at private gatherings, you will make a steady, dignified living. I am hosting a small private concert at my house next Wednesday. Will you come sing for the guests?”

Mirah’s hands flew together, her face glowing. “Oh, I would be so grateful. That is exactly the life I wanted—far better than screaming for crowds in a public theatre. What should I work on most to improve?”
Klesmer rumbled a few half-spoken notes, shaking out his fingers, before he spoke clearly again. “I will introduce you to Astorga. He is the foster-father of all good singing, and he will give you every piece of guidance you could ever want.” He turned back to Mrs Meyrick. “Mrs Klesmer will call on you before Wednesday, if you will permit it. She is a thorough musician, and has more ears in her soul than most singers you will ever meet. Your singing will satisfy her. *Vor den Wissenden sich stellen*—you know the rest?”
“*Sicher ist’s in allen Fällen*,” Mirah finished promptly, the old Goethe line about safety before those who truly understand rolling off her tongue like second nature. Klesmer nodded, charmed, saying *Schön!* and held out his hand to say goodbye.

Then he suddenly fixed his gaze on Mab. “That young lady is musical, I can see it,” he said decisively. Mab blushed so hot she felt like she was on fire, certain he would order her to sit down and play for him, her heart turning to molten wax in her chest. Mirah spoke up for her quickly. “She is, she has a lovely little touch on the piano.”
“Please, it’s just a scramble, not a touch,” Mab moaned, half in agony. But her joy burst so wide she could barely contain it when Klesmer turned to Mrs Meyrick and said, “Would she like to come along to the recital on Wednesday, to hear the rest of the music?”
“There could not be any greater pleasure for her,” Mrs Meyrick said, smiling.

Klesmer bowed grandly to all three sisters, so deep the diagonal of their small parlour could barely contain his elegant bend, his face as grave and stately as the old Holbein portraits hanging on their walls. Mrs Meyrick slipped out to the landing with him, closing the door behind them. He nodded his frowning, kind nod. “She will do well, if she never tries to overstrain her voice. Deronda told me supporting herself is her top priority. She will make a solid income. You are taking good care of her. She looks like a good girl.”
“She is an angel,” Mrs Meyrick said, warm and fierce.
“No,” Klesmer said, playful. “She is a pretty Jewess. The angels don’t get to take credit for her. I think she has found a guardian angel, though,” he finished, bowing and letting himself out.

Back inside, the four young women stared at each other in silence till the front door banged shut behind him. Then the room erupted. Mab clapped her hands and danced wildly around the room, nearly tangling herself in the great embroidery stretch. Mrs Meyrick kissed Mirah and blessed her. Amy said, very practically, “We can never get her a new dress made before Wednesday!” Kate laughed and exclaimed, “Thank heaven my drawing table didn’t get knocked over!” Mirah had sat back down on the piano stool, silent, tears rolling down her cheeks as she looked at her new family.

“Come now, Mab, sit reasonably,” Mrs Meyrick said, laughing. “Let’s talk this through.”
“Yes, talk!” Mab said, dropping down onto her low stool, hugging her knees. “I feel like I’m a normal size again, not a silly toy. Hans said he was coming this afternoon. I wish he’d been here—though there wouldn’t have been room for him. Mirah, why do you look sad?”
“I’m too happy,” Mirah said, wiping at her tears. “I’m so full of gratitude to all of you, and he was so very kind.”
“Only at the very end!” Mab said, sharp. “I hated him while he was frowning and just saying ‘continue’. I thought he was dreadfully ugly from the top of his shiny hair to the tip of his polished boot. People shouldn’t hoard all their praise just so they can pop it out like a surprise later, make you miserable before they make you happy. But I forgive him everything, now that he invited me to the recital. How did he know I was musical? Is it my bulging forehead, ma? Do I look like a newt peeking out from under a stone?”
“He saw the way you were leaning forward, listening to every note of Mirah’s singing,” Mrs Meyrick said. “He’s got magic spectacles, that man, he sees everything.”

Just then Hans Meyerick walked through the door. They immediately laid out their problem, asking his artist’s eye what Mirah should wear. Hans knelt on a hassock, surveying her plain black merino dress from a few steps back. Mirah pleaded that it was good enough, the kind of costume she would have worn on stage playing a poor Jewish girl singing to fashionable drawing rooms. But Hans, suddenly uncharacteristically fastidious, shook his head. “It’s a little too theatrical. You don’t want everyone there to look at you and think you are a charity case. You have talents that will soon make people indebted to you. You’re not going to sit out on ruins. Take her to Regent Street, little mother. Buy her a proper black silk dress, the kind any lady would wear to a private concert. There’s more than enough time before Wednesday.”
Mrs Meyrick nodded. “I think Mr Deronda would like that, too. For her to look like she belongs.”
“Of course he would,” Hans said, a little sharp, turning away to flip idly through Kate’s drawings, a little stung that Mirah had immediately agreed when they mentioned Deronda’s name. He plopped down at their little cottage piano, throwing his head back, mimicking Klesmer’s grand, sweeping air as if he were surveying a whole opera house full of people, then turned to Mirah, half shy. “Stop me if you think this mockery is rude, won’t you?”
Mirah smiled, soft and sweet, warm as summer. “I could never dislike anything that comes from you,” she said, glancing over at Mrs Meyrick.

Hans’s chest swelled with quiet, bright hope. How could he help it, when it felt like every little sweet thing Mirah said to him was a sign that he might be the one to pluck that soft rose for his own?

*“Within the soul a faculty abides, That with interpositions, which would hide And darken, so can deal, that they become Contingencies of pomp; and serve to exalt Her native brightness, as the ample moon, In the deep stillness of a summer even, Rising behind a thick and lofty grove, Burns, like an unconsuming fire of light, In the green trees; and, kindling on all sides Their leafy umbrage, turns the dusky veil Into a substance glorious as her own, Yea, with her own incorporated, by power Capacious and serene.” —WORDSWORTH: *Excursion*, B. IV.*

Daniel Deronda stepped out of the narrow Chelsea house with a restlessness coiled tight in his chest, craving hard, physical exertion to burn off the fuming, unnameable temper he could not quite shake. He was bound for the City, and the sight of the Chelsea Stairs, with their line of waiting wherries, settled his decision at once: he would not suffer the jolting, frustrating inaction of a cab ride, but hire a boat and pull the oar himself.

His errand was Ram’s bookshop. He had arrived too late to meet Mordecai at his midday post there the day before, and had been told the quiet, remarkable Jewish man never failed to return between five and six. Deronda ached to deepen their strange, fleeting acquaintance before he redeemed his ring, desperate to avoid the sharp, sudden drop of Mordecai’s interest that had felt like a drawbridge slamming shut on their last meeting, threatening to seal off all easy future connection for Mirah’s sake. As the work of the oar warmed his muscles, he fixed his thoughts on the purpose ahead, and as was his wont, his perspective shifted gently: he stopped seeing Mordecai only as a source of useful information, and chided himself softly for being little better than a recruiting sergeant, blind to the full, complicated inner lives of the people he sought help from.

“If I got every piece of information I most want from him,” he thought, “I daresay I’d be perfectly content to never hear more of his story, never learn why he looked at me as if he had a long-held expectation that I had disappointed. That strange, sharp curiosity he stirs in me would die out. And yet what if we were two passing ships, each carrying an exile who would have recognized the other at once, if only they’d leaned over their rails at the same moment? There’s no great chance of some peculiar tie between that poor, ailing man and me — his voyage can’t last much longer, I’d wager. But I wonder how many thousands of momentous, missed connections happen every day between strangers who only glance blankly at each other, or even hurry to get out of each other’s way in a crowd. And all because people go about their business like that recruiting sergeant, never stopping to see the full soul beside them.”

The wherry was gliding toward Blackfriars Bridge, where Deronda planned to land, when the clock struck half past four. The gray day was dying gloriously, western clouds streaked into narrowing purple strata beneath a wide, soft saffron sky that held the quiet grandeur of a carved monument. On the river, that light danced and shifted, gilding every ripple, turning the brown sails of passing craft to sudden flame, leading laden barges out of shadow and into honeyed color as if the whole waterway was singing a response to the sinking sun’s brooding glory.

Thoroughly warm now, Deronda set down the oar and pulled his Inverness cape back over his shoulders. As he lifted his head to fasten the top button, his eyes caught a face he had never been able to stop thinking of, looking straight toward him over the bridge parapet.
