Lit by that western glow, it blazed with startling, unearthly distinctness: a portrait of bodily emaciation burning with spiritual eagerness. It was Mordecai. He, too, had been watching the river westward, and had caught sight of the approaching boat, held it fast in his gaze, first out of idle curiosity, then with a rush of returning memory that made him quiver as if caught in a premonition — until the figure on the boat lifted its face to his, the face of all his long-held visions, and lifted a white, outstretched hand to wave at him again and again.

Deronda, desperate that Mordecai see him and wait, had signaled at once. The answer came immediately: Mordecai tore off his cap and waved it, every part of him thrumming with the certainty that his years-long inner prophecy was fulfilled. All obstacles, all absurd, unlikely coincidences, melted away in the flood of completion that swelled his soul. His exultation was no different from that of a scientist watching the first stirrings of a long-experimented reaction he has dreamed of for years. The friend he had prefigured for so long had come from out of that golden sunset light, and signaled to him. It was real. The rest would follow.

In three minutes Deronda had landed, paid his boatman, and was striding to join Mordecai, who stood perfectly still, rooted to the spot by instinct, waiting for him.

“I was so glad to see you standing here,” Deronda said. “I was heading straight for the bookshop to look for you. I was there yesterday — I expect they mentioned it to you?”

“Yes,” Mordecai said, simple and grave. “That is why I came to the bridge.”

The answer was startlingly mysterious. For a split second Deronda wondered if Cohen’s hint of mental unbalance in the strange scholar was true. “You had no way of knowing I was at Chelsea, did you?” he asked after a pause.

“No. But I expected you to come down the river. I have been waiting for you these five years.” His deep, sunken eyes locked on Deronda’s with a look of affectionate, solemn dependence that was at once heartbreaking and awe-inspiring. Deronda’s heart softened completely, even as he could not help but assume this fated connection Mordecai spoke of was built on illusion.

“It would make me happier than I can say if I can be of any real use to you,” he said earnestly. “Shall we take a cab to wherever you wish to go? Your breath is short — you must have tired yourself out walking already.”

“Let us go to the bookshop. It will soon be time for me to be there. But first look up the river,” Mordecai said, turning back toward the water, his voice low and thrumming with a kind of excited calm, so wrapped up in the sense that his long wait was over he could not imagine any barrier to perfect, total understanding between him and Deronda. “See how the sky is fading slow. I have always loved this bridge. I stood on it when I was a little boy. It is a meeting place for spiritual messengers. What the old masters said is true — every order of things has its angel, its full message from far away. Here I have listened to the messages of earth and sky. When I was stronger I would stay here to watch the stars come out in the dark heavens. But this time of day, just before sunset, was always my favorite. It sank into me and lived with me — fading, slow fading, it was the mirror of my own decline. It paused, it waited, until at last it brought me my new life — my new self, who will live on long after this breath of mine is all breathed out.”

Deronda did not speak. He felt strangely, powerfully moved. His first, easy suspicion that Mordecai was delusional, a monomaniac driven mad by disease, melted away into quiet, submissive expectancy. His nature was too wide, too ready to imagine worlds beyond his own narrow experience, to fall back on the lazy, cruel label of “madness” whenever he met a soul whose full conviction fell somewhere where his own was blank. It was in his nature to meet, rather than resist, any claim of another’s need on him — and this claim wrapped him in a solemn hush that made Mordecai’s ragged poverty vanish completely, lifting him into something like authority, as if the mean disguise of a wandering poor scholar had fallen away to reveal a sacred, preordained guide.

“Let us go now,” Mordecai said after a long, silent pause, and once they were settled in the rattling cab he added, “We will get down at the end of the street and walk the rest of the way to the shop. You can browse the books, and Mr. Ram will leave soon, and we will be alone.” The man who had seemed so wrapped up in his visions was as cautious, as attentive to the judgment of other minds, as any worldly, practical man of business could be.

Mirah was still at the back of Deronda’s mind through every strange beat of this experience, but he knew at once that the conversation from here on would be steered by Mordecai, not himself. He was no longer certain what questions he would be able to ask, and he thought to himself, half laughing at his own awestruck mood: “I suppose I am completely superstitious now, waiting for an oracle to reveal my destiny. But what greater, more powerful force has ever moved the world than faith, even when it is misplaced? Than long-held expectation, even when it has been disappointed a thousand times? If it is ever in my power to fulfill what this man expects of me, I will not let him down.”

Ten minutes later, the two men, thrumming with the same intense, unspoken awareness as two unconfessed lovers, found themselves alone in the small, gas-lit bookshop, turning face to face. By instinct they both bared their heads, wanting to see each other fully, no shadows, no pretense. Mordecai leaned his back against the little counter, and Deronda stood against the opposite wall barely four feet away. There was no denying the contrast between them: Mordecai’s face, sharp and gaunt as an abandoned temple, the mark of consumption softening his features but making his eyes burn brighter, the black hair and beard throwing the yellow pallor of his skin into stark relief, his wasted hands folded across his chest. His gaze held all the yearning of a dying mother looking at the only son she knows will carry her memory forward, that same flicker of fragile, bright gladness.

Across from him, Deronda’s face held the rich, unspent health of youth, his masculine gravity lending a quiet weight to the reverence with which he met Mordecai’s gaze. His rare, exquisite sympathy, his instinct to meet another’s deepest feeling without pre-judgment, was never more fully tested. He did not truly believe Mordecai’s conviction that they were fated to meet was rooted in fact, but he heard the cry from the very depths of the other man’s soul, and felt the clear summons to be receptive, rather than proud and dismissive. That receptiveness gave his face an expression of calm, benignant force that nourished Mordecai’s confidence, and cleared the path for him to speak.

“You cannot know what has guided me to you, to bring us together at this moment. You are wondering.”

“I am not impatient,” Deronda said. “I am ready to listen to whatever you wish to tell me.”

“You see some of the reasons why I needed you,” Mordecai said, quiet, careful not to waste his remaining strength. “You see I am dying. You see I am like a man locked behind bars by the side of the road, who if he speaks to passersby will only get head shakes and pity. The day is closing, the light is fading — soon we would not have been able to make each other out. But you have come in time.”

“I rejoice that I am here in time,” Deronda said, thick with feeling. He would not say I hope you are not mistaken in me — the word “mistaken” would have been unforgivably cruel in that moment.

“But the hidden reasons I needed you began long, long ago,” Mordecai went on. “They began in my youth, when I studied in a different land. Beloved ideas came to me, because I was a Jew. They were a sacred trust I had to fulfill, because I was a Jew. They were an inspiration, because I was a Jew, and I felt the great heart of my people beating inside my chest. They were my whole life. I was not fully born until that moment. I counted every breath, every waking moment, every bite of food I ate to keep my body going, every sight I saw, nothing but fuel for that divine flame. But I was like a man who wanders into rocky solitudes to carve his thoughts into stone, before he can make his way back, care, labor, and disease block his path, and bind him with chains that eat into the soul. And then I asked myself: how do I save the life inside me, before it is stifled along with my failing breath?”

He paused to rest his ragged breath, and to calm his rising excitement. Deronda dared not speak: the silence in that narrow little shop hummed with awe and compassion, wrapped around Mordecai’s fervor.

“You may misunderstand me,” Mordecai said after a moment. “I am no ignorant dreamer, reared in inland valleys, thinking ancient thoughts new and not knowing they are old, who never stood by the great ports where the world’s knowledge passes to and fro. English is my mother tongue, England is the native land of this body, which is nothing but a crumbling pot of earth around a fruit tree whose seed could make the desert bloom. But my true life was nourished in Holland, at the feet of my mother’s brother, a Rabbi of great learning. When he died I went to Hamburg to study, then to Göttingen, to take a wider view of my people, and of the Gentile world, drinking knowledge from every source. I was young then, free, I traveled to all the great Jewish communities in Germany, and I was not in utter poverty. I learned a handicraft too. I told myself, it does not matter if my lot is like that of Joshua ben Chananja: after the great destruction he earned his bread making needles, but in his youth he had sung on the steps of the Temple, and carried the memory of the glory before it departed. I chose that fate knowingly. People called me a man who feeds on visions, and I did not deny it — for visions are the creators and feeders of the whole world. I see the world exactly as it is, and the vision will remake it. You are not listening to a madman who raves apart from the lives of his fellow men.”

Mordecai paused, and Deronda, sensing he waited for a response, said, “Do not think I would ever call your words raving. I listen to know, without prejudice. I have known enough myself to feel deep interest in the story of a spiritual destiny embraced willingly, in youth.”

“A spiritual destiny embraced willingly in youth?” Mordecai corrected gently. “It was my soul being born fully, when I was still a boy. It brought its own world — a medieval world, where men made the ancient Hebrew language live again in new psalms of exile. They wove Gentile philosophy into Jewish faith, and still yearned for a center to gather our scattered people. One of their souls awoke in me, and traveled through their world, into Spain and Provence, debating with Aben-Ezra, sailing with Jehuda ha-Levi, hearing the roar of the Crusaders and the shrieks of tortured Israel. And when its tongue was loosed, it spoke that old language, made alive again with the blood of their ardor, their sorrow, their martyred trust. While it is imprisoned in me, it will never learn another.”

“Have you written entirely in Hebrew, then?” Deronda asked, thinking back to that first meeting, when Mordecai had asked if he knew the tongue.

“Yes,” Mordecai said, his voice heavy with sadness. “In my youth I wandered into that solitude, not knowing it was a solitude. I had all the great dead around me, the martyrs gathering to listen. But soon I found the living were deaf to me. At first I saw my life stretching long before me — I thought part of my Jewish heritage is unbreakable patience, the skill to find a foothold even where all others say nothing can grow. But then new messengers came to me from the Eternal. I had to bow under the same yoke that weighs on every person born of woman: family troubles called me away, I had to work, to care for others, not just for myself. When I was left alone again, the angel of death had already turned to me, and I felt his robes brushing my heels every step I took. I never stopped trying. I begged for help and understanding, went to every man of my people with influence, or wealth, or learning. But none would listen. I was rebuked for error, offered a few coins in charity. No wonder. I looked poor, carried a bundle of Hebrew manuscripts, and said our leading teachers are misleading the hope of our whole race. Scholar and merchant were both too busy to listen. Scorn stood between me and every one of them. One man said the Book of Mormon would never work in Hebrew, and that it was absurd to think a ragged stranger like me could teach the learned men of our people anything. He was not entirely wrong.”

“Even if you wrote in Hebrew for years,” Deronda said gently, trying to offer him comfort, “you surely command English better than almost anyone.”

Mordecai shook his head slowly. “Too late. Too late. I can write no more. My writing now would be as ragged as this gasping breath. If I wrote in English now, I would be like a man beating on a wooden board to summon people who have only ever answered the ringing of a bell. My soul knows the flaws of its own speech. Any new writing from me would be just like this body — it might hold the divine breath of thought, but people would smile and say ‘only a poor Jew’ and the cruellest smilers would be my own people.”

His head sank, despondency wrapping around him like a dark cloud for a moment, and Deronda leaned forward, his voice clear and warm, a comfort all its own. “I feel with you. I feel very strongly with you. But forgive me for speaking too fast — nothing you have written has to be buried forever. The means to publish it are well within your reach. If you trust me, I can make sure everything you need to see it done is provided for.”

“That is not enough,” Mordecai said, looking up at once, the light of his confidence blazing back. “That is not all I trust you for. You must not only be my hand — you must be my soul, believing my belief, moved by my reasons, hoping my hope, seeing the vision I point to, beholding the same glory I behold

“This, too is probable, according to that saying of Agathon: ‘It is a part of probability that many improbable things will happen.’”
—ARISTOTLE: *Poetics*

The fire in Deronda’s blood had not cooled hours after he’d parted from Mordecai, the gaunt, fervent Jewish visionary who locked eyes with him on the bridge as if he had been waiting for Daniel his whole life. For a man far less sensitive, far less prone to turning every small encounter over in his mind until he uncovered its hidden threads, that meeting would have been little more than a curious oddity of a London afternoon. But for Deronda, who felt every current of the world around him far deeper than most, who had spent years questioning the quiet, unmoored state of his own life, that chance meeting had torn a hole in the fabric of the predictable days he had been drifting through, and now he found himself fighting not to be swept away entirely.

He was sharp enough to feel quiet alarm at how thoroughly Mordecai’s burning certainty, his absolute, unshakable trust that Deronda was the long-awaited companion to carry his life’s work forward, had wrapped around his heart. It was never his nature to brush off a soul that had laid itself bare before him, to dismiss as trivial a moment that felt as sacred as the turning points he had read about in old histories—the moments that changed lives and shaped centuries. He found himself thinking: if he had read this story in an old scroll, of a young man just as unmoored as himself, living two thousand years ago in Palestine or Rome, who stumbled on a dying dreamer who claimed he was fated to be the keeper of a great, burning purpose—would he not have understood completely why that young man’s whole world shifted? Why, simply because he wore a white tie to dinner, lived in a world of polite society that laughed too easily at any earnest passion as pretension, should he feel ashamed of being moved? He would not cower before the mocking wit of people too dull to feel any great emotion, who sneered at anyone who dared to take their own life’s purpose seriously.

But nor would he let himself be swept along by mere feeling, by the contagion of Mordecai’s fervor, without giving his reason its due say. He would not let his pity for a man wasting away before his eyes drag him down a path he could not yet see clearly.

What had actually happened, after all? He could hear Sir Hugo’s practical, worldly voice in his head, clear as day: A consumptive Jew, driven half out of his mind by the double weight of his failing body and the consuming passion of his faith, has fixed on you as the hero of his own private dream, a figure he conjured out of despair for his own short life and desperate hope that his work would not die with him. It’s a strange case, but far from rare. Fanatics are not quite as common as bankrupts, but you find them everywhere: the man who swears he has rewritten Newton’s laws of physics and all the learned professors are conspiring to hide his discovery, the man who has invented a perfect metaphysical system no one else can see, all of them begging for someone, anyone, to listen to them. This Mordecai is more poetic, more touching in his speech than most, but he falls right in with the rest. You can be kind to him, help him where you can, but there is no reason to take his wild notions seriously. Any man of the world would tell you that. And when he realizes you are not the savior he imagined, that will be the cruelest, final disappointment of his life.

Deronda let all those cold, reasonable thoughts wash over him, repeated them to himself slowly, testing their weight. He had faced this question before, but never so urgently: how do you tell the difference between a true prophet, the man who will change the course of human history, and a harmless monomaniac chasing a fantasy of perpetual motion? Both hold their convictions with the same unshakable ferocity, both defy every skeptical voice that tells them they are wrong. You cannot sort them out with a simple test, a quick label to dismiss one or exalt the other. If we wait for the ages to judge who was right and who was mad, then nothing will ever get done. The steam engine would have never existed if James Watt had not been taken seriously in his own time, by the people who could see what he was reaching for, before it was proven real.

That line of thinking was old, familiar ground to Deronda, and it kept him from writing Mordecai off out of hand, even before he considered the strange, personal claim the dying man had laid at his feet: the quiet, almost feverish certainty that Deronda was of Jewish blood. That claim, on the surface, looked absurd, impossible to prove. But it did something far deeper than appeal to his ready sympathy: it laid hold of his unquiet, searching conscience.

Deronda’s conscience was not a rigid set of rules. It was stitched together from a thousand small, empathetic imaginings, a habit he had built since he was a boy of stepping fully into the lives and hearts of other people. This man was asking him to share his beliefs, to see the vision he had spent his whole life chasing, to hope the same hopes he hoped. To say yes outright, to pretend he already believed what Mordecai believed, would be a lie, a cruelty he could not bring himself to commit. But the strangest part of it all was that the entire claim rested on a single unproven assumption: that Daniel himself was Jewish.

And Deronda had lived his whole life around an unproven assumption, too. Since he was thirteen, he had been told Sir Hugo was his father, a fact he had never been able to confirm, a secret that had been the source of every deep, fierce conflict of his young life. He was already used to shaping his life around a truth that might one day be overturned, of waiting for a revelation that would redefine every duty he owed to the world. What if this wild, impossible claim of Mordecai’s was the foreshadowing of that very revelation? What if the purpose he had been searching for, the missing piece that would turn his aimless, drifting life into something with weight and meaning, was waiting for him in Mordecai’s ideas?

He thought: if that pull had come from a respected professor at a great university, from a famous philosopher the whole world admired, no one would dare call his willingness to listen weakness. It was only the vulgar, small-minded prejudice of society that dismissed the ideas of a poor Jewish workman who lived in a tiny room above a public house as automatically worthless. He remembered the old legend of Emperor Domitian, who sent soldiers to arrest the supposed Messiah descended from King David, only to release him unharmed when he saw the man’s calloused workman’s hands. The old Rabbi waiting at the gates of Rome was just as sure the Messiah would be found among the destitute, the ragged, the forgotten. Neither of them was right about outward signs—but no one could deny that some of the most powerful, world-altering inspirations in history had belonged to people with rough, work-worn hands, no money, no fine name to back them. To dismiss Mordecai out of hand just because he was poor would be the height of foolishness.

Of course, Mordecai’s fevered, visionary temperament did make him leap to conclusions, turn every small coincidence into the fulfillment of his dreams. But Deronda thought of Columbus, too, who clung to his own wild, supposedly foolish visions even when every other scholar laughed him out of the room. Columbus had his own superstitions, his own flawed arguments, and everyone who refused to listen to him is a joke in the history books now. He would not make that same small mistake, Deronda swore to himself, writing Mordecai off as a madman without giving him a full hearing.

He traced the threads that had led him to that meeting, step by step, and saw that none of them were accidental. If he had not met Mirah, the young Jewish singer he had pulled from the Thames, he would never have begun to care so deeply for the fate of her people. He would never have wandered the bookshops of London searching for her lost brother Ezra Cohen, never stopped at that old stall to ask the price of a copy of Maimon’s writings, never been seen by Mordecai, who had already spent years imagining the face of the disciple he was waiting for.

What if Mordecai’s hunch was right? What if he really was Jewish? The very thought sent a strange, thrilling shiver through him, that sense of romantic adventure that stirs in every young man’s breast, the pull to uncover the secret of his own birth and claim the legacy that was meant for him. He would not admit he was eager for the belief that Sir Hugo, the man who had raised him, was not his real father—his lifelong love for Sir Hugo was too deep, far stronger than any small resentments he had ever felt. But nor could he dismiss the possibility out of hand.

At the very least, he decided, he would not rush to dig for proof of his birth right now. If Mordecai was wrong, if Deronda was not Jewish at all, that uncertainty would soften the blow of his disappointment, when the dying man realized he would never get the disciple he had dreamed of. He could be Mordecai’s friend, help him, care for him, without making any promises he might not be able to keep.

For four long days, these thoughts circled through his head, even as Sir Hugo dragged him out to dinners and meetings that ran far too late to let him make the trip out to Holborn, to Ezra Cohen’s house, to keep the promise he had made to Mordecai.

*"If there are ranks in suffering, Israel takes precedence of all the nations—if the duration of sorrows and the patience with which they are borne ennoble, the Jews are among the aristocracy of every land—if a literature is called rich in the possession of a few classic tragedies, what shall we say to a National Tragedy lasting for fifteen hundred years, in which the poets and the actors were also the heroes?"*
—Zunz, *Die Synagogale Poesie des Mittelalters*

Daniel Deronda had turned that passage over in his chest for days, so it rose sharp and unbidden in his mind as he walked toward the Cohens’ pawnshop, a bright brass-trimmed storefront that bore not a single trace of the sublime, age-worn tragic nobility he’d been reading of. Ezra Cohen cut no figure as a suffering martyr; his knack for turning every shilling he touched into profit was exactly the kind of exasperating, thriving good fortune that had marked Jewish tradespeople for centuries of petty, bitter resentment. This cheerful, unpretentious pawnbroker was no grand symbol of 1500 years of national grief—yet Deronda could not shake the quiet, striking pathos of it: Mordecai, that frail, breath-thin living vessel of his people’s collective consciousness, nested right here in the Cohens’ loud, warm, unthinking little bubble of hard-won prosperity.

The whole family’s faces lit up like lit lanterns the second Deronda stepped over the threshold. Cohen himself made a point of saying he could have made more interest by holding onto Deronda’s diamond ring a few days longer, but he would not trade that profit for anything—his wife, his mother, the children had done nothing but chatter about his first visit since he left. Young Mrs. Cohen first fretted that the baby was fast asleep, then brightened that Adelaide was still up, and begged him not to linger in the drafty shop front, but come straight back to the parlor to sit with the family. Deronda accepted gladly: he had tucked small gifts in his coat pockets on the walk over, a set of cut paper dancing figures for Adelaide, an ivory cup and ball for Jacob.

The old grandmother was hunched over the table teaching the children card games, and had just landed a perfect “card plate” that did not shatter.
“Stop!” Jacob yelped, skidding to a halt at Deronda’s feet the second he walked in. “Don’t step on my plate. Watch me toss it up again.”
Deronda laughed and obliged, exchanging a soft, knowing smile with the grandmother as the flimsy card plate spun three times in the air before it finally crumbled. He noticed the door Mordecai had stepped out of during his last visit was shut, but he did not rush to ask after their singular boarder first: he wanted to show the Cohens, the rough, unrefined family that had sheltered him, that their kindness mattered too.

He settled Adelaide on his knee, setting the paper figures to twirl across the tablecloth while Jacob fumbled eagerly with his new cup and ball, before he asked gently, “Is Mordecai in just now?”
Cohen, popping in from the shop to watch the fun, turned to his wife. “Where is he, Addy?”
“Back in the workroom there,” she said, nodding toward the closed door.
“Truth is, sir,” Cohen said, tapping his forehead lightly, “he’s always been a touch odd, you know? Not quite as grounded as you or me, but steady as clockwork otherwise—works hard, dotes on that boy Jacob more than anything. But these past two days? He’s wandered about like a sleepwalker, or sat stock still like a wax figure for hours.”
“It’s that consumption, poor dear soul,” the grandmother said, her voice soft as down. “I doubt he’ll hold out much longer against it.”
“Oh no, I think it’s something caught fast in his head,” young Mrs. Cohen cut in. “He’s pored over his old papers nonstop. When I call his name it takes him minutes to hear, to answer.”
“You might think we’re a little soft for keeping him,” Cohen added, apologetic, “but me wife and mum wouldn’t part with him even if he was twice the burden. It’s not that I can’t count costs—I’m no fool to lose money on purpose. It’s principle, see?”
“Mordecai carries a blessing right inside him,” the grandmother said, firm.
Jacob trotted over, determined to correct her. “No, grandma, he’s got a sick thing inside him. Said he couldn’t play with me yesterday, wouldn’t even have a bite of my bun.”

Deronda told them then, that far from judging their fondness for the man, he had already grown attached to Mordecai himself, met him at Ram’s bookshop some weeks prior, and had promised to come fetch him here so they could walk out together.
Cohen slapped his knee, delighted. “That’s it! He’s been waiting for you. That’s what’s got him all stirred up. Real kind of you, sir, to pay him mind—can’t be much use in all that old learning of his, else he wouldn’t be living here with us, no.” A customer’s bell jingled from the shop front, and he hurried back out.
Jacob leaned in helpful as ever, offering to go call Mordecai for him, but his mother shushed him, telling him to turn the knob quiet so Deronda could step in unannounced.
Jacob turned the doorknob so slow not a single creak sounded, and Deronda stepped behind him to the threshold. The tiny back workroom was lit only by a dying fire and one shaded candle, with half-finished pieces of jewelry scattered across the work board under the window, and dog-eared books stacked in the far corner. Mordecai was perched on a tall stool at that board, back to the door, hands folded over the wood, a watch propped up on a stand right in front of him. He had been waiting, sick with the tight, desperate hope of a prisoner listening for his long-delayed pardon, when Deronda’s voice wrapped around him soft: “I am come for you. Are you ready?”

He did not speak a single word. He just spun around, grabbed his furred cap off the edge of the board, and moved to Deronda’s side in two short steps. They were back in the sitting room in an instant, and Jacob, gawking at how bright Mordecai’s hollow eyes had gone, shoved his new cup and ball up near his face, yelling “Watch me catch it!” as if he was cheering up a man who had only just woken from a near-death fever. Mordecai smiled, thin and wobbly but real, and said “Fine, fine!” before young Mrs. Cohen reminded him he had forgotten his wool overcoat and thick comforter, and he ducked back to the workroom to grab them.

Cohen had stepped back into the parlor, whispering to Deronda under his breath, “See? He’s come alive again, didn’t I tell you? I’m usually right about these things.” Then he spoke in his normal tone, “Well, sir, I won’t keep you, but I do hope this isn’t the last time you stop by.”
Jacob ran over, holding the cup and ball out to him. “Will you come back? I bet I can catch this every single time, no misses, if you come visit again.”
Deronda smiled at the grandmother, noting how clever the boy’s small hands moved. “That boy has wonderfully deft hands. Which side of the family does he get that talent from?”
The grandmother nodded toward her son, who puffed up proudly. “My side! My wife’s family’s not the handy sort. Ours? It’s clever as gutta percha—twist it any way you want. There’s nothing us old lot can’t turn our hands to if we set our minds to it.” He winked at Jacob, who cackled nasally and stamped his feet, singing “Old gentlemen, old gentlemen!” all around the room.

Deronda realized then that he would soon have to ask Cohen straight out if he had lost a little sister named Mirah when she was six years old—but the decisive moment still felt too fragile to grasp. For the first time, the faint repulsion he had once felt at the family’s rough, unpolished manners melted away into warm, soft affection. They might not speak in refined, well-turned sentences, but their steady, tender care for a sick, half-mad boarder whose grand visions they barely understood was a quiet moral refinement far rarer than any polished drawing room manners.
“The Cohens seem to have a great affection for you,” Deronda said as soon as they stepped off the doorstep into the crisp evening air.
“And I for them,” Mordecai answered immediately. “They carry the heart of the Israelite inside them, even if they are like the horse and mule, who see no further than the narrow path they walk each day.”
“I fear I made you anxious,” Deronda said, apologetic. “I meant to come yesterday, but I could not get away.”
“I trusted you,” Mordecai said, his voice thin and wobbly with barely contained emotion. “But it is true: my youth’s old spirit woke up in me, and this weak, sick body of mine can not bear the beating of its wings. I am like a man bound in prison for decades, suddenly set free, brought face to face with the one person he has longed to speak to. He weeps, he staggers, the joy in him threatens to burst the fragile flesh that holds his soul.”

“Do not speak too much out in this cold night air,” Deronda said, his chest tight with the weight of the trust this man laid on him. “Wrap your scarf over your mouth. We are heading to the *Hand and Banner*, are we not? We shall have a private nook there?”
“Ah, that is my trouble,” Mordecai said. “I wished you had come yesterday. Tonight is the night of that little club I told you of. We might not get a minute alone until late, when everyone else has left. Perhaps we should go somewhere else, but I do not know any other place well. Strange places make the outer world press in too hard on me, blur the visions I hold inside my head. But the people there know my face.”
“I do not mind the club at all,” Deronda said, relieved. “If it is the place you are most comfortable, that is where we will go. We can talk as long as we can, and I will come back to finish our conversation if we run out of time. What is this club like?”
“It is called The Philosophers,” Mordecai explained. “Only a handful of us, all as rare and scattered as the cedars of Lebanon. Poor working men who love to think. None poorer than I am. Sometimes people of higher station have come as guests. We are allowed to bring a friend who cares about our subjects. Everyone buys a pint of beer or a drink, that pays for the use of the room. Most of them smoke. I go as often as I can, because there are other men of my people there, and sometimes I can speak, say the things that burn in my chest. I like to think of these poor, quiet thinkers as faint echoes of the great Masters of our race, the men who worked with their hands for crusts of bread, but preserved and passed down our people’s memory, kept the soul of Israel alive like a seed buried safe among the tombs. A heart can find such small resemblances so dear.”

Deronda agreed eagerly to join them, glad for the small reprieve before the heavy, private conversation he knew waited between them. Three minutes later, they pushed open the glazed door with the red curtain, and stepped into the tiny parlor barely fifteen feet square. Gaslight glowed through a thin haze of pipe smoke, and Deronda saw a scene he had never witnessed before: half a dozen men, ages from twenty to fifty, all in worn, shabby clothes, most holding clay pipes, leaning in completely focused on a broad-chested man in a pepper-and-salt suit with blonde hair, who held his pipe up in one hand and thumped his knee with the other, finishing a line from Shelley’s *Prometheus Unbound*:
“As thought by thought is piled, till some great truth
Is loosened, and the nations echo round.”

Their entrance broke the hush, and everyone shifted around the cramped semicircle of chairs around the fireplace to make room. The voices that greeted Mordecai were warm, laced with gentle, long-standing affection for the sick man, before all eyes turned to Deronda.
“I have brought a friend who cares for our subjects,” Mordecai said. “He has traveled far, studied much.”
“Is the gentleman a mystery man? A Great Unknown?” the broad Shelley quoter joked.
“My name is Daniel Deronda,” Deronda said, a soft, gentle smile tugging at the corner of his grave face, warming the whole room. “I am unknown, but not great.”
“Your name is a good one, sir, you are very welcome,” the broad man said, nodding at Mordecai. “C’mon, take this corner seat next to me—it is the warmest.”
Deronda sat across from them, looking around the motley group of sharp, thoughtful faces: Miller the bookseller who read every book he sold, Buchan the sharp-tongued Scotch saddler, Pash the tiny dark vivacious Jewish watchmaker, Gideon the red-haired Jewish optical instrument maker, Croop the dark-eyed shoemaker, plus three plain English working men: the wood inlayer, the laboratory assistant, the pale copying clerk. These were no cheap caricatures of working-class poverty to laugh at—they were men who had scrabbled for knowledge with every spare minute they could steal, the select of the select among the poor, drawn together by a love of ideas almost no one in their station shared. Deronda pulled out his cigar case (he never smoked himself, but always carried it for guests, hating to seem stiff and priggish) and offered it around, and the group relaxed, no less at ease than they had been before he walked in.

Miller, the unofficial moderator, explained they had been debating the nature of progress, arguing over whether new ideas were the primary force that transformed societies. The conversation twisted and turned, until the watchmaker Pash joked that the idea of nationalities was little more than a ghost, soon to fade away entirely. Mordecai turned to him, his face soft with quiet sadness, and said “An idea has no force unless there is a feeling behind it.” Deronda pushed back gently, saying that a sentiment that seemed dead could wake up stronger than ever, that nations could rise again, that the Arabs were even now being stirred by a new, bright zeal. At his words, Mordecai’s whole frame straightened, his worn, haggard face lighting up like the first break of dawn. The conversation spiraled on, until Mordecai spoke, his voice ringing soft but with a fire no one in the room had heard from him before:
“Woe to the men who see no place for resistance in this generation! I believe in a growth, a passage, a new unfolding of life whereof the seed is more perfect, more charged with the elements that are pregnant with diviner form. The life of a people grows, it is knit together and yet expanded, in joy and sorrow, in thought and action; it absorbs the thought of other nations into its own forms, and gives back that thought as new wealth to the world. But there may come a check, an arrest; memories may be stifled, and love may go faint for lack of them. Who dares say the fountain of their life is dried up, they shall forever cease to be a nation? Not he who feels the life of his people stirring within his own breast. His very soul is resistance, and is as a seed of fire that may enkindle the souls of multitudes, and make a whole new pathway for events.”

“My spirit is too weak; mortality
Weighs heavily on me like unwilling sleep,
And each imagined pinnacle and steep
Of godlike hardship tells me I must die
Like a sick eagle looking at the sky.”

The hush of the small room wrapped around them slowly, soft as wool, until the unfamiliar stillness seeped through Mordecai’s awareness. He lifted his eyes to Deronda not with bewilderment or surprise, but with a gaze brimming with restful, unshakable satisfaction, as if he had known this exact quiet moment was coming for months. Deronda rose without a word and dragged his chair nearer, close enough that there would never be any need to raise his voice, no matter how softly either spoke. Mordecai leaned into the small, careful gesture the way a fevered patient leans into a hand adjusting their pillow to ease the ache, and began to speak in a low, warm tone, as if he was not addressing another person at all, only thinking his thoughts out loud for the one soul that would not need them explained.

“In the Kabbalist tradition, souls are born again and again in new bodies until they are perfected and purified. A soul that slips free from its worn, tired flesh may seek out the fellow soul that waits for it, so that the two may finish the work they were meant to do together, grow whole side by side, before they leave the mortal realm to make space for new souls to be born from the eternal store. It is the lingering imperfection of the souls walking the earth right now that delays the coming of the Messianic age. That is no mere fable, you see: it is the shadow of a deeper truth we can almost touch. When my long-wandering soul is finally free of this weary, failing body, it will join yours. Then our work will be made perfect.”

His pause felt like a gentle question, and Deronda could not bring himself to leave it unanswered. He spoke as truthfully as he could, his voice thick with unspoken affection and reverence, “Every single thing I can do, in all good conscience, to make your life’s work count, I will do.”

“I know it,” Mordecai said, quiet and certain, no further proof needed. “I heard it in your voice the first day we spoke. You see the vision clearly—you stand with me on the mount of sight, and you can see the paths to the fulfillment that everyone else turns away from.” He fell silent for a beat, then drifted back into memory, soft and meditative. “You will pick my life up exactly where it broke. I can feel that day so clearly now. The sun was bright on the quayside at Trieste, the garments of sailors and merchants from every nation glinting like scattered jewels in the light. The boats were shoving off from the docks, and the Greek ship that was to carry us to Beyrout was set to sail in an hour. I was to go as clerk and companion to a merchant, I was going to see the holy lands of the East, and see the world with a full, unclouded vision. I breathed easy then, the way you do now, no ache in my chest. I had the light step and endurance of youth, I could fast for days, sleep on hard packed dirt, I had wedded poverty and loved it, for poverty was freedom to me. My heart swelled so big it felt like it held the strength of Moses ben Maimon, whole and ready to fill the sixty years of work I had dreamed of. It was my first time traveling south, and the soul inside me woke to the sun it had ached for for lifetimes. I stood on that quay and the ground beneath my feet seemed to glow, the shadows all tinted with the soft blue glow of made visible spirits, and I was so swept up in that flood of glorious life that my own small, numbered years melted away until I could not feel them at all, and a great sob rose in my throat from the sheer weight of bliss too big to hold. I stood there waiting for my travel companion, and did not see him until he spoke my name: ‘Ezra, I have been to the post, there is a letter for you.’”

“Ezra!” Deronda exclaimed, unable to hold the name back, a strange, thrumming shock running down his spine.

“Ezra,” Mordecai repeated, soft and sure, fully lost to the memory. “I had been expecting a letter, I wrote to my mother constantly. That sound of my name was like a wand tapping me, yanking me back from the ocean of universal existence I had melted into, back into the constraints of my own individual flesh. I opened the letter, and the name came again, a cry that would have woken me even if I had been curled in the bosom of heaven: *Ezra, my son*.”

He paused, frozen in that long-ago moment of anguish, and Deronda held his breath, his mind spinning with a sudden, wild, bright possibility. Mordecai stared at his lap for a few quiet moments before he went on. “My mother was the kind of woman of whom it might well have been said ‘her children rise up and call her blessed.’ When I was small I understood the old story of the master who heard his mother’s footsteps approaching and stood to greet her, saying ‘The Majesty of the Eternal draws near.’ That letter was her cry from the very pits of desolation and grief: the cry of a mother robbed of her last little ones. I was her eldest. Four babes had died one after the other, until my little sister was born late, the light of all her eyes. The letter tore into me: ‘Ezra, my son, they have stolen her away. The man took her, and left nothing but disgrace behind. They will never come back.’”

Mordecai looked up suddenly, his hand settling light and shaking on Deronda’s arm. “Mine was the lot of Israel. For the sin of our father, my soul was sent into exile, my work was broken, the day of fulfillment delayed. The woman who bore me was desolate, shamed, penniless. I turned back that very instant. Her spirit, and the spirit of her worthy Jewish forebears, pulled me home. The God who holds the whole universe inside himself was my strength, my reason to obey. I traveled home with nothing, saving every last penny for her need, leaving the warm southern sun behind to trudge north into freezing cold. On the last leg of the journey, I spent a full night out in the snow and ice. That was the start of this slow, lingering death.”

He pulled his hand away, his gaze drifting off, and Deronda bit back all the urgent questions burning on his tongue. Not now, not when Mordecai was so raw with emotion, no revelations but the ones he chose to give freely. The young man felt a kindred ache in his own chest, so big he feared his own speech would shatter the fragile, sacred air between them.

“But I worked, to keep us fed when all our belongings had been seized,” Mordecai went on quietly. “My mother was ill, her grief had wrapped itself around a hidden sickness in her bones. Some days she could not stand for the pounding of her heart, and her dreams turned into chambers of terror where she saw my little sister being raised in cruelty and sin. In the dead of night I would wake to her crying out for her child, and we would rise together, stretch our hands to the dark, and pour out our whole souls in prayer, begging that Mirah would be saved from harm.”

“Mirah?” Deronda repeated, soft, half afraid his own ears had tricked him. “Did you say Mirah?”

“That was my little sister’s name.” Mordecai smiled, faint and tender. “After we prayed for her, my mother would rest a little. Those last years lasted barely four, and the minute before she died, we were praying that exact same prayer: me aloud, her silent, her last breath drifting out on that name. She found peace at last. But I have never heard a single word of Mirah, not since. I do not know if our prayer was answered. Who can map the paths the wicked walk? Their poisoned will is strong. It poisoned my life, and it is slowly smothering this breath I draw. But there is no grief left in the withering of my body now.” His hand returned to Deronda’s arm, his face lit with the bright, hectic joy of a dying man who has just found his whole world. “The work will be done all the better now. I once thought that work was mine to do, and mine alone. But I shall do it. I shall live in you.”

His grip grew tight, almost convulsive, and Deronda’s heart hammered hard in his chest, his lips paling. He had never been so moved in all his life. The certainty that this man before him was Mirah’s long-lost brother wrapped all their strange, fated connection in a new layer of solemn, tender warmth. He dared not speak, dared not shatter that glowing high point of Mordecai’s exaltation by revealing he had already found Mirah, safe and well. He only lifted his own firm, gentle hand to cover the one clamped around his arm. Mordecai’s hand relaxed all at once, turning upward of its own accord to press palm to palm with Deronda’s, and the dying man seemed to come back to himself, quiet and sated. “Let us go now,” he said. “I cannot talk any longer.”

They parted at the door of the Cohen house without another word, only one last firm, lingering press of their joined hands. Deronda walked away with a strange weight on his chest, half bright joy, half soft, tender anxiety. The joy of finding that Mirah’s brother was every bit as noble, good, and worthy of her as he could have ever hoped was tangled in a quiet solemn sadness: the coming reunion between the two siblings would be the first gentle step toward the final parting that would tear Mirah away from her brother soon after, the kind of farewell that feels like a greeting, the last loving glance that stings sharper than any sorrow. He ached to make their first meeting perfect, no jarring, messy interruptions from the rest of the Cohen household, no awkwardness, no poverty or chaos to taint the sacred moment when the last two remaining pieces of that shattered family found each other again. He was determined to find a sunlit, clean lodging near Chelsea, perfect for Mordecai’s frail lungs, to fill it with his favorite old vellum books, his softest armchair, the plaster bas-reliefs of Milton and Dante he loved so well, to make it a soft, gentle home for the two of them. He did not for a moment doubt Mirah would understand her brother’s greatness, that she would pour all her gentle love into tending him through the last precious months of his life. Deronda thought of all the obscure, lonely souls who burn themselves out for a great dream, never finding a single soul to recognize their work before they reach the invisible shore, and he knew he would not let that fate befall Mordecai. He would go first to Mrs. Meyrick, to confide the whole discovery to her, and plan every last detail of the reunion. No tapestry, no fancy carpet, no fine teacup could ever make a home as perfect as Mirah’s soft face, or her sweet, lulling voice drifting through the rooms, he thought. This, after all, was the great reward for Mordecai’s lifelong devotion: he had not only found the man to carry his life’s work forward, he had found his sister, whole, unharmed, and waiting with all her love to meet him at last.

Fairy folk a-listening Hear the seed sprout in the spring. And for music to their dance Hear the hedgerows wake from trance, Sap that trembles into buds Sending little rhythmic floods Of fairy sound in fairy ears. Thus all beauty that appears Has birth as sound to finer sense And lighter-clad intelligence.

---

And Gwendolen? Her thoughts looped back to Daniel Deronda far more often than his ever drifted to her. She spent hours daydreaming what he made of the world, what quiet, secret parts of his life he never showed anyone else. To her, gilded and sheltered as she was, trying to guess at the shape of his days was like a lap-dog attempting to map the whole wide wild world of canines beyond its drawing room cushion: she could no more imagine Deronda’s quiet, lifelong tie to his people’s ancient historical destiny than she could picture him climbing onto a brass flying horse and vanishing over the treeline to turn into a twinkling star.

For all the moments of shame and inferiority he’d stirred in her, it never crossed her mind that she occupied only a small, far corner of his thoughts. Only the very old and very wise learn to stop assuming their own sharp, thrumming anxieties and joys are mirrored exactly in the minds of others—and Gwendolen, young, lonely, and used to being the center of every room she walked into, could not be blamed for clinging to every small sign of kindness he’d shown her, the first person who had ever made her want to be softer, better, than the sharp, spoiled girl she’d always been.
She kept turning his words over in her head: *Find more interest in other people. Read more. Care for things that matter.* But how was she ever supposed to start? She wondered what books he would tell her to bring up to her room, smirking to herself half the time at the stack of heavy, famous classics she’d dragged home—Descartes, Bacon, Locke, all those writers everyone said were geniuses, that she’d always found unbearably tedious—wishing she could tease him and ask if they were really just bitter medicine to force your mind to be good. Then she’d repent of her sauciness, and tuck the books onto her shelf, convinced if she skimmed them fast enough with her quick wits, she might scramble up to stand on the same level he occupied, just a little.

But she barely had a spare minute for those grand, heavy mental journeys. Every waking hour she was on stage, performing the part of Mrs. Grandcourt, and her husband’s exacting, cold eyes watched every move she made. He’d set his sharp, stubborn will to making their marriage deliver every single thing he wanted from it—and the more he sensed the quiet streak of rebellion thrumming under her perfect, polite mask, the harder he pushed to break it. No part of her wanted to fail at the performance, though. She could not bear to let the world see a single crack, a single hint that her grand, lavish marriage was not the triumph everyone assumed it was. The one person she could let all that bottled-up pressure spill out to, the one person she dared show how broken she was, was Deronda—her secret, quiet anchor, the only hand she could imagine pulling her back from the edge of herself when she was one breath away from screaming or breaking something.

All around Diplow, those early weeks of the new year, everyone agreed Mrs. Grandcourt wore her new rank like a queen. She hunted, rode, paid calls and hosted visitors with such effortless, cool poise that Mrs. Arrowpoint was heard to sniff, “She carries it like she condescended to marry him, not the other way up. I always knew there was something double-faced about her.”

Most of all, Gwendolen was determined to convince her own mother she was perfectly happy. Poor Mrs. Davilow was fooled enough that she blamed the unexpected distance between them on Gwendolen’s new married life, not Grandcourt’s icy dismissiveness. All she was allowed was an occasional invitation to lunch with the Gascoignes, a ride home at dawn the next morning, and rare brief calls where Gwendolen could only spend five minutes with her before Grandcourt called her back to their carriage or his horse, waiting impatiently outside.
The second time Gwendolen suggested inviting her mother and her aunt and uncle to stay for a week, Grandcourt had stayed silent for long enough to make her stomach twist, then drawled, “We can’t have those people underfoot all the time. Gascoigne talks too much. All country clergy are bores, with their confounded fuss over everything.”
Those words hung over Gwendolen like a bad omen. She couldn’t risk bringing her mother any closer, not when Grandcourt looked at her whole family like they were annoying pests, and she certainly couldn’t tell her mother the truth: that if she saw too much of her married life, she would immediately realize how miserable Gwendolen was. So she waved off her mother’s hints about renting a small house near Ryelands, telling her it would be far nicer for her to stay close to the rectory, that they would probably be travelling away for most of the year anyway.
Strangely, the more Grandcourt tried to shut her family out, the fonder Gwendolen grew of them. She found herself looking back on her uncle’s bumbling cheer, his silly over-eager efforts to keep the household running smoothly, even when he got things wrong, as a far warmer comfort than the cold, perfect neutral grandeur of Ryelands that chilled her a little more every single day. It was the strangest sort of growth, far more real than anything she could glean from skimming those heavy philosophy books that demanded she forget all her messy, roiling daily anxieties just to make sense of a single paragraph.

One bright day, to the whole family’s shock and delight, Gwendolen cantered up to Offendene alone, no husband beside her, only a trailing groom. Everyone at the lunch table, her four little sisters included, stared out the dining room window at her, radiant on her prancing chestnut horse that held its head like it was proud to carry her, half-convinced she was some storybook happy heroine, Harriet Byron or Miss Wardour, come back to visit the quiet home she’d left behind. Her uncle hurried to the door to help her dismount, and she sprang off the saddle with such bright, easy energy she could have convinced anyone she was perfectly, deliriously happy. She was determined to set her mother’s mind at ease that day, even for an hour, the rare taste of freedom riding over alone making her feel strong enough to mask all the pain gnawing at her. The seven family kisses they greeted her with didn’t even feel tedious, for once.

“Henleigh’s out riding, so I decided to slip away and come see you, mamma,” she said, dropping her hat on the table and plopping down next to her mother, teasing her playfully, “And I’ve come to scold you for wearing that terrible shoddy lace on your head. You never thought I’d turn up and catch you being so careless with yourself, did you?” She brushed a soft hand over her mother’s hair.
“Scold all you like, dear,” Mrs. Davilow said, her worn, delicate face flushing pink with pure joy. “I wish we had something proper for you to eat after that ride, not these scraps. Jocosa will make you a cup of chocolate, the way you used to love it as a girl.”
Gwendolen tried to protest, but Miss Merry was already hurrying off to make it. “Oh, don’t bother, a biscuit is fine. I haven’t come to eat, I’ve come to say goodbye.”
“Goodbye? Are you going back to Ryelands already?” Mr. Gascoigne asked.
“No, we’re off to London,” Gwendolen said, picking at a piece of bread without taking a single bite.
“Rather early for London, isn’t it? Henleigh’s not in Parliament,” Mrs. Gascoigne said.
“There’s only one last hunt left this season, and he’s got some lawyer business to take care of in town,” Gwendolen lied easily. “I’m glad to go. There’s so much to see there.”
“You’ll get to see your new house in Grosvenor Square!” Mrs. Davilow said, her eyes shining, the girls all staring at Gwendolen like she was a goddess about to vanish back to a world of unimaginable luxury.
“You know what I hope, my dear Gwendolen?” her uncle said, leaning forward with earnest, paternal good intention. “I hope you use your influence with Henleigh to push him to run for Parliament. A man of his position owes it to the country to make his weight felt. Everyone says the government is going to call a new election over the Reform Bill soon, and he should be ready for it. Wives have so much quiet influence over their husbands, after all. Use yours.”
Gwendolen had to bite her tongue so hard to not laugh she almost tasted blood. Once, not long ago, she’d believed that too—believed she could wrap any man around her little finger, bend him to whatever will she wanted. Now she knew exactly how much influence she had, zero, in the grand scheme of things. “I’d be happy to try, uncle,” she said, “But I don’t think Henleigh would ever want to make speeches at election rallies.”
She jumped at the sight of Jocosa coming back with her chocolate, escaping having to promise anything she could never deliver. The good rector, for all his earnestness, never for a second imagined that Grandcourt would laugh in her face if she ever dared bring up the idea of running for office. Even Mrs. Gascoigne, for all her quiet irritation at Grandcourt’s haughty treatment of her husband, wouldn’t let herself believe the couple was anything less than perfectly happy.
They gushed about their son Rex, who was on track to graduate with top honors and get a fellowship, and how they planned to meet him in London at Easter, how Lord Brackenshaw had already invited them to stay at the Castle. Gwendolen told them they were more than welcome to visit her in Grosvenor Square, though privately she hoped she would never have to see any of her family under Grandcourt’s roof for the rest of her life.
Then her mother told her about little Mr. Jodson’s death, the tiny white cottage hidden by trees down the lane to the church, that would be free for them to move into, small and perfect, only a mile from the rectory. Gwendolen’s throat tightened. “You have no furniture for it, mamma,” she said, melancholy seeping into her voice.
“Oh, I’ve got money for that,” Mrs. Davilow said, laying a soft hand over hers. “You know who made me comfortable, dear.”
Gwendolen’s heart swelled so much she thought she might cry. “Come on, let’s go up to the bedroom and fix my hat before I leave, mamma,” she said, fumbling at her hair like a stray pin had come loose. As soon as the door to their old shared bedroom clicked shut behind them, she sank into the chair in front of the mirror, pale and breathless, holding back tears so hard a little groan escaped her like she was in physical pain.
“Are you unwell, darling?” Mrs. Davilow asked, alarmed.
“Just the chocolate made my stomach turn,” Gwendolen said, letting her mother take her hand and press it to her chest.
“I would come nurse you, if you were ill, wouldn’t I?” Mrs. Davilow said softly, and for the first time she was sure Gwendolen still needed her, more than ever.
“Of course you would,” Gwendolen said, leaning her head on her mother’s shoulder, forcing a smile. “But I never get ill. I’m as strong as a horse. Don’t you ever fret over me, alright? Be happy with the girls. They’re far better daughters to you than I ever was.”
“Nonsense, you’ve always been perfect,” Mrs. Davilow said.
Gwendolen laughed, forced and bright, jumping to her feet to grab her hat. “The only good thing I ever did for you was marry Henleigh Grandcourt, and I only did it because I wanted to, no other reason. You’ve got to be half happy now, alright? That’s an order.” She pulled a stack of thirty pounds’ worth of notes out of her pocket and pressed them into her mother’s hand. “Henleigh gives me piles of money I never know what to do with, and I don’t want to waste it on boring charity. Make the girls buy whatever silly, pretty little things they want for the new cottage, alright?” She hurried out before her mother could protest, the emotion bubbling over too hot to hold.
She said all her goodbyes with perfect, easy poise, and rode away from Offendene telling herself sarcastically, *I think I’m making a very fine Mrs. Grandcourt, after all.*
She knew perfectly well where Henleigh had gone that day, not to any hunting meet or any London lawyer. He’d gone to Gadsmere, that run-down ugly little house in the dark mining country, the “dog hutch” he’d once joked about, where Mrs. Glasher and his secret children lived. The thought burned her like a brand on her skin. She’d married him knowing all of it, to save her family from ruin, and now she was trapped. She couldn’t say a single word to anyone, couldn’t make a single move that would blow the secret wide open and turn her into a spectacle for every gossip in the county to tear apart. The only other person who knew the truth besides her and Mrs. Glasher was Deronda, the only person she dared confide in, the only person who might ever understand.
For his part, Grandcourt never fully guessed exactly how sharp and wild Gwendolen’s quiet rebellion was—but he didn’t need to. He had an instinct for every flicker of resistance in her, every little swallow of pride she had to force down, and it delighted him. Taming that bright, stubborn, untouchable girl had become his favorite game. He could not imagine any of the soft, shadowy, tender parts of her that loved her mother, that longed for Deronda, that ached to be free—but every time he won a small victory over her will, it only made him more satisfied that his prize, his perfect new wife, was exactly where he wanted her.

Behold my lady’s carriage stop the way,
With powdered lacquey and with charming bay;
She sweeps the matting, treads the crimson stair,
Her arduous function solely “to be there.”
Like Sirius rising o’er the silent sea,
She hides her heart in lustre loftily.

The Grandcourts had settled into their Grosvenor Square mansion just in time to receive an invitation to Lady Mallinger’s musical soirée. Sir Hugo had known for weeks that his less-than-beloved nephew would be arriving in London on pressing business, and the third evening after their move, Gwendolen could barely summon the attention to admire her ornate new ceilings and rich silk draperies. Her whole mind was fixed on two certainties: she would speak to Daniel Deronda again that night, and she would finally meet the Miss Lapidoth he had spoken of so tenderly—the girl who had suffered so much, and who he said was “capable of submitting to anything in the form of duty.”

Gwendolen had not forgotten a single word Deronda had said about Mirah, and that phrase looped bitterly in her head, sharp as a pin. She would have been forced to admit, if pressed, that the weight she carried could never bear the name of duty. It was a yoke, forged by a shameful, reckless mistake she had made months before, pulled tight around her neck by her own selfish choices, no room left for any gentle, noble purpose to ease its weight.

Lady Mallinger’s Park Lane drawing rooms, all dressed in white paint, gilded trim, and soft deep crimson upholstery, were half full of guests when the Grandcourts arrived. The stretch of formal instrumental music had just ended, and the room hummed with low, cheerful chatter. Klesmer stood at the piano, preparing to accompany Mirah in her performance of Leo’s *O patria mia*—a piece he had personally recommended to her, far more suited to her rare, haunting voice than any of the overplayed society favorites. Mirah was poised beside the instrument, all quiet grace, when Gwendolen was ushered to a seat of honor that gave her an unobstructed view of both of them. Draped in pale green velvet, the famous “poisoned” diamond necklace glittering at her throat, her sharp sight and iron self-command let her map the entire room in seconds. She gave Klesmer a small, cool bow as she passed, and the flash of their shared smile transported them both back to that morning, not long ago, when Gwendolen’s greatest ambition had been to stand exactly where Mirah stood now: an artist, commanding the adoration of a grand audience by the power of her talent, not just by the glitter of her jewels. A faint, bitter resentment curled in her chest. *He thinks I’ve found the proper path now, the life I was always meant for*, she thought, the silk of her gown feeling like a cage.

She had not spotted Deronda on her way to her seat, and while she made polite small talk with Sir Hugo, her eyes drifted lazily around the room, bowing to acquaintances here and there, careful not to let anyone—least of all her husband—guess she was searching. She dared not risk him later sneering at her for a behavior he’d dismiss as “damnably vulgar.” But a slow, sweeping glance across a crowded room often lands on things you never wanted to see. One pair of eyes met hers, forcing her to give a tiny, involuntary bow: it was Mr. Lush, the amateur of Meyerbeer she had all but banished from her life, the man Sir Hugo still found useful as a go-between with the less reputable set of gentlemen in town. He stood right at Grandcourt’s elbow, while her husband pointedly turned his shoulder to him, pretending to listen intently to Lord Pentreath.

A cold, unpleasant jolt shot through Gwendolen. She had banished Lush from her sight after their wedding, and had not given him a single thought since, pushed completely out of her mind by the turbulent, secret drama playing behind her own eyes. Now there he was, hovering at her husband’s side, and a sudden, vivid certainty bloomed in her like a memory pulled straight from a dream: this man knew every shameful, hidden detail of Grandcourt’s past. He knew the secrets that made her own new life feel like a slow, suffocating punishment. It took every ounce of her self-control to turn her head away from him, pretending to admire the painting on the wall as she continued her slow scan until she found Deronda. He was not looking at her, and she pulled her gaze back, consoling herself that he must have seen her come in.

He was lingering near the door with Hans Meyrick, who Deronda had personally added to Lady Mallinger’s guest list, and both were thrumming with quiet anxiety that Mirah would not get the attention she deserved. Deronda felt his chest tight with emotion: Mirah’s presence was tangled up in a hundred hopes and memories, all centered on the dying brother Mordecai he was soon to reveal to her. He had only just escaped Lady Pentreath, who had boomed in her deep, cello-like voice, “Well, your Jewess is pretty—there’s no denying that. But where’s her Jewish impudence? She looks as demure as a nun. I suppose she learned that trick on the stage.” Indignation flared hot in his chest, the same fierce protective anger he had felt as a boy when Sir Hugo had once asked him if he wanted to be a great singer, when strangers treated talent and vulnerable people like a commodity to be judged carelessly by the fashionable crowd. He winced to think how much Mordecai would hate to hear the word “Jewess” tossed around like nothing more than a stamp on a cheap imported silk scrap.

That was when he saw the Grandcourts sweep into the room, and Hans immediately leaned over to exclaim over Gwendolen, calling her “that Vandyke duchess of a beauty.” For a split second, old, ungenerous repulsion for Gwendolen flickered in Deronda’s chest, as if her glittering beauty and her sharp, unhappiness were somehow to blame for the world undervaluing soft, good Mirah. It was a petty, class-driven resentment, the kind that makes you bristle when something or someone you love deeply is dismissed by people who have never had to fight for anything, and he snapped at Hans, “I thought you swore you could only admire your Berenice style of woman.”
“That’s the one I worship, not merely admire,” Hans laughed back. “Other women I might make myself wicked for, but for Berenice? I’d make myself pretty good, which is far harder.”
“Quiet,” Deronda said, nodding toward the piano, where Mirah was about to start singing. He was relieved when Hans wandered off to get a better view, still unsettled by his own unkindness.

He had never heard Mirah sing *O patria mia* before. He knew every line of Leopardi’s ode to Italy by heart— the poem of a disconsolate mother in chains, hiding her face on her knees and weeping— and the few lines set to music hummed with the grandeur of the whole work, wrapping the room in that heroic, lonely passion. Mirah sang it perfectly. When the applause broke out, Klesmer leaned in to whisper to her, “Good, good—the crescendo was far better than before,” but her eyes scanned the crowd immediately for Deronda. Nothing else mattered half so much as making him proud tonight, singing in the house that was his home, after he had pulled her from destitution and given her a new life.

The crowd of admirers parted to make way for Gwendolen, who Mrs. Klesmer was leading over to introduce to Mirah. Now that his worry for Mirah had faded, Deronda’s heart softened toward the glittering, unhappy Gwendolen, and he felt a little ashamed of his earlier petty hardness. He thought of all the confessions she had made to him, of how badly she too needed rescue—far harder rescue than pulling a lost girl from the banks of the river, and he had felt so helpless to give it. *Cowardly to turn away from her for that*, he thought, resolving to go to her at the first chance, to honor the trust she had placed in him, even against Sir Hugo’s unsubtle hints that he should keep his distance.

Gwendolen sat down beside Mirah on the plush crimson divan, the contrast between the two of them sharp and vivid to Klesmer, who lingered nearby smiling his quiet, lopsided smile. “You must let me say how very much I owe you,” Gwendolen said warmly to Mirah. “Mr. Deronda told me I was in for a great treat, but I could never have imagined how beautiful your singing would be.”
“You are far too kind,” Mirah replied, her eyes wide as she stared at Gwendolen. To her, a real grand lady with real diamonds and a life of luxury felt like a character stepped straight out of some unknown, dramatic play, where the happy parts were only surface deep, and the tragedy waited just around the next line.
“I would give anything to learn to sing like that,” Gwendolen went on, glancing playfully up at Klesmer. “I sing very badly, he will tell you, and I once hated the idea of being merely middling at anything. I was told that was a foolish attitude— that’s a very different lesson from the one you must have learned, isn’t it?”
“Not at all,” Klesmer cut in quickly. “If Miss Lapidoth would be willing to teach you, it would be a pleasure for her, and a fine chance for you to study properly.” He walked away, and Mirah, earnest and guileless, said, “If I can help teach you, I would be so glad. I am only just starting out myself, and I plan to pass on every good lesson my master ever gave me.”

Gwendolen was caught off guard by her sweet, simple eagerness, and to shift the conversation away from singing she asked, “You haven’t been in London very long, have you? Did you meet Mr. Deronda abroad?”
“Oh no,” Mirah said. “I never saw him once before I came to England this summer.”
“But he’s heard you sing so many times, and knows you so well, doesn’t he?” Gwendolen pressed, half eager just to hear anyone talk about Deronda, half fumbling for something to say to fill the silence. “He spoke of you in the highest terms. It seems like you are great friends.”
Mirah’s face softened with that reverent, grateful glow that always came over her when Deronda’s name was mentioned. “I was poor. I was in terrible trouble, no friends at all. He took pity on me, and gave me all the good friends I have now. That is the only way he came to know me— he was kind to a stranger in need. I owe every good thing in my life to him.”

Gwendolen could not explain the bright, strange joy those words gave her. She knew what that kindness felt like, too: the same ready, gentle compassion he had once extended to her, in the darkest hour of her own life. She moved away soon after, certain she had nothing to fear from Mirah, no premonition that this quiet young Jewish protégé would ever alter the course of her life more deeply than a few singing lessons, even if the gilded world of Mrs. Grandcourt ever left her the time and peace of mind to practice.

On a sudden reckless impulse, she chose not to return to her old seat, but settled herself on an empty settee where there was only room for one other person. She was far closer to Deronda now than she had been before. So it felt almost like fate when he walked over to shake her hand before Klesmer started his next piece, then lingered for a moment by the empty end of the settee, until the roaring, crashing swell of his bass and treble made the trivialities of society manners feel absurd, and he sat down beside her.

But when the music ended and the room erupted in chatter, the moment Gwendolen had been waiting for to speak freely was spoiled completely. Mr. Lush was leaning against the wall not ten feet away, well within earshot. She felt a hot flush of anger rise up her neck, but she kept her face carefully neutral as she said, “Miss Lapidoth is every bit as wonderful as you described her to be.”
“You were very quick to spot that,” Deronda said, a little dry.
“No, I don’t mean I’ve found all the deeper excellences you told me of,” Gwendolen hurried on. “But her singing is charming, and so is she. Her face is so lovely, nothing common about it at all. She’s such a complete, lovely little person. I am sure she will be a huge success.”

The words grated on Deronda, and he did not reply, only stared gravely ahead of him. Gwendolen knew instantly she had displeased him, and the frustration of being trapped, unable to say a single honest word with Lush hovering nearby, made her heart race. They sat in stiff, painful silence for what felt like an eternity, neither of them looking at the other, until Lush finally pushed off the wall and wandered off to join a group across the room.

Gwendolen turned to him at once, voice low and urgent. “You despise me for talking artificially.”
“No,” Deronda said, his voice cool. “That sort of polite small talk is perfectly excusable sometimes. I did not think what you were saying was entirely artificial, though.”
“There was something in it that upset you,” she insisted. “Tell me what it was.”
“Those small niceties of feeling around words and tone are impossible to explain,” Deronda said. “You cannot make someone else understand them if they have not felt them for themselves.”
“You think I am too dense to ever get it,” Gwendolen said, a faint tremor in her voice she could not quite hide. Her eyes were glistening with unshed tears, fixed on his face. “Have I ever been that blind to anything you have ever said to me?”
“Not at all,” Deronda said, softening immediately. “Different people have different experiences, that is all. We do not all wince at the same hurts. I have more than enough proof that you are not dense.” He gave her a small, gentle smile.
“But feeling something is not the same as being able to make yourself better,” Gwendolen said, not returning his smile. The distance his words put between them chilled her. “I think the only way we ever get better is to have people around us who bring out the good feelings in us. You must not be surprised by anything you see in me. I think it is too late for me to change. I do not know how to be wise, like you told me to be.”
“I almost never do any good with all my unsolicited preaching,” Deronda said, sadly thinking of the ill-fated necklace incident, and how she had ended up trapped in a marriage far more dangerous than any roulette table. “I ought never to have meddled at all.”
“NO—don’t say that,” Gwendolen said, panic rising in her throat, sure this was the only chance she would ever get to say the words. “If you give up on me, I will have nothing left. You telling me I did not have to stay selfish and ignorant was the only strength I had. If you say you wish you never spoke to me— that means you despair of me, that you are going to forsake me. And if you do that, I will never be good. You are the one who could have made me different, if you just stayed near me, and kept believing in me.”

She did not wait for him to reply. She rose quickly and walked back to her old, empty seat, just as Mirah began to sing *Per pietà non dirmi addio*—the melody rising soft and searching, every note throbbing with emotion, as if the tune itself was made only of unspoken longing.

For Deronda, the song wove itself straight into the pleading of Gwendolen’s voice, a painful, urgent demand for something he could not name, something that clashed with every sacred promise he had made to Mordecai, every duty he owed to Mirah. He saw her clearly in his mind then: glittering in her silk and gems, her wounds all hidden, terrified of herself, reaching out for him to hold her up. It felt like he was being dragged out to sea by a strong tide, one hand grasped by the dying Mordecai pulling him toward a far-off, sacred purpose, the other caught by Gwendolen’s outstretched, desperate fingers, begging him not to leave her behind. He shook his head a moment later, laughing at his own foolishness. He was not that important. He could not possibly hold that much power over Gwendolen Grandcourt’s life.

“Lucky devil you are,” Hans clapped him on the shoulder. “Sitting on that settee with that Vandyke duchess, having a dramatic little lovers’ quarrel! She stormed off in the most perfect regal fashion. I want to paint her and her husband together. He’s got exactly the handsome physique the Duke should have in Lucrezia Borgia— if only he had a fine baritone voice to go with it.”
Deronda only grunted, hoping Hans had not seen nearly as much of their private conversation as he thought.

Gwendolen, for her part, was halfway home in the carriage before she realized she had spent the whole evening half-waiting for Grandcourt to reproach her for her tête-à-tête with Deronda, for losing that perfect, frozen air of social equanimity she was supposed to keep up at all times in public. But he said nothing about her behavior at all.
