He only turned to her, his voice flat and cold, as their carriage rattled through the dark London streets, and said, “Lush will dine with us tomorrow, among the other guests. You will treat him civilly.”

Gwendolen’s heart began to hammer so hard she could barely breathe. The words leapt to her tongue, ready to strike back like a blow: *You are breaking your promise to me. The very first promise you made after we married.* But she could not say them. She was terrified of the fight, as if she could already feel his hands tightening around her throat. After a long, heavy silence, she said, in the defeated tone of a woman who has just realized her chains are far heavier than she ever guessed, “I thought you did not intend for him to ever step foot in our house again.”
“I need him right now. He’s useful to me. And he will be treated civilly.”

No more words passed between them for the rest of the ride. It was the exact moment a man who once promised not to smoke in front of his new

> “If any one should importune me to give a reason why I loved him, I feel it could no otherwise be expressed than by making answer, ‘Because it was he, because it was I.’ There is, beyond what I am able to say, I know not what inexplicable power that brought on this union.” — MONTAIGNE: *On Friendship*

The long-planned moment had arrived: the moment to tell Mordecai that the little lost sister he had grieved for decades was alive, and to move him to a sunlit Brompton lodging a mere ten minutes’ walk from the Meyrick home, so that when Mirah at last met the brother she’d never dared hope to find, they would both be wrapped round in Mrs. Meyrick’s warm, motherly care. Deronda had confided every detail of Mirah’s story to the “little mother” except the strange, unspoken bond that had tied Mordecai’s hopes to him, and she had guarded the secret fiercely—no hint had slipped to her daughters, or to Hans, for any accidental whisper reaching Mirah would have stirred up restless, painful suspicion and spoiled the gentle reveal they had carefully orchestrated to secure her long-sought independence, that quiet, dignified kind of self-sufficiency that lets a loved one care for you without robbing you of your pride.

Mrs. Meyrick’s mix of Scottish steadiness and Gaelic warmth had made her an ideal keeper of the secret, though she harbored small, soft misgivings of her own. She was sure Mordecai was good, of course—Deronda’s judgment was never wrong—but she had quietly hoped Mirah’s sharp, painful longing for her Jewish roots would soften over time, tangled gently into the new, bright life she was building with the Meyricks. Her own secret daydream had never centered on long-lost kin: she had been quietly waiting for the romance between Hans and Mirah to bloom, and this fervent, dying brother who would drag Mirah’s heart back to the deep, vivid dye of Jewish devotion felt like an unlooked-for complication, the sort of intense real-life figure you read about with delight after midnight, but panic a little to find knocking on your front door at breakfast.

“I’m as glad as you are the coarse pawnbroker wasn’t her brother,” she admitted to Deronda, as they walked through the empty Brompton lodgings, tucking soft wool bedding into place. “There are Ezras and Ezras in the world, and I can’t bear to think Mirah’s only remaining kin would be the sort of shopkeeper who’d hustle you out the door before you’d even finished looking at a teacup. But the things he said to you about his mother and his little sister? That makes my heart soft for him. I’m just not fond of fanaticism, that’s all. Heard too much fire-and-brimstone preaching as a girl, lost my taste for it.”

“Mordecai isn’t a fanatic,” Deronda said gently. “A fanatic’s enthusiasm is narrow, blind to all that doesn’t fit his small path, cruel to those who walk a different road. Mordecai is an enthusiast—the kind of man whose heart burns for great, widespread good for all his people. He’s not strictly orthodox, he makes allowance for every man’s flaws, and the Cohen family who shelter him adore him even though they don’t understand a word of his grandest, dearest dreams.”

“Alright, alright,” Mrs. Meyrick laughed, patting the drafty bedroom window with a fresh sheet of thick paper to block the cold draught. “I’ll take your word for the parts I don’t get. My poor old husband used to say the world would be a dismal place if there was nothing but common sense in it. I’ll have his bedding turned down soft, and when we break the secret to the girls and Hans, they’ll deck these rooms out with every little pretty trinket they own. But promise me you won’t tell Mirah before I warn the children the evening before, so they can slip out the next morning and leave the two of them to their first meeting, no fuss, no prying eyes?”

Deronda agreed, teasing her playfully for not comparing Mordecai to the fire-breathing Covenanter Habakkuk Mucklewrath from Scott’s novels when she talked to the girls, and she waved him off promising she’d talk them all into being overjoyed, even if she had to work herself into a happy state to do it.

He headed for the Cohen home that evening, careful to write ahead that he wished to speak to Mordecai in his own workroom, that he would not intrude if the family thought it rude. He did not want to hurt these kind, rough people who had given Mordecai a home and love when no one else in the world had cared for him.

The whole Cohen household had dressed up for his visit: the women had fresh ribbons in their hair, the children wore their cleanest stockings, and Cohen himself held his tongue out of respect for the mysterious “great business” his odd lodger had going on with the handsome, gentle young gentleman. Little Jacob, the bright scruffy boy who adored Mordecai, trotted up to Deronda’s knee before he could reach the workroom door.

“What are you going to talk to Mordecai about?” he demanded.

“Something that will make him very happy,” Deronda said, pinching his ear. “Something you won’t understand yet.”

To prove he was far more learned than the stranger assumed, Jacob poured out a long string of throaty, nasal Hebrew verses he’d memorized from Mordecai, nodding his head fiercely as if he’d delivered an unbeatable challenge. When Deronda admitted he could not recite a single line of it, the boy whooped in triumph, danced around in his scuffed scarlet socks, and dragged the workroom door wide open yelling “Mordecai! Here’s the young swell!” before his mother and grandmother could hush him.

The room had been fussed over too: a scrap of soft carpet laid by the hearth, Deronda’s chair pulled close to the fire, the lamps trimmed bright. Mordecai was standing by the flames, his face bright with a solemn, trembling expectation, as if he had spent the whole day waiting for Deronda to tell him the secret the two of them had danced around for months: that Deronda was his long-lost blood kin, the heir to his people’s legacy he’d dreamed of finding.

“You have come to tell me my soul’s dearest wish is true,” he said, clasping his hands tight as Deronda sat beside him. “That you are my brother by blood, that the heritage is yours, there is nothing left to divide us.”

Deronda’s heart ached at the look on his face. “I have learned nothing new about my own origins yet,” he said softly, breaking the inevitable disappointment gently. Mordecai sank back in his chair, eyes closing, the whole day’s sharp tension draining away into sickening, hollow reaction.

“Except,” Deronda hurried on, “that some time ago I found myself connected to you through someone you love more than almost anyone. Someone who was precious to your mother, and precious to you. Someone you thought lost to the world.”

Mordecai’s eyes flew open, his hand spasming tight around Deronda’s wrist, his face white with terror—afraid, Deronda knew, that he was about to hear that his sister had come to harm.

“What you prayed for has come to pass,” Deronda said, his own voice soft with tremor. “Mirah is safe. She was delivered from all the evil that chased her, and she is here in London, looking for you.”

Mordecai’s grip loosened, he panted out a tearless sob, sinking back against his chair as the wave of joy hit him. His eyes filled with a soft, luminous sweetness Deronda had never seen before, and for the first time he could see the clear family resemblance to Mirah, the same gentle, glowing light in his face that lit hers when she spoke of their mother. For long minutes he whispered quiet Hebrew prayers under his breath, as if he was talking face to face with the mother they had both lost.

Deronda told him the rest then, softening the worst parts of Mirah’s story, skipping the part where she had nearly drowned herself in despair, only telling him she had run away to England to find her mother and brother, that she had found a loving home with the Meyricks, that she had never stopped talking about the brother she barely remembered, but had always ached for.

“I came to this very house looking for Ezra Cohen, thinking he was Mirah’s brother,” Deronda said, smiling. “I feared for so long that her only kin would be coarse, unkind people who would hurt her. But I found the brother she deserved long before I knew your name was Ezra Mordecai Cohen.”

The only kinship Mordecai shared with the Cohen family was the broad, beloved kinship of the Jewish people, he told Deronda. “They sheltered me, gave me bread and love when no one else would. I would not trade that kindness for anything. But I will go with you to Mirah,” he added, before Deronda could even make his plea. “Our mother would have wished it. I will accept the home you have prepared for us.” His voice softened. “But I cannot tell the Cohens myself. My heart will fail me. You must speak for me.”

They walked back out to the front parlor together, all the Cohens huddled by the fire: baby wrapped in her scarlet quilt in the corner, little Adelaide Rebekah on grandmother’s lap, Jacob squeezed between his father’s legs. The warm firelight gilded their dark, gentle eyes as Deronda broke the news: Mordecai’s long-lost little sister was alive, a talented, lovely young singer who would care for him for the rest of his days, they had a soft new home waiting for him not far away in Brompton.

The wail that went up was immediate. The grandmother sniffled, wiping her eyes on her apron: “So you’re going to leave us, Mordecai, no more little ones running around to cheer you up?” The young mother took up the wail, and Cohen blurted that Jacob would have to go to proper school now, no more casual Hebrew lessons from their lodger. At that, Jacob’s face crumpled, he burst into loud sobs, Adelaide Rebekah wailed in sympathy, and the baby woke screaming in her cradle, the whole household in an uproar of soft, unselfish grief at the thought of losing the man they had loved like family.

Cohen, gruff and big-hearted, wiped his own eyes and made a stolid speech to Deronda, squaring his elbows proudly: “We’re not the sort to grudge a man his good luck, sir. Not one bit. The women and the kids are just soft, that’s all—any Jewish woman’s heart is tender to anyone she’s fed bread to, you know that. We’ll be glad for him soon enough, once we’ve gotten used to the idea.”

Mordecai reached out for Jacob, pulling the sobbing little boy against his chest, and said gently that Jacob could visit him any time he liked, Brompton was only a short walk away, he would teach him all the Hebrew verses he’d promised him, still. Jacob stopped crying mid-sniffle at the word “visit,” brightening at the thought of treats and adventures on the trip. The grandmother sighed in resignation, saying only that she hoped he would get good kosher meat in his new home, and Cohen winked at Deronda, saying he had always known there was some grand, lovely secret between the two of them, far more interesting than boring lessons.

As Deronda walked home that night, his chest felt light, warm with that soft, quiet joy that comes when every long, careful plan at last falls into place. Tomorrow, he would bring Mirah to the new lodgings. The brother and sister she had spent half her life praying for would finally be face to face.

---
“And you must love him ere to you He will seem worthy of your love.” — WORDSWORTH

It would have been easy to envy Deronda as he hunted through soft wool and fine cotton for new clothes for Mordecai, daydreaming the way an artist sketches a beloved scene: the drape of the warm brown dressing gown, cut loose like a Franciscan’s simple frock, framing Mordecai’s sharp, thoughtful face and slender neck above soft grey flannel shirts. Half his pleasure came from seeing every small detail through Mirah’s eyes, determined that the reunion she’d grieved for for twelve long years would never be marred by a jarring, unkind impression. Even so, once every garment was laid out ready, a quiet doubt niggled at him: was he being foolish, prioritizing a polished outward effect over the deeper truth of who Mordecai was? Would Mirah not cherish the ragged, worn clothes her brother had survived all his wandering in, stitched through with memories of all the years they’d been apart? But the Meyricks had not yet learned to see the quiet, sacred grandeur of the man Mirah longed for — that soft, sun-set glow of reverent love that wraps every beloved person in quiet magic, that Deronda knew the Meyricks had not yet found space for in their untested sympathies. It was easier to make sure nothing stood in the way of them welcoming Mordecai fully, than to fight unspoken, unacknowledged prejudices.

His resolve would have only grown stronger if he’d sat in on the Meyrick household’s late fireside chat that night, after Mirah had gone up to her room. Hans, now settled in his chaotic Chelsea artist’s rooms, had stayed late teasing the girls over their terrible volumes of bad poetry, when Mrs. Meyrick poked the coals until flames leapt bright. “Kate, blow out that candle, all of you huddle in close to the hearth,” she said. “Hans, love, stop laughing at those dreadful poems for the hundredth time, come sit too. I’ve got something wonderful to tell.”
“As if we didn’t all know that, ma!” Kate laughed, tucking her feet under her on the fender. “I’ve seen that secret glint in your eye all day, and the fake errands you kept running just to hide it.” Hans swung his chair around, straddling it and resting his chin on his folded fists, curious despite himself.
“Then you will all be shocked to hear that Mirah’s brother has been found,” Mrs. Meyrick announced, clear and bright.
“Confound it,” Hans blurted out, at the exact same moment.
“Hans, that’s awful!” Mab chided, squeezing her knees to her chest. “Imagine if we lost you, how miserable we’d be!”
“I can’t help but be a little sorry,” Kate admitted softly. “What about her mother? Where is she?”
“Her mother is dead,” Mrs. Meyrick said gently.
“Hope the brother isn’t a bad man,” Amy put in.
“Or some grinning, over-the-top showman all decked in cheap jewelry, like a Crystal Palace Assyrian in a hat,” Hans groused, already sour.
“Ungrateful children, all of you!” Mrs. Meyrick said, half-laughing, half-stern. “Not one of you is thinking a single bit about how happy Mirah must be right now.”
“Mirah barely even remembers her brother, ma,” Kate pointed out. “People who get lost for twelve years should just never come back. They’re always in the way,” Hans huffed.
“Hans!” Mrs. Meyrick reproached him. “If you lost me for twenty years, you’d never say that.”
“Twelve years is the exact cutoff for long-lost relations to stay out of sight,” he shot back.
“But finding people is lovely!” Mab protested, bouncing a little. “It gives you a grand story to tell! Is he as exciting as Prince Camaralzaman?”
Mrs. Meyrick then told them every detail Deronda had shared, ending with: “Mr. Deronda admires him more than anyone else I’ve ever heard him speak of. He says Mirah is exactly the sister who will understand every part of him.”
“Deronda has gone perfectly absurd over these Jewish people lately,” Hans snapped, jumping up and slamming his chair hard against the floor. “He’s spoiling Mirah, feeding all these little prejudices of hers, soon she’ll stop being the brilliant young artist she is and turn as narrow as a cloistered nun. We’ll never have a quiet evening in this house again — we’ll have to invite this self-appointed prophet Elijah over for tea every Sunday, and she’ll spend all day moping over the ruins of Jerusalem. Everything we have is going to be ruined. I might as well take to drinking.”
“Oh really, Hans,” Kate said, rolling her eyes. “Men are the most selfish, ridiculous creatures alive. The whole world has to bend to their exact mood, else they throw a fit like a toddler.”
“Oh, it’s so dreadful!” Mab teased, mock-gasping. “I feel like ancient Nineveh just moved in next door.”
“After all that university education, you’re acting more childish than we are,” Amy chided. “We have to be kind to him. So do you.”
“I hope you all have fun with his new Lamentations of Jeremiah, serialized one tedious story beat at a time,” Hans grumbled, grabbing his wide-brimmed hat. “There’s nothing worse than people with one fixed grand idea in their heads, staring at you blankly and expecting every little thing you say to be a tiny footnote to their grand text. If I have to stand under that petrifying shadow all the time, I’d rather be an old discarded boot, and I am not an old boot.” He bent to kiss Mrs. Meyrick’s forehead in a quick, desperate gesture, then mumbled “Goodnight, girls” on his way to the door.
“Wait till Mirah hears how you behaved!” Kate called after him, only to be answered by a resounding slam of the front door. “I can’t wait to see her face when Deronda tells her the news,” she said to her mother afterwards. “She’s going to look more beautiful than ever.”

But Deronda, after turning the thought over, sent a note to Mrs. Meyrick the next morning, asking her to break the news to Mirah instead of him. He didn’t share his real reason — that he hated the thought of recounting the whole story and accidentally framing himself as some grand, generous hero — and instead explained he wanted to stay with Mordecai while Mrs. Meyrick brought Mirah over for their first visit, so they could ease gently into the new life they would build together.
Deronda carried a quiet, unspoken worry in his chest: after all these years Mordecai had spent alone, lost in his grand, solitary ideas, his body growing weaker with every passing month, would he welcome Mirah’s soft, warm presence as something more than a pious duty to fulfill? He loved the Cohens dearly, little Jacob most of all, who he’d known for years, but lately Mordecai had barely asked a single new question about Mirah, quiet and unresisting as he accepted all the changes coming to his life. He put on the new clothes without complaint, only saying with a faint, soft smile, “Keep my old rags for me, please. They hold all my old memories.”
As they sat waiting for Mirah to arrive, he didn’t say a single word, his eyelids half-closed, but his hands twitched and his face was tight with that special, sharp nervousness only people who’ve spent years locked in one solitary train of thought know, when the past they thought was gone forever comes crashing back, heavy with a joy so bright it feels almost dangerous. Deronda felt his own heart race too, and when he heard the knock at the door, he stepped out to greet Mirah before she walked in, and froze.
She was wearing the exact same shabby dark cloak and worn hat he’d first seen her in, the one that had once been soaked through so badly she’d thought to wrap it around herself as a winding sheet when she wanted to end her life by the river. Mrs. Meyrick had asked her, confused, why she was wearing that tattered old thing, and Mirah had answered softly: “My brother is poor. I don’t want to look fancy and make him feel like a stranger. I want to look like I belong with him.” Deronda felt a quiet surge of shame at his own fastidious, over-planned arrangements, when Mirah had known the perfect, gentle thing to do all along. They shook hands in silence, Mirah pale, her eyes wide with awe, like she was about to step into a dream she’d waited half her life to wake up inside.

Deronda opened the door. Mordecai was on his feet, eyes fixed on the threshold, eager as a small child’s. Mirah took two, three small steps forward, then stopped dead. They stared at each other, perfectly still, perfectly silent. It was not their grown, worn, present selves they saw first — they saw the little boy and little girl they’d been, back when their mother was alive and their whole family was whole, and all the lost years melted away.
“Ezra,” Mirah said, her voice exactly the same soft tone she’d used when she first told Deronda about their mother calling his name.
Mordecai stepped forward in one quick, sharp movement, resting his thin hand gently on her shoulders. He was a whole head taller than her, looking down at her with a tenderness so thick it hung in the air. “That was our mother’s voice,” he said. “You remember her calling for me?”
“Yes,” Mirah breathed. “And you answered her, ‘Mother!’ and I knew how much you loved her.” She threw her arms around his neck, her small hands clasped tight behind his head, and pulled his face down to kiss it again and again, childlike and unselfconscious. Her hat fell back onto the floor, spilling all her dark curls loose.
“Ah, my dear head, my dear dear head,” Mordecai murmured, brushing a light finger over her curls.
“You’re so sick, Ezra,” Mirah said, her voice thick with tears as she looked at him properly.
“Yes, my little one,” he said, quiet and unafraid. “I will not be long with you in the flesh.”
“That’s alright,” Mirah said, the words spilling out as sweet and natural as birdsong. “I will love you. We will talk every hour of every day. I’ll tell you every single thing that ever happened to me, and you will teach me to be the good Jewess our mother wanted me to be. I work now — I sing, I make money, I will take care of us. I have had the kindest friends in the whole world.” It was only then she remembered Deronda and Mrs. Meyrick were standing there, turning to them with the sweetest, softest shift, one hand still clinging tight to her brother’s arm. She pulled Mrs. Meyrick’s hand, pressed it firmly into Mordecai’s, then wrapped her own two hands around theirs and kissed their joined knuckles.
“This is the lady who took me in when I was lost, alone, and starving,” she said. “Who treated me like her very own daughter.”
“The Eternal Goodness has walked with you,” Mordecai said gently to Mrs. Meyrick. “You helped fulfill our mother’s last prayer.”
Deronda laid a light, gentle pressure on Mrs. Meyrick’s arm. “I think we should go now, and come back later,” he said, and she nodded immediately, understanding. He did not want to risk accidentally mentioning any of the secrets about his own past he had not yet shared with Mordecai, and he knew the brother and sister needed this time, completely alone, to start mending all the twelve long years they had lost.

There is no crueler irony than the fate that mocks the most carefully laid plans of those who map every variable to secure their own comfort—Machiavelli marked it well, writing of Caesar Borgia, who had braced for every possible disaster following his father’s death, provided against every ill chance he could name, and never once thought to foresee that his own death would follow fast on his father’s heels.

Sir Grandcourt’s entire standing in the realm rested on that grand, passive power that comes of inheriting thousands of acres of prime land. Wars abroad, political reforms at home, even the worst commercial panics only ever touched him through the line that fed his rental income; his most diligent biographer would never need to parse Bismarck’s policies, trade union demands, or household suffrage debates. He skimmed the top lines of every respectable newspaper, and his views on all these noisy, distressing topics were admirably broad, lumping every German, every tradesman, every voter who might use ill-scented soap under the single neat label “brutes.” He never bothered to speak on them, only peered at anyone who dared bring them up from under half-lowered lids, his silence heavy enough to shake the resolve of any timid thinker who dared argue in his presence.

But within the small, tight circle of people and desires he claimed as his own, Grandcourt had the cold, calculating cunning of a master continental diplomat, the kind who pulled off bloodless, unlooked-for coups no one saw coming.

Not a single movement Gwendolen made around Daniel Deronda escaped his notice. He would have scoffed if anyone called him jealous—jealousy, to his mind, would have implied he harbored some foolish doubt of his own power to stop anything he had set his will against. It would not have pained him a bit if his wife preferred another man’s company to his own; what he required was that she understand her longing was as useless as a hand trapped in a locked pair of cuffs, that not a single whim of hers could ever outweigh his unshakable resolve. He had been fleeting, whimsical even, in his choice to marry, but he left no room for wavering when it came to enforcing the terms of their bond. He had never once regretted the union: it had given his idle, aimless days a new sharp purpose, a new object for his will to press against. He did not regret his choice of Gwendolen, either. His taste was fastidious to the point of absurdity, and she satisfied every last box: he would never have stood a wife who did not gain a lift in social standing from marrying him, who did not turn every head with her proud bearing and her beauty, whose nails were not perfectly shaped, whose earlobes were even slightly too large or too flushed, or who, even with all those physical details correct, was a simpering ninny who could not snap back with a sharp, spirited retort. And far from minding that Gwendolen seethed with a barely restrained urge to defy him, that she had married him out of desperation and not passionate devotion, he found that temper perfectly suited him: for a man who has always preferred absolute command to any messy, reciprocal love, there is no reason to change one’s habits the second the wedding ring is on a finger.

This was not jealousy, he assured himself. But his behavior was identical to the jealous man’s, the exact shade of pale bitter yellow that can be produced by two very different sources.

He had come up to London far earlier than usual that spring to meet with his lawyers, iron out the terms of his new will, arrange the transfer of old mortgages, and finally push his uncle into signing over the succession to the Diplow estate, the deal he had lured the old man into with careful, unpressured offers of ready cash. But the pleasantest side effect of being in town early was parading his new, beautiful wife before every drawing room that mattered. Grandcourt would have sneered if anyone suggested he cared a whit for public admiration—but that very state of not-caring craved its required audience, a crowd of people staring, smiling, envying, for what is the point of stonily ignoring everyone’s praise if no one is there to praise you? For the first time in his life he brought a wife out to society, and he let their days be filled with glittering receptions, conspicuous rides through Hyde Park, public appearances at every distinguished event in the season. He wanted Gwendolen to be sought after, he enjoyed watching “fellows” cluster to talk to her, to escort her within his line of sight; he would not have even objected to a little lofty, playful coquetry on her part. The only thing he could not abide was the way she acted around Daniel Deronda.

After the musical soiree at Lady Mallinger’s, when he had watched the pair talk for ages on the settee, his gaze sharp as Hans Meyrick’s own, he did a very characteristic thing: he added Deronda’s name to the invitation list for their own upcoming party, right alongside the Mallingers, making sure no one could ever suspect him of caring whether Deronda came or went. He said nothing directly to Gwendolen about her behavior that night, too proud to let his disgust show loud enough to betray that it mattered. But a few days later, over breakfast, he remarked offhand, as if he had no particular thought in mind,

“Nothing makes a woman look more like a gawky fool than craning her neck to hunt for people and showing off her temper in public. A wife ought to have good manners. Otherwise it’s absolutely intolerable to be seen with her.”

Gwendolen’s stomach dropped. She knew the line was aimed straight at her, and the thought of being seen as gauche and foolish stung her sharply—even in her deep, unshakable melancholy, she still wanted every admirer around her, still wanted to be the most desired woman in every room. But the constant, hanging threat of his quiet rebuke only made her lean harder on every chance encounter with Deronda, stretching their rare brief conversations out in her mind, certain he could not fail to see how much she relied on him. Deronda never avoided her: far from it, he went out of his way to be gentle, to make it clear he still respected her, that the confidence she had placed in him was never misplaced. He liked being near her, after all: she was far more than a problem to solve, a lovely, sad-eyed woman whose fate felt like a weight on his shoulders, a responsibility he could not set down even when he knew his own future would never intersect with hers for long.

Grandcourt inadvertently pushed her closer to that desperate, clinging trust when he brought Mirah Lapidoth into their conversation. Gwendolen had been half-planning to take singing lessons from the young Jewish singer, a quiet promise to follow Deronda’s advice, but the days slipped by, unmarked by any real work to give them shape, and Grandcourt’s constant silent, languid presence hung over her like thick London fog, flattening every small burst of energy she tried to summon into the same bored, hollow indifference he lived in.

One morning over breakfast, though, she rallied the old sharpness that had once made her the toast of every party, picking at the prawn on her plate without taking a bite.

“I think I’ll make myself more accomplished while we’re in town,” she said, light and playful. “I’ll take singing lessons.”

Grandcourt lifted a languid eyebrow. “Why?”

“Why?” she echoed, feigning sauciness, “Because I can’t force myself to eat pâté de foie gras until I’m sleepy, I can’t smoke, and I can’t loiter at the club until I’m dying to come home. I need new kinds of ennui to keep things interesting. What time would work best for you, when you’re busy with your lawyers, for me to take lessons from that little Jewess everyone’s raving about, the one whose singing is all the rage right now?”

“Whenever you like,” Grandcourt said, pushing his plate away and leaning back in his chair, that cold, unblinking lizard stare fixed on her as he stroked the ears of the tiny spaniel curled on his lap— the dogs Gwendolen had come to hate, for the way they fawned all over him. “I don’t see why a lady needs to sing, anyhow. Amateurs only make fools of themselves. No lady ought to risk looking silly performing in company. And I’ve no interest in hearing squawking going on in my own house.”

“How frank you are,” Gwendolen said, tipping her chin up and turning her eyes away from his cold gaze, fixing them instead on the prawn’s pale boiled eyes, far more pleasant to look at than his. “That’s the greatest charm a husband can have, I think. But I don’t suppose you’d object to Miss Lapidoth singing at our party on the fourth? I thought of booking her. Lady Brackenshaw had her perform, you know, and the Raymonds, who are very particular about their musical entertainment. Mr. Deronda, who’s a wonderful musician and the best judge of singing I know, says no one sings half so tastefully for a drawing room. His opinion carries a great deal of weight with me.”

She had meant the line as a tiny, playful jab at him.

“It’s thoroughly indecent of Deronda to go about the place praising that girl,” Grandcourt said, his tone utterly flat, utterly indifferent.

“Indecent?” Gwendolen exclaimed, her face flushing bright red, staring at him in shocked disbelief, too stunned to stop and question how much truth there was to that casual, offhand claim.

“Of course. Especially when she’s under Lady Mallinger’s patronage. He ought to keep his mouth shut about her. Every man who’s been around town long enough can see exactly what his relations with her are.”

“Only men who judge everyone else by the filth in their own minds,” Gwendolen shot back, the red draining from her face to leave her pale, and a second later she was seized by a cold terror that she had spoken out of turn.

“Naturally. And a sensible woman takes other men’s judgment on these things,” Grandcourt purred, his voice soft as he dug his mental pinchers into that pale, shaken creature across the table from him. “Else she’s very likely to run her head straight into trouble. I suppose you think Deronda’s some sort of saint?”

“Good heavens no,” Gwendolen said, summoning every last scrap of her legendary self-control, her voice high and sharp and hard. “Only slightly less of a monster than everyone else.”

She stood up slow, pushed her chair back without hurry, and walked out of the room moving with the deliberate care of a man trying not to show he has had too much wine. She turned the key in her dressing room door behind her, and sat down, pale and still, as if she had not moved at all from the breakfast table. Even when she had read that terrible poison-pen letter about her family’s shame, she had not felt pain this sharp, this physical, as if a blade was twisting right in her chest. The image of Deronda as something other than the kind, honorable man she had trusted hit her like a hideous apparition, unshakable even before she could stop to ask if it was true. Worse, she suddenly realized how little she knew of him, how thin her faith had always been, how foolish she had been to pour out her secrets to him. His gentle rebukes, his quiet kindness, all the lofty, noble things he had said to her, suddenly felt like a sickening mask, the kind of pretense any selfish, immoral man could wear.

Then a stream of golden London sunlight broke through the gray fog outside her window, wrapping around her where she sat frozen on the edge of her bed. She stretched her arms up over her head, lacing her fingers tight in the old familiar gesture she used to fight off suffocating despair, and paced the room in the sunbeams. It was not true. The hateful dream meant nothing. She would not sit here doubting him, not a second longer.

She rang for her maid, ordered her carriage the second she heard Grandcourt had left for his lawyer appointment, and dressed as fast as her shaking hands would let her. She paced the gilded, empty drawing room like a caged animal, not even recognizing her own reflection in the mirrored panels, not caring that Grandcourt would almost certainly find out where she had gone the second he got home. She did not care what punishment he dreamed up for her, not as long as she got one single scrap of proof that her trust in Deronda had not been misplaced.

Her carriage pulled up outside Mirah’s small lodgings minutes later, and she climbed the stairs, her fine silk skirts swishing around her ankles, her heart hammering so hard she could hear it in her ears. Before the door was even fully opened, she heard Deronda’s low, familiar voice coming from the back room, reading aloud. She fumbled with her gloves, pretending to fix the buttons to hide how violently she was trembling, until Mirah stepped into the doorway, soft and quiet, her sweet face lighting up in recognition.

Relief flooded Gwendolen at the sight of her. She stepped inside, sat down, and leaned in close, her voice barely more than a whisper. “Perhaps you’re surprised to see me. I should have written, but I wanted to ask you a very particular favor. I hoped you would agree to sing at my party on the fourth, the same kind of small performance you did for Lady Brackenshaw. I would be so very grateful.”

Mirah smiled. “I would be happy to. Shall I perform around ten?”

Gwendolen nodded, and could not make herself stand up to leave. Deronda’s voice was still drifting through the wall. The words spilled out of her before she could stop them. “Mr. Deronda is in the next room, isn’t he?”

“Yes,” Mirah said, calm as ever. “He’s reading Hebrew with my brother.”

“You have a brother?” Gwendolen said, she had heard the mention once before but never paid it any mind.

“I do. He’s an invalid, consumptive, and Mr. Deronda is the kindest friend he has ever had, the same way he was kind to me,” Mirah said, her voice softening with the force of her gratitude.

Gwendolen reached out and laid her hand on Mirah’s, her fingers cold and shaking. “Tell me the truth. Are you sure he is completely good? That there is no evil in him at all? That all the cruel, ugly things people say about him are lies?”

Mirah’s eyes flashed bright with indignation. “Who dares speak evil of him? I would not believe a single bad word about him if an angel from heaven came to tell it to me. He found me when I was so miserable I was about to drown myself in the Thames, ragged and homeless, no one in the whole world to care if I lived or died. And he treated me like I was a king’s daughter. He brought me to the kindest woman I ever knew. He found my long-lost brother for me, and honors him even though we were both as poor as beggars. My brother is a very learned, great-minded man, and he adores him. There is no one half so good in all of London.”

It was like drinking cold, clear water after days of wandering in the desert. Gwendolen did not even notice that Mirah was angry at her for daring to doubt him. The ugly lie Grandcourt had planted in her head dissolved completely, vanishing like smoke in the sunlight. Deronda was nothing like the vile picture her husband had painted. She squeezed Mirah’s hand, whispered “Thank you, thank you” in a hoarse rush, and fled out the door before Deronda could step out and see her, suddenly desperate to be gone.

She climbed back into her carriage, and by the time she pulled up to her own door in Grosvenor Square, Grandcourt was just arriving home, a cigar between his fingers. He tossed it aside, handed her out of the carriage, and followed her up the stairs. She ducked into the drawing room to put space between them, sat down, pulled off her gloves, and rubbed a hand over her forehead, acting as if he was not even in the room.

“Might I ask where you’ve been at this very unusual hour?” Grandcourt said, his voice low and cold.

“Oh, yes,” Gwendolen said, not lifting her eyes from her gloves. “I went to Miss Lapidoth’s house, to invite her to sing at our party.”

“And to pump her for details about her relations with Deronda?” Grandcourt said, a cruel, icy sneer in his voice that sounded diabolical to Gwendolen’s overtaxed ears.

For the first time since their wedding, all her fear snapped. She lifted her eyes to his, her voice sharp and biting. “Yes. And what you said is a low, wicked falsehood.”

“Did she tell you that, then?” Grandcourt drawled, his sneer deeper, more contemptuous.

Gwendolen went mute. The fiery anger in her throat curdled into dumb, helpless rage. What proof did she have? All the bright, warm certainty she had felt at Mirah’s lodgings shriveled up under his cold gaze. She had no evidence, nothing but her own stupid, foolish feelings, and he would laugh that right out of existence. She turned her head away, staring furiously at the far wall.

Grandcourt knew he had won. “It makes no difference to me, as far as her singing goes,” he said, his tone lazy and dismissive. “You can have her perform if you like. But you will observe this, from now on: you will never go near that house again. As my wife, you will take my word for what is proper for you. When you married me, you agreed not to make a fool of yourself. That is exactly what you did this morning. If you keep acting this way, every man at every club in London will be whispering about you soon, and you will hate what they say. You do not know how the world works. You married me. You will follow my rules.”

Every slow, soft sentence landed like a weight on Gwendolen’s shoulders, and she was utterly helpless against it. He never mentioned the incident again. He knew exactly how much power his words held. That cold, sharp-faced, white-handed man could have governed the most unruly colony in the empire and won endless praise for it—he would have known far better than to cajole the people he meant to control, and he would never have flinched from making their chains as unbreakable as the ones he had just clamped around Gwendolen’s throat.

Deep in Daniel Deronda’s soul lived that rare, generous brand of larger justice that always let gratitude rise higher than any petty resentment. It was the mark of a spirit made for more, a heart big enough to hold joy far fuller than the cynical little wits who spend their days picking goodness apart like bug specimens, sneering that nothing is worth a single “thank you.” Those men are the devil’s own acolytes, poor as he is when it comes to love and trust, forever begging scraps of human warmth from a world they insist has none left to give.

When Daniel parted from Gwendolen that day, he bit back the words “I will not see you again for a long while — I am going away.” He dared not say them, for fear the watching Grandcourt would twist that statement into proof that his absence mattered to her, and land her in more of the quiet, cruel trouble he already knew she lived under. He was heading off to keep a promise to call on her already weighed down by a solemn, thrumming emotion, a sense that something life-altering loomed, that woke the oldest, deepest buried feeling of restlessness he had carried his whole life.

Sir Hugo had summoned him to his chambers with a sharp, urgent note scrawled in his familiar hand: “Come immediately. Something has happened.” Daniel had braced himself for the worst, his chest tight with dread all the way to the baronet’s study, so that when he stepped through the door and was met with grave, warm affection instead of the white-faced grief he’d feared, the rush of relief made his knees feel soft. “It is nothing to grieve me, sir?” he said, taking the outstretched hand, his tone far more steady with recovered hope than it was a question.

There was a weight to Sir Hugo’s gaze he had never seen before, his voice soft with subdued emotion as he gestured to a chair. “No, Dan, no. Sit down. I have something to say.” Daniel obeyed, a faint, heavy presentiment curling low in his stomach. It was almost unheard of for the usually jovial, lighthearted Sir Hugo to show such unguarded seriousness.

“Nothing to grieve you, my boy, no. At least, I hope there is nothing in this that will cut too deep. I never thought this moment would come, truly. There have been reasons for years I never prepared you for it, reasons I never told you a single thing about your parentage. But every day I have tried to make sure that secret never became a harm to you.”

Sir Hugo paused, and Daniel found he could not speak at all. He could not even say he had never felt that lack of knowledge as a wound — not when his throat was so thick with unshed feeling. More than anyone else could ever guess hung on this very second, the moment the decades of secrecy finally broke. Sir Hugo had never seen his favorite face so deathly pale, his lips pressed tight together with an ache so raw it was almost visible. He spoke again, his tone softer, laced with anxious tenderness, as if he was suddenly terrified he would wound the boy he had raised.

“I acted all those years at your mother’s bidding. The secrecy was her wish. But now, she has asked to lift it. She wants to see you.” He held out a thin letter, its page made of soft, foreign paper. “This is from her. You can read it later, at your leisure. It only tells you what she asks of you, and where you will find her.”

Daniel slipped the letter straight into the inner pocket of his coat, pressed right over his heart, relieved he did not have to force his shaking hands to read the words right now. The raw, overwhelming emotion on Daniel’s face had shaken Sir Hugo’s usual composure too, and for a long moment neither man could find the words to speak. Daniel’s whole soul was seized by the one question he had never dared to voice all his life, the question that felt too sacred to waste on a casual moment. He could not let this hush, this perfect, reverent tension pass — if he did, he knew he would never get the chance to ask it honestly, and hear the answer unvarnished. His eyes stayed cast down for long, quiet seconds, the air between them thick with all the unspoken thoughts neither had ever said out loud. Finally, he lifted his gaze to Sir Hugo, his voice soft and tremulous, no hint of blame for all the years of hidden truth: “Is my father also living?”

The answer came immediately, low and firm. “No.”

In the tangled rush of feeling that flooded him after those words, Daniel could not tell joy apart from grief, relief apart from the old soft sorrow he had carried for his unknown family. After a long silence, where Daniel felt as if the old, unformed beliefs he had held about his own past were melting away before he could embrace whatever new truth waited for him, Sir Hugo spoke, his voice thick with quiet confession. “Perhaps I was wrong, Dan, to take on that secret. Perhaps I was even a little selfish about it — I liked having you all to myself, like the son I never got. If I ever caused you any pain I could have spared you, I ask you to forgive me.”

“The forgiveness was yours long ago,” Daniel said, his throat loosening at last. “The only real pain I ever carried was for that unknown person, the mother I never got to meet, the one I am going to see now. It never once dimmed the affection I have for you — that love is the biggest, brightest part of every memory I have of my life.”

As if moved by a single shared impulse, the two men reached for each other, their hands clasped tight for a long, wordless beat.

“If some mortal, born too soon,
Were laid away in some great trance—the ages
Coming and going all the while—till dawned
His true time’s advent; and could then record
The words they spoke who kept watch by his bed,
Then I might tell more of the breath so light
Upon my eyelids, and the fingers warm
Among my hair. Youth is confused; yet never
So dull was I but, when that spirit passed,
I turned to him, scarce consciously, as turns
A water-snake when fairies cross his sleep.”
—BROWNING: *Paracelsus*

That was the verse humming soft and unplaceable under Deronda’s skin the day Sir Hugo Mallinger slid a sealed letter across the oak table to him. The handwriting on the envelope was unfamiliar, sharp and pale, and the greeting sent a jolt straight to his ribs: *TO MY SON, DANIEL DERONDA.*

My good friend and yours, Sir Hugo Mallinger, will have told you that I wish to see you. My health is shaken, and I desire there should be no time lost before I deliver to you what I have long withheld. Let nothing hinder you from being at the *Albergo dell’ Italia* in Genoa by the fourteenth of this month. Wait for me there. I am uncertain when I shall be able to make the journey from Spezia, where I shall be staying. That will depend on several things. Wait for me—the Princess Halm-Eberstein. Bring with you the diamond ring that Sir Hugo gave you. I shall like to see it again.—Your unknown mother, LEONORA HALM-EBERSTEIN.

Its plain, colorless lines gave him no hint of what lay waiting at the end of the Italian road. He did not press Sir Hugo for details, could see in the old baronet’s carefully averted gaze that he had been bound by a long-ago pledge not to steal the moment of revelation from the woman who had been absent from his life for twenty-odd years. The realization that every quiet, years-long hunch he had nursed about his parentage—all the soft, confused guesses that Sir Hugo must be his true father—shattered cleanly, and he refused to let his imagination run wild with new possibilities. He would not stoke any one hope or dread into a consuming fire, would not let himself crave a birth story more than another, only steeled himself to meet whatever truth came, head on.

He could not speak of the errand to anyone, not even those he loved most. Least of all Mordecai. If he told his dear, ragged, glowing friend that he was going to unlock the secret of his own birth, Mordecai’s fragile, long-nursed hope that Deronda was the heir to his own lost people’s legacy would spark into a wild, dangerous excitement that might break him if the truth did not fit. So he framed the journey as a trivial errand at Sir Hugo’s behest, put all the casual nonchalance he could muster into his voice as he said he did not know how long he would be gone, that it might end in less than a week.

Mordecai’s face softened after the first flash of sad, surprised disappointment. “I will ask if little Jacob can come stay with me while you are gone,” he said, as if the boy’s bright chatter would stitch the gap Deronda’s absence left behind.

“I’ll drive round to the Cohens’ right now and ask,” Mirah said immediately, her dark eyes lighting. “The grandmother will not say no to anything I ask these days.”

“Thank goodness you two were both wrong about her,” Deronda said, smiling at Mordecai. “You swore old Mrs. Cohen would never bear to lay eyes on Mirah again.”
“I underestimated her heart,” Mordecai said gently. “She is the sort of woman who can rejoice that another’s plant blooms, even when her own has withered in the pot.”

“Oh, they are all such dear, good people,” Mirah laughed, a soft, merry lilt in her tone. “I feel as if we are all tangled up in the same family now.”

Deronda’s tone turned playful, a little mischievous, teasing her for warming so quickly to the family that had once caused him such worry on her behalf. “How would you have felt if that Ezra Cohen had turned out to be your long-lost brother, instead of the Ezra you found?”

Mirah blinked at him, surprised for a heartbeat, then her cheeks flooded scarlet. “He is not a bad man,” she said softly, staring down at her hands. “I do not think he would ever forsake anyone who needed him.” She darted a timid glance at Mordecai before turning away to busy herself folding a stack of linen, and Deronda knew instantly what memory had made her flush: her own missing, wandering, dangerous father, the shadow that hung over her and her brother both, the fear that he might turn up around any street corner, that the bright daylight of London might turn as haunted and dark as a forest where every tree hid an unseen apparition.

He understood. How could he not, when that same kind of hidden, painful filial longing was thrumming loud in his own bones now? The soft, tender image of a mother who had never gotten to hold him, who he had spent years imagining as a woman who must have grieved to be parted from him, had dissolved the second he held her cold, distant letter in his hands. A veiled, enigmatic stranger had shoved aside the gentle, loving ghost his own heart had built for her.

All the thoughts that crowded his mind as he packed his bags had less to do with the mother he had never met, and everything to do with Mirah and Mordecai. When Sir Hugo shook his hand at the door of the Abbey, his gruff voice thick with unspoken feeling, he clapped Deronda’s shoulder hard enough to make him stagger a little. “God bless you, Dan. No matter what changes for you out there, nothing’s ever going to change the fact that I’m the oldest friend you ever had, the one who’s cared for you more than anyone else all these years. I couldn’t have loved you better if you’d been my own flesh and blood. I’d have liked it better, truth be told, if you were going to be the master of this Abbey after me instead of my fool of a nephew, and you’d have gone into politics like you ought to. But—things go how they go, I suppose.” He rambled off on a trivial, offhand story to hide the fact his eyes were glistening, the way gruff men always do when their feelings are too big to say out loud.

When Deronda arrived at the *Albergo dell’ Italia* in Genoa, the Princess was nowhere to be found. A note waited for him the second morning, saying her arrival might be a week away, or might be delayed a fortnight or more—circumstances out of her control kept her from naming a day, and she begged him to wait as patiently as he could.

Faced with weeks of suspended waiting, with the most important truth of his life hanging over him, Deronda set out to wander the sun-drenched city to still the restlessness in his bones. He had only passed through Genoa once before, in a rush, and now he spent every cool hour wandering its winding streets, the bustling quay, the quiet lanes on the city’s edges, often hiring a small boat to row out on the bay to gaze back at the grand, sunlit stretch of palaces rising from the water. Every sight he saw wove itself back to Mirah and Mordecai: he would sit in the boat staring at the harbor, and imagine the thousands of Spanish Jews, driven destitute from their homes centuries before, crowding these very docks, dying of famine and plague on the stone quays, mothers clutching their dead babies to their chests, haggard fathers staring at their sons as if they had been turned to ghosts. No matter how hard he tried to push the fanciful thought away, he could not stop weaving those old histories into daydreams about his own unknown ancestry. He would not let himself admit he hoped those daydreams were true, that he was one of them. To wish for it felt like a betrayal of the fact that he did not get to choose where he came from, that he had no right to disown whatever kin fate had given him. But still, he could not deny that the sacrifice lay heavy on one side, if he turned out to be no part of the legacy Mordecai had dreamed of sharing with him.

And over all that, another soft, persistent weight settled in his chest: the thought of Gwendolen. There was a kind of fondness for her that was not friendship, not the burning passion of great love, but something softer, sharper, something that felt like “I would have loved her, if only”—if only his heart had not already been claimed by the quiet purpose he had found with Mirah, if only the two of them had not met when she was already tangled in the cruel chains of her marriage. He could still hear her voice ringing in his ears, begging him not to leave her, not to forsake her, the words piercing him like little arrows tipped with pity, as Dante had written so long ago. Her lot was small, sharp, personal, while his own was swelling with wide, far-reaching purposes he could not yet name, but he could not shake the feeling that if he let her fall, a piece of his own heart would fall with her.

The days blurred into one another. Italy hummed with the news that war had been declared against Austria, the whole world rushing headlong toward the earth-shaking battle of Sadowa. The Genoa noons grew hotter, dust thick and white coating the roads leading out of the city, the oleanders in their tubs along garden edges drooping like tired holiday-makers. At dusk, the whole city poured out of its shaded houses: mule bells tinkled down the cobblestones, stringed instruments thrummmed from open windows, footsteps pattered light and happy along the quays, the heights crowned with forts and draped in villas coming alive after their long midday siesta. When the moon rose, it washed the streets silver, turning the steps of the cathedrals and the faces of the great stone palaces into something dreamlike, before the moon sank and the only lights left were the glow of the great Lanterna lighthouse on the dark water, and the faint glimmer of stars overhead. Deronda watched all of it unfold like the slow turning of a great magic clock, waiting for the signal that would announce his mother had come.

He began to chafe at the waiting. His books sat unread on his table, every line of them feeling distant, irrelevant, when his whole future hung balanced on the edge of decision. He would stay up half the night leaning out his open window, staring at the dark, vast stretch of sea and sky, swinging between moods: sometimes bitterly angry that fate had kept his parentage hidden from him his whole life, sometimes soft with a quiet devotion that made even sacrifice feel like a gift, sometimes glowing with a sweet, unshakable hope that he might get everything he ever wanted—love that wrapped around his whole heart, and a purpose big enough to hold all of it.

Then the knock came. It was his third week of waiting, early one morning. A servant in the fine livery of a chasseur stepped across the threshold, and delivered his message in crisp French: Her Highness the Princess Halm-Eberstein had arrived. She would rest through the day, and requested that Monsieur dine early that evening, so that he might present himself to her at seven o’clock.

Deronda’s fingers curled tight around the diamond ring he had carried in his pocket since he’d packed, and his heart began to thud so loud he could hear it over the distant clink of carriages on the street. The waiting was over, at last.

The chapter opens on that soft, thrumming verse, humming in Daniel Deronda’s chest as he lifts his hand to rap on the door of his mother’s suite at the Hotel Italia, the boyhood jitters he thought he’d long outgrown surging back unbidden. The two servants loitering in the antechamber stare, perplexed; they’d assumed the distinguished lady their mistress had summoned a doctor to meet would be receiving a physician, not this striking young gentleman who even makes the severe lines of a tailored evening coat glow like something stitched to adorn a prince. But Deronda notices nothing of their curious gazes, not until the second door swings open, and he steps into a lamplit velvet-draped great room, to find a figure waiting for him at its far end.

She is swathed from the crown of her silver-threaded raven hair to the hem of her sweeping train in loose, flowing black lace, only her face and bare elbows peeking out above a set of heavy, glinting gold bracelets, her head held with the sharp, fine poise of a performer who has held thousands of crowds breathless. Deronda crosses the room in two long strides before he has time to think, lifting the outstretched hand she offers to his lips. She keeps her fingers curled in his, studying him so intently he can feel her gaze prickle under his skin, his face flushing hot and girlish even as he marvels at the strangeness of it: for years he has spun endless, vivid fantasies of this meeting, of the lonely, grieving mother he imagined waiting for him, and none of them ever felt half as real, or half as unknowable, as this. He cannot even guess what language she will use to speak to him, when suddenly she drops his hand, presses both of hers to his shoulders, and her face lights up so bright every line of age and pain melts away, softening into something like the glow of a young woman in her prime.

“You are a beautiful creature,” she says, her voice low and melodious, every syllable rounded with a soft, charming foreign lilt. “I knew you would be.” She presses a cool, formal kiss to each of his cheeks, and he returns the gesture, the greeting feeling less like a mother welcoming her long-lost son than two monarchs meeting for a state audience.

The deep lines of grief and fatigue creep back across her face a moment later, her tone turning cold, flat, unapologetic: “I am your mother. But you can have no love for me.”

“I have thought of you more than any other living soul on earth,” Deronda says, his voice trembling so hard he can barely get the words out.

“I am nothing like what you imagined,” she says, stepping back to fold her arms again, looking him over as if inviting him to see every sharp, unlovely part of her clearly. He had always pictured his mother sharing his face, soft and tender; there is a likeness there, yes, but shadowed by so many sharp, vivid differences she feels almost otherworldly, a Melusina tied to some realm far removed from the quiet, gentle life he has always known.

“I used to lie awake wondering if you were suffering,” Deronda says, desperate not to wound her. “I used to dream I could be the one to comfort you.”

“I *am* suffering. But it is not the kind you can fix,” the Princess says, her tone hardening as she crosses to a velvet sofa piled high with silk cushions arranged for her ease. “Sit.” She gestures to the empty seat at her side, then relents a fraction, her voice softening. “Not right now, at least. I am at ease enough to speak.”

Deronda sits, and for a long quiet moment he waits, feeling less like he is in the presence of the mother he has pined for his whole life, than face to face with some ancient, unyielding fate he has chased unconsciously for years.

“I did not send for you to comfort me,” she says, after a long pause. “I never expected you to love me, not when we have never met, never shared a single day of your life. But I thought I chose a better path for you than the one I was forced down. I did not think I was robbing you of anything worth having.”

“Surely you cannot believe your love would have been something worthless to me?” Deronda says, when she trails off, clearly waiting for him to speak.

“I am not here to pretend I was some perfect saint of a mother,” she snaps, a bright, proud fire flaring in her tone. “I never had much affection to give you. I was suffocated by obligation, by other people’s demands on my life. I wanted to live out the fire in me, not be tied down to other lives that were not my own. You wonder what I was? I was no princess back then. I was the greatest singer in Europe, and I could act better than any performer on the stage. Men followed me across borders just to hear me sing one note. I lived a thousand different lives in a single lifetime. I did not want a child weighing me down.”

Her tone is sharp, defensive, unapologetic, and Deronda can almost feel the blood thrumming in his ears, as if he is watching her perform some strange sacred rite he does not understand, clinging to his coat collar like he is treading water. She goes on, the pale fire still burning in her face.

“I never wanted to marry. I was forced to wed your father, your cousin Ephraim, by my father’s commands — but it was my escape hatch. I could rule my husband, but I could never rule my father. I had a right to freedom, to escape the bondage I hated.”

She sinks back into the sofa cushions, her eyes and pressed lips still thrumming with unspoken words, and Deronda stays standing, breathless, until she looks up at him, her defiance softening into a quiet plea.

“And the bondage I hated for myself, I kept you far away from. What more loving mother could have done? I freed you from the curse of being born a Jew.”

“Then I *am* a Jew?” Deronda bursts out, his voice ringing so deep and loud it makes her flinch back against her cushions. “My father was Jewish, and you are a Jewess too?”

“Yes. Your father was my cousin,” she says, watching him, her gaze sharpening with a flicker of fear, as if she has only just now realized he might not react the way she expected.

“I am glad,” Deronda says, the words tearing out of him unbidden, hot with a wild, unnameable passion he has never dared name before. He is furious at her — furious that she bore him unwillingly, that she chose to be a stranger to him, that she only agreed to meet him now under duress — and that anger makes him bristle, even as he fights to get ahold of himself.

The Princess is shaking now, visibly, her whole frame trembling with a rage of her own, her eyes huge and bright with pale, angry color. “Why would you be glad? I made you an English gentleman! I fought to give you that life!”

“You had no right to steal my birthright from me, no right to choose it for me,” Deronda says, dropping sideways into his chair, leaning his arm over the back, staring away from her, forcing himself to breathe, horrified at how sharp he has been with this dying woman, at this moment that will define the rest of both their lives.

A long, heavy silence hangs between them before she speaks again, her tone steady, hard, unyielding. “I chose for you what I would have chosen for myself. How was I to know you would carry my father’s stubborn, pious fire in you, that you would love everything I spent my whole life running from?”

Deronda breathes slow, deep, fighting to master his anger, to meet her with the gentle understanding he has spent his whole life cultivating. He turns back to her, his face calm, soft, and her gaze locks on his, fascinated, transfixed, no soft motherly warmth in it, only a sort of awed, wary curiosity.

“Forgive me for speaking so hastily,” he says, quiet and grave. “Why did you decide now, after all these years, to tell me the truth, when you worked so hard to keep me ignorant? Why, if you are angry that I am glad to know it?”

“Ah — the reasons people do things!” she scoffs, a sharp, sarcastic lilt in her voice. “No one ever wants to admit they are not following some neat, socially acceptable list of motives. Everyone thinks a mother has to feel exactly the same soft, tender love as every other mother, or she is a monster. I am not a monster. I just never felt what other women claim to feel. I was glad to be free of you. But I made sure you were provided for, gave you all your father’s fortune. Now I am dying. I have a fatal illness that has been eating at me for a year, and the shadows are closing in all around me. The dead man — my father — he won. He haunts me. I had to do the last thing he commanded me to do.”

She goes on to tell him the whole story: of her childhood in Genoa, her iron-willed father who tried to cram her into a tiny box of pious domesticity, who forbade her from singing, who demanded she obey every last Jewish law, bind tefillin, keep kosher, marry the cousin he picked out for her, shrink her enormous, talented soul down to the size of a “good Jewish wife.” She tells him how she pretended to obey, waited patiently for him to die, then ran off to the stage, how Deronda’s father gave up his whole banking career, his whole life, just to follow her, to wait on her hand and foot, because he loved her more than he loved his own soul.

Deronda reaches out, takes her small, ringed hand — the very same hand as his, long, delicate, made for art — and all his anger melts away. “Mother,” he says, his voice soft, thick with reverent tenderness. “Take us all into your heart — the living, the dead. Forgive all the hurts of the past. My affection is yours, I have carried it for you my whole life.”

She looks at him, not with maternal love, but with the awe of one great artist beholding another, leans in to press a soft kiss to his forehead, then sinks back into her cushions. “I reject nothing,” she says, her tone thick and tired, beautiful as a fading aria. “But I have nothing left to give.”

Deronda goes pale, stinging with the pain of that gently rejected tenderness, and she sees the hurt flash across his face. For the first time, her perfect, performer’s poise cracks, softens, just a little.

“The same firmness that serves to resist love also serves to make it violent and long-lasting; weak souls, who are forever tossed about by passing passions, are almost never truly filled by any one of them.” — LA ROCHEFOUCAULD

When Deronda sorted through his post the next morning, one letter from Hans Meyrick stood out immediately, its four quarto pages covered in the tiny, elegant cursive that ran in the Meyrick bloodline.

*In exchange for your sharp breakdown of Italian political shifts and your level-headed take on global affairs, I’ll have you know the wisest consensus at home right now about the fallout of everything going on is simply that “time will tell.” As for what caused all last year’s messes? Everyone’s decided those late fraudulent telegrams are the real culprit behind the cattle plague — which handily debunks all that fake philosophy nonsense and justifies every penny the farmers got in compensation. My own theory, that a murrain of sorts is about to sweep through the commercial class, and the cause will turn out to be them paying top dollar for all the paintings real judges turned down? They called that a misuse of analogy, but there are cruel minds out there who won’t even let a neglected painter keep his silly little solace. I think it’s quite beautiful, the idea that some idiot critic could overpay wildly for my Berenice series, and all those greedy city men will get their just deserts for funding my utterly unearned good luck.*

*Meanwhile, I’m making the most of your absence instead of moping — shining bright as Hesperus now that Hyperion’s gone off to do his grand hero stuff. I’ve been sitting with our Hebrew prophet Mordecai for hours you used to spend with him, sketching his head, and he’s taken to me as a learned young Gentile who’d convert to Judaism tomorrow if he could work out the logistics. We’re in full agreement that everything that is good and great is Jewish by nature. I’m no master of strict logic, obviously, but even I can see the math: if all that is best is A, and B happens to be best, then B must be A, no matter how surprising that feels. That same logic once fueled a whole pamphlet I found proving all good art is Protestant, so you see how flexible it is.*

*Anyway, Mordecai is a far more fascinating sitter than Rembrandt ever had for his famous Rabbi portraits. I walk away from every session with some new revelation. For one thing, for all the blazing passion he carries for his people and their traditions, he’s no rigid, narrow-minded Orthodox Jew who spits after he says the word “Christian” and daydreams about us all drooling uselessly for a bite of Leviathan’s roasted flesh while Israel passes their plates around for seconds. You know I used to write off all your stories about him as over-generous, you always have that soft spot for making even the most archaic views sound reasonable instead of silly. But sitting with him in the flesh? He’s this wild, brilliant mix of philosopher, allegorist, and mystic, and he’s got a sharp enough dialectic wit to prick any windbag arguing with him silent in ten seconds flat. It must be a Jewish superpower.*

*He’s so broad-minded that all my little carefully held correct opinions fit into his worldview perfectly comfortably, and if they don’t line up with all the rest of his grand ideas? That’s his problem to solve, not mine. I long ago gave up on trying to find a “solid basis” for my beliefs — every last one of them is just a world-bearing elephant, expensive to feed and hard to keep upright, and I don’t make enough as a painter to afford a private elephant. I stick to mystery instead, it’s cheaper, longer lasting, the kind of gas that keeps bubbling up as those elephants slowly decompose. If an opinion looks nice, I treat it politely, no grubby suspicious digging. I’m quite fond of his idea that every whole Christian is three-fourths a Jew, and every great, broad thinker from the Alexandrian era onward has been essentially Jewish — because it gives me a perfect excuse to point out to Mirah that Arabic and all that life stuff aside, there’s barely any difference between me and Maimonides.*

*I’ve realized lately only a shallow lover rushes to blurt out a grand declaration. If Mirah’s little ways weren’t so endlessly distracting, if just being in her presence and watching her wasn’t the closest thing to heaven I’ve ever known, I’d have flung myself at her feet ages ago, begged her to tell me point-blank if she wanted me to go blow my brains out. But I’ve got this knack for hoping, you see, which is as good as a reversion estate if you just don’t rush to seize certainty and ruin the whole magic. My Hope wanders the orchard blossoms, feels the warm snow fall through the sunshine on its face, doubts nothing — until it spots Certainty far off, that ugly Janus-faced monster with a leering, dubious grin, and it turns tail and runs fast. You, with your overdeveloped reasonableness, your habit of erasing yourself to prepare for the worst? You’ve no idea what Hope is like — that immortal, delicious maiden forever courted, forever kind, whom fools call deceitful, as if Hope was the one holding the cup of disappointment, when it’s her enemy Certainty that carries that, whom she only escapes by shape-shifting. (See my new allegorical streak? Impressive, right?)*

*But seriously. Truth wins out. Prejudice melts. Merit makes even the oddest differences feel like fascination. No virtuous, earnest wish ever gets frustrated. All those are tenets of the schools, aren’t they? Which means the Jewess I’m head over heels for will eventually prefer me. Any blockhead can quote generalities. The real master of the mind sees exactly the specific case those generalities were written for.*

*I don’t flatter myself that my company makes up for Deronda being gone, but we do get another visitor sometimes — Jacob Cohen, that wild little six-year-old boy from the Jewish neighborhood. It’s a sight to see Mordecai with him perched on his knee, pouring out that Semitic wisdom with that look of sublime melancholy, patience, and devoutness. Sometimes little Jacob decides Hebrew sounds far more fun if he presses his palms over his ears and mimics the muffled noises Mordecai makes. When Mordecai gently pulls his little fists down and holds them tight, Jacob’s face lights up with a hundred little tics, like he’s walking through a zoo and trying to imitate every animal at once — he’s got the owl and peccary down pat, anyway. He treats me like a second-hand Christian commodity that’s bound to drop in price, comments on all my flaws with such frank cheer you’d swear he’s already planning to buy me cheap later. But the second Mirah walks in? He turns soft as a lamb, the kind of dog that was raised entirely by women and won’t listen to anyone else. He’s still fond of Mordecai, though, brings him sugarplums to share, shoveling half his own mouth full so he can watch Mordecai eat the smaller portion and look suitably pleased. Judging by this little modern Jacob, I’m astonished his whole race hasn’t bought all of us up ages ago, pocketed us all as stock and slave property. Though I can think of one Jewess I’d be perfectly happy to be a slave to.*

*Though lately I’ve noticed something that doesn’t make me happy: Mirah’s been getting a little sadder, and she’s trying so hard to hide it. It makes sense, of course — she’s watching her brother slowly waste away, and she looks at him with such loving, devout reverence that I catch myself half-wishing I was the one lying sick, getting all that soft, adoring attention.*

*Other than that, we’ve been merrier than usual. You remember Rex Gascoigne, the law student with the sharp, good upper lip whose sketch you once admired? He’s got rooms in town not far from us now, and his very neat, equally good-lipped sister has been staying with him for a fortnight. I introduced them to my mother and the girls, and they found out Miss Gascoigne is cousin to that Vandyke duchess you’re always going on about — your Lady Mallinger, that is! Those exclamation marks are for how shocked I was at first, before I realized there’s zero reason I should have been surprised, unless I genuinely thought no one could be anyone’s cousin without my prior knowledge.*

*Anyway, that relation came out when Mirah was over at their place talking about the Mallingers. Speaking of which, I’ve got rival invitations now. Gascoigne wants me to come down to his father’s rectory in August and see the countryside there, but I think enlightened self-interest is going to drag me to Topping Abbey. Sir Hugo invited me, and bless his rash, kind heart — he wants me to paint a portrait of his three daughters sitting on a bank, Gainsborough style. He stopped by my studio the other day and told me I ought to devote myself to portraiture. Of course I know what that means: he’s trying to say my attempts at historic, poetic art are pitiable, that my brush is perfect for portraiture — just enough truth, a whole lot of facility for pretty falsehood — that my idealism will never make gods and heroes look right, but it’ll make a fortune flattering rich people. But he says it all so cheerfully, so kindly, that I don’t mind at all. His kindness to me tastes extra sweet, because it’s all coming from how much he loves you, old boy. He told me your duchess — that fair Supralapsarian Gwendolen, whose weird religious tenets you once argued about — has gone yachting to the Mediterranean with her husband. I suppose it’s easy enough to land from a yacht, or get picked up by one from shore. Any chance you’ll run into her and pick up that old theological debate? Is the Duke Alphonso theological too? Maybe an Arian who objects to the whole trinity thing. (Stage direction: As Deronda reads this, profound scorn builds on his face, until at that last stupid joke he flings the letter down, grasps his coat collar like a marble statue, and stands there looking thunderous for a full minute while he mentally rants about how absolutely ridiculous his friend is.)*

*Sorry for the brevity, you’re used to me only spouting bare facts, no digressions or commentary, of course. One fact I left out: the Klesmers, right before they left town, were magnificently generous, the kind of grand gesture you expect when two stars of genius and fortune align. Mirah is loaded down with their beautiful oriental gifts, she’s practically a princess now.*

*What luck it’ll be if you come back and show up at the Abbey while I’m there! I’ll be the soul of discretion, win everyone’s golden opinions. Though I’ll run up to town every now and then, just for a peep into our little Garden of Eden. Look how far my Hebrew has gotten — I’m right up there with Lord Bolingbroke, who knew no Hebrew at all but “understood that sort of learning and what is writ about it.” If Mirah commanded me, I’d dive deep enough to get past all the triliteral roots. I don’t even care if the vowel points are there or not now. But while her brother lives? I suspect she won’t listen to a lover, even one whose “hair is like a flock of goats on Mount Gilead” — and I flatter myself very few heads could pull that comparison off better than mine. So I’ll stay here, with my hope wandering the orchard blossoms. — Your devoted, HANS MEYRICK.*

Only a few months prior, this letter would have gnawed at Deronda, annoyed him no end. Hans’ silly, starry-eyed infatuation with Mirah would have stung, sharp and unkind, with no trace of sympathy for the heartbreak his friend would almost certainly face. But everything had shifted since March. Mirah was no longer in the desperate, vulnerable position she’d been in when the Meyricks first took her in, and Deronda’s own life had been upended, the secret of his birth and his true Jewish heritage just laid bare to him. The new future stretching out before him, even though he dared not wrap it in definite fantasies, cast a soft new light over every old memory and present moment.
