So what Hans called his “hope” looked to Deronda now not like a foolish, harmful unreasonableness that made him indignant, but just an extravagant, silly little dance of his friend’s overactive fancy — and he felt fully capable of pitying whatever pain Hans might face, if he could even imagine Hans ever caring enough to hurt deeply. A whole day stretched ahead of him, no new summons from his mother, and his thoughts turned over and over the quiet argument that Hans was not the kind of man whose love could sink those deep roots that turn disappointment into lifelong grief. He was too restless, too easily charmed by any new novelty, too quick to turn every hurt into material for his imagination, to wear sorrow like some dramatic, fancy new costume.

“Already he’s playing at love,” Deronda murmured to himself. “He’s treating the whole thing like a comedy. He knows full well there’s no chance for him. So typical of him — never stopping to wonder if I might have any feelings about his constant gushing over Mirah. Poor old Hans! If we were stuck out in a hailstorm of fire together, he’d howl like a Greek, and if I didn’t howl right along with him, it’d never cross his mind I was in just as much pain. But he’s good-hearted, affectionate, even if he does always twist everyone else’s inner life to fit his own daydreams.”

That little touch of dry, fond annoyance was enough to burn off the faint heat Hans’ naive openness had stirred in him. The stupid joke about Gwendolen, confirming she was gone yachting with her husband, only brought a soft, lingering restlessness, a memory of that strange, charged parting they’d had. But one line in the letter sparked a far sharper, more immediate anxiety. Hans had noticed Mirah’s hidden sadness, unprompted by any of his own wishes for what he’d find, so Deronda couldn’t dismiss that observation out of hand. He immediately began to wonder what could be hurting her. Was it something that had happened while he was away? Some new fear she was hiding? Was there something wrong with the new life they’d built for her, that could be fixed? Or — had Mordecai, against his usual rock-solid resolve, told her all those precious, secret hopes he had about Deronda? Had Mirah’s quick, sensitive nature been hurt, realizing her brother’s fierce, single-minded conviction had been pressing him and Deronda together, that maybe Deronda’s kindness to them was more pity and self-suppression than equal, genuine regard? Mirah had spent her whole life being dismissed, patronized, until she’d met him — her soul thirsted so badly for equal respect, that it would cut her deep if she realized the reverence she’d thought he held for her brother was tinged with condescension.

He was right about that part of Mirah’s nature, of course. The long, quiet protest against the way the world had treated her all her life was the exact reason her gratitude to Deronda was so fierce, the sharp contrast between how he treated her and how everyone else had seen her. But he was wrong to think Mordecai had broken his silence. Mordecai had breathed no word of their shared connection, no hint of the secret of Deronda’s birth, to any living soul but Deronda himself. Those things were too sacred, too precious, to share without the heaviest, most urgent reason — and he’d seen Deronda flinch every time anyone brought up his parentage. The same strict reserve that had kept him from prying into the Cohen family’s private affairs made him hold this secret tighter than ever.

One day, Mirah had turned to him gently and said, “Ezra, how strange it is. I keep almost speaking to Mr. Deronda as if he were a Jew.”

He smiled at her, soft and calm. “That’s only because he treats us like we are his brother. But he does not like to have the difference of his birth dwelt upon.”

“Mr. Hans says he never lived with his parents,” Mirah went on, curious, as she was about every person she cared for.

“Do not seek to learn such things from Mr. Hans,” Mordecai said gravely, laying his hand lightly on her curls, the way he always did. “What Daniel Deronda wishes us to know of himself, he will tell us himself.”

Mirah felt rebuked, the same quiet way Deronda had once felt, but being corrected by Ezra only made her feel proud of him.

“I don’t think there’s anyone in the world as great as my brother,” she told Mrs. Meyrick a few days later, stopping by the Chelsea cottage on her way home, and finding the little widowed mother alone as she’d hoped. “It’s hard to believe he’s ever been part of the same world as the people I used to live with. I told you once how they made life feel like a madhouse. But being with Ezra, he makes me feel that life is a great, beautiful good, even after all the suffering he’s borne. Unlike me — who once wanted to die after I’d only suffered a little, for a little while. His soul is so full, he could never want death the way I did. I got that same feeling yesterday, when I was tired, and walked home through the park after the sweet rain had fallen, and the sunshine lay all over the grass and the flowers. Everything in the sky and under it looked so pure and lovely, that all the weariness and trouble and stupid hurt felt like just a tiny, tiny part of everything that is, and I felt a little more patient, a little more hopeful.”

There was a soft, dovelike note of melancholy in her voice that made Mrs. Meyrick set down her needlework, turning her full attention to the girl. Mirah took off her hat, smoothed down her curls with a little gesture of fatigue, and settled into her usual chair across from her friend, feet and hands gently crossed. From a distance, she could have been a serene colored statue. But Mrs. Meyrick spotted a new, suppressed look of suffering in her face, exactly matching her quiet hint that being patient and hopeful took far more effort than it used to.

“Is there any new trouble weighing on you, my dear?” she asked.

Mirah hesitated, then shook her head softly. “I think I’m too quick to talk about my troubles. It feels unkind to put painful things into other people’s heads, unless I’m sure it will stop something worse from happening. Maybe I’m just too hasty, too fearful.”

“My desolation does begin to make A better life.”
—SHAKESPEARE: *Antony and Cleopatra.*

For a full day after his first heart-thundering interview with the mother he had never known existed, Deronda waited on tenterhooks, the empty space of their unspoken bond hanging heavy in every corner of the home he had always shared with Sir Hugo. The only message that came was a brief note saying she was too worn thin by emotion to receive him again—until the third morning, when a crisp, urgent line arrived: I leave to-day. Come and see me at once.

He was shown into the same dim drawing room as before, its blinds drawn low and heavy curtains draped across the windows to soften the autumn light into something muted, almost sacred. The Princess was not there at first, but a moment later she stepped through the door, wrapped in a loose, flowing gown of soft dusky orange silk, black lace drifting like mist around her head, her arms bare from beneath the wide, billowing sleeves. In the dim gloom her face looked more arresting than ever: her eyes larger, the sharp lines of her maturity carved deep and vivid, as if she might have been a sorceress who could weave youth and joy for everyone else, but had long since wearied of brewing potions for herself, having drunk enough of life’s fire to last a dozen lifetimes.

She crossed the room in two strides, slipped her arms around his shoulders, and pressed soft kisses to both his cheeks before sinking back into her nest of velvet cushions, her bearing steady, regal, no trace of the fitful, fraying emotion that had shaken her during their first meeting. “Sit by me,” she said, and he obeyed, his chest tight with unspoke hope that they might yet find some common ground.

“You are feeling better, I trust?” he said gently.

“At peace, at last.” She tilted her head, regarding him with the calm authority of a queen rather than the soft fondness of a mother. “Is there anything more you wish to ask of me?”

“Can I find the old house in Genoa where you lived with my grandfather?” Deronda asked, the question he had turned over a hundred times as he walked the previous night, imagining the threshold he might cross to touch the roots of the identity he had only just uncovered.

A small, dismissing movement of her bare arm was his answer. “It is pulled down. Gone, no trace left. But every detail of our family, every place my father lived, all the old stories—they are all written down in the papers locked in the chest I told you of. Better than I could ever tell you. He was a physician, you know. My mother was a Morteira. I heard all those tales as a child, and never listened. I was born into that life without my consent, and I banished it all as soon as I was old enough to run free.”

Deronda bit back the sharp, pained flinch that threatened to cross his face. “Anything I could ever hope to hear from you would only be what you chose to give, what might ease your own heart, not satisfy a list of demands.”

“I have told you everything that could possibly be asked of me,” she said, her gaze distant and coldly meditative. She had steeled herself the night before: I have confessed all. I will not tear open those wounds again. I will not let agitation break me. But for Deronda, whose whole life of quiet filial yearning had been leading him to this moment, it felt like a cruel blow. It was as if he had walked for years on a pilgrimage to a sacred shrine, only to find the altars bare, no emblems of the love he had ached to find.

“Then are we to part,” he said, his voice trembling a little, “and I am never to be anything to you at all?”

“It is better so,” her voice softened, deeper, richer. “There would be nothing but hard, tedious duty for you if you tried to step into the place of the son I never let you be. You would never love me, truly. Don’t deny it.” She held up a hand, sharp and commanding, cutting off his protest. “I know the truth. You hate what I did. You think I robbed you. You are on your grandfather’s side, and you will carry a quiet condemnation of me in your heart forever.”

Deronda fell silent, pinned by her certainty. He stood, rather than stay seated so close to her, the ache in his chest too big to bear while he was forced to hold back every impulse to reach for her and argue that he did not judge her. But she looked up at him then, a new, warm spark of admiration lighting her dark eyes. “You are wrong to be angry. You are better for what I did.” She paused, then spoke abruptly. “What will you do now? What will this news of your birth change for your life?”

“Do you mean immediately, or the whole path ahead?” Deronda asked.

“Your future. Will you turn yourself into the perfect image of my father, the Jew he wanted you to be?”

“The education I was given can never be undone,” he said, his tone steady, firm with conviction. “The Christian sympathies I was raised with will never fade. But it is my duty—my deepest, most urgent impulse—to bind myself as closely as I can to my people, my heritage. If I find work I can pour my whole soul into for their sake, that is the path I will choose.”

She stared at him, her gaze sharp and curious, as if she was tracing the lines of his face to decode a language she did not fully understand. He held her gaze steady, rooted in the full weight of that new purpose. Then she leaned in a little, her voice sharp and certain. “You are in love with a Jewess.”

Color flooded Deronda’s cheeks, hot and unbidden. “My resolve has nothing to do with any such person.”

“I know better. I have seen how men are,” she said, dismissive, peremptory. “Tell me the truth. She is a Jewess who will not accept anyone who is not one of her own. There are a few such.” There was a faint, sharp edge of scorn in her tone.

Deronda felt the familiar, helpless frustration of speaking to someone whose mind was already made up, who would not be swayed by any explanation he could give. He could not bring himself to confirm or deny the secret, tender, unspoken longing he harbored for Mirah Lapidoth, so he stayed silent. She nodded, as if his stillness was confirmation. “You love her the way your father loved me. She pulls you after her, the same way I drew him.”

The words tugged at his imagination, softening his gaze, and she took that as assent, her voice rising with a rush of old, long-buried passion. “But I was leading him the other way. And now my father has his revenge through you.”

“Mother,” Deronda said, his voice gentle, pleading, “do not see it that way. I do not resent the education you chose for me. I would rather be grateful for all the good it gave me than dwell on any wrong you might have done. It would have been a gift to grow up knowing I was Jewish, but I will never regret that I was given such wide, open sympathies, such a broad view of the world. And now you have given me back my inheritance—events have restored to me more than you ever could have taken. You did not rob me of my duty, or my people of my service. Can you not let your heart soften to that?”

He paused, and she looked at him, listening, as if the cadence of his voice had wrapped around her and stolen her attention away from her old, hard grief. She shook her head slowly. “You told me you sought the best for me,” he pressed, more urgently. “Soft your heart to my grandfather, who sought the best for you, in his own way.”

“Not for me. Never for me.” She shook her head, more fiercely, folding her arms tight across her chest. “He never saw me as anything but an instrument. Because I had wants outside of his plans for me, he wanted to lock me in a frame and torture me. If that is the law the world runs on, I will not pretend to love it. If my acts were wrong—if God is making me give back what I stole, punishing me for deceiving my father, for breaking his trust—well. I have confessed all. I have done all I can. And your soul consents. That is enough. I turned out to be the instrument he wanted after all. ‘I desire a grandson who shall have a true Jewish heart. Every Jew should rear his family as if he hoped that a Deliverer might spring from it.’”

She narrowed her eyes, nodding her head slowly, her voice dropping to a low, chesty rumble, as if she spoke the words against her will.

“Were those his words?” Deronda asked, his throat tight.

“Yes, they are written down in the papers,” she snapped, a burst of the old passion flaring up again. “I wanted to thwart him.” Her tone softened, slow, bitter. “You want me to love what I have hated since I was no higher than”—she held her left hand a yard above the floor—“a small child. That can never happen. But what does it matter? His yoke has been on me no matter how much I hated it. You are the grandson he wanted. You speak like a wise young man, full of purpose. But what does it all mean?”

Her tone was sharp, scornful, but Deronda held fast to the bond of blood between them, refusing to let anger win. “Mother, I do not claim to be wise. We are all set in the middle of such messy, tangled difficulties. The only clarity I know of is to be truthful, to not hide the facts that carry obligation within them, that guide us toward what is right. No human’s selfish will can ever outrun the tides of generations, the things that are rooted deep in the sacred foundations of the world. Your will was strong, but the trust my grandfather held that you tried to break, that yoke you hated—it was stronger than you. You renounced me, you banished me as a son—but that greater Something has made me exactly the grandson you once tried to erase.”

She watched him fixedly, and admiration gathered in her face again. After a long silence, she patted the cushion beside her. “Sit down again.” He obeyed, and she rested her hand light on his shoulder. “You rebuke me. Well, I am the one who lost. And you are angry because I send you away. What could you do for me but wear out your own patience? I am a shattered woman now. My sense of life is almost nothing but the memory of what was, except for the pain that comes sharp and present. You grieve that I parted with you. I had joy enough without you back then. Now you have come back to me, and I cannot make you a joy. Do you carry that old Jewish curse in you? Can you not forgive me? Will you be glad to think I am punished for not being a good Jewish mother to you?”

“How can you ask me that?” Deronda said, stung. “I have begged you to let me be your son now. My grief is that you say I can never comfort you. I would give up every dear thing I own to soothe your pain.”

“You will give up nothing,” she said, hurrying, agitated. “You will be happy. You will let me think of you happy. I did you no harm. You have no reason to curse me. You will think of me the way people think of the dead they pray for—you will wish me free of all pain, all punishment. I will see your face instead of my father’s every time I close my eyes. If you think the Kaddish will help me, say it. Say it. You will stand between me and the dead. When you think of me, you will look as you do now—always a tender son, as if I was always a tender mother.”

Her hand trembled on his shoulder, and Deronda wrapped his arms around her, pressing her head gently to his chest, no words enough for the great, swelling compassion that filled him. They sat that way, quiet, for long minutes. Then she lifted her head, a great sigh heaving out of her, as if she had cast off a lifetime of heavy weight in that one breath. The moment of softness passed, and one of her swift, abrupt changes came over her.

“Is she beautiful?” she said.

Deronda flushed. “Who?”

“The woman you love.”

“There is no point in hiding it,” he said softly. “Yes. She is.”

“Not ambitious?”

“No, I do not think she is.”

“Not one who must carve a path all her own?”

“Her nature is not one to make great demands of the world.”

She pulled a jeweled miniature from her wallet, held it up before his face. It was her own portrait, painted when she was young, bright with all the fire and genius of her youth. “I had a right to be more than just a daughter, more than just a mother,” she said, her voice tight. “My voice, my talent, matched that face. Whatever else I did that was wrong, acknowledge I had a right to be an artist, even when my father’s will was against me. My nature gave me that charter.”

“I do acknowledge that,” Deronda said, his eyes moving from the miniature to her worn, pale face, which held a living force no painter could ever capture.

“Will you take it?” she said, her voice softer now. “If the girl you love is a kind woman, teach her to think of me kindly.”

“I will be so grateful for it,” Deronda said, “but I must tell you—there is no certainty that the woman I love will ever return my feelings. I have never dared speak of them to her.”

“Who is she?” the Princess asked, her tone sharp, almost a command.

“She was raised to be a singer for the stage,” Deronda said, reluctant. “Her father took her away from her mother when she was very small, and her whole life has been unhappy. She is only twenty. He tried to raise her to hate her Jewish heritage, but she has clung to the memory of her mother, and to her people, with every bit of her heart.”

“Ah, like you. Attached to a Judaism she barely knows,” the Princess said, dismissive. “Poetry, pretty enough to last the length of an opera. Is her singing any good? Does she love the artist’s life?”

“Her singing is exquisite. But her voice was never fit for the stage. Her life as a performer has been made bitter, hateful to her.”

“Why then, she is made for you. Sir Hugo told me you were dead set against ever marrying a singer, and I can see you would never have let yourself be swallowed whole by a wife, the way your father was by me.”

“I tell you again,” Deronda said, urgent, “there is no certainty we can ever be together. Painful roads may lie ahead of me. I have always made a point of preparing myself to renounce that hope, not cling to it. But I suppose that is how one should feel about all happiness. You should be ready to do without it, even if it never comes.”

“Do you truly feel that way?” She rested her hands on his shoulders, searching his face, speaking low and meditative, pausing between every word. “Poor boy. I wonder what would have happened if I had kept you with me. If you would have turned your heart to the old traditions against mine, and we would have fought and fought, with my father’s blood running in you, tangling up my life with your young roots from that old Jewish root.”

“I think my love for you would have outlasted every quarrel,” Deronda said, his heart heavy. “It would not have hampered you. It would have enriched your life, surely.”

“Not then. Not back then, when I wanted nothing but freedom.” A bitter, melancholy note crept into her voice. “I might be glad of it now, if I could be glad of anything.”

“Do you love my other siblings? Do they love you?” Deronda asked, anxious.

“Oh yes,” she said, as if he had asked if the sun rose in the east. Then her tone deepened, turning dark and honest. “But I am not a loving woman. That is the truth. To love is a talent. I do not have it. People have loved me, and I have let them. I know what love does to you—it is subjection. You take another person and make them larger than yourself, wrap your whole self around them.” She tapped a finger to her own chest. “I never would willingly be subject to any man. Men have been subject to me.”

“Perhaps the man who was subject to you was the happier of the two,” Deronda said, his face grave, sad at the great empty gap in her life that she had never let love fill.

“Perhaps. But I was happy, for a few bright years I was happy. If I had not been so afraid of failing, of losing, I might have kept that joy. I miscalculated. What of it? It is all over. People talk of another life as if it only starts after you die. I have been living in my second life for a very long time.”

She lifted her arms then, bare to the elbows, her brow creased in one deep, sharp line, her eyes closed, her voice smothered. Draped in that dusky, flame-colored gown, she looked like a spirit visiting from some far land of long-lost ghosts. Deronda’s chest ached so sharply he could not hold back a soft, audible sob. She opened her eyes, rested her hands back on his shoulders.

“Goodbye, my son. Goodbye. We will never see each other again, never hear from each other. Kiss me.”

He wrapped his arms tight around her neck, and they kissed. Deronda never remembered walking out of that room, or down the stairs, or across the park to his own lodgings. He felt years older than he had that morning. All the boyish, wandering yearning he had carried for a mother he never knew was gone. He had walked through a tragedy that would solemnize every day of his life from that point on, making every promise he made to the people he loved, every step he took toward his heritage, more sacred than he had ever imagined it could be.

*“The unwilling brain Feigns often what it would not; and we trust Imagination with such phantasies As the tongue dares not fashion into words; Which have no words, their horror makes them dim To the mind’s eye.” — SHELLEY*

Dante’s Madonna Pia, taken by her wronged husband to rot in his isolated Maremma castle amid the malarial swamp flats, lingers in memory as one of the purgatory’s most pitiable figures: a woman erased by petty marital cruelty, left only the hope that strangers would remember her gently. We know almost nothing of the small, bitter grievances that curdled that Siennese couple’s marriage, but we can guess for certain the husband was never a pleasant companion, and the desolate flats of the Maremma only amplified his worst, most cutting traits. In his frenzy to punish his wife, he hardly noticed that ridding himself of her also gave her the freedom he thought to steal away.

We need not grieve for that long-departed Tuscan lady half so keenly as we grieve for Gwendolen, whose purgatory has not yet ended. There is no deliverance waiting for her in some afterlife, no cleansing of the fateful choices that have trapped her, tight as a snare. Her worst torments are not written on the surface for others to see: they coil inside her, private and sharp, far more painful than any outward hardship could ever be.

When Grandcourt dragged his wife off on a months-long yachting voyage, he had no intention of getting rid of her. The exact opposite: he wanted to seal the fact that she was entirely his, to do with as he pleased, and to make her feel that truth down to her bones. He loved the unchallenged, dreamy indolence of life at sea, where no social calls, no tedious obligations could intrude on his absolute control, and he never for a second thought to compare the bright Mediterranean to the drear, deathly Maremma flats. He had his reasons for carrying Gwendolen far out of reach, none of them sounding particularly foul on their face: he’d sensed a spark of defiance growing in her, and that soft, hungry look she sometimes turned on Daniel Deronda struck him as something he would have called unforgivable jealousy in any other man. To himself, it was merely a sensible resolution to stamp out the silly little foolery he’d already caught a whiff of when he’d interrupted Deronda’s pre-planned visit to their home.

He could even have argued he was perfectly within his rights to make his wife honor the bargain she’d struck. Their marriage was a contract where every visible advantage was on her side: he’d given her the rank, the luxuries, the steady income that had lifted her mother and sisters out of ruin, and he’d kept every last term of his end of the deal. He knew perfectly well she had never married him out of love, that she’d only swallowed her quiet disgust for his old secrets because of the wealth and status he offered — and she had gotten every penny of what she’d bargained for.

Gwendolen, for her part, was under no illusions. She could not lie to herself and claim their unspoken agreement had included a clause that she would get to rule the roost and have every little wish met. For all her girlhood tendency to dominate everyone around her, she was not the kind of narrow, selfish woman who counts all her own demands as natural rights and every small expectation laid on her a personal insult. She had a living, thrashing root of conscience inside her, and her purgatory had begun long before they ever set sail: she knew she had sinned, selling herself for comfort.

Try then to step inside the skin of that young woman, stranded on that tiny floating plank of a yacht, with nothing but blue Mediterranean stretching between her and the rest of the world. She had sold her truthfulness, her sense of justice, to this man — and he held them throttled into silence, dragged along behind him like a collared hound, forced to watch whatever he chose to do, without a single word of protest.

What was there to complain of, exactly? The yacht was the prettiest thing afloat, its cabin fitted out to perfection, smelling of sweet cedar, lined with silk cushions and gilded mirrors. The crew was hand-picked to be picturesque and unobtrusive, one of the young sailors even had soft ringlets, tanned skin and bright white teeth. Mr. Lush was long gone, packed off back to England the second he’d finished checking all their supplies. Gwendolen herself loved the sea, never got seasick, and could pass hours happily learning the names of the rigging, forecasting shifts in the wind. The weather was endlessly fine, and they cruised slowly south along the coast, where even the sun-cracked clay cliffs glowed purple in the shadow, and you could drift between endless stretches of blue sea and blue sky, fooled into dreaming for a second that the world has forgotten all its sorrows.

But no beautiful view can sate that hungry ache in the heart that makes sweetness turn sour, that turns soft luxury into a weight pressing on your chest. What paradise could quiet the roiling, burning moral repulsion that gnaws at you like a slow poison, concentrating every thought on that one sharp, unbearable pain? While Gwendolen sat propped on her cushions at evening, watching the sunset gild the waves, praying Grandcourt would not stop his pacing, would not look at her, would not speak a single word to her, some other woman half a continent away walked under a smoky London sky counting the cost of eggs for her dinner, waiting for the sound of her beloved’s footstep on the path outside the door. Some young couple leaned cheek to cheek over a piece of half-finished embroidery, counting their small earnings day by day, saving up for a long holiday wandering the moors where the gorse bloomed golden.

Had Grandcourt the faintest clue what was seething in his wife’s breast? He knew she did not love him, but that never mattered to him. He was not the sort of man who needed to convince himself he was universally beloved to feel happy. What he could never, ever fathom was that she could feel a deep, personal revulsion for him. He knew what personal disgust felt like — better than anyone — he spent half his life sneering at the vulgar, smirky, ridiculous manners of other men and women, at their foolish perfume and overbright handkerchiefs and unwanted silly small talk. Before their marriage, that shared sharp eye for other people’s absurdities had drawn them together, and he knew perfectly well why she loathed Lush. But he could not wrap his head around the fact that she could loathe him. He had gone his whole life being told he was handsome, refined, far above every other man any woman would ever meet — he could not believe, if you had spelled it out to him, that a woman could grow so resentful, so disgusted, that even his polished good looks would become more hateful than the ugliest brute’s face, because they hid cruelty under a mask of grace.

No observer on board would have guessed a thing was amiss, not even the foreign maid who was never seasick, not Grandcourt’s long-suffering valet, and certainly not the pretty, uncomplicated crew, who thought the pair a perfect example of elegant high society. Their companionship was all quiet, well-bred silence. Grandcourt never made stupid jokes she could refuse to laugh at, never picked petty fights over nothing. He was perfectly polite, draping an extra shawl over her shoulders when the wind picked up, handing her any book or fan she reached for, and she could not bring herself to be rude enough to snub that small show of courtesy.

He would lift his telescope, nod out towards the shore, and say “There’s a sugar cane plantation at the foot of that rock. Would you like to look?” and she would answer “Yes, please,” forcing herself to care about the cane as something outside her own private torment. Then he would pace the deck smoking for hours, pointing out a distant sail now and then, until at last he would sink down on a seat opposite her and stare, his narrow, unblinking gaze fixed on her as if she were just another fine piece of furniture belonging to the yacht. She would have to strain every nerve not to meet his eyes. At dinner, he would observe that the fruit was going stale and they must dock somewhere to restock, or notice she wasn’t drinking her wine and ask if she’d prefer another vintage. A lady could not very well snap at him over such small, harmless remarks — and besides, fighting Grandcourt was as useless as picking a quarrel with a venomous serpent that had coiled itself uninvited in your cabin. Where could you even storm off to, on a yacht in the middle of the sea?

Grandcourt was intensely, quietly happy to hold his wife captive this way. Their small, floating world was like a tiny absolute kingdom, stripped of all familiar faces, where every person on board had to do exactly as they were told no matter what private grievances they harbored — and that secret, unspoken protest from Gwendolen only made his little despotism taste sweeter.

Gwendolen, who in all her sheltered girlhood had never once seen even a flicker of true heroism or grand purpose in her life, saw the whole world filtered now through the lens of her marriage and the man beside her. The people we are tied to, for good or for ill, become the lenses through which we see every other thing: a silly, shallow man or woman who seems no more than a trivial acquaintance to a stranger can become a whole warped theory of life to the people forced to live with them, like a pane of yellow wavy glass that distorts every shape and turns every bright color into an ache. Their petty opinions, their low suspicions, their loveless boredom can turn a whole life into a walk through a temple full of ugly, false idols. Gwendolen looked at the whole world through that warped pane Grandcourt had placed in front of her eyes. Some unhappy wives find comfort in the hope of bearing a child, but Gwendolen shuddered at the very thought: to long for a baby, to her, would feel like agreeing to make the terrible mistake she’d already made complete, irrevocable. She dreaded becoming a mother. In her worst fits of despair, the only glimmers of hope she could cling to were the thought of some random, happy accident that would set her free. To dream of benign luck stepping in was the only thing that kept far worse, far more terrible temptations from creeping in.

Hatred can grow as unaccountably, as uncontrollably, as the most devoted love, its strength having very little to do with the small outward grievances other people would notice. It feeds on something deep inside, roots down until every current of your life bends to serve it. The most dangerous kind of hatred of all is the kind rooted in fear: it forces you to silence, drives all your rage into hidden, private fantasies where you imagine destroying the thing you loathe, the same dark little secret rites of vengeance that persecuted people have clung to for centuries just to make their suffering bearable. Gwendolen performed those rites every minute she was alone in her head — but they never soothed her. They only stoked a new terror, a dread of herself, terrified of the violent, impulsive urges coiling under her skin. She remembered Deronda’s words, ringing in her ears over and over: Turn your fear into a safeguard. Keep your dread fixed on the idea of increasing your remorse. Take your fear as a safeguard. It is like quickness of hearing. It may make consequences passionately present to you.

Her prayers were wordless, no more than a quiet cry sent out into the vast silence of the sea, broken only by the sound of Grandcourt’s breathing, the splash of waves against the hull, the creak of the masts. Whenever she dared to imagine help coming, it always took the shape of Deronda’s face, his steady calm voice, the gentle guidance he could offer her. It was only after one of those dark, murderous temptations had crept through her thoughts that those soft, tearful moments of desperate clinging for rescue would come, and she would lie awake wide-eyed, knowing with all her heart: I will not mind any suffering, so long as I can keep myself from turning wicked.

The days drifted by, carrying them past the Balearic Islands, around the coast of Sardinia, then slowly drifting north again towards Corsica. But that soft, gentle floating life, so peaceful on the surface, had turned into a waking nightmare for Gwendolen.

“How long are we to stay out yachting?” she dared to ask one day, after they had docked briefly at Ajaccio. The small taste of solid land under her feet had given her a temporary reprieve from the dark thoughts that had started clinging to the very rigging of the ship, that had mixed with the cedar scent in the cabin and turned the smell of salt air into something repulsive.

“What else should we do?” Grandcourt shrugged. “I’m not tired of it. I don’t see why we can’t stay out here indefinitely. There’s far less to bore you out here than anywhere on land. Where else would you go? I’m sick of all the usual foreign resorts. And we’ll get more than enough of Ryelands soon enough. Would you rather be at home?”

“Oh no,” Gwendolen said flatly. Every place on earth felt the exact same, as long as she was forced to share it with him. “I just wondered how long you planned to stay.”

“I like yachting better than anything else,” he said. “I didn’t get to do any of it last year. I suppose you’re starting to get tired of it. Women are so absurdly whimsical, always expecting the whole world to bend to them.”

“Oh, never,” Gwendolen said, a cold little note of scorn in her voice. “I never expect you to give way to anything.”

“Why should I?” he murmured, not even bothering to hide his smirk as he reached for a ripe orange on the table.

She steeled herself to face months more of this endless floating, but the next day, after a sudden squall that had made her seasick for the first time in her life, he came down to her cabin and said, “There was hell to pay last night. The skipper says we’ve got to stay in Genoa for at least a week while we repair all the damage.”

“Do you mind that?” Gwendolen asked, her face still pale under her white linen bedclothes.

“Of course I mind. Who wants to swelter in Genoa?”

“It will be a change,” she said, her languor making her careless enough to say what she truly thought.

*“I* don’t want a change. Besides, the city’s insufferable, the roads are dust and stone. I’ll take a small boat out every day, the way I used to, and sail around the coast on my own. Get a few hours of peace instead of suffocating in some terrible hotel.”

Gwendolen’s heart leaped. That meant hours of time alone, hours where Grandcourt would not be breathing down her neck, watching her every move. That unlooked-for glimmer of hope made all kinds of wild, contradictory fancies bloom in her head: the possibility of slipping away, running off, the thing she had told herself a hundred times would be worse than any suffering she was already enduring, suddenly seeming like a real escape from the dark, violent part of herself.

That new spark of hope revived her, and she went about her days with a lightness, a cheerfulness, so obvious even Grandcourt noticed the shift in her. She sat up late watching the moon sink below the horizon, no longer drowning in that terrible lonely despair she was used to, but with a soft, vague certainty that somewhere in all this vast, indifferent world, rescue might be waiting for her. After all, the storm had just happened to land them exactly in Genoa, just when she needed a break. That small possibility of hope, after months of nothing but fear, felt like the first taste of food to a person who has been starving for weeks.

She was woken the next morning by the anchor dropping in Genoa’s port, pulled out of a strange, vivid dream where she was escaping over the Mont Cenis pass, confused that the snow under the moonlight felt so warm — until suddenly she ran straight into Deronda, who looked at her and told her to go back.

Less than an hour later, she met Deronda in the flesh. Not on a snowy mountain pass, but on the grand marble staircase of the Hotel Italia, where she was walking in her light woolen dress and straw hat, the heat of the city wrapping around her. Grandcourt was right at her side.

Deronda’s face flickered with surprise before he lifted his hat politely and passed on. The moment felt all wrong for a warmer greeting, and the memory of how abruptly Grandcourt had interrupted their last meeting made him doubt the man would welcome any kind of friendly chat.

That doubt would have turned into cold, hard certainty if he could have seen Grandcourt’s face in that moment. The second Deronda vanished around the corner, Grandcourt’s mind was already racing, jumping to the only possible conclusion he could imagine: Gwendolen and Deronda had planned this whole thing, had arranged to meet here in Genoa. It was impossible to piece together how she could have foreseen the storm that damaged the yacht, impossible to imagine her sending secret letters across hundreds of miles of sea to call Deronda here — but none of that mattered. He was already furious, certain the two of them were conspiring behind his back. He sat sipping his coffee later, angled to watch her across the table, and spotted that glow on her face: the unmistakeable light of secret, giddy delight. Her suffering had not marred her beauty — Gwendolen the wife was far more striking than Gwendolen the girl had ever been, her face softened and deepened by all the pain and grief she had carried, making her more vivid, more human than she had ever been before.

That morning, the hope of their unexpected arrival in Genoa seemed to burst out in every line of her, in the easy grace of her movements, in the quiet brightness of her eyes. Grandcourt, sharp and suspicious as a terrier who knows his master is about to go for a walk, picked up every single one of those small signs, and interpreted them in the narrowest, most unforgiving way possible. He knew exactly what she was waiting for: the second his back was turned, she would slip off to find Deronda, for that secret interview she had been daydreaming of for months.

For the first time since the yacht had set sail, Gwendolen dared to believe her rescue might be right at hand.

---
“Turn back to your own truth, which holds: the more perfect a thing is, the more keenly it feels joy—and every grief.”

When Deronda rounded the staircase and nearly collided with Gwendolen and Grandcourt that afternoon, his whole being thrumming with the weight of the second, life-altering interview he’d just finished with his mother, the Princess Halm-Eberstein. Barely two hours after he’d said his final goodbye to her, he learned she’d already left the Genoa hotel, and technically, every last obligation of his journey had been fulfilled. He could have caught the next train straight to Mainz, delivered the letter from Joseph Kalonymos, and claimed the ancient family chest that held the entire legacy of his hidden heritage. But a tangled mess of feeling, too raw and new to untangle into neat, logical reasons, held him fast in place.

Long after the Princess had gone, he sat motionless, bowed under the soft, heavy weight of retrospection. Every sharp, revelatory moment of their conversation replayed in his memory, bright and burning, as if he’d not just learned his true origins that day, but lived them. Alone in his room, he let himself weep, with a tenderness sharper than any woman’s, over the strange, lonely life of the mother who was so bound to him by blood, and yet a stranger he’d only just met. The whole world felt shifted under his feet: new ties he’d never guessed at had rebalanced every old hope and fear, woven him into a belonging he’d never dared to imagine. It was as if he’d wandered all his life thinking he belonged to one small band of exiles, only to wake at dawn and see the tents of his own kith and kin stretched out far across the horizon. He could almost feel the ghost of his grandfather’s presence beside him, the same fiery passions, the same quiet beloved dreams, stirring awake deep in his own chest. Through every beat of that fevered, loving meditation, Mordecai and Mirah were there too, silent, steady, as if they were holding his hands across the miles in perfect, unspoken sympathy.

That was the soft, responsive core of Daniel Deronda, beneath the calm, self-restrained reserve he’d built up over years of quiet, lonely childhood. When the hotel’s dinner bell pealed insistently to remind him of the hour, he pulled out the railway guide to look up the next train to Mainz, made a half-hearted move to pack his case—and then did nothing at all. His thoughts pulled him one way, to Mainz and the family chest, to London and the two people who were the deepest loves of his life. But another, stronger pull anchored him to Genoa, the same quiet, stubborn urge that makes you linger after a goodbye you already know will be the last, overshadowed by a premonition of grief too big to name.

He never said out loud to himself, *I will stay tonight, because it is Friday, and I want to go to the evening synagogue service, and besides— I might see Gwendolen and Grandcourt again*. Instead, he sat idle, his mind drifting first to the little Genoese synagogue, picturing the faces there no different than they would have been in his grandfather’s day, listening to the ancient Spanish-Hebrew liturgy that had outlasted centuries of wandering, like a hardy weed whose seeds blow across every far shore to tie even the loneliest exile back to home. Then his thoughts drifted to Gwendolen, too, recalling the fear and unspoken despair he’d seen in her eyes the last time they’d parted, and the quiet certainty that it would be cruel to slip away without even a word, ignoring Grandcourt’s obvious hatred of him, to let her know he still cared.

He pushed back his departure, ate his whole dinner without tasting a single bite, then hurried out to find the synagogue. On his way past the porter’s desk, he asked if Mr. and Mrs. Grandcourt were still staying there, and what their room number was. The porter gave him the number, and added offhand that the couple had gone out for an evening row on the bay.

That news tangled with the thoughts he carried from the synagogue, where he’d watched the small congregation of dark-eyed, weathered Italian Jews wrap their tallit around their shoulders, pray with a familiar, unselfconscious warmth that made him smile, and think that his grandfather, whom the Princess had described as such an exceptional, unorthodox Jew, must have been exactly like Mordecai—one of those rare, fiery souls whose far-reaching dreams feed the whole rest of the world, without which ordinary life would shrink down to nothing smaller than the narrow instincts of insects. That quiet, restless ache mingled with his constant, anxious care for Gwendolen, and pushed him to leave the synagogue early, and head straight for the quay, his old favorite spot to wander, hoping he’d be there in time to see the Grandcourts’ boat come back in. He resolved then, that if he saw them, he would walk straight over to greet them, openly, and ignore every petty glare Grandcourt might send his way.

The sun had sunk low behind a bank of heavy cloud, leaving only faint streaks of honeyed gold to kiss the tops of the choppy waves, whipped by a sharp, brisk wind. Deronda wandered slowly along the waterfront, and noticed the crowd of onlookers clustering tight, all their eyes fixed on a rowboat speeding toward shore, two men straining at the oars. Above the babble of half a dozen different languages, Deronda didn’t waste time calling out for news. He pushed his way to the front of the crowd, watching, his chest tight. People held up telescopes, yelling out wild guesses: one man shouted that it was the English milord who’d gone out sailing, drowned. Another swore the limp figure draped in the bottom of the boat was miladi. A French tourist who had no telescope joked loudly that the English husband had probably rowed his wife out to drown her, as was their national custom— a quip an English skipper immediately dismissed as utter nonsense, declaring plainly that the still, reclining figure was most definitely a woman.

Deronda’s blood ran cold. Every possible, terrible scenario flashed through his mind in the space of a single oar-stroke, wondering if the woman they were hauling out of the waves was Gwendolen. Soon there was no doubt left. The boat bumped against the shore, and he saw her, half pushing herself up on trembling hands, swaddled in heavy tarpaulins and thick pea jackets, pale as a corpse pulled from the grave, shivering so hard her teeth must be chattering, her wet dark hair streaming down her shoulders, a wild, dazed horror in her eyes as if she’d woken in a world where divine judgment was about to fall, and every stranger around her had come to drag her away.

The first rower jumped to shore soaking wet and ran off to get help, the crowd of sailors blocking Deronda’s path so he could only stand and watch as the rough, bronzed fishermen lifted Gwendolen tenderly, carefully, out of the boat, her wet clothes clinging heavy to her legs, making her weak limbs even harder to move. Then her wild, wandering eyes landed right on Deronda, standing straight in front of her, and she immediately tried to stretch her arms out to him, as if she’d been searching for him the whole time, calling out in a muffled, shaking voice: “It’s come. It’s come! He’s dead.”

“Hush, hush,” Deronda said, his voice steady, authoritative, soft as he stepped forward. “Calm yourself, you’re safe now.” He turned to the men holding her. “I’m a relation of her husband’s. Get her back to the Hotel *Italia* as fast as you can. I will take care of every last arrangement.”

He stayed behind a moment to hear from the remaining boatman: Grandcourt had gone over the side in the sudden gust of wind, and sunk before they could reach him, his empty sailing boat still drifting out on the waves. He and his friend had heard Gwendolen scream, rowed over as fast as they could, and pulled her straight out of the water when she’d jumped in after him, before she could do herself any real harm.

Deronda hurried back to the hotel at once, to make sure the best doctor in the city was called immediately. Once he’d confirmed that, he sent two telegrams: one to Sir Hugo, begging him to come to Genoa as soon as possible, and one to the rectory where Gwendolen’s uncle Mr. Gascoigne lived, so someone could break the news gently to her mother. A long-forgotten memory of Gwendolen’s, from a time she’d been half mad with grief and ready to confess everything, had popped into his head unbidden: she’d said once, that the only comfort she’d ever craved in the worst moments of her life, was her mother’s presence.

*“The unripe grape, the ripe, and the dried. All things are changes, not into nothing, but into that which is not at present.”* — Marcus Aurelius

Deeds are the pulse of Time, his beating life,
And righteous or unrighteous, being done,
Must throb in after-throbs till Time itself
Be laid in darkness, and the universe
Quiver and breathe upon no mirror more.

The salt wind drifted soft through the open windows at dusk, the hour drawing near to the exact moment the night before when they had carried Gwendolen back, soaked and shivering, from the shore where her husband had drowned. The blinds were drawn low to gild the room in faded amber light, and she sat propping her cheek on her hand, staring unblinking out at the grey rolling waves. She looked less haggard than when Deronda had left her hours earlier, but a deep, heavy melancholy clung to every line of her face, softening into skittish, anxious timidity the second he stepped across the threshold. She did not reach out for his hand, only breathed, “How long it has felt.” Then she added, quiet and pleading, “Will you sit near me a little while again?”

He settled into the chair at her side as he had the night before, and saw her turn to him with that unnameable, wavering expression that said she had something desperate to confess, so he waited, gentle and still, for her to speak. Twice she turned back to stare out the window in silence, then turned again with that same tight, wanting look, the words trapped behind her teeth. Some fear was holding her fast, so Deronda turned his face away to give her space, to ease the weight of his gaze.

It was only a few moments before he heard her voice, trembling and imploring. “You will not tell anyone else? No one else can ever know?”

“Most certainly not,” he said at once. “There is no action that should ever be taken because of this secret. No injury could be mended by spreading it, no retribution any living person could hand down would be just.”

She went so still for a long pause he thought she might be holding her breath, holding back a sob, before she whispered, “But if I had not had that murderous will, just for that one moment — if I had thrown the rope to him right away, would it not have stopped him from dying?”

“No, I do not think so,” Deronda said slowly. “If he could swim as well as you say, he must have been seized by sudden, fatal cramp. Even if you had run as fast as your legs could carry you, thrown the rope with all your strength, there is no way you could have reached him in time. That one dark, murderous thought in your heart never changed a single thing about how he died. Its weight lives only in you, in your own breast. Evil will inside us matters deeply, sooner or later it will leak out of us — sometimes it breeds more terrible wicked acts, but sometimes it stings us with such self-loathing it pushes us to be better, to strive for good we never thought we could reach.”

“I do not have to rob anyone,” she said, voice still shaking. “His family will get everything they are owed, all that they should have. I found that out long before I left London. You do not think I am still greedy for those things, do you?”

“I had not thought of them at all,” Deronda said. “I was far too preoccupied with the hurt you are carrying.”

“Perhaps you do not know how all this began,” Gwendolen said, slow, like she was forcing the words out through a wall of reluctance. “There was another woman he should have married. I knew all about her. I even told her I would never stand between them, that I would step aside. That was the first day you ever saw me, at Leubronn. But then we lost all our money, all at once, and I was so miserable I let myself be tempted. I told myself I could marry him, make all of it right, get everything I ever wanted. But it was nothing like that. It was dreadful, all of it. Full of coldness, and cruelty, and slowly that hatred grew in me, and all those wicked thoughts. I told you once I was afraid of myself. I tried to do what you said, to make that fear keep me safe. I lay awake thinking what would happen if… if I gave in. I imagined how I would dread every morning, wish the dark would last forever, but never get any peace in the dark — because all I would see there was death. If you had not known how miserable I was, you might have… but none of that effort was any use. All I care about now is making sure no one else finds out, least of all my poor mamma, who has never known a day of real happiness.”

Silence stretched long again, before she spoke, her throat tight with a repressed sob. “You cannot bear to look at me anymore, can you? You think I am far too wicked. You do not believe I can ever get better, ever be worth anything, ever be good enough. I will always be too wicked to —” Her voice broke off, helpless, tearful.

Deronda’s heart ached so sharply it felt pierced. He turned his gaze back to her tear-streaked, beseeching face. “I believe you may become far worthier than you have ever been in your life — worthy to lead a life that is a blessing, to yourself and everyone around you. No sin dooms us to hopelessness, except the sin we love, and cling to, and never try to escape. You have already tried to escape. You will keep trying.”

“But you were the one who made me start trying. You must not forsake me,” Gwendolen said, leaning forward with her clasped hands resting on the edge of her chair, her face crumpled with the raw, concentrated pain of the last twenty-four hours, that terrible new reality that waited on the other side of a wish that almost turned into murder. “I will bear any penance. I will live any kind of life you tell me to. Only do not leave me. If you had been near me all along, if I could have told you everything as it happened, I would never have fallen this far. You will not forsake me?”

“It could never be my impulse to turn away from you,” Deronda said, quick and sure, his voice soft and steady — the same voice, the same warm, earnest gaze that always made his ready sympathy feel far more personal, far more special, than he had ever intended it to be. Even in that moment, a quiet, sharp foreboding pricked at him. His overwhelming care for this broken, stricken woman could not hold back a flood of images of future difficulty, of the days ahead. He held her gaze as he spoke, but he was painfully aware that his words to her now sounded like a promise he might not be able to keep one day: he was making an indefinite promise to a hope that had no clear shape. Anxieties, small immediate ones and large distant ones, crowded into his thoughts, and after a short silence he said, “Sir Hugo Mallinger is set to arrive no later than tomorrow night. I have every reason to hope your mother will not be long after him. Her presence will be the greatest comfort possible to you, will it not? It will give you every reason to spare her all unnecessary pain.”

“Yes, yes,” she said. “I will try. But you will not leave before she comes?”

“Not until Sir Hugo has arrived.”

“But we will all go back to England soon?”

“As soon as it is possible,” Deronda said, not wanting to get tangled up in small details.

Gwendolen turned back to the window, her face softening like she was slowly waking up after a hard blow to the head, the twilight deepening fast around them.

“You will always be with Sir Hugo now, won’t you?” she said after a while, turning back to him. “You will always live at the Abbey, or at Diplow?”

“I have no idea where I will end up living,” Deronda said, the heat rising to his cheeks.

She saw his flush at once, realized she had spoken too rashly, and fell quiet. After a little while, staring away again, she said, “I cannot imagine how my life will ever go on now. I think it would be better if I were poor, if I had to work for every bit of bread I eat.”

“New hopes and new purposes will come to you as the days pass,” Deronda said. “When you are back among your friends, you will see new duties laid out before you that you cannot even picture now. Make it your first small task now to get well, to grow calm, to be as much like the woman you were meant to be, before —” he hesitated.

“Before my mother comes,” Gwendolen finished. “Ah, yes. I have to be different. I have not even looked at my own face. Would you have known me,” she added, turning to him, “if you had passed me on the street right now? Would you have known that this was the same girl you saw at Leubronn?”

“Yes, I would have known you,” Deronda said, his voice soft and mournful. “The change to your face is not great. I would have known it was you at once, and seen straight away that you had walked through some terrible, great sorrow.”

“Do not wish now that you had never met me,” Gwendolen begged, tears brimming over her lashes. “Do not ever wish that.”

“I would despise myself for making such a wish,” Deronda said. “How could I ever know what was best for me, before it happened? We have to find our duties in what life brings us, not in the imaginary what-ifs of what could have been. If I were foolish enough to waste time on wishing, I would not wish I had never met you. I would wish I could have saved you from all this pain.”

“You already saved me from far worse,” Gwendolen sobbed. “I would have been far more wicked than I am now if you had never been kind to me. If you had not been good to me, I would have sunk so much deeper.”

“It is time for me to go now,” Deronda said, his spirit worn thin from the hours of holding her grief with her. “Remember the small task we spoke of: get well, grow calm, before your other friends arrive.”

He stood up as he spoke, and she held her hand out to him, soft and submissive. The second the door clicked shut behind him, she sank to her knees, hysterical tears wracking her whole body. The space between them felt as vast as an ocean. She was a banished soul, staring across the waves at a bright, good life she had sinned her way right out of reaching. They found her there, crumpled on the floor, and no one who looked at her thought anything of her grief — it was only the natural, expected sorrow of a poor young lady whose husband had drowned right in front of her eyes.

Time is never a fair measure of how much life shifts, not really. The sun’s long journey across the sky can no more tell you what a soul has lived through than the acreage of a field can reveal the tender, fierce growths pushing up under the grass. A man might wander south, stumble over an old bone, and walk away with a whole new framework for anatomy; head east, and unearth a linguistic secret that rewrites everything we know about the history of races. He might lead an expedition that opens whole new continents, leave half his body maimed, and walk a year of heroism and endurance that would fill a whole epic poem – and come home to find his neighbors still grousing over the exact same parish grievance, the same elderly gentleman still treading the pavement muttering to himself, shaking his head at the same loud butcher’s boy, pausing at the same shop window to stare at the same old prints.

The fastest human thought runs as fast as a greyhound, after all, while the slowest creeps like a limpet, seeming to stick fast to its rock for months before you spot the tiny, quiet progress it has made. That gulf between speeds had never been clearer than in the twelve months that turned the bright, glittering, self-assured Gwendolen Harleth of the Archery Meeting into a broken, penitent young woman, burning to confess how unworthy she felt of any care, any kindness, that she would once have laughed to doubt she deserved. And all the while, the quiet little parish of Pennicote barely shifted at all.

The rectory was just as warm and pleasant as it had always been. Red and pink peonies still bloomed thick on the lawn, hollyhocks towered bright along the hedgerows, and the rector still carried his cheerful, unshakable faith that his patrons would reward his steady diligence, even when they never bothered to check what work he did. The only new project he’d taken on was writing two unsigned ecclesiastical articles, which everyone except the handful of patrons he’d sent special copies to attributed to far more senior clergymen. They never read the pieces, of course, but he didn’t mind: he scribbled little marginal notes on his own copies to make them more fun to lend to friends, and was perfectly happy that the Archdeacon and all his superiors had no complaints about his arguments. It was such peaceful, gentle authorship, no harsh criticism breathing down his neck, no bitter Dantesque leanings from endless toil – it gave him far more quiet satisfaction than writing an entire *Divina Commedia* ever could.

Best of all, his son Rex had come back to him, and that joy outweighed every penny of the eighteen hundred a year the family had lost when their fortunes crashed. No one could miss that the heartbreak of his first love had changed him a little, deep down, but that change paled next to the fierce, new ambition that had woken in him the day the family fell on hard times. The rector even liked to pretend that old disappointment had just been excess moisture burning off, the final step of baking that made his boy’s character solid, perfect. Rex had brought his sister Anna home for a summer visit, and while he laughed and teased his younger brothers and sisters as easily as he ever had, he stuck to his scholar’s routines even on holiday: up before dawn, shutting himself away in his study long after the sun went down, working his way steadily through a fixed course of study.

“You don’t regret picking law as your profession, do you, Rex?” his father asked him one afternoon.

“There’s no other profession I’d choose before it,” Rex said, eyes bright. “I want to end my life as a first-rate judge, help write a whole new code of laws. I’ve reversed that old famous saying – give me the work of making the laws, and let whoever wants to write the songs do it.”

“I expect you’ll have to wade through heaps of useless rubbish for your work, though, that’s the worst of it,” the rector said.

“I don’t think law rubbish is any worse than any other kind,” Rex laughed. “It’s far better than all the garbage novels people stuff their heads with, that rots their wits. Some of our sharpest, wittiest men were lawyers, after all. Learning to look at every problem as a case, weigh every piece of evidence, feels far more useful than drifting through a mess of random odds and ends that don’t add up to anything. And from a higher view? The way law grew, the foundations it’s built on, that’s the most fascinating kind of philosophy and history there is. Of course there will be drudgery, tedious work that makes me want to scream. But no great prize in life comes easy. I see that now.”

The rector grinned, warm with fatherly pride he tried not to show too openly. “The surest sign a man will succeed at his work is that he thinks it’s the finest work in the world. That’s how it is when you love what you do. Brewitt the blacksmith said to me just the other day that his apprentice hated the trade – and ‘yet sir,’ he said, ‘what’s a young fellow supposed to want if he doesn’t like blacksmithing?’”

It was only to his wife that he said, firm and sure, “Rex will be a distinguished man, Nancy. I’m as certain of it as Paley’s father was that his son would make a name for himself.”

“Was Paley an old bachelor?” Mrs. Gascoigne asked, a little absently. The rector waved the question off, not remembering that trivial detail, and she felt silly for speaking out of turn.

That same slow, sun-drenched quiet wrapped around the group that had left the faded grandeur of Offendene behind to move into the small white cottage a mile away, tucked thick with evergreens, that all the villagers called Jodson’s. Mrs. Davilow’s gentle face only had a little more soft sadness around the eyes, a few more silver strands in her hair, after all the trials of the last year, and her four younger daughters had bloomed brighter now they were no longer living in Gwendolen’s shadow. Their old housekeeper Jocosa still went about her work with that calm, neutral air, as if all the glories and pleasures of the world were made for people who didn’t have to work for a living.

That July afternoon, the narrow, low-ceilinged drawing-room was crowded and warm: the lattice windows were wide open, letting in the scent of monthly roses, the soft hum of bees in the garden, and the rare, distant clop of hooves that only made the silence feel sweeter. Anna had been chattering nonstop about her life in London, the Meyricks, and their extraordinary Jewish friends, and the younger cousins had plied her with endless questions about Gwendolen’s grand new house in Grosvenor Square, what she’d been saying, what other people said about her. The girls had all been wildly curious ever since they’d heard Gwendolen and Grandcourt set off on a yachting trip around the Mediterranean: she’d sent two letters, one right before they sailed, and one from Marseilles gushing about how elegant the cabins were, how much she was loving it, and that she’d send back a long diary full of all the little details soon. Their adventures had even been written up in the newspaper, so the book-mad Isabel had already woven half a dozen corsair subplots into her own private daydream of Gwendolen’s grand Mediterranean voyage.

But no one ever brought up Gwendolen when Rex was in the room, per the family’s unspoken rule, and that afternoon they’d all been teasing each other about how absurdly exotic the idea of real, living Jews felt to them, like a weird race straight out of Pliny’s old natural history books. Bertha couldn’t imagine what Jews believed these days, Miss Merry was sure Mirah and her brother could never be argued into the right faith, and Alice declared she was certain she couldn’t bear to be around them at all, until Mrs. Davilow gently corrected her, saying the wealthy Jewish families in high society in London and Paris were perfectly respectable, even if the poorer, unconverted ones were rather vulgar. The whole room had dissolved into laughter, Rex playfully exaggerating each girl’s silly theories about Mirah, Anna begging everyone to see he was only joking – when the maid came hurrying in, holding a letter for Mrs. Davilow, sent over by a running messenger from the rectory.

It had a telegram tucked inside. Mrs. Davilow read it once, then twice, her face draining of color, all the laughter dying dead in the room. No one dared speak. When she finally looked up, and saw all the young faces painted white with fear, she remembered they might be imagining something far worse than the news she’d just gotten.

Her sob was half relief, half horror. “My dears. Mr. Grandcourt –” She paused, swallowing hard. “Mr. Grandcourt is drowned.”

Rex shot to his feet as if someone had thrown a burning missile straight into his lap. His eyes flew straight to Anna, who was already staring at him, terrified the old wound of his love for Gwendolen had been torn open raw. But he forced himself to steady himself, even as Mrs. Davilow read out the note the rector had scrawled on the back of the telegram, saying he was ready to travel with her to Genoa at half past six that very night.

“Can I do anything, Aunt?” Rex said, his voice steadier than he felt. “Do you need me to carry a message to my father?”

“Yes, dear,” she said, already bustling to give Jocosa and Alice orders to pack her bags. “He’s so good to offer. Gwendolen is safe, thank God – but she must be so ill, so terribly upset. Tell your father I’ll be ready the second he is. I wouldn’t waste a single night traveling, I have to get to her.”

Rex and Anna hurried back to the rectory through the golden sunshine, not saying a single word to each other the whole walk. Anna was half mad with worry that he was hurting far worse than he let on, while Rex was fighting a wild, chaotic storm of thoughts that he knew was a sin against every bit of decency he had. When they reached the rectory gate, he stopped, his hands shaking. “Nannie, you go tell my father everything. If he needs me right away, come find me. I’ll just walk in the shrubbery for ten minutes. Only ten minutes.”

Who among us has never had that shameful, unbidden thought, the selfish flash of a possible better future for ourselves when we hear news of another’s great misfortune? The friend’s unexpected death that leaves you the unexpected heir, the boss’s sudden retirement that hands you the promotion you’ve been craving – that temptation makes speech, even prayer, a hard, deliberate fight to push away the greedy, insistent thoughts, until you’re left burning with shame, sick of your own selfishness.

Rex was burning with that shame right then. The second he’d heard the words Grandcourt is dead, a single, wild, shining thought had burst to life in his chest: Gwendolen is free.

The shame of it washed over him like cold, ugly light. Even the thought felt like he was loitering on a battlefield to strip the dead of their valuables. What right did he have to hope for anything right now, when the woman he’d loved for so long was out there somewhere, shattered by grief? He knew, even at the first spark of hope, that it was all useless anyway. Back when they were both young, their lives unburdened, she had turned him down flat, rejected his love like it was something unpleasant. Why on earth would her heart be open to him now, when she was a wealthy, celebrated widow, courted by every man of rank in the country? He was nothing but a struggling law student, still working his way up, not likely to make anything of himself for decades. He had nothing to offer her.

It felt like all the careful, hard work he’d done over the last year, building his new calm, his new ambition, had been undone by three simple words, leaving him back in the old throes of that hopeless, painful longing he’d thought he’d buried for good. His love for Gwendolen had never been a casual, fleeting fancy. It was that deep, ancient, clinging kind of love the old poets sang of – the kind that seeps into every corner of your soul, and stays there, even when you know the object of your love will never return it, even when she hurt you cruelly when you bared your heart to her. He’d thought he’d made his peace with it, that he could live a full, rich life even without the joy of having her. Now, he realized, his peace had been as fragile as the republican government of Florence, his heart nothing but an alarm bell sending chaos and noise ringing through every quiet part of him.

He told himself off, harsh and unforgiving. Stop being a coward. Behave like a man. She would never love you, and that doesn’t even matter right now. She’s grieving. The last thing she needs is you hovering around, selfishly hoping for something. The thought of being of use, of helping his father get ready for the journey, steadied him. He walked straight into the study, where the rector was packing his traveling desk.

“Can I help you, sir?” he said, his courage fully back now.

“Yes, my boy,” the rector said, looking up, and Rex began strapping his father’s railway rug for him. “While I’m gone, keep an eye on my letters, answer the ones that need answering, send me updates on everything. Dymock will run the parish perfectly well. You stay here with your mother, pop back and forth to the parish when you need to, until I get back – whenever that is.”

“You won’t be gone very long, I suppose?” Rex said, forcing himself to say her name out loud for the first time. “Will you be bringing Gwendolen back to England with you?”

“That depends,” his father said, taking the question as perfectly natural. “Her mother might want to stay in Genoa with her, then I’ll come right home. The telegram doesn’t give us many details, and it’s very worrying. But no doubt Grandcourt’s will left her very, very well provided for. I expect she’ll be splendidly looked after.”

“It must have been an awful shock for her,” Rex said, wincing a little as the first sharp stab of pain went through him. “I suppose he was a devoted husband.”

“Of course he was,” the rector said, firm and sure. “Very few men of his station would have stepped forward to marry her the way he did, when her family had lost all their money, and provided for her mother and sisters as generously as he did.”

Rex had never met Grandcourt. No one in the family had ever talked to him about how Gwendolen had run away to Leubronn to avoid his suit. All he knew was that Grandcourt had fallen madly in love with her, asked her to marry him when she was at her lowest, and been far kinder than most men would have been. He’d been a lucky man, Rex thought. He’d gotten the girl Rex had ached for, and he’d had months of happiness with her before he drowned.

Rex’s hands stilled on the strap for a second. He wondered, quiet and unspoken, if Gwendolen had ever truly loved Grandcourt back. Or if, somewhere deep in her heart, she’d still carried that same old quiet hatred of being wooed, of being desired by any man who wasn’t – well, wasn’t him.

---
“I count myself in nothing else so happy
As in a soul remembering my good friends.”

Sir Hugo Mallinger had not rushed to Genoa with the same urgent haste that had carried Mr. Gascoigne to his niece’s side, and Daniel Deronda would never have dreamed of departing before he could meet the baronet face to face. So much hung unspoken between them: the sudden, shocking drowning of Grandcourt, yes, but far more, the cataclysm that had torn through Deronda’s own world these past weeks—the spectral arrival and swift, painful departure of the mother he had never known, whose existence rewrote every line of the story he had always been told about his birth. He could never have laid that raw, tangled truth out in a letter.

It was not until the fifth evening that Deronda lingered on the Genoa station platform, waiting under the hum of arriving trains for Sir Hugo’s carriage, due between eight and nine. Even after all the recent tragedy that hung over him like a damp veil, a faint, soft smile tugged at the corner of his mouth when he pictured the baronet’s familiar, ruddy face—part of every happiest memory of his boyhood. It was a warm, private amusement, thinking of Sir Hugo’s quiet glee at being finally free of Grandcourt’s endless meddling with the Mallinger estates, free to leave his property to his daughters, or as the old whimsical way of thinking about inheritance went, save the best of it for a grandson, a proper heir to carry the name.

What a sour, small world we would live in, Deronda thought, if we could not take joy in our dearest ones’ small, imperfect pleasures, just because they do not line up exactly with our lofty ideals of how life ought to be. We would go through life with permanently pursed lips, frozen, unkind eyes, never able to make space for the soft mess of human imperfection. Even if Sir Hugo had done wrong, hiding the truth of his birth all these years, Deronda’s heart ached to excuse him, not condemn him. He had never known the sharp, private pain Deronda had carried as a boy, wondering who he was, why he never fit, never guessed at the quiet struggles his ward had endured. To rage at that gentle, well-meaning ignorance as if it was deliberate cruelty would be a far worse unkindness. Ten days of bearing witness to other people’s hidden, tangled griefs had softened Deronda even more, made him loath to rush to sharp indignation where affection had lived for decades. So when he saw Sir Hugo’s broad, familiar figure step down from the railway carriage, all the new, sharp knowledge of the secrecy that had wrapped his whole life melted away under the flood of lifelong, deep affection.

“Well, Dan,” Sir Hugo said, his voice thick with unspoken feeling, squeezing Deronda’s hand hard. There was no light, trivial greeting, no quip—for a long moment, the two men just stood there, the weight of all that had happened hanging between them, no words needed. The baronet gave his courier quick orders with a wave, then suggested they walk slowly through the mild, perfumed evening air, no rush at all to get to their hotel.

“Travel went easy, I’m in top form,” he said, as they stepped out under the faint starlight, the last pale gold glow of the lingering sunset still gilding the edges of the dark sky. “I took my time setting off, wanted to dig into a few details first, and I read your letter to Lady Mallinger before I left. Now—how is the widow holding up?”

“Calmer, day by day,” Deronda told him. “She escaped the dangerous fever we all feared after she was plunged into the sea, and all that terrible, shattering excitement. Her mother and uncle arrived two days ago, so she’s well cared for now.”

“Any chance she’s carrying an heir?” Sir Hugo asked, glancing sidelong at him.

“From what Mr. Gascoigne said, I don’t think so,” Deronda replied. “He spoke as if the only real prospect of her holding the estates for her lifetime is if there’s no Grandcourt heir born to her.”

“Hard to imagine her grieving that brute of a husband much, isn’t it?” Sir Hugo said.

“His death was so sudden it knocked her completely off her feet,” Deronda said gently, evading the question, not wanting to speak of the ugly unhappiness of Gwendolen’s marriage to anyone else.

“Wonder if he ever told her what was in his will?” Sir Hugo mused.

“Do you know the contents, sir?” Deronda parried.

“I do, worse luck,” the baronet huffed. “If there’s no legitimate heir, he’s left every last inch of the property to that boy he had with Lydia Glasher. You never heard of that affair, I suppose? She was as good as his wife for years, they have three little girls before the boy came. The lad’s supposed to take his name: Henleigh Mallinger Grandcourt.
