That Mallinger name won’t do him a bit of good, but the little rascal will have more than enough to live on while he’s still a minor. The worst of it is, he gets that fifty thousand I sank into Diplow that Grandcourt never had any right to, in the first place. Meanwhile, our beautiful young widow gets a paltry two thousand a year, and Gadsmere House—of all places. That’s a soft little banishment for her, if she chooses to shut herself up there, which I don’t for a second imagine she’ll do. Mrs. Glasher has been living there for years, for God’s sake. I’m so disgusted with Grandcourt I could spit. Just because he drowned doesn’t mean I’m going to pretend he was any better than he was, though I will admit, nothing he did his whole life served him half as well as dying.”

“I don’t fault him for leaving his land to his son,” Deronda said, a little dry. “I fault him for marrying Gwendolen in the first place, knowing what he did to her.”

“Right you are, about the rest,” Sir Hugo agreed. “He should have set her up properly, given her four or five thousand a year for life, and the London house, after he married her. That’s what any decent man would have done, put her in a position fitting the rank he dragged her up to. She was penniless, so her family couldn’t push for a proper settlement, and that fool let him get away with murder. Men always hide their worst petty cruelties in their wills, don’t they? Grandcourt clearly meant to snuff her whole light out the second he died, if she didn’t pop out an heir for him.”

“And if she had given him a son, he would have snuffed out all the Glasher children’s claim, instead?” Deronda said, a sharp edge of scorn in his voice.

“Exactly. Then it would be Gwendolen getting Gadsmere and two thousand a year, not the boy. Funny, that. One headache out of this: Grandcourt named me an executor. But he was my own brother’s son, so I can’t refuse the job. And it won’t be so bad if it means I can look out for the widow. Lush swears she knew all about his other family, what was in that will. Says the two of them never got along, at all. But you, eh Dan? You’re the one who spent all that time with her, who knows what she really feels?”

Sir Hugo wasn’t teasing, for once. His voice was low, curious, earnest. Deronda knew evasion would only lead to misunderstanding. “She was never happy. The two of them were completely wrong for each other,” he said gravely. “But as for the property? I’ve spent enough time with her to know she’ll be perfectly content with what he left her.”

“Then she’s one of a kind, unlike the rest of her sex,” Sir Hugo shrugged. “Though I suppose she must be extraordinary. Lady Mallinger said the exact same thing when that telegram came: ‘Isn’t it strange that Daniel is the one sending word of Grandcourt’s death?’ I’ve had that kind of weird coincidence happen to me before, once. I was staying at a hotel abroad, heard a lady got stranded there by her husband, no money at all. Turned out to be an old flame of mine, fool enough to marry an Austrian baron with a long mustache and no loyalty to speak of. I wasn’t playing knight errant then, just happened to be there for my own reasons, same as you weren’t chasing the Grandcourts when you came to Genoa.”

The two of them walked on in silence for a minute, the distant glint of the Genoa port fading in the dark. Sir Hugo had started with the Grandcourt business because it was easier, less loaded, than the secret hanging between them, the secret that had brought Deronda all the way to Italy. But both of them wanted to finally lay all the cards on the table, no more hiding. The baronet paused, turning to get one last look at the port glinting far off, his voice softening.

“And about your own business here in Genoa, Dan? I hope nothing you learned cut too deep. Nothing that’s going to change your place in the world, right? You know whatever happens to you, it matters more to me than almost anything.”

“I want to be completely honest with you, sir, repay all your kindness with full truth,” Deronda said. “I can’t say yes or no to that, though. Much of what I learned about the past hurt deeply, and it pained me to meet my mother, ill and suffering, and have to say goodbye. But knowing who my parentage is? That’s not pain. That’s clearing away all the old doubts I carried my whole life, and I’m grateful for it. Nothing will ever change how grateful I am to you, for the fatherly care you gave me all these years. But finding out I was born a Jew? That’s going to change the whole course of my life, in ways I can’t even fully explain to you yet.”

Deronda held his breath, he had never said the words out loud to the man who raised him, and he knew their two very different ways of seeing the world were about to reveal themselves more clearly than ever before. Sir Hugo shot him a quick, startled glance, and started walking on again, thinking hard for a few long moments.

“I always knew you were going to do something remarkable, Dan,” he said at last. “But for God’s sake, don’t go chasing after any silly eccentricities, all right? I don’t care what a man believes, but he doesn’t have to act like a lunatic to make people pay attention. This is the modern world, if you want people to take you seriously, you steer clear of melodrama. I’m not saying you’re going to go mad, far from it. But you’ve always had this soft spot for people everyone else is throwing stones at, Dan. I feel sorry for them too, but making a habit of their company is never a good idea. But don’t mind me, no rush to tell me anything until you’re ready. And if whatever path you choose needs money? I’ve got sixteen thousand pounds of your own that’s been piling up, over and above the income you’ve been drawing all these years. Now that I’m here, I suppose you’re eager to get back to England as soon as possible?”

“I have to go to Mainz first,” Deronda told him. “To collect an old chest that belonged to my grandfather, and maybe find a friend of his. It’s been sitting there for twenty years, but I have this silly, overanxious urge to get it under my own care, like something bad might happen to it if I wait. And I don’t regret lingering here after my mother left, not at all. If I hadn’t, Mrs. Grandcourt would have had nothing but servants to rely on, in the worst moment of her life.”

“Right, right,” Sir Hugo said, a little flippant, to cover the quiet vexation he felt at the news of Deronda’s new path. “I just hope you aren’t going to put the wishes of a dead Jewish man above the needs of a living Christian woman who’s depending on you.”

Deronda’s face flamed hot, and he bit back the sharp retort that rose immediately to his throat. Just then, they turned and stepped under the warm, glowing gas lamps of the Hotel Italia.

---
“But I shall say no more of this at this time; for this is to be felt and not to be talked of; and they who never touched it with their fingers may secretly perhaps laugh at it in their hearts and be never the wiser.”

Old legends tell of a Roman emperor who put ten learned Jewish sages to death to avenge Joseph’s sale at the hands of his brothers. There have always been men of his ilk, whose piety bends only to punishing what they perceive as a slight, who can see the justice of a grudge but never the grace of gratitude. You will never convince a man in the grip of a fierce feeling that his logic is flawed, nor persuade someone who has never loved that there is good, true reason for any heart to bend to affection. The very shape of our language proves this: the word love was spoken long before the word lovable ever came to be.

Daniel Deronda’s hand shook just a little as he handed his calling card across the counter of the bank on Mainz’s Schuster Strasse, asking to see Joseph Kalonymos. The clerk led him straight back to a sunlit inner office, and there behind a table stacked with open letters was the white-bearded man he had glimpsed a year prior in the Frankfurt synagogue. He still wore that same faded old felt hat, and a leather portmanteau sat packed at his elbow, a wool wrap and overcoat folded atop it. He rose when Deronda stepped over the threshold, but did not move forward or offer his hand at once. His small, sharp eyes glinted like polished black gems against the soft gold of his weathered face and the cloud of white hair framing it, and he spoke in warm, guttural German: “Good. Now at last, you are the one coming to seek me, young man.”

“I came to you full of gratitude, as a friend of my grandfather’s,” Deronda replied easily, his own German flowing soft and natural. “I owe you so much trouble taken on my behalf, for which I can never thank you enough.”

Kalonymos’s stern face softened at once, and he stretched out his hand to clasp Deronda’s firmly. “So you are no longer angry to learn you are more than just an Englishman?” he asked.

“Angry? Never.” Deronda’s smile was bright, unburdened at last. “I thank you from the bottom of my heart for saving me from a life of ignorance of where I came from, and for safeguarding the chest my grandfather left in trust for me all these years.”

“Sit, sit.” Kalonymos gestured to a chair beside his table, lowering his voice to an eager murmur as he sank back into his own seat. He took off his hat, revealing a thick, full head of silver hair, and stroked his long white beard slowly, his gaze roaming over Deronda’s face as if he was mapping every familiar line. That moment wrapped around Deronda like something sacred: here before him was the last living link to the grandfather who had dreamed of him, longed for him, long before he was ever born. He could almost feel the electric hum of the chain of his own ancestry, stretching back through generations he had never known existed, and he bore Kalonymos’s quiet scrutiny with a soft, awed delight, the same kind of hush you feel standing in a place where old, long-ago acts still shape every breath you take today.

His face must have shown all that quiet, tender feeling, for Kalonymos nodded, pleased, and spoke a line from an old beloved Hebrew hymn: “As thy goodness has been great to the former generations, even so may it be to the latter.” He paused a beat, leaning forward. “I rejoice I had not yet set off on my travels again, that you got here in time for me to see my oldest friend’s face as it looked in his youth—no longer pulled away from the fellowship of his people, no longer shrinking in proud hurt from the hand that reached to claim him as a Jew. You come of your own free will to claim the heritage and kindred that cruel trickery would have stolen from you. You come with a willing heart to say: ‘I am the grandson of Daniel Charisi.’ Is that not the truth?”

“It is,” Deronda said firmly. “And I must add, I would never have treated any Jew unkindly simply for who they are. Before I knew all this, it was only the shame of not knowing anything of my own mother that made me shy away from speaking of it to a stranger.”

Kalonymos shook his head, closing his eyes for a moment as if the injustice of it still stung. “A robbery. A sin. As wicked as when our young boys and girls were raised to serve the Roman tyrant Edom, torn from their people. But that plot is broken. I broke it. When Daniel Charisi—may his Rock and his Redeemer guard him!—was a stripling no taller than my shoulder, we made a solemn vow we would be friends for all our days. He said, ‘Let us bind ourselves to duty, as if we are sons of the same mother.’ That was always his way: he built his soul out of promises. He used to say, ‘Bind love fast with duty, for duty is the love of law, and law is the very nature of the Eternal.’ And though we were apart for decades in our later lives, that bond never snapped. When he died, those who would rob him tried to take the truth of his grandson too. But they could never rob him of me. I rescued the part of him he treasured most, the things he preserved for his unborn grandchild. And now I get to hand that grandson back to the legacy he was meant for.”

He stepped out of the room for a few minutes, returning with a clerk who carried a heavy, carved chest set down on the floor between them, drawing a soft leather cover off its surface. It was not large, but its gilded iron braces and handles made it feel heavy with weight, the wood intricately incised all over with flowing Arabic script.

Kalonymos drew a small leather pouch from his coat, pulling out an odd, ornate key. “Here it is. Guard it carefully. I hope you are methodic, and wary of the careless mistakes of youth.” He gave Deronda a look of gentle, age-old caution.

“I will guard this better than any other thing I own,” Deronda smiled, tucking the key into the inner breast pocket of his coat, right over his heart. “I have never owned anything that carries so much love, so much hope, so much effort behind it. I will never forget that half of that effort was yours. Do you have time to tell me more of my grandfather? I don’t want to trespass on your schedule.”

“Stay a little while longer. I leave for Trieste in an hour and eighteen minutes,” Kalonymos said, glancing at his pocket watch. “My sons will be here soon, and I want to introduce you to them, so they can welcome my oldest friend’s grandson into their home. They live here in Mainz, comfortable and well-settled, even if I prefer to wander the world.”

“I would be glad to make their acquaintance another time,” Deronda said gently. “There are people waiting for me back in England, friends who need me more than I can say. I’ve already stayed away longer than I meant to, thanks to all these unexpected turns. But the chance to come back to Mainz, to get to know you and your family better, is reason enough to travel across the continent again soon.”

“Good. You will not find me here, though. I am past seventy, and I wander always, carrying my winding sheet with me wherever I go. But my sons, and their children, live here, wealthy and united. The days are better for our people now, ever since Charlemagne brought my ancestors over from Italy, to bring a little light of learning to their rough German kin. My generation had to fight for every bit of that safety, when we were young in harder times. Now we grow our wealth in peace, and the scholarship of all Germany is fed by Jewish minds—though not all those minds still hold Jewish hearts. Were you raised to know nothing at all of the life of your people, young man?”

“Not at all,” Deronda said quickly. “Even before I had any hint of where I truly came from, I found myself drawn to study every part of Jewish history, more than any other subject in the world. It turns out I was already unconsciously preparing myself, to understand my grandfather a little before I ever learned he was mine.”

The line pleased Kalonymos, and he launched into stories of the two old boys’ decades of adventures: Daniel Charisi’s iron will, the deep upright line etched between his brows even when he was barely out of boyhood, the way he used to say it was better to have a steady will than no will at all, better a steadfast enemy than a wavering friend, better a sure belief than no belief at all. He scorned nothing more than lazy indifference. But he was no narrow man: he drank in learning as easily as a plant drinks rain, studied medicine, traveled every corner of the world, spent his fortune chasing knowledge. What he insisted on always was that the strength of all humanity lay in the balance between holding fast to your own identity and reaching out to the rest of the world. He hated the thought of his people melting into the Gentile population, losing all that made them distinct: “It is no different,” he would say, “than letting every kind of grain grow together until all the distinct, precious varieties blur into one dull sameness.”

The two men were as different as the inside and outside of a bowl, Kalonymos laughed. He loved to wander, to do business, to stare up at the great bright stars on the deck of a ship in the East, and be perfectly content with just looking. Charisi was never content to just look: he wove every sight he saw into plans for the future of their people, poured every part of his soul into that core of their faith. But their vow held fast, all their lives, and Kalonymos had kept his end of it to the very last.

“And you have done right by him, and by me,” Deronda said, his voice thick with feeling, as they both stood to say goodbye. “It would have been the greatest robbery of all, to never know the inheritance he spent his whole life preparing for me. I thank you with all my soul.”

“Be worthy of him.” Kalonymos’s hand rested heavy on Deronda’s shoulder, his eyes sharp but kind. “What is your life’s work, your vocation?”

Deronda hesitated, but he could not lie. He had been reading law, but it was never his true calling. “I do not have one, not yet.”

“Get one. The Jew must never be idle. Will you call yourself a Jew now, and hold fast to the faith of your fathers?”

“I will call myself a Jew,” Deronda said, his face flushing pale with the weight of the truth that settled over him in that moment. “I cannot say I will believe exactly as my fathers believed, for even they stretched the bounds of their understanding, learned from every people they met. But I will hold fast to my grandfather’s idea: separateness, and connection. My first duty is to my own people. If there is work to be done to restore, to build up our common life, that work will be my vocation.”

In that moment, the act of speaking the words out loud seared them into a resolve he had never been able to name before, a quiet new purpose thrumming in his bones. Kalonymos smiled, tears glistening in the corners of his old eyes, and spoke a soft Hebrew benediction over him. They parted then, and before Deronda had even settled back into London, the old wanderer was out at sea again, leaning on the ship’s rail to greet the bright familiar stars, no curious questions, no urgent striving, just peace.

“Within the gentle heart Love shelters him,
As birds within the green shade of the grove.
Before the gentle heart, in Nature’s scheme,
Love was not, nor the gentle heart ere Love.”
—GUIDO GUINICELLI (Rossetti’s Translation)

The small, sun-dappled Meyrick parlor hummed with quiet routine that Wednesday afternoon: Mab fumbled her way through a wobbly rendition of *Lascia ch’io pianga*, Mirah’s soft fingers guiding the accompaniment from the piano bench beside her, and Mrs. Meyrick twisted her knitting needles while she waited for her son to arrive. Hans Meyrick never missed bringing the *Times* on Wednesdays — he would joke that it was for his mother’s news habit, that she devoured everything from Continental politics to the marriage notices, claiming the latter let her finish all the fashionable novels without wading through three hundred pages of silly drama to see the happy ending. But everyone who knew him well guessed the real reason: he timed his arrival exactly for the end of Mirah’s weekly music lesson with Mab, so he could linger and listen to her sing.

That day, though, he burst through the door on his latchkey far louder than usual, shaking the crackling newspaper high above his head, interrupting Mab’s wobbly aria mid-phrase. The piano cut off abruptly. Mirah jumped as if the sharp rustle of the paper had been thunder, turning wide eyes toward him, while Mab groaned.
“O-o-o, Hans! You’re making a worse racket than my bad singing!”
Mrs. Meyrick set her knitting down, leaning forward. “What is it? News from Italy? Have the Austrians given up Venice at last?”
“Nothing about Italian independence — but news *from* Italy,” Hans said, his tone thick with the kind of giddy, secretive certainty that came when a long-dormant private suspicion was suddenly proven true. “Not bad news for anyone we care about, I’d say. Uncommonly lucky, in fact. I’ve never heard of anyone dying so conveniently before.”
“Not bad news about Deronda?” Mrs. Meyrick asked, instantly anxious, and Mirah’s heart seized tight in her chest, the same fear coiling cold in her bones.
“Hardly,” Hans laughed, tapping a paragraph on the front page. “Duke Alfonso — Grandcourt, that is — drowned off the coast of Genoa. The Duchess is alive. It seems she jumped right in after him, and the fishermen pulled her out in time, no harm done. And Deronda? He was staying in the very same hotel. Saw them drag her out of the water, was there to take care of her the whole time. What a stroke of luck for the man.”
Mirah sank back onto the piano stool, her eyelids fluttering shut, her hands clasped so tight her knuckles went white.
“Poor thing,” Mrs. Meyrick murmured, soft with sympathy. “She must have loved her husband terribly, to risk her own life like that.”
“More of an absent-minded lapse, if you ask me,” Hans grinned, flopping into the chair nearest Mirah, his tone still teasing. “Who could ever grow fond of a man like that? A jealous, glowering brute, always throwing icy asides like a baritone villain in some bad opera. He got exactly what was coming to him, and now the Duchess is free to marry a man with a full head of hair and a gaze that melts instead of freezes. I’ll put money on an invitation to their wedding before the year’s out.”

Mirah shot to her feet then, her face deathly pale — the usual rose bloom of her lips gone stark white — and her eyes blazed at Hans with a fury none of them had ever seen on her gentle face.
“Mr. Hans, you ought not to speak that way,” she said, her voice shaking with suppressed rage. “Mr. Deronda would never approve of such jokes. Why do you call this lucky? Why do you toss words about life and death around as if they are nothing, when what is deliverance to one person is ruin to another? How do you know it would be a blessing for him to love Mrs. Grandcourt? It could be a terrible harm. She would take him away from my brother. I know she would. Do you think Mr. Deronda would count it luck to break Ezra’s heart?”

The whole room went silent. Hans stared at her, his face flushing bright crimson as if he were a shy girl caught in a misdeed, and he stammered, “I’m a fool. A brute. I take every single word back. I’ll go hang myself like Judas, if that’s what it takes to make amends.” Even in his flustered remorse, he could not keep a flicker of drollery from his words, but Mirah’s anger did not soften. She had snapped the way wounded creatures bite through their own flesh to make agony bearable, and no one dared speak until she turned back to the piano, her shoulders set, and smoothed out the sheet music in front of her.
“Mirah is perfectly right to scold you,” Mab said, tossing a pillow at her brother. “You’re always taking Mr. Deronda’s name in vain with those silly jokes. It’s horrible to speak of him marrying that woman like it’s some trivial amusement.”
“Quite right,” Hans mumbled, turning to stare out the back window, not daring to meet his mother’s eyes.

Mirah lifted her voice, steady now, and turned to Mab. “We still have time for your lesson. Would you like to try that aria again, or shall I sing it for you?”
“Please sing it,” Mab said, eager to let the tension dissolve.

Mirah sang *Lascia ch’io pianga* then, pouring every unspoken, unnameable ache into the notes, the melody swelling with sobs she would never let fall, her voice fuller and more raw than Mab had ever heard it. When the last chord rang out, she stood up quickly. “I must go home now. Ezra is waiting for me.”
She pressed Mrs. Meyrick’s hand, not daring to lean in for her usual goodnight kiss, too conscious that she had been too sharp, too ungrateful in her anger. But the little mother pulled her face down to press a soft kiss to her forehead. “God bless you, my dear.”

Hans had already grabbed his hat, holding the front door open for her before anyone could stop him.
“Hans, don’t you dare walk her home,” Mab called after him, teasing, sharp with sisterly fondness. “I’m sure she’d rather not. You’re dreadfully disagreeable today.”
“I’m going to see she gets there safe,” Hans said, stepping out beside her, and Mirah did not protest. They walked the quiet London streets side by side, neither speaking, each lost in their own whirring thoughts.

Hans’ earlier mischief had curdled into a sharp, sober realization. Mirah was not just fond of Deronda as a friend, as the savior who had reunited her with her brother — she loved him. The revelation stung, not just for his own unspoken, long-nursed fondness for Mirah, but for the raw grief he saw in her. He had long been certain Deronda cared deeply for Gwendolen Grandcourt, that their quiet, charged chemistry would end in marriage once she was free, and he had never once imagined Mirah could be anything more than a beloved little sister to Deronda, not when Deronda was widely known to be Sir Hugo Mallinger’s heir, a man outside her Jewish faith. For the first time, his usual flighty optimism wavered. He longed to say something soft, apologetic, to wrap his silent companionship in tender penitence, to soothe the bruise he had accidentally exposed.

When they reached the door of her lodgings, Mirah paused, looking up at him with eyes full of quiet, sad gentleness. “Will you not come in and see Ezra?”
Hans followed her in, half his melancholy melting away at the invitation. If Deronda was going to marry Gwendolen, he told himself, then surely there was space for him, for his own devotion, to grow, to mend the crack in Mirah’s heart.

They found Mordecai glowing, his emaciated face lit up with quiet, triumphant joy, a closed letter in his hand.
“A letter from Daniel Deronda,” he said, answering Mirah’s unspoken question before she could ask. “He writes only that unexpected claims have detained him in Genoa, but he will be home soon. The promise of his return is like the bow in the cloud to me. To you both, it must be gladness too. Who else has two friends half as good as him?”

While Hans answered him, Mirah slipped away to her tiny bedroom. She did not scream, she did not sob. She had spent years of her girlhood hiding every last flicker of pain from her cruel, selfish father, performing and singing and smiling on stage even when her heart was breaking. She took off her hat, splashed cold water on her face until the dewy curls at her nape glistened, and sat still on the edge of her bed, breathing deep. The quiet old familiar sorrow she had known all her life had returned to sit with her, and she did not fight it. She knew now that the quiet, secret love she had never dared name to herself, that soft reverent fondness for Deronda she had mistaken for only gratitude, was jealousy, sharp and hot as a pincer in her chest. She would never speak of it. She would hide it, bear it alone, no burden to anyone else.

Downstairs that evening, Mordecai spoke to her at length of his faith, of the Shemah that taught the divine Unity, and how that unity promised a future where all of mankind would be one. He told her an old midrash story of a Jewish maiden who loved a Gentile king so deeply, she traded places with the woman he was fated to marry, dying in her stead to give him the happiness he deserved.
“No, Ezra,” Mirah said, her voice soft but firm. “That was not selfless. She would have died so that the king would remember her, so she could hold the first place in his heart, even in death. There was jealousy in it. You imagine only the greatest, most selfless love in your stories. But I do not think it works that way.”
Mordecai was quiet for a long moment, smiling faintly. “You have spent too long watching the passions portrayed in plays, little sister. You judge by the stage, and not by your own soft, good heart, the one that is so like our mother’s.”
Mirah said nothing. She sat quiet, stirring his tea, and tucked that sharp, secret, shameful love of hers deeper into the dark, hidden part of her heart, where no one would ever see it.

“Good Fortune is a flighty lass who never tarries long in one place: she brushes the hair from your forehead, steals a quick kiss, and flutters off before you can grasp her. But Dame Ill Luck? She presses you tight to her heart, swears she’s in no hurry, pulls a chair up to your bedside, and settles in to knit beside you for hours.”

The quiet, heavy sorrow Mirah had been bracing herself for, the lingering threat she’d half expected to creep up on her for weeks, had finally arrived, and it came dragging all its old, heavy baggage behind it.

She’d just left a charity morning concert in a stately Knightsbridge townhouse, where Klesmer had recommended her to sing for a crowd of wealthy patrons. Her performance dress was plain, unadorned black, wrapped over with a light dust cloak to shield it from the grime of the London streets, nothing about her marked to draw unwelcome notice. But the steady, even footsteps trailing her as the last of the departing guests climbed into their carriages sent a cold prickle up her spine, and her feet went heavy as lead. She knew that pace. It was the ghost she’d fled, the father she’d never thought she’d see again once she’d found her way to Mrs Meyrick’s hearth, once she’d been reunited with Ezra. She could not bring herself to turn and face him, every muscle tensed against the rush of old, tangled grief and shame that would crash over her the second their eyes met. If it was him, he would make her stop soon enough. She walked on, not speeding her steps—there was no outrunning the past—all the while wishing she had not given Mrs Meyrick her word not to hide anything about him. That regret hardened into a sharp, urgent purpose: she would not let him surprise her at her own door, not when Ezra might be caught off guard, not when she could soften the shock for the brother she cherished. She would face him of her own will, before they turned into the little quiet square that held her new home.

She was just rounding the square’s stone entrance when the footsteps drew level, a calloused hand curled firm around her wrist, and a voice with that old, honeyed, familiar lilt purred, “Mirah!”

She did not start. She had been waiting for that voice. Her face was grave as if she stood before an executioner, while his was arranged carefully to seem soft, penitent, eager to win her over. Once he had been a strikingly handsome man, bright of cheek and quick of smile, but now his skin was sallow, lined deep by years of wheedling favors and shrugging off consequences, and he carried that easy, impudent suavity of a man who has long since learned to court affection while expecting no real respect. Slim, agile, he looked far younger than his fifty-seven years, and his threadbare, ill-fitting coat was just the same as the one she remembered from the last awful day she’d run from him. The old, tangled anguish of shame and grief, revulsion and pity, swelled in her chest sharper than it ever had before: now she belonged to a world of people who loved her, who valued her, and every line of his worn, shabby frame made him a target for the very contempt she would have given her own life to spare him.

“It is you, father,” she said, her voice low and trembling.

“Why did you run away from me, little one?” he began, words tumbling fast, as if he had rehearsed them a hundred times before, his hands darting in little quick gestures like a shorthand language. “What did you ever have to fear from me? I never made you do a single thing against your will. I broke that terrible engagement for you in the Vorstadt because I saw it didn’t suit you, and you repaid me by vanishing, leaving me to face the consequences. I’d landed you a far better spot at the Dresden Vorstadt Theater, you see— I never told you, I wanted to surprise you. And you left me stranded there, forced to slip out of town in the dead of night to avoid being locked up for breaking contract. That was hard, after I’d given up every last thing to give you the singing education that was supposed to make your fortune. No other father on earth ever poured more of himself into his daughter than I poured into you. You saw how I bore that blow when your voice didn’t turn out to be the great fortune we’d hoped for, and I made the best of it. And when I had no one else in the world but you, when I was getting worn down fighting my way through life with nothing but my wits? You chose that exact moment to abandon me. Who else did you owe every single thing to, if not me? For all you cared, I could have died in a ditch.”

He stopped short, not for lack of excuses, but to hit that carefully calibrated pathetic climax, and he let out a sharp, feminine-sounding sob, fumbling for an old yellow silk handkerchief from his pocket. He truly, in that moment, believed Mirah had treated him cruelly— the kind of selfish sensitivity that grows strong in unscrupulous men, who keep a running tally of what the world owes them and never once bother to subtract the harm they have done.

But Mirah had held firm to her newfound courage, and for the first time in her life, she spoke to him with quiet accusation. “You know exactly why I left you, father. I learned I could not trust you, that you had lied to my mother. If I could have believed a single word you said, I would have stayed by your side and worked for you until my hands ached.”

“I never meant to deceive your mother, Mirah,” he said, sniffling and tucking his handkerchief away, voice thick as if he was fighting back more sobs. “I was going to bring you both back to her, but delays and bad luck got in the way, and then word came she was already dead. It was better for you to stay with me, anyway— Ezra was old enough to look after himself. No one had any claim on me but you. A close friend of mine wrote to tell me the news, and I sent him money to cover all the funeral expenses. Though, to be sure, there’s always the chance—” he added quickly, already guarding against the unlikely possibility Mirah might have proof otherwise “—that he lied to me just to get his hands on that cash.”

Mirah said nothing. She could not bear to say out loud the truth burning in her throat, that she did not believe a single word that came out of his mouth, and she only motioned for them to keep walking, afraid that their odd mismatched pair—her, neat and polished in the fine dress her new friends had gifted her, and him, shabby and foreign-looking, gesticulating wildly at her side—would draw stares from passersby.

“You seem to have done very well for yourself, little Mirah. You’re not wanting for anything, are you?” he said, eyes raking over her new coat, her neat boots, sharp as a crow picking over scraps.

“Good friends found me when I was destitute, and helped me find work,” Mirah said, her mind half already on the words she would have to say when they reached her door. “I give singing lessons, I perform at private houses. I just finished singing at a charity concert, today.” She paused, then added pointedly, “I have very good friends, who know every single thing about my past.”

“And you’d be ashamed for them to see your own father turned out like a beggar? I don’t blame you. I came all the way to England with nothing but the faint hope of finding you. A mad, foolish quest, but a father’s heart is superstitious— it feels a magnet drawing it somewhere, no matter how far. I could have done perfectly well staying abroad, when I didn’t have you to care for. I could have rolled through life as easy as a marble. But it’s a cruel thing to be all alone in the world, when your spirit is starting to wear thin. I thought my little Mirah would come to her senses, and repent leaving her old father behind. I’ve scraped and scrimped to get by, and I don’t know what I’ll be reduced to next. A man with my talents is useless in this country. Once your coat’s frayed at the cuffs, no one will give you a second look, no matter what you can do. I’ve already had to sink pretty low just to earn a single shilling.”

Mirah’s heart ached, her mind racing with ways to keep him from sinking further into degradation, before he cut in sharply: “Where do you live, Mirah?”

“Right here, in this square. We’re almost at the door.”

“Lodgings?”

“Yes.”

“Anyone there to look after you?”

“Yes,” Mirah said, meeting his sharp, darting gaze full on. “My brother.”

Lapidoth’s eyelids flinched as if lightning had struck right in front of his face, his shoulders twitching almost imperceptibly. After a long, visible pause, he said, “Ezra? How on earth did you find him?”

“It’s a long story. This is our door.” Mirah stepped up onto the stone doorstep, turning back to face him where he stood on the pavement below. Her heart was thudding hard at the thought of the meeting that was coming, of the son he had abandoned so many years before. Standing there, looking down at him, seeing the faint, shrinking unease in his frame, she felt that sharp, tender filial shame, that stab at the heart of the reverence she had always held for him no matter what he did. “He will never ask me to shut the door on you.”

“Wait a minute, my dearest,” Lapidoth said, voice dropping low and cajoling, the old familiar tone he had used to get his way when she was small. “What sort of man has Ezra grown into?”

“He is a good man. A remarkable man,” Mirah said, slow and firm, trying to steady her trembling voice as she tried to warn him, to prepare him for the man he would be standing before. “He was very poor when our friends found him, a humble workman. Twelve years ago, he was strong and happy, making his way to the East that he loved so dearly, but our mother called him back— because she had lost me. He went to her, and took care of her through every terrible hardship, worked himself to the bone for her until she died of grief, heartbroken. The cold bit through him on his journey back to her, and his strength never recovered. For years he’s grown weaker, always poor, always working— but he’s full of wisdom, great and noble in his soul. Everyone who meets him honors him. To stand before him is like standing before a prophet of God.” Mirah’s voice broke, her heart hammering in her chest. “Lies will do you no good there.”

She cast her eyes down, unable to bear the sight of the frustrated, ugly look that settled over his face. But Lapidoth never missed a beat, already spinning a new plan.

“Mirah, my love,” he said, soft and wheedling, “wouldn’t you rather I make myself a little more presentable before my son sees me? If you could lend me a small sum, say ten pounds, I could get myself a new set of clothes, and come home to you both as a father ought to, and then I could find myself a decent post. With a decent shirt and coat on my back, people would jump at the chance to hire me as a courier, instead of turning up their noses at a ragged-looking old mountebank. I want to come home to my children, forget all the hard times, and forgive all that’s past. You’ve never seen your father look so ragged. If you can give me the ten pounds right now, or meet me somewhere to give it to me, I could be properly fitted out by the day after tomorrow.”

Mirah could feel the temptation pulling at her, but she fought it. She looked back up at him, steady. “I hate to say no to you, father. But I promised I would never do things for you in secret. It hurts so bad to see you looking so needy, but we can bear that for a little while. Then you can have all the new clothes you need, and we will pay for them together.” She saw now exactly how wise Mrs Meyrick had been, to make her swear that promise.

Lapidoth’s easy smile dropped away, and he sneered. “You’re a hard little thing, aren’t you? Learned all sorts of fine little virtues, keeping promises that stop you from giving your own father a couple of pounds when you’re running around in fine silk dresses, the father who worshipped you as his little idol and gave up the best years of his life to raise you.”

“I know it feels cruel,” Mirah said, her lips going suddenly pale. This moment felt worse than the day she had walked to the river to drown herself. “But it is far crueler to break the promises that the people who love you trust in. That is what broke my mother’s heart. That is what broke Ezra’s health. We have to bear the bitterness of all our past mistakes now, together. Come in with me, just as you are. We will take care of you.”

“Tomorrow, then,” Lapidoth said, half turning on his heel away from her, from the trembling pale girl who now had the whole world of good, honorable people behind her. But he spun right back, hands patting restlessly at his pockets, voice soft and appealing again. “This has all knocked me for a loop, Mirah. I’ll gather my spirits by tomorrow. But if you’ve got a little loose change in your pocket, it wouldn’t break your promise to give me a trifle, just enough to buy a cigar to calm my nerves on the walk back.”

Mirah could not think straight. Her cold, trembling hands went into her pocket for her purse, and she held it out to him. He grabbed it immediately, squeezing her fingers in his, and said, “Goodbye, little girl. Tomorrow, then!” and vanished down the street.

He had not gone more than a dozen steps before he fumbled open the purse, counting out the two half-sovereigns and loose silver, and found pasted inside the fold a scrap of paper: Ezra’s beautiful, neat Hebrew script, their mother’s name, her birth, marriage, and death dates, and the prayer scrawled at the bottom: May Mirah be delivered from all harm. Mirah loved to tuck little pieces of Ezra’s writing in every one of her possessions. Lapidoth read it, and for a split second he saw his own wedding day, the bright, young, blameless man he had once been, full of plans to make his fortune writing, head over heels in love with his beautiful bride Sara, soft and sensitive to every one of her moods. He had traveled so very far from that young man. The memory was hollow, no feeling attached to it, like looking at a meal you could no longer taste. In the long lazy, selfish years of his life, he had gambled away his capacity for remorse, for unselfish regret, the same way a dying man loses the feeling in his extremities, and watches the numbness creep higher.

He snapped back to the present quickly enough, already calculating how much he could pawn the pretty, fine gift purse for, on top of the cash inside, and how he could squeeze more money out of Mirah without ever having to move into that house and live under the watchful, unblinking eye of that formidable, honorable son.

Back inside the little Brompton house, Mirah could not hold her pain in any longer. She found Ezra sitting by the fire, sorting through his old handwritten manuscripts, planning to give them to Deronda. All her months of careful self-control shattered, and she fell to her knees at his feet, clinging to his sobs tearing out of her, crying “Ezra, Ezra!”

He did not speak at once, his fear spurring him to imagine every worst possible harm that could have come to her, but Mirah fought to catch her breath, and blurted it all out through tears. “My father. Our father. He followed me home. I wanted to bring him inside, I told him you would never turn him away. But he said no, not now. He said he would come tomorrow. He begged me for money, and I gave him my purse, and he left.”

Ezra’s face softened, his fears fading. “Wait until you are calm, little one, and tell me every single thing that happened.” He took off her hat, and laid his warm, gentle hands on her head, and the quiet calm of him seeped into her until she could tell him the whole story, word for word.

“He will not come tomorrow,” Mordecai said, quiet and sure. Neither of them said what they both knew: that he would likely lurk around the square, waiting to catch Mirah alone again, to beg more money from her.

“Dost thou see?” he said after a long, quiet moment, his voice soft and ancient, like a Rabbi speaking wisdom handed down for a thousand years. “This is the lot of Israel. Grief and glory are tangled together as smoke and flame. It is because we have inherited the good of our line that we feel the weight of the evil. These things are wedded to us, just as our father was wedded to our mother.”

The street outside was ordinary Brompton, the clatter of omnibuses rumbling past the window, but his words might have come straight from the halls of the Babylonian Talmud. The old Rabbi said once that the Holy One spends His time making marriages— and the saying is only light to the ear of the man who hears it wrong. By marriages, he meant all the strange, wondrous, tangled combinations of the universe that weave together the stuff of all our joy, and all our sorrow, and all the messy, precious love that holds a family together no matter what.

“Moses, trotz seiner Befeindung der Kunst, dennoch selber ein großer Künstler war und den wahren Künstlergeist besaß. Nur war dieser Künstlergeist bei ihm, wie bei seinen ägyptischen Landsleuten, nur auf das Kolossale und Unverwüstliche gerichtet. Aber nicht wie die Ägypter formierte er seine Kunstwerke aus Backstein und Granit, sondern er baute Menschenpyramiden, er meisselte Menschenobelisken, er nahm einen armen Hirtenstamm und schuf daraus ein Volk, das ebenfalls den Jahrhunderten trotzen sollte . . . er schuf Israel!”—HEINE: *Geständnisse*.

Imagine the gulf between Daniel Deronda who sailed for Genoa, unmoored and adrift, and the man who stepped back onto English soil. He had left not knowing if the truths he was about to uncover would pull him far from the quiet, half-cherished paths his heart had begun to wander down—paths that led straight back to Mirah Lapidoth, ever since that rainy evening in Chelsea when her tearful farewell look had wrapped itself around his soul, settling there as soft and permanent as ivy on old stone. He returned now not just with answers, but a charter: proof of the heritage he had never known he ached for, a duty he had been unknowingly groomed for all his life, sweeter than any empty freedom ever could have been. Now he dared finally name the secret love he had hidden even from himself: that quiet, reverent devotion to Mirah that had made every other woman’s charm feel thin and hollow by comparison, even when Gwendolen’s desperate, clinging dependence on him had stirred only self-sacrificing pity, no romantic fire.

He had fought this feeling for months, telling himself it was wrong to woo her, when the circumstances that had brought them together made any advance feel like an unfair imposition—until his own mother had snapped at him, mid-confrontation, that he was in love with a Jewess, and he had realized that to lie about it, to evade the truth, would be an unforgivable betrayal. That moment of decisive admission had unlocked a flood of energy he had never known he held: no more self-suppression, no more second-guessing every impulse. The closer he drew to England, the more every remaining mile felt like an unbearable delay. It was as if finding his ancestry had gifted him a second soul: no longer was he adrift in the vague, impartial sympathy of a man with no roots, no people, no purpose. Now he had a path, a community, a mission he could throw his whole heart into—and he ached to share it first with Ezra, Mirah’s brother, the man who had lit that flame of purpose in him long before he knew the truth of his own birth. He had no real fear Hans Meyrick would win Mirah’s heart, but he knew how shocking it would be for a woman who had only ever seen him as a steady, reliable protector to suddenly find he wanted to be her lover. He meant to build that transition gently, step by soft step.

No wonder he went straight from the London railway terminus to their tiny Brompton square lodgings, without stopping first to drop his bags, even though the sun had already set, and he had promised Lady Mallinger he would visit her at the Abbey the very next day. He was desperate to deposit his precious family chest with Ezra, to let his old friend pore over its ancient manuscripts at his side, and nothing could have felt more right than to walk through their door unannounced, his chest thrumming with a joy he could barely contain. He hardly even noticed his own ordinary summer drab suit, though the faint flush in his cheeks, the bright, unguarded glow in his eyes, would have made even the plainest clothing look like the regalia of the long-awaited heir Ezra had spent years waiting for. He slipped into the house as quietly as a shadow.

It was the very same evening Mirah had returned shattered from her brutal, devastating meeting with her father. Ezra, shaken to his core by her sobs and the cruel memories the encounter had dragged to the surface, had stopped sorting through his papers in the first moments of his distress; pages lay scattered across the floor, neither of them had bothered to tidy them. They had sat perfectly still for what felt like hours, hand in hand, listening to the mantel clock tick while the light faded to dusk. Mirah had thrown off her travel cloak and sunk down beside him, too sick at heart to think of eating, watching his face fall slack, his breathing grow shallow, looking so pale and still she was certain he would slip away from her at any moment. Her grief was doubled now: she had lost her mother, she had thought she was free of her father, and now he was back to haunt her, and she feared she had broken her one remaining brother with the violence of her tears. Her oval cheeks were pale, her dark eyes glistening with the faint leftover sheen of unshed young tears, her curls tangled like a child’s just woken from a nightmare, and the future stretched out before her nothing but a repeat of all the old miseries she had thought she was finally escaping.

Then the front door creaked open, and a familiar, soft voice called out: “Daniel Deronda—may he come in?”

“Come! Come at once!” Ezra cried, leaping to his feet, his face lighting up as if he had seen Deronda that very morning and expected this visit all day long, no trace of surprise in his shining eyes. Mirah started up, her face flooding with hot color, her heart thrumming with a confused, half-alarmed, half-delighted flutter.

But when Deronda stepped over the threshold, the sight of him felt like bright, clear sunlight breaking through a storm. He reached out to take Mirah’s hand in his right, rested his left light and warm on Ezra’s shoulder, and stood that way for a long quiet moment, holding both of them close, reading the traces of grief on their faces, before he leaned forward, voice soft with anxiety. “Has anything happened? Any trouble?”

“Speak no words of trouble now,” Ezra cut in, saving Mirah the need to find an answer. “There is joy written all over your face—let that joy be ours first.”

Mirah thought to herself that he must carry some secret too big to share, too wonderful to put into ordinary words. They all sat down, Deronda pulling his chair right up close in front of Ezra.

“You are right,” he said, his voice thick with feeling. “I carry a joy that will hold for us even through the worst of trouble. I did not tell you why I traveled abroad, Ezra, because there was no use in speaking of possibilities until I knew the truth. I went to learn my parentage. And you were right, all along. I am a Jew.”

The two men clasped hands, the jolt of the moment so vivid it shot through Mirah like an electric shock. Deronda did not pause, speaking as much from Ezra’s heart as his own. “We are of the same people. Our souls hold the same calling. Nothing on earth, not life nor death, will ever separate us now.”

Ezra answered in a quiet, reverent whisper, the ancient Hebrew liturgical words that had bound generations of their people to their faith: “Our God and the God of our fathers.”

The weight of the moment was too heavy for the usual flow of Ezra’s passionate, quick words, and he fell silent for a long moment, breath catching.

Mirah sank to her knees by her brother’s side, staring up at his suddenly luminous face, which had looked so deathly pale not ten minutes before. The motion was entirely instinctive, the only possible outlet for the sudden tidal wave of gladness that washed all her despair away, as solemn and sacred as if she was witnessing a holy rite. For that moment, she thought only of what this news meant for her brother’s long, suffering life, for the dream he had carried for decades that was finally coming true.

“It is not only that I am a Jew,” Deronda went on, smiling that rare, bright smile that broke through his usual quiet gravity like sun over a lake. “I come from a long line of Sephardic Jews, the Charisi family, who spent generations devoting themselves to our people’s scholarship and good works. My grandfather, Daniel Charisi, guarded a chest full of ancient manuscripts, family records stretching back centuries, hoarding them for the grandson he hoped would come one day to carry on his work. His wish has come true, after all the years of people trying to hide my heritage from me. That chest is downstairs right now, in the house with us. I want to leave it here with you, Ezra, so we can pore over every page together. I can read the Spanish and Italian manuscripts easily enough, and we will work through the Hebrew and the Arabic ones side by side, just as you always dreamed.”

When his bright, happy glance drifted from Ezra to Mirah, it felt like too much warm sunshine on her skin, and she shifted, flushing bright, rising from her knees to sit back in her usual chair, hands folded tight in her lap, trying to still her pounding heart. Deronda, sensitive as ever, thought he had let too much of his hidden love show in his gaze, that he had startled her, that he had revealed a feeling she did not return. He was terrified that any clumsy advance would ruin the perfect peace of this moment, turn their easy, gentle bond into something awkward, something that would make Mirah wretched in all the inevitable days they would spend together, caring for Ezra. If she did not love him back, he would never force that weight on her.

While the two of them sat wrapped in their own quiet, flustered thoughts, Ezra was already speaking, his old eloquence fully restored, bright with exultation. “Daniel, I told you from the very first that we do not know all the paths our lives walk. Look how they wove together, as if one single soul was drawing all the scattered pieces to itself, feeding on every small kind act, every quiet choice, to bring us exactly here. The misguided, unloving choices of other people tried to keep you from your birthright, but your own good, loving will overcame them—you cared for my sister, you sought me out when I was lost, and that was the path that made your soul ready to receive this news, to hear the call of all your waiting brethren.”

“It is true you and Mirah were my teachers,” Deronda said gently. “If I had learned this truth before I met you two, I think I would have rebelled against it. I would have thought, given the choice, I would never have chosen to be a Jew. But now? My whole being consents to it, completely. It was the slow, steady accord between our hearts, the trust we built little by little, that brought me to that full, willing acceptance.”

He thought vividly of that first afternoon in the old bookshop, when he had stood back, distant and doubtful of Ezra’s wild, prophetic confidence, and he leaned forward, voice warm with new fervor. “It was your inspiration that showed me what my life’s work could be. All my life, I have carried a vague, inherited longing for a purpose, a mission that would make me feel like the heart and brain of a community—no prize I had to fight for for myself, but a duty that found me. You have shown me that work: to bind our people back together, beyond all the divides of doctrine, to remind them they were one nation long before they split into quarreling sects. I am going to do that work, in your spirit. Failure will not be shameful, but it would be a thousand times more shameful if I did not even try.”

“Even as my own brother that fed at my mother’s breast,” Ezra murmured, sinking back in his chair, his face soft with the repose of a man who has seen his life’s greatest hope come to pass.

For Mirah, the moment felt like dawn breaking over a dark land she had wandered in for years, revealing paths she had never dared imagine could exist. But after a moment of quiet, Ezra started to ask about Deronda’s parents, then caught himself, and said he would not press for any secrets Deronda did not choose to share.

“Gradually, in time, you will know all of it,” Deronda said. “But now tell me of your days, while I was gone. I can see there has been trouble. Mirah has been so distressed about something.”

Mirah turned quickly to Ezra, begging him with her eyes not to speak of her father, not to taint this perfect, holy new joy with the old disgrace that clung to her family, that would now cling to Deronda too, by his own choice. She rose to get her hat and cloak, to retreat to her room and leave the two of them to speak freely, but Ezra spoke first.

“Today we faced a great grief. A duty we thought long buried returned to find us, bringing dread instead of gladness. But for now, we are free of its yoke. Let us speak no more of it tonight. This evening is the start of our festival—we offer our first fruits of joy here, and we will mix no mourning with them.”

Deronda respected the quiet boundary, rising to his feet as he saw Mirah stand. “I must leave very soon, to help Mrs. Adam bring the chest up from the carriage, and give you the key, Ezra. May I call you Ezra now? I have heard Mirah call you that for so long, it feels like the right name for the man who is my brother.”

“Please call him Ezra,” Mirah said, her voice faint and soft, her skin flushing under his gaze. Was he truly different, or was all the new warmth she felt just in her own heart? She was so worn out, faint from no food and hours of tears, that Deronda saw her hands tremble, and his whole ached to reach for her, to hold her steady, but he dared not. She held out her hand to him with a small, wobbly smile, and he opened the door for her to step out. That was all.

A man of refined pride might shrink from confessing love to a woman of far higher rank or greater wealth, for fear his motives would be questioned—but Deronda’s difficulty was far softer, far more tender. He knew Mirah felt she owed him a debt of gratitude larger than she could ever repay, and he feared that if he told her he loved her, she would think his affection was a demand that she pay him back by becoming his, that she would say yes out of obligation, not desire. The thought of forcing that weight on her, of making their quiet, loving care of Ezra feel like a burden, was unbearable to him. He would never speak of his love until he was certain, beyond any doubt, that her heart had already chosen him first.

Even a man who has grown glib and cynical from years of casual courtship can fall prey to the lover’s awe eventually, trembling and stammering in the face of a love that shakes every part of him—so how could Deronda, whose whole life had kept his heart tender, his capacity for passion fresh as new leaves by a mountain spring, not shake a little at the very thought of telling Mirah Lapidoth what she meant to him?

— DANTE, *Il Purgatorio*

Barely a handful of days had passed since her mother arrived in Genoa, before Gwendolen finally consented to let them make arrangements to leave. The glittering Mediterranean, once the stuff of her gilded daydreams, had curdled into a circle of unspoken punishment under her gaze: no flowery Enna valley could have felt more like a prison, its “blooms” no more than flickering flames that burned the soles of her feet with every step she took along the waterfront.

“I never want to look at the Mediterranean again,” she told her mother one quiet afternoon, and Mrs. Davilow thought she understood the ache in her daughter’s bones, even when Gwendolen made it clear no one was ever to speak aloud of her late husband, or the hour he slipped beneath the waves. For all the outward signs of crushing calamity that hung over them, Mrs. Davilow was happier than she had been in years, ever since Gwendolen’s ill-fated marriage. Her darling girl had come back to her, not just with the same old affection, but with a tender, clinging cherishing of her that one gives only to a treasure they nearly lost forever.

“Are you there, mamma?” Gwendolen called out in the dead of night, long after the candles had been snuffed, sounding exactly like the scared, wide-eyed girl she had been at 17, who would cry out if she woke alone in the dark. Mrs. Davilow had made up a bed for herself in the same room, just to be near her.
“I’m here, dear. Can I get you anything?”
“No, thank you. I just needed to know you were real. Did I wake you?” It was a question Gwendolen would never have bothered to ask in the old days, when her own wants were the only ones that mattered.
“I wasn’t asleep, darling.”
“It felt like you weren’t really here, even though I can see you. I can bear anything, if you’re with me. But you mustn’t lie awake worrying over me. You’re going to be happy now, and I’m going to make you happy — I swear it. If I can’t do that, what’s left for me?”
“God bless you, my love. There’s no greater happiness for me than being close to you.”

The next night, when Mrs. Davilow heard her sighing restlessly for hours, she offered her the sleeping draught the doctor had left, but Gwendolen turned it down sharp. “Don’t tell me what’s good for me, mamma. You don’t know. Neither you nor uncle must ever try to talk me into doing something that feels all wrong, not after what I’ve been through.”

Mrs. Davilow held her tongue, soft with pity for her poor irritable child, till Gwendolen blurted out, voice cracking: “I was always wicked to you, wasn’t I? That’s why I’m so miserable now.” She burst into great heaving sobs, all the locked-up grief and secret guilt of her marriage spilling out in tears, none of the unutterable facts ever finding voice between them.

Slowly, pieces of the truth had trickled through to Mrs. Davilow, via the letters Sir Hugo had sent to Mr. Gascoigne the rector, who then passed on the gentlest parts of the news to her. The good-natured baronet, overseeing all the formalities of his nephew’s death and the grim wait for the body to wash ashore, had thought it kindest to break the news of Grandcourt’s will to the rector gradually, so he wouldn’t be blindsided by the full shock once he returned home. The truth, when it finally came out, was brutal: all Grandcourt’s vast fortune, all his estates, went entirely to his illegitimate children, the product of a long-standing prior entanglement with a woman named Mrs. Glasher. There was no legitimate heir, and Grandcourt had chosen to leave the vast bulk of his wealth to the children he’d never publicly acknowledged, rather than the wife he’d married barely a year prior.

The rector was deeply stung. He remembered, now, all the times Grandcourt had treated him with icy, arrogant disdain, all the old rumors of his wild dissipations before he’d moved into Diplow Hall — but he had never dreamed those old follies would rear up to rob his niece of every bit of the grand future he’d once imagined for her. He held his tongue, though, behaving like the worldly, conscientious clergyman he was. “A young man making his will in good health always expects to live a long life,” he said, at last. “He never imagined it would play out this way.”

Sir Hugo nodded, agreeing. “What unforgivably grates on me, though, is the way he shortchanged your niece. Our niece. All she gets is two thousand a year, and Gadsmere — that little house plopped right down in the middle of a coal-mining district. It’s shameful, to leave a beautiful young widow with no better a lot than some country doctor’s widow. A man ought to take pride in making sure his widow is comfortable, no matter what. I’d never do that to my wife.” He leaned forward, earnest. “You must urge her to count me as a friend, through all of this. I owe her that much. And mark my words, if you ever arrange another marriage for a niece, even if it’s to the Archbishop of Canterbury himself, you bind that man down with an ironclad settlement before the wedding. Don’t trust anyone’s ‘apparent liberality’ ever again.”

To the rector’s gentle mind, two thousand a year hardly seemed like a paltry sum, but the humiliation of having his niece’s marriage to a man of Grandcourt’s standing revealed to all their acquaintance as a complete farce, with her set aside for another woman’s children, stung worse than any financial loss. He had no idea that Gwendolen already knew every last detail of Mrs. Glasher and her children, though; he assumed, as most men of his time did, that a well-bred wife could never have been privy to such sordid secrets. But Mrs. Davilow was not so naive: all the odd, sharp moments in Gwendolen’s behavior before and after her wedding suddenly made perfect sense, and she resolved to break the rest of the news of the will gently, during their long journey back to England.

She never got the chance.
“I hope you’re not expecting me to be some grand rich widow, mamma,” Gwendolen said one morning, as she sat by the window fully dressed, deep in quiet thought. “I may choose to take nothing at all from his estate.”

Mrs. Davilow stared, stunned. “Oh no, dear, Sir Hugo has already confirmed you’re entitled to that two thousand a year, and Gadsmere house —”
“What I take depends entirely on what I choose to accept,” Gwendolen cut her off, sharp and firm. “You and uncle are not to interfere with me on any of this, not a bit. I will do everything in my power to make you happy, but every choice that touches on my husband’s legacy is mine to make. Would eight hundred a year be enough for you to live on, mamma?”

“More than enough, my love — don’t even think of giving me that much.” Mrs. Davilow paused, soft. “Do you know who inherited all the estates and the rest of the money?”
“Yes,” Gwendolen waved the subject away, like she was swatting at a gnat. “I know everything. It’s all perfectly as it should be. I never want it mentioned again, not ever.”

Mrs. Davilow turned her face away, her cheeks flushing hot, rising to fetch a fan-screen to hide her tears. What unimaginable misery her child had been hiding, all this time, that could never be spoken of between them. But Gwendolen saw her grief, her face softening with that new, gentle intuition the last months had given her. She patted the space on the couch beside her. “Come sit closer, mamma. Don’t be sad. I’m going to be very wise now, I promise. And so good to you, you won’t even recognize me. Just don’t cry, please.”

Beneath that quiet resolve, Gwendolen’s mind was set: the very first thing she would do, once they reached London, was find Daniel Deronda. She would ask him, the only man whose judgment she trusted entirely, whether she ought to accept a single penny of Grandcourt’s money — even if it was to make sure her mother was cared for. She felt, for the first time, strong enough to do anything that would make her worthy of his good opinion, that would lift her a little higher in the light of his approval.

Sir Hugo’s invitation for them all to stay at his Park Lane house in London, while they sorted through all the mourning arrangements and collected Gwendolen’s things from her old home in Grosvenor Square, could not have been more perfect. There, she would have the chance to send Deronda a note, to beg him to come see her. She did not even have his address, but she would not let that stop her.

During the long train ride across the continent back to England, Sir Hugo chattered merrily, doing his best to keep the mood light, talking of possible plans for the future: they could easily lease out Gadsmere to one of the local coal mine managers, he said, it was a perfectly pretty little place, with a lake and a tiny tower, even if it was dotted with soot from the pits. Mr. Gascoigne sighed, mentioning that their old family home Offendene was empty still, no one had taken the lease after all, and she could have lived there rent-free for a year if she’d taken Lord Brackenshaw’s offer.

At the mention of Offendene, Gwendolen’s face softened. She could see it all clearly, in her mind’s eye: the gray broad shoulders of the downs, the speckled cows dotted across the fields, the shadowy plantations and rutted country lanes, the avenue of limes outside the front door, her mother waiting on the step to greet her. Once, she had thought that quiet country life was the dullest prison imaginable, a thing to run from as fast as she could. Now, it looked like a sanctuary, a place to breathe the clean morning air and hear birds sing unblamed, after she had run headlong into a glittering Satan’s masquerade, all gilded masks and hidden serpent tongues, that had ended in a shriek of fear out on the open sea. For a long moment, she lingered over that vision, saying nothing, while the three others in the carriage chattered on about telegraph cables and Church Rate abolition bills.

All that while, one unshakable purpose was the only thing that anchored her: she had to see Deronda. Without him, she had no compass, no moral north star. It was his opinion that had become the breath of her life, the only thing that kept her from lying to herself and letting her old selfishness slide back in. He was her living conscience, the voice of all that was good and invisible that she could never reach on her own.

She did not care that the gossips would whisper, that her old pride would have died before she admitted she needed anyone so badly. She was not a spoiled girl anymore. She was a woman who had almost been swallowed by the sea, and she would not let anyone’s side glances stop her from going to the one man who could save her.

So no sooner had they arrived at the Park Lane house, and she heard Sir Hugo was planning to pop down to his family estate for a day or two before he handled her legal affairs, than she spoke up, no trace of hesitation in her voice, right in front of her mother: “Sir Hugo, I need to see Mr. Deronda as soon as possible. I don’t have his address. Will you give it to me, or send word to him that I’m asking for him?”

A quick, bright thought crossed the baronet’s face, and his easy smile never slipped. “I’m not sure if he’s at his London chambers or at the Abbey right now, but I’ll track him down this very moment. I’ll send a note to his chambers straight away, and if he’s at home he’ll be here before the hour’s out. I know he will be only too happy to hear from you.”

Privately, Sir Hugo was delighted. He had long suspected that there was a quiet, deep affection blooming between his favorite ward Daniel and the beautiful Gwendolen, and he thought it was the most perfect fate possible, that the cruel, selfish Grandcourt had made his exit just in time. He was already determined to defend them both from any whispers, to smooth the way for whatever happiness they might find together. His only small worry was that his stubborn, noble-hearted Daniel might have some grand, secret scheme in his head that would make him pass up the chance to claim the woman who loved him so deeply. But he picked up his pen at once, scribbled the note, and sent a footman running with it to Deronda’s chambers — where the letter landed, right in his hands.

“O, welcome, pure-eyed Faith, white-handed Hope, Thou hovering angel, girt with golden wings!” —MILTON.

Deronda did not answer Gwendolen’s urgent new summons without a sharp, thrumming agitation in his chest. Not vanity, but his boundless, keen sympathy, made him acutely aware of the risk: that another’s heart might stake a claim on him far larger than he could ever honor. It was no longer a theory to him, but a visceral, unshakable truth: Gwendolen’s soul clung to his with a ravenous, desperate need, as if she hung over a precipice of despair and only his hand kept her from falling. We do not argue the anger or the scorn we hear thrumming in a stranger’s voice; we simply feel it, no proof needed. And Deronda could feel the weight of Gwendolen’s whole fate tangled up with his own.

Anyone who knew him would not have been surprised by his quiet, unspoken admission: if all this had unfolded just a year prior, he would never have stopped to ask himself if he loved her. The impetuous, overwhelming drive in him would have been to wrap her in safety, shelter her from every loneliness for the rest of her days, and see the rescue he began when he redeemed that fateful necklace through to its very end. But now, love and duty had woven other, unbreakable bonds around him. That old impulse no longer got to steer his life, but it lived in him still as a tender, aching yearning, a sharp pang just at the thought of meeting the desperate appeal in her eyes and her words again and again, knowing he could never give her everything she ached for. The firmer his resolve to keep his distance out of honor, the more searing his pity for the lonely path she now had to walk.

He waited for her in the back drawing room, the same white-and-crimson space where they had sat side by side at the musicale, where Gwendolen first whispered that her lot depended on him never forsaking her, her soft plea melting into the tender, sad strains of *Per pietà non dirmi addio* — “Do not tell me goodbye out of pity” — that had poured from Mirah’s dear, familiar voice. Deronda wandered the room he had known like the back of his hand for years, with the strange, disorienting feeling that his whole life had metamorphosed under his feet. Every familiar object, from Lady Mallinger’s soft-smiling portrait to the friendly, urbane lion faces carved into the mantel pilasters, felt like they belonged to a past life he was only revisiting in memory, not reality. The revelations of the last months had transformed him so deeply, shifted the foundations of his identity so completely, that the house he had once called home felt new and unmoored now, as he stood hat in hand waiting for a young woman whose own life had been ripped apart by tragedy, her future hanging fragile and uncertain, with Deronda’s choices still tangled up in what would become of her.

Then Gwendolen stepped through the door. She looked transformed, not just by the deep black of her mourning dress, but by a quiet, soft serenity he had never seen on her face during their last meeting in Genoa. That quiet satisfaction came entirely from seeing Deronda there, but no smile passed between them as they met and clasped hands. Both of them were brimming with shared memories, heavy with anxious premonition.

“It was good of you to come. Let us sit down,” she said, sinking at once into the nearest chair. He took the seat across from her.

“I asked you here because I need you to tell me what I ought to do,” she began immediately. “Don’t soften it for me just because it feels hard. I’ve made up my mind I will do it. Once I was terrified of being poor, I could not bear the thought of being dependent on other people’s mercy — that was why I made that terrible mistake, why I married. But I’ve borne far worse things now. I think I could bear poverty, if you tell me I ought to.” She paused, her fingers twisting in her lap. “Do you know the terms of my husband’s will?”

“Yes, Sir Hugo told me,” Deronda said, already guessing the question weighing on her.

“Ought I to take anything he left me?” she asked, her tone sharpening into nervous eagerness. “Let me tell you what I’ve been turning over in my head. You may not fully realize that even when I was being selfish, even when I agreed to that marriage, I was thinking of my mother. I did love her, and the thought of her living in poverty was the thing that drove me most. The only small comfort I had at first, when every day of my marriage was misery, was knowing she was better off because of what I’d done. The hardest thing for me now would be to see her poor again. I thought if I only took enough from the will to keep her comfortable, and nothing at all for myself — that it wouldn’t be wrong. He took me away from her, and he knew I cared for her, and he would have wanted to provide for her, wouldn’t he? If she knew —”

Gwendolen broke off, her voice trembling. She had rehearsed this conversation over and over, fixating only on the question of doing right by her mother, but the terrible, unspeakable memories tangled beneath those thoughts swarmed between her words, making her speech shake more and more. She stared helplessly down at her hands, which now bore no rings at all save for her plain, heavy wedding band.

“Don’t hurt yourself trying to say the rest,” Deronda said, his voice soft with tenderness. “There’s no need. The case is very simple. I can hardly be wrong about this. You consult me because I am the only person in the world who knows the most painful part of your story, so I understand why your conscience is pricking you.” He fell quiet for a moment, letting her catch her breath, and Gwendolen felt that silence wrap around her like the gentle sympathy in his voice. She found the courage to lift her eyes to his as he went on. “You carry a weight of guilt, you feel as if you have no right to claim anything as his wife, you shrink from taking profit from his death, you even want to punish that old, selfish part of yourself that gave in to temptation. I have known that exact kind of remorse myself. Do I understand you correctly?”

“Yes. I just want to be good — not the person I used to be,” Gwendolen whispered. “I’ll try to bear whatever you say I ought to bear. I told you the worst of me. What do I do now?”

“If only your own peace were at stake here, I would never dare argue against any prompting of your remorse,” Deronda said. “But I take your love for Mrs. Davilow as my guide, and that feeling of yours is perfectly just. I do not believe your actions, no matter how wrong you feel they were, can cancel out the duty your husband owed you as his wife. He chose to enter your life, to twist the course of it in the most irreversible way possible. But even setting that aside, it was his obligation as a wealthy man to provide for your mother, and he knew full well that if his will took effect, she would share in that provision alongside you.”

“She’s been living on eight hundred a year,” Gwendolen said. “I was thinking I could take exactly that much for her, and leave every penny of the rest.” She had argued herself into that line as a small, hard-won permission for weeks, and could not adjust to a different point of view at once.

“I do not think it is your duty to draw that line,” Deronda said gently. “You would create a painful, unanswerable riddle for your mother. An income you refuse to share in would taste bitter to her, she would never be able to enjoy it, wondering why you would not touch it. And your own path of penitence would become far too heavy. We agreed in Genoa that no other person ever needs to know the secret weight you carry. The kind, good life you want to build for yourself will be far easier to foster if you keep everyone you love safe from the pain of that knowledge. In my opinion, you ought simply to accept the full terms of the will, and let your remorse guide you in how you use that new independence to do good.”

As he finished the last sentence, Deronda automatically reached for his hat, which he had set on the floor beside his chair. Gwendolen, sharp to every small shift in his movement, felt her heart lurch hard in her chest, as if it had a will of its own, trying to stop him from leaving. Before she could think, she was on her feet, and Deronda, startled, stood too, stepping a little toward her.

“I will do exactly what you tell me,” she said, breathless. “But what else? What else am I supposed to do?”
