Those were the only simple words she could find, but they tore through her like a mirror held up to her own utter helplessness. Her old, proud secrecy had crumbled to ash, and she could not hold back the sob that sent huge, hot tears spilling down her cheeks. Deronda felt a crushing weight of pain in his own chest, but he knew the stakes of this moment too clearly, and forced himself to speak steadily, gently.

“You will be leaving for the country soon with Mrs. Davilow, I imagine.”

“Yes, in a week or ten days.” Gwendolen’s eyes drifted vaguely to the window, as if she was staring out at some imaginary, unwritten landscape. “I want to be kind to all of them. They can be happier than I will ever be. Is that the best I can do?”

“I think so. That duty is one there is no doubt about,” Deronda said, every word heavy with care. “Other good things will grow from it. Looking at your life as a debt you have to pay back might sound dreary from far away, but it cannot stay dreary. The only thing that makes life empty is having no purpose. Once you start acting with that tender, penitent goal you have set for yourself, you will find small, unexpected joys, new little needs to tend to that will carry you forward day by day. You will see your life grow, soft and steady, like a plant pushing up after a hard frost.”

Gwendolen’s eyes flew back to his face, as if she was a parched traveler drinking in the sound of unseen running water. Deronda felt that look like she was reaching out for him from the edge of a forsaken, drowning shore. His voice softened into an imploring, affectionate lilt.

“This grief that has cut you right down to the roots came to you while you are still so young. Try not to see it as something that spoiled your life, but as something that is preparing you for it. Think of it this way: you were heading down a dark, selfish road, and a severe, loving angel grabbed you by the wrist to show you the horror of the life you almost fell into. And it happened to you in the springtime of your life. You can be one of those good, gentle women who make everyone around them glad they were ever born.”

The words felt to Gwendolen like the touch of a miraculous hand. A flood of mingled hope and new strength coursed through her, the first stirrings of a new life that felt entirely tied to Deronda, as if he was the very breath her new soul was breathing. For the first time since that terrible day out on the stormy sea, a warm flush rose up over her cheeks, her brow, her neck, deepening for a heartbeat before fading slowly away. She said nothing.

Deronda stepped forward, holding out his hand. “I must not tire you out.”

She started, shocked at the thought he was leaving, and put her hand in his, still silent.

“You still look unwell — nothing like the girl I once knew,” he said, holding her hand a moment longer.

“I can hardly sleep,” she said, her tone dulling back into hopelessness. “All the old memories keep replaying in my head. They come back. They will keep coming back,” she shuddered.

“Little by little, they will stop pressing at you so hard,” Deronda said. He could not drop her hand, could not pull away abruptly.

“Sir Hugo says he is coming to stay at Diplow soon,” Gwendolen blurted, grabbing for the words she had practiced saying earlier, which had slipped her mind. “You will come with him, right?”

“Probably,” Deronda said, and when the word felt too cold, he corrected it at once. “Yes. I will come.” He released her hand, with that final, gentle, friendly pressure of someone who has already said goodbye.

“And you will see me once more before I leave town?” Gwendolen asked, her voice soft with timid sadness, her face as pale as ever.

What could Deronda say? “If I can be of any use to you — if you wish it — of course I will.”

“I must wish it,” Gwendolen burst out, impulsive. “You know I must wish it. What other strength do I have? Who else is there?” Another sob rose in her throat.

A sharp, stabbing pain crossed Deronda’s face. He looked utterly miserable as he said, “I will certainly come.”

Gwendolen saw the pain in his face, but the overwhelming relief of knowing he would return drowned everything else out, and that new, brave hope he had planted in her surged back bright.

“Don’t be sad for me,” she said, her tone soft and sure, full of gentle care for him for the first time. “I will remember every single word you said. I will remember how good you think I can be. I will try.” She looked at him steadily, and held out her hand again, as if she had forgotten they had already said goodbye once that afternoon. No smile had touched her lips since her husband died. Standing there quiet and still, she looked like a melancholy statue of the bright, laughing Gwendolen whose glee had once been so sharp and vivid when everyone around her was grave. It was no wonder she clung to him so openly, that she laid her desperate dependence on him bare — she was trapped in a fire of her own making, and he was the only one who could carry her to safety. She had tied him irrevocably to the new, good life she was trying to build.

No one could blame her for assuming her need was mirrored in his heart. She was too wrapped up in the terror of her own suffering to imagine a future without him at her side. Marriage, love, the kind of courtship she had once daydreamed of, could not even cross her mind now. The great, heavy Love that had laid its hand on her had asked only for acceptance of her fault, the hard work of changing herself, confession, and endurance. Her cries to him were the cries of a small child who has tripped and fallen backward in the dark, screaming to be taken by the hand so she does not lose her way entirely.

The cry pierced Deronda to the bone. There could be no more impossible position for a man full of tenderness, but clear-eyed enough to see the future: he was the only person alive who knew the full truth of her suffering, and to pull away from her now would leave her stranded in a dangerous, empty loneliness. He could not bear the cruelty of making her feel like he was rejecting her when she needed him most. And yet, he could see the inevitable wrench coming down the line, where every new bond they wove in these soft meetings would only make that final parting hurt worse. He chose to risk that hurt.

He visited her at her Park Lane home twice more before she left London, and their meetings were always chaperoned by Mrs. Davilow, making them far less fraught and painful. Now that she had resolved to accept the full inheritance, Gwendolen had a gentle new project she loved talking about: she would move herself, her mother, and her sisters back into Offendene, the old house where they had once been so happy, and stitch her life back as close to that innocent, joyful time as she could. She had mentioned the plan to Sir Hugo, who was already working to arrange a rental for Gadsmere that would more than cover the cost of Offendene’s lease. Deronda was glad to dwell on the plan, glad to see it give Gwendolen something soft and hopeful to pour her energy into. Neither of them spoke of the life Deronda was building for himself. Gwendolen’s whole world had narrowed down to waiting for him to come visit her at Diplow before autumn ended, and she thought no more of the little Jewish singer Mirah and her brother than she thought of the quiet social and political shifts that were beginning to reshape the world outside her small, carefully tended sphere. All of her mind was lit only by the path of atonement Deronda had showed her, and the bright, quiet hope of seeing him again soon.

“One day still fierce ’mid many a day struck calm.” —BROWNING: *The King and the Book*

Gwendolen Harleth had never once spared a thought for Ezra and Mirah Lapidoth when she daydreamed of Daniel Deronda, but in that quiet, lull between storms, the threads tying all three of their fates together were weaving tighter, glowing brighter, drawn closer by the sharp, unshakable bond of shared care.

The man who was their father, Lapidoth, had vanished from Mirah’s doorstep the second her purse was in his hand, ruled entirely by that ravenous, unthinking urge to stake every coin he laid claim to, no matter how small, on a game of chance. As long as he could afford to sate his hunger for the turn of a card or the roll of the dice, he never spared a single thought for Mirah, or for the uncomfortable, stern encounter with his son that he had instinctively shrunk back from the moment they parted. The itch of gambling is fiercer than bodily hunger, after all: a meal can be forgotten if a thought or a feeling burns bright enough, but the fevered, breathless hush of waiting for luck to swing your way eats every other kind of pleasure or discomfort alive. At its most unforgiving stage, it is the hollow, joyless pastime of demons, chasing cheap thrills across the hot burning marl of their own impending ruin.

Every vice, no matter how abstract, still needs a crust of bread to keep its host breathing, though. Lapidoth cared little enough for fancy food or drink, but he had long since slipped into a shabby, hardscrabble life where a bite to eat required ready coin. He lost all thirty of Mirah’s shillings in short order at a parlour that advertised “Pyramids” behind its frosted blind, doubling and trebling his stake before the wheel turned against him one final time, and he stepped back onto the street with her empty purse in his pocket already weighing two options: pawn the purse for a few pennies to fund another bet, or march back to Mirah, play the wronged, repentant man, and tell her he’d spent every last penny paying off an overdue debt he could no longer put off. He still clung to that old, unshakable belief that his children owed him everything they owned, that his son’s anger could never override his natural right to leech off whatever good fortune they had stumbled into. The longer he thought about facing Ezra, the less he flinched at the idea: he was far more fixated on the easy, risk-free money he could charm out of them than any possible sting of public humiliation. His luck had been bad for weeks, it had to turn soon—wouldn’t that turn start with Mirah, and the wealthy, generous friends she’d mentioned making? Lapidoth still believed his clever, quick-witted charm was an unbreakable spell, a trick that had gotten him out of trouble since he was a bright, curly-haired young man, and he set off for their quiet Brompton square determined to wait around the corner until he spotted Mirah leaving or returning, so he could slip into the house without awkwardness.

Dusk was falling fast, the evening after he’d first run into his daughter on the street, and when weariness overtook him after an hour of waiting, he decided he would simply ring the bell, and if Mirah was out, he could ask when she would be home. But before he even lifted his hand to the knocker, he knew she was inside: he could hear her voice, rising soft and bright through the closed front door, pouring out the notes of *Herz, mein Herz* at the piano, while Ezra sat nearby with his eyes closed, drinking in every note.

Mrs. Adam opened the door a few minutes later, her face flustered with embarrassment. “A gentleman downstairs says he’s your father, miss.”

Mirah jumped to her feet at once, her gaze flying straight to her brother.
“No, Mirah, don’t go down to him,” Ezra said, steady and unyielding, before turning to Mrs. Adam. “Show him up here.”

Mirah stood twisting her fingers tight in the fabric of her sleeves, her stomach twisting with sick anxiety, never taking her eyes off Ezra. He had risen too, his frame visibly shaking, but there was a look on his face she had never seen before: his brows were knitted hard, his lips set into a sharp, unforgiving line, the same unyielding fire glinting in his gaze.

Mrs. Adam stepped back to let Lapidoth in, and could not help glancing between the two men before she closed the door behind her, thinking to herself, well, the family likeness is plain as day. Their bone structure, the outline of their jaws, was identical at first glance—but a lifetime of wildly different choices had carved their expressions into total, unrecognizable opposites, like the stern, unyielding face of a revolutionary Cromwell sprung from the exact outward mould of a mild, ordinary parish clerk.

Lapidoth had schooled his face to a soft, melancholy expression before he knocked, but he still flinched a little as he stepped forward. “Ezra, my boy—after all these years, I doubt you even know me.”

“I know you,” Ezra said, slow and solemn, every word sharp enough to cut. “Too well, father.” That last word fell not as a title, but as a rebuke.

“Ah, you’re displeased with me. I don’t blame you at all, appearances have been stacked against me,” Lapidoth rattled off, his old glib charm snapping back into place like it had never faded. “When a man gets trapped in straits he can’t climb out of, he can’t do right by himself or anyone else. I’ve suffered more than you could ever imagine, I swear it.” He turned to Mirah, holding out her empty purse. “Here’s your little purse, my dear. I knew you’d be worried about that scrap of writing tucked inside. I emptied it, I know, but every penny went to paying off a long-overdue bill for food and lodging. I knew you’d want me to clear that debt, and here I stand, not a single farthing to my name, at the mercy of my own children. You can turn me out without a fuss, no police needed. Just say the word, Mirah: say ‘Father, I’ve had enough of you—you spoiled me, spent every penny you had on me when I was small and couldn’t survive without you, but I can do better on my own now,’ and I’ll vanish like a spark in the wind. I’ll never spoil your happiness again.” His voice cracked, thick with simulated tears, just like it always used to when Mirah was a little girl.

“I could never say that, father,” Mirah answered, even as every part of her ached, even as she saw right through every one of his lies, and knew all he wanted was to stay under their roof.

“Mirah, my sister—leave us,” Ezra said, in that quiet tone of authority that brooked no argument. She looked at him, faltering, pleading, awed by the unshakable resolve on his face, yet unwilling to abandon the man who had once been all the family she had known. She stepped close to him, lacing her fingers through his, her voice low but not quiet enough for Lapidoth to miss: “Remember Ezra—you said our mother would never have shut him out.”

“Trust me. Go,” Ezra said.
Mirah slipped out of the room, but sank down onto the top step a few feet away, her heart hammering so hard she could hear it in her ears. What if Ezra drove him away? The thought made her breath catch.

Lapidoth thought he had the situation handled. This gaunt, grave son of his, speaking like he had stepped straight out of a sepulchre, would no doubt launch into a tedious moral lecture, and Lapidoth would pull out his well-worn act of sorrow and repentance, and it would all blow over. He had built up a thick, callous skin for just such moments over decades of selfish living, the kind of man who would duck into an open cathedral to escape a hailstorm and let the hymns wash over him without ever bothering to listen to the words. This was not a trait he had been born with—it was a habit he had earned, over years of choosing ease over decency.

“This home we share here,” Ezra began, his voice tight with the weight of decades of unspoken grief, “is sustained half by the generosity of our dearest friend Daniel Deronda, who supports me, and half by your own daughter’s hard work, that earns her the bread she eats. As long as we have a roof over our heads, we will not shut you out. We will not cast you out to be torn apart by your own vices. You are our father. You broke every bond you ever swore to keep, but we will not break ours. But I will never trust you. You ran off with stolen money, left your debts unpaid. You abandoned my mother. You stole her little child away from her, and broke her heart piece by piece. You became a gambler, and where shame and conscience once lived, there is nothing now but that ravenous, insatiable hunger. You were ready to sell my sister—you *had* sold her, only the buyer was denied you. No man who has done these things will ever earn our trust again. We will share our food with you. You will have a warm bed, clean clothing. We will do this duty to you, because you are our father. You are an evil man, who destroyed our mother’s happiness. That you are our father is a brand burned into our flesh, that will ache until we die. But the Eternal has laid this cross upon us. Even if human justice dragged you through the streets and flogged you for your crimes, even if your body fell helpless before the scorn of every stranger who passed by, we would still step forward and say ‘This is our father. Make way. Let us carry him out of your sight.’”

Lapidoth had braced himself for a lecture, but he had never expected this: raw, unflinching words searing straight out of his son’s very soul, a force so new to him it felt like lightning had struck straight through his chest. He collapsed into the nearest chair, sobbing like a woman, burying his face in his arms against the table. Half that hysterical weeping was genuine shock at the ferocity of Ezra’s grief, half that old physical trick he had mastered as a young man, the one he had always used to melt away anyone’s anger or disapproval before it could turn against him.

Ezra sank back into his own chair, silent, completely drained. For years he had carried all that rage, all that sorrow, locked up alone in silence, and the effort of pouring it all out left his thin hands trembling on the chair arms. He felt so worn out he could barely have found the breath to answer a simple question, like he had taken one step straight toward calling Death to his side.

Mirah’s sharp, waiting ear caught the sound of her father’s ragged sobs, and she could not stay outside the door another second. She burst into the room, her first thought for Ezra, rushing straight to his side to take his trembling hand in hers. He squeezed back, drawing strength from her touch, but did not speak, did not even lift his head to look at her. Lapidoth, his face still streaked with tears, felt her presence at once, and lifted his head to press his handkerchief to his eyes, reaching a shaky hand out to her. “Goodbye, Mirah. Your old father will never trouble you again. I deserve to die like a stray dog by the side of the road, and that’s what I will do. If your mother had lived, she would have forgiven me. Thirty-four years ago, I put the ring on her finger under the Chuppa, and we were made one flesh. She would have forgiven me, we would have grown old together. But I don’t deserve that. Goodbye.” He stood up, as if he was about to walk straight out the door forever.

Mirah caught his hand before he could move a single step further, her face white with fear. “No, father, no!” She turned to Ezra, her voice breaking. “Ezra, you have not forbidden him to stay? Stay, father, please. Leave all the wrong things behind. Ezra, I cannot bear this. How could I ever say to my own father, ‘Go and die alone’?”

“I did not say it,” Ezra forced out, with every last scrap of his remaining strength. “I said he may stay. He will be sheltered here.”

“Then you will stay, father, and be taken care of,” Mirah said, tugging him gently toward the door. “Come with me.” That was exactly what Lapidoth had wanted all along. For a moment, wrapped in his daughter’s soft, dutiful care, he even felt a flicker of genuine comfort, like changing his old, ruinous habits might actually be possible. She led him down to her cosy parlour on the ground floor, and showed him the small bedroom tucked off the back. “This is all yours. Think that you have come back to my mother, and she has forgiven you—she speaks to you through me, right now.” Her voice was soft and imploring, but she could not bring herself to give him the kind of warm, careless hug she had showered on him when she was a small, trusting child.

Lapidoth recovered his old easy composure within minutes, chattering cheerfully to her about how much her singing voice had improved, and other light, harmless topics. When Mrs. Adam came to lay out his supper, he charmed her too, determined to prove he was far from some common vagabond, even if his clothes were shabby and worn. But late that night, lying awake in his new bed, Ezra’s stern, blazing speech faded fast from his mind. He was already wondering how much money Mirah had stashed away somewhere, replaying every old roulette game he had ever played on the Continent, turning over his old betting strategies to find the trick that would finally beat the wheel. Ezra’s shadow drifted across the edges of his gambling-fogged mind only once, a thin, insubstantial ghost, while the spinning numbers and the roll of the dice ate away at every other thought, weaving themselves into the very fabric of his dreams.

There is a spark of divine goodness in every human soul that drags our bravest, noblest choices out of us even when every selfish, reluctant part of our being fights to hold back.

It was an unpleasant, sharp shock for Daniel Deronda to return from his stay at the Abbey and find the last man he ever wished to see comfortably settled in the quiet Brompton lodgings Ezra and Mirah shared. Mirah had felt she could not keep Deronda’s existence from her newly returned father, and had done her best to explain how Ezra and Daniel had forged their deep, purpose-driven bond through shared faith and devotion to their people. She glossed over every part of what Daniel had done for her, entirely omitting the night he had pulled her from the cold Thames, and framing her time sheltered with the Meyrick family as the only reason she had ever crossed paths with him. The tender, fragile tie between her and Deronda was far too precious, far too intimate, to ever let her father’s rough, unworthy breath sully it. For his own unspoken, self-serving reasons, Lapidoth did not press her for details of her flight from him or how she had arrived destitute in England. But he was instantly, greedily interested to learn his two children had won the favor of a wealthy, well-connected gentleman of high standing.

Ezra was the one to break the news to Deronda, his voice soft but steady, as if he had spent weeks training himself not to flinch at the sound of his father’s name. “I am calm now, when I look at him,” he finished. “I keep hoping that Mirah’s gentle kindness, the quiet taste of a peaceful, honorable life we are giving him, will be enough to turn him away from every old temptation. I have bidden Mirah promise me, and she has sworn it, never to give him a single coin. I have shown her plainly that any money he gets for his own use will only buy him his own destruction.”

Deronda did not visit until the third day after Lapidoth’s arrival. The older man was still waiting for the new suit he had been measured for, and refused to make an impression in his ragged old clothes, so he hid behind the window curtain to watch Deronda come and go from the street below. He was surprised to see how young Deronda was, a detail Mirah had never mentioned; he had pictured the serious, scholarly friend of the grave, almost ethereal Ezra as a middle-aged, gray-bearded man. In an instant, Lapidoth’s shrewd mind jumped to the obvious conclusion: Deronda’s real, primary motive for being so generous to the pair of siblings must be that he was in love with Mirah. That was even better than if his loyalties had been tied only to Ezra: affection for Mirah promised far more willingness to indulge her father’s whims. Lapidoth had no doubt he could charm Deronda, smooth over any harsh first impressions.

He played the amiable, reformed man perfectly. He praised every note that came from Mirah’s piano, never dared smoke in the parlor that the strict landlady Mrs. Adam forbade tobacco in, and took his German pipe out to stroll the public square with the small supply of mild tobacco Mirah had bought for him. He was far too sharp to argue with Mirah when she told him she could not give him money, that she was bound by a sacred promise to her brother. He was comfortable enough to wait.

The next time Deronda called, Lapidoth was dressed head to toe in his brand new suit, preening with pride at his own reflection as he sat across from Ezra. Ezra was forcing himself, every single minute an act of iron will, to tolerate his father’s presence in the small room where he kept his most precious family papers. The very first sight of Lapidoth sent a cold, physical revulsion curling through Deronda’s chest: this was the man who had blighted his wife’s life, who had abandoned his two children to ruin, and Deronda could barely stand to breathe the same air as him. But Lapidoth did not flinch, asked politely if he might stay to hear them sort through the old documents from their locked chest, and even proved useful puzzling out the scrawled, difficult German handwriting on one tattered manuscript. He offered to transcribe every last page, pointing out that Ezra’s young eyes had started to strain from long hours of reading, while his own were still perfectly sharp.

Deronda took the offer as a small, hopeful sign that the man was finally willing to do honest, useful work, and he saw a flicker of quiet relief cross Ezra’s face before Ezra spoke. “All of that writing must happen right here, in this room,” he said firmly. “I cannot let any of these papers leave my sight, not even for an hour, for fear of accident, or fire, or them being lost.” Poor Ezra felt exactly like a warden guarding a convict on temporary leave, not his own father. Until he saw his father’s pen moving across the page with his own two eyes, he could not bring himself to trust the man to do an honest day’s work. But the arrangement chained Ezra to his father’s company, a torment made sharper by every old, painful memory it stirred, and by Lapidoth’s restless nature. The longer he settled in, the less he feared Ezra, and he would slip out constantly to smoke, wander around the room chattering nonstop about old plays he used to adapt, old adventures from their shared past, repeating jokes and stories he thought would make Mirah laugh. Every one of those minutes was a slow, quiet torture for Ezra. When she was home, Mirah would do her best to pull her father downstairs to the parlor to give her brother a few minutes of precious peace. It is not one single grand, brave choice that makes duty hard: it is the daily, unflinching weight of swallowing irritation, of ignoring small shames, of waking up every morning knowing the whole stretch of the day ahead holds more of the same, no end in sight. Mirah already feared this quiet, painful new normal might stretch on for the rest of her life.

Worst of all, Lapidoth’s leering, unspoken assumption that Deronda was courting Mirah hung between the two of them like an invisible, smudged veil. Both of them shrank back, afraid their quiet, tender feelings for each other would be soiled by his crude inferences, and each mistook the other’s new shyness and reserve for coldness, for disinterest.

But Deronda would soon get the clarity he had not dared hope for. As soon as he got back from his brief stay at the Abbey, he headed straight for Hans Meyrick’s painting rooms, knowing he owed his oldest, dearest friend the full story of what had driven his sudden trip, and the life-altering revelation he had come home with. Hans was not there; the servant said he had gone away to the country for a few days. Deronda left a long note, then waited a full week for a reply, worrying that his impulsive, soft-hearted friend had taken some quiet hurt to heart. When no note came, he called again, and stepped into the studio to find Hans in a loose light coat, no waistcoat, his long hair still dripping wet from a cold bath, his face gaunt and drawn, nothing like the ruddy, relaxed look of a man just back from the country. He had grabbed his palette and brushes and was standing posed in front of his easel like he had thrown the whole pretense together in a hurry the second he heard Deronda knock.

“You don’t look as if you’ve been out of London at all, old fellow,” Deronda said as they shook hands. “Did you go to Cambridge, then?”

“No,” Hans said shortly, tossing his palette down like he was annoyed he had even tried to keep the lie up. He pushed a chair over for Deronda, then flopped into his own, leaning back with his hands laced behind his head. “I’ve been in No Man’s Land. A very unpleasant, gray sort of place, no roads, no sun, nothing to do but mope.”

“Surely you haven’t been drinking yourself into a stupor, Hans?” Deronda said, leaning forward to study his friend’s worn face, worried.

“Nothing nearly that pleasant. I tried smoking opium. I always meant to test it someday, see what all the fuss about bliss was, and since I was fresh out of every other kind of bliss lately, I thought it was a fine time to try. But I swear to you I’ll never touch the stuff again. It disagrees with my constitution horribly.”

“What happened?” Deronda asked. “You were in such good spirits the last time you wrote to me.”

“Oh, nothing in particular. The whole world just started to look seedy, like a cabbage patch where all the cabbages had been chopped down. It’s a malady of genius, you can be sure,” Hans said, forcing a lopsided smile. “And I was just tired of being virtuous with no reward, especially in the middle of this sweltering London summer.”

“No real trouble, nothing that’s truly hurting you?” Deronda pressed.

Hans shook his head. “I came here to hear you tell me all your new secrets, but I can’t do that if you’re going to hide all your own affairs while you grill me. I’ve got no affairs worth mentioning except a petty quarrel with a bric-a-brac dealer. Besides, this is the first time in all our years of friendship you’ve ever told me anything personal about yourself. You’ve got a very long debt to pay.”

Deronda could see Hans was putting on a false front, but he trusted his old friend’s usual frankness would come back if he laid his own heart bare first. “You laughed at how mysterious I was about that trip to Italy, Hans,” he began. “That trip touched the very roots of my happiness. I never knew anything about my parents growing up. I went to Genoa to meet my mother. My father died when I was an infant. My mother was the daughter of a great, respected Jewish scholar; my father was her cousin. I suspected the truth for a long time before I left, but I was still so glad to find it confirmed. I am glad to know I am a Jew.”

“You don’t have to look surprised that I already knew,” Hans said, shifting in his seat to pick at the heel of his slipper. “My mother heard it all from the Meyricks the day after you visited their house. Brother and sister both told her. We can’t celebrate it quite the way Ezra and Mirah do, you know. But whatever makes you happy, I’ll be happy for it too—eventually. I can’t promise exactly when that eventually will land.”

“I don’t expect you to understand how I feel about it,” Deronda said. “But I couldn’t let silence hang between us when this changes every part of my future. I’ve taken Mordecai’s hopes and ideas for our people to heart. I intend to spend the rest of my life working to make them real, as much as one man can. I’ll probably travel to the East soon, and be gone for years.”

Hans did not say a word. He stood up abruptly, grabbed his palette, and stood with his back to Deronda, pretending to work at his half-finished painting. The silence stretched out thick, and both men felt the discomfort hum in the air. “I hope you don’t mind me asking,” Hans said quietly, still not turning around. “Does Mrs. Grandcourt know all this?”

“No. And I beg you, Hans,” Deronda said, sharp with anger, “to stop making jokes about that woman. Every silly assumption you’ve ever had about me and her is the exact opposite of the truth. I have never once had, and I never will have, the position of a lover to Gwendolen Grandcourt.”

For a long minute, neither spoke. “Maybe I’ve been wrong about another assumption, too,” Hans said finally.

“What assumption?”

“That you had no wish to be a lover to another woman—one who is neither wife nor widow.”

“I don’t need to pretend I don’t know who you mean, Meyrick.” Deronda’s voice was soft now, but tight with feeling. “It hurts me that what we want clashes. Tell me, if you can, if you have any reason to think your feelings for her might be returned.”

“That’s a very superfluous question for you to ask, isn’t it?” Hans said, irritation flaring in his tone.

“Why superfluous?”

“Because you’re perfectly sure of the answer already. And you’ve had far better proof than I ever got to convince you of it.”

“I’ll be more frank with you than you’re being with me right now,” Deronda said, his own temper rising a little, even as he felt deeply sorry for Hans. “I have never had the tiniest scrap of proof that my feelings would be returned. I have almost no hope at all.”

Hans spun around to glance at his friend, then immediately turned his back on him again. “And even if there was no one else in the world to worry about,” Deronda went on, stung by the thought that Hans thought he was lying, “I can’t deliberately tell her how I feel. If she didn’t return my love, I would poison the only little peaceful, happy home she has. She and I are both tied to Ezra for the rest of our lives, we’d have to see each other every single day. If I forced that pain on her, I’d be no better than a cruel, thoughtless brute.”

“I never actually told her how I feel about her, either,” Hans said, like he was defending himself.

“Then we’re on equal ground, aren’t we? You have no reason to envy me.”

“Not a single bit,” Hans said, his voice thick with bitter irony. “You know how big my conceit is, you know I think I’m better than every one of your advantages.”

“I’m a nuisance to you, Meyrick. I hate that, but I can’t help it,” Deronda said, standing up. “After all we’ve talked about before, I owed you this explanation. I don’t see how any of my quiet hopes have hurt you. And they’re not going to do me any good, not right now, not with her father living under the same roof as her. Things could drag on for two whole years, and I’d never know how she feels about me. That’s the whole truth of it, Hans. Neither of us has wronged the other. We just have to make our peace with this quiet, silly rivalry over a hope that will probably never come to anything. Our friendship is strong enough to bear that, isn’t it?”

“No, it isn’t,” Hans burst out, throwing his brushes down hard on the table, shoving his hands in his coat pockets, and spinning around to face Deronda. Deronda stepped back, stunned by the fire in his old friend’s face. “Our friendship—my friendship—can’t bear the strain of me pretending to be an ungrateful, petty coward, grudging you the one thing that makes you happy. Because you’re the luckiest man alive. If Mirah loves anyone better than she loves her own brother, it’s you.”

Hans dropped back into his chair, staring up at Deronda, his face hard and raw with unhidden pain. A shock ran all the way through Deronda, so warm and bright it felt like sunlight breaking through a storm. “That’s a very kind lie to make up for me, Hans,” he said, his voice unsteady.

“I’m not in a kind mood at all,” Hans snapped. “I hated realizing it, when I first saw it clear as day, especially back when I thought your whole heart was tied up with that duchess. But now, blast you! You turn out to be in love exactly where you should be—with a Jew, and everything perfect, everything right.”

“Tell me what made you sure,” Deronda said, the joy he had never dared to dream of bubbling up so fast he could barely breathe around it.

“Don’t ask. My own mother saw it, plain as day. The long and short of it is that Mirah is jealous of the time you spend with Mrs. Grandcourt. You ought to tell her how you feel as soon as possible, get it all out in the open. There! I’ve said it. Now I have full permission to curse you for getting the very best luck any man could ever deserve.”

“God bless you, Hans,” Deronda said, holding out his hand. Hans took it, and wrung it hard, neither of them needing to say another word.

*“All thoughts, all passions, all delights, Whatever stirs this mortal frame, All are but ministers of Love, And feed his sacred flame.” —COLERIDGE*

Hans’s easy assurance that Mirah was quietly pining, unknowingly hurt by the unspoken space between her and Deronda, had lit a fire under the quiet, long-buried longing Deronda had carried for months. He set out for Ezra’s rooms that day resolved to be bold: no more hiding, no more lingering in limbo, he would ask for a moment alone with her, and lay his whole heart bare. If she returned his love, he knew he could face every obstacle ahead—least of all, the growing, unspoken threat of her father Lapidoth’s restlessness. As her betrothed, he would have the right, the gentle protective authority, to shield both Ezra and Mirah from whatever petty schemes or cruel humiliations the old man might dream up next.

Deronda would have been far more worried if he could see inside Lapidoth’s head that week. The quiet routine of the small flat, the soft restraints Ezra and Mirah had put on his old reckless habits, felt like a prison to him, only bearable because he was certain he’d soon find a way to weasel a large lump sum out of Deronda. His gambling itch burned under his skin every waking moment, weaving all his thoughts together into a web of schemes, and he’d been hunting for days to find where Mirah stashed her savings and her keys. But Mirah, sharp and practical, had kept her promise to Ezra perfectly: every penny beyond her immediate spending money was locked safe with Mrs. Meyrick, and Lapidoth was seething, trapped in the flat like a man in a carefully guarded asylum, given every comfort but no access to a single spare coin. He’d convinced himself taking any money from his own daughter, or from Deronda—who was practically family, after all—was no theft, no disgrace. The fact that no loose cash was within reach only made him hungrier, and he’d half-plotted to beg Deronda for a large sum to fund a “lucrative opportunity abroad,” claiming the whole family would be far happier without him hanging around. He was too intimidated by Deronda’s quiet, steady honor to ask outright, though, and kept putting the plan off day after day.

When Deronda walked in that afternoon, glowing bright with his secret, barely concealed joy, Lapidoth was at his wits’ end, and Deronda’s warm, easy friendliness only stoked his greedy schemes higher. He could barely pretend to pay attention to the men’s reading, so after a few minutes he slipped out to smoke and pace the square, leaving Deronda and Ezra to relax in their quiet work. Mirah was out, but Deronda knew she’d be back before he left, and every nerve in his body hummed with anticipation: he was sure when he saw her this time, he’d catch the same soft, hidden love in her face he’d only ever dared to dream of.

“This little room is far too hot for you, Ezra,” he said, pausing mid-read, fanning himself with his handkerchief. “The summer heat here is worse than Genoa’s, where you can sit in the shade of huge, cool rooms. I’m going to arrange for you to have a far nicer home now—you can’t argue, I’m the stronger half here.”

Ezra smiled back, quiet and soft. “I lack nothing but breath in here. You, who could be in a sprawling country estate with miles of green fields around you, must feel this like a tiny prison. But I will never ask you to leave.”

“Country would be banishment while you’re here,” Deronda laughed, strolling the short length of their double room, before he paused to tug off his tight cravat and the heavy, meaningful old ring he always wore, setting both down on the small side table behind Ezra, piled high with books and papers. “This is the happiest room in the whole world to me. Besides, I’m practicing for our eventual trip to the East—no stuffy cravats, no heavy rings allowed out there.”

They were soon absorbed again, Deronda reading a passage of rabbinical Hebrew while Ezra corrected him, their voices low, when Lapidoth slipped back inside and sat in the shadows at the back. His roving eyes landed immediately on the ring, glittering bright on the dark mahogany of the little table. All the half-formed schemes of asking Deronda for hundreds of pounds vanished for a second—here was a solid, easy sum, no begging required. He told himself it wasn’t theft, not really: Deronda was practically family, he’d have given Lapidoth far more than the ring’s worth if he’d asked. He told himself he’d just grab it, slip away, sell it, and be on a ship before anyone noticed, no arguments, no awkward pleas. Before he even had time to second-guess it, his fingers brushed the ring noiselessly, he slipped it into his pocket, grabbed his hat, and was out the front door, already hurrying toward the nearest pawnshop. Deronda and Ezra barely noticed he’d left.

Not long after, Mirah stepped through the door, her hat still pinned to her hair, her cheeks pink for no reason she could name, flustered by the bright, warm energy rolling off Deronda. “I only popped in to drop off Ezra’s new medicine,” she said, half flustered. “I need to head straight back to Mrs. Meyrick’s to fetch something.”

“Let me walk with you,” Deronda said urgently, standing to greet her. “The heat’s turned my brain to mush, I’m happy to get out a minute.”

Mirah nodded, blushing harder, that strange new softness in his eyes making her heart flutter as she poured out Ezra’s dose. Deronda stood for a second, caught up in thoughts of their walk, before he suddenly remembered he’d taken his cravat off. “Forgive my messy state,” he laughed, turning toward the side table. Then his voice broke, sharp with shock. “Good heavens—where has my ring gone?”

He dropped to his knees, patting the floor, shifting small pieces of furniture, searching every nook of the variegated carpet, certain it must have slipped off the table and rolled out of sight. But the color drained straight from Mirah’s face. She leaned in fast to whisper in Ezra’s ear. “Was my father in here just now?” Ezra nodded, his face falling, and they shared a single, terrible understanding.

Mirah darted out of the main room, checking his empty bedroom first, then the hook where his hat usually hung—bare. She stumbled back to the main doorway, hands clasped tight, knuckles white, staring out the window at the square, the hot burn of shame searing through her. Her own father had stolen from the man she adored, the man she’d secretly loved for years, and run off, leaving her to face this humiliation right in front of him.

But Deronda didn’t see shame. He stepped forward, gently wrapping both of his hands around her cold, clasped ones, his voice soft, reverent, like he was speaking a sacred truth. “Mirah, let us count him as our father, not just yours. There is no sorrow, no disgrace, no joy that I want to keep separate from you. I would rather take this grief, this burden of yours, for my own, than take the brightest, most perfect joy any other woman on earth could offer me. Don’t reject me—please. Let me share every part of your life. Marry me. I’ve hidden this love for so long, been too scared to speak. Let me prove to you, for the rest of our lives, that my love for you is whole and unshakable.”

Mirah didn’t leap straight to joy. At first she thought this was only his kindness, extended out of pity for her and Ezra. But then she looked up at his face, and saw the raw, glowing love there, unhidden at last, wrapping around her like sunlight. Warmth flooded her cheeks, and she couldn’t find the words to answer, no speech worthy of the gift he was giving her. She only leaned in, soft and trembling, and pressed a light, sweet kiss to his lips—her simple, perfect yes.

They stood there for long minutes, just holding hands, looking at each other, too full of quiet joy to move, until Mirah whispered, soft against the air between them: “Come. Let’s go in and tell Ezra. Let us comfort him, now that we have this good thing to share.”

*The human nature unto which I felt That I belonged, and reverenced with love, Was not a punctual presence, but a spirit Diffused through time and space, with aid derived Of evidence from monuments, erect, Prostrate, or leaning toward their common rest In earth, the widely scattered wreck sublime Of vanished nations.* — WORDSWORTH, *The Prelude*

By early October, Sir Hugo Mallinger’s long-planned autumn stay at his Diplow estate had spread soft, easy cheer across every corner of the neighborhood: from the gilded drawing rooms of Brackenshaw Hall to the neat, linen-draped shop parlors in Wanchester. A genial, open-hearted Liberal of old, well-lined lineage, Sir Hugo loved nothing more than to show himself affable to everyone around him, confident that reform would never erode that quiet, universal English fondness for the comfort of long-established, gentle rank. He turned Diplow into the most welcoming house in the shire, extending invitations to gray-haired Wanchester solicitors and fresh-faced young village curates alike, careful to curate his guest lists so no one ever felt they’d been lumped in with company that would make their invitation feel like a trivial afterthought. Easygoing Lord Brackenshaw would not have minded sharing a dinner table with Attorney Robinson, but Robinson would have been quietly stung to find himself seated beside a crowd of men he’d have seen as his peers rather than his social betters, and Sir Hugo knew the fine lines of local sentiment well enough to win widespread affection for himself while gifting others nothing but warm pleasure.

The rector of Pennicote, Mr. Gascoigne, found a reception at Diplow that was the polar opposite of the haughty, grudging tolerance he’d endured during the late Grandcourt’s reign. It was not that Sir Hugo particularly liked the rector: it was that he was determined to make a public show of friendship to him for the sake of the new Mrs. Grandcourt, whose quiet, unspoken suffering had roused his chivalric protectiveness more and more with every passing month. The main reason he cared for her so deeply was one he could not even bring himself to share fully with his own wife, Lady Mallinger — he would never betray one woman’s secret to another, no matter how loyal his spouse was, a rare delicacy that spoke volumes for his quiet good manners.

When Deronda got engaged to Mirah, he knew he owed Sir Hugo, the fatherly friend who had raised him, full disclosure of his new position and plans, and chose to break the news in a letter. He had more than a hunch the older man would feel at the very least dissatisfied, if not heart sore, at this sharp turn in his life’s path. Reading bad news on a page, rather than hearing it spoken aloud, spared the recipient the risk of blurting out a hot, impatient remark they would regret later — and Deronda dreaded that kind of fleeting verbal clash that could turn an otherwise forgivable hurt into something lasting and ugly.

Sir Hugo, though not completely caught off guard, was thoroughly vexed the second he finished the letter. His first instinct was to carry it straight to Lady Mallinger, who was certain to express the shocked distress he could then argue was unreasonable, splitting the weight of his discontent in two. Sure enough, when she wailed in astonishment that all Daniel’s brilliant talents, and the warm comfort of having him in their home, had led him to throw his lot in so completely with the Jewish people, the baronet was quick to soothe her.
“Oh nonsense, my dear! You can be sure Dan will never make a fool of himself. He’s got grand, wide-reaching views on Judaism — political ideas you can’t possibly follow. He’s far too sharp to ever sink under them.”

But when it came to the upcoming marriage, Lady Mallinger offered him no such easy counterbalance. The gentle, soft-spoken woman said without the slightest trace of bitterness that she’d never dreamed of such an outcome when she’d invited Mirah to sing at her musical soirees, and to give singing lessons to her youngest daughter Amabel. After a long, hesitant pause, she admitted the thought had crossed her mind that after a decent period of mourning, Daniel might marry Mrs. Grandcourt — it had always seemed so unlikely a coincidence that he’d happened to be in Genoa exactly when the late Grandcourt had drowned. Though she’d never been particularly fond of widows, she couldn’t help but murmur that that match would have been far better than Daniel tying his future entirely to the Jewish community.

Sir Hugo agreed so wholeheartedly that he could not dismiss the thought as a silly feminine fancy, and his ill humor at the destruction of his quiet, hopeful vision for Gwendolen had no outlet at all. He made Lady Mallinger swear not to breathe a word of the engagement to anyone, telling himself quietly: “If this is a cruel blow to that poor girl (meaning Gwendolen), the longer she goes without hearing it, the better, in her current fragile, nervous state. And she ought to hear it from Dan himself, not from gossiping third parties.” Between his prior knowledge and his own sharp guesswork, Sir Hugo had convinced himself he understood the full shape of the situation perfectly.

All the while, his family’s residence at Diplow let him keep up his gentle, fatherly attentions to Gwendolen, and Lady Mallinger, despite her small reservations about widows, was happy to help him look after her.

Gwendolen had moved back to Offendene, and had settled into her new home with a calm that far exceeded her poor mother’s most hopeful expectations. She was drifting through a soft, peaceful melancholy that came from renouncing all the old selfish demands she’d once made on life, and learning to treat every small ordinary good — even the loyalty of a stray dog that followed her on her garden walks — as a precious, unhoped-for gift. Who, after nearly falling into a lightless pit, would complain when they stepped out into sweet open air and bright daylight? She had begun to see every new day as a narrow escape, to greet the soft return of dawn and dusk, and far more than that, the star-like glimmer of a kind word or generous impulse cutting through the dark of her old guilt, as a small daily salvation that made even the hardest hardship feel bearable. Anyone who had ever looked inward and railed at their own failures as sharply as Hamlet did would understand that quiet, constant feeling of being rescued, and that was exactly the feeling wrapping around Gwendolen as she re-lived every terrible moment of her past: from the first alluring, false whispers of self-satisfaction that made her fight to throw off the hold of her conscience, to the last desperate, furious wave of hatred that had dragged her toward satisfying her worst impulses, while she’d sobbed and begged for the mercy of that very conscience she’d once abandoned. Now she clung to every single word Deronda had ever spoken to her about being saved from the worst of her own darkness, every phrase that had given her the strength to fight against the urge to sink into total self-loathing.

But she was buoyed even higher by the quiet certainty that she would see him soon. It never crossed her mind that his life might take him far from her: her desperate, all-consuming need for him blinded her to the separate, independent shape of his world, and she filled every corner of her imagined version of his life with his role as her guide and her savior. That passionate, egoistic habit of imagination is not unique to Gwendolen: we all tend to wrap even the people we love, even our idea of God, up in the narrow frame of what we need from them. The future she stepped toward, eager and unafraid, was one where she would spend every day growing into the better version of herself that he would hold out before her. Hadn’t he first appeared to her as a quiet corrective presence, a man she’d resented at first sight, and come to trust and love with every fiber of her being? She could not imagine that reliance ever ending: to her, it felt as steady as the ground beneath her feet, the only firm surface she could walk on without fear of falling.

It was not long before Deronda came to Diplow, far more conveniently located for quick trips back to London than the Abbey. He had been planning to take Ezra and Mirah to a mild, warm spot on the coast while he arranged a new home they could move into as husband and wife, where they could both care for Mirah’s brother together. But Ezra had begged not to be moved anywhere, unless it was to join them on a voyage to the East. All small social demands were growing more of a burden to him every day, but his mind clung to the vision of that journey with a wild, unearthly joy. Deronda, hoping to marry no later than two months out, needed to sit down with Sir Hugo to go over his full resources and arrange all his affairs, and that gave him a reason not to delay his visit. But he had an equally pressing reason to come: his promise to Gwendolen.

His own joy with Mirah carried a sharp, aching edge of anxiety he could not shake, a paradox no romantic trope of the perfectly happy lover could erase. Human experience is always full of such incongruities, after all, and Deronda’s care for Gwendolen was no betrayal of Mirah: it was the very fullness of the nature that made his love for Mirah worth having. For what is love, even for the person you hold dearest? It is an enfolding of endless, tender cares that are far more precious than any trivial, selfish joy you could ever know apart from them.

Deronda visited Diplow twice, and saw Gwendolen both times — but he left for London without ever uttering a word about his engagement, or the massive shift it was making in his future. He blamed himself bitterly, but every heavy conversation that risks inflicting pain seems to wait for the exact right turn of phrase, the exact right mood from the other person, to fall gently. The first time they met, Gwendolen was so full of things she needed to tell him, brimming with questions about how she ought to arrange her life, what she could learn to make herself less ignorant, how she could be kinder to everyone around her, how she could make amends for all her old selfishness and strip that flaw out of her for good, that Deronda could not bear to set aside her immediate, urgent need for his guidance just to talk about his own life, much less drive a sharp wound into her when she was leaning on him so hard for strength. The second time he arrived fully resolved to steer their conversation toward a soft, gradual lead-up to the news, he found her sunk in a deep, crushing depression, overwhelmed by cruel, vivid old memories that felt far more real and tangible than any new, hopeful future she could try to build. She sobbed hysterically, over and over, that he would always despise her, and he could do nothing but speak soft, soothing words of encouragement. When she lifted her head at last, her lashes still beaded with tears, that soft, childlike, curious look back on her face, it was impossible to lay another heavy burden on her shoulders.

As the days slipped by, he knew it was a pressing, unavoidable duty to tell her the truth. Gwendolen had never once thought to ask about his personal affairs, it had never even crossed her mind to wonder why he’d been in Genoa that fateful day — and that total, unknowing innocence would make a sudden revelation that determined the whole shape of his future hit her twice as hard. If he let someone else break the news to her, she would feel he’d treated her with cruel, unthinking disregard. He could not bear to write it in a letter: his tenderness could not stand the thought of her reading his virtual farewell alone, in an empty room, imagining his words full of cold, giddy joy for his own happiness and total indifference to her pain. He set off for Diplow a third time, determined that no amount of discomfort or risk would make him turn back before he told her the truth.

This third time, Deronda found Hans Meyrick installed at Diplow, his easel set up in the drawing room, working on a Gainsborough-style portrait of Sir Hugo’s three daughters sitting on a grassy bank. He broke from painting to ramble over to Pennicote to sketch the village children, and had already grown quite friendly with the Gascoigne family. Hans seemed to have recovered all his old bright vivacity, but Deronda could see it was a little forced, the same way you can spot artificial blush on a lady’s cheek when it sits just a little too bright and unchanging. For all his deep grateful friendship and admiration for Deronda, Hans could not help a flicker of sharp irritation: open, impulsive natures like his always feel a little stung when a friend’s long-held secret comes out, and it turns out to be the exact opposite of what you’d hoped and guessed. For months he’d babbled freely to Deronda about his own crush on Mirah, never once stopping to wonder if Deronda might have feelings of his own, and the realization that Deronda had hidden his quiet rivalry all this time felt like a sharp, unexpected slap. Hans, to his credit, had not let that sore feeling change his behavior toward his fortunate friend — he’d told himself proudly that his own character had rung far truer than he’d ever expected it to, given that he’d always joked he was a dilettante in matters of virtue. But the little edge in his chat remained, and he was so wrapped up in the giddy joy of Deronda’s engagement that he had no idea of the weight of anxiety Deronda carried for Gwendolen, and spoke of her completely without hesitation.

“Hans, when did you get down here?” Deronda asked, joining him in the grounds as he sketched the bank and trees for his portrait background.

“Oh, ten days ago, before Sir Hugo even expected me! I ran down with Rex Gascoigne and stayed a couple of nights at the rectory. I’m up to my ears in all the local gossip now — I know exactly what the wheelwright’s parlor looks like, and I even sat through the infant school exam. Anna Gascoigne, the one with the sweet gentle upper lip, escorted me, else three barefoot urchins and the village idiot would have mobbed me for my long hair and generally un-Pennicote appearance. The whole village is pure idyll, the only flaw being a broad-shouldered, dark-cloaked curate who should have been selling heavy drapery instead of preaching. The Gascoignes are perfect — related to that Vandyke duchess, you know. I caught a glimpse of her, all in black robes, from a distance the other day, though she doesn’t receive visitors.”

“She wasn’t staying at the rectory?” Deronda asked.

“No, but they took me over to Offendene to tour the old house, and naturally I met the duchess and her whole family. I assume you’ve been over there, you know all their business?”

“Yes, I’ve been there,” Deronda said, quiet and even.

“Fine old place, perfect setting for a widow with romantic, dramatic fortunes. I think I’ve worked out there was once a little romance between her and my friend Rex, too.”

“Wait — before she married Grandcourt?” Deronda said, suddenly very interested. “They’d only been at Offendene a year before the wedding. How did you figure that out?”

“Oh, I know what it’s like to be miserable, so I’ve got a sharp eye for the signs of unhappiness in other people. Rex never sets foot on Offendene property, hasn’t seen the duchess once since she came back widowed. And Miss Gascoigne let a tiny little detail slip when we were talking about old charade nights — I was hamming up all my old nonsense to amuse the younger kids — that made it perfectly clear Rex was once hovering around his fair cousin close enough to get properly singed. I don’t know what she felt about it. I suppose the duke swept in and carried her off. That’s always the way it goes for the exceptionally good young men, right? Now I see why Gascoigne’s always talking about making the law his mistress and staying a bachelor forever. But those are young man’s resolutions. Since the late duke didn’t drown for your sake, maybe it turns out he drowned for my friend Rex’s. Who knows?”

“Is it absolutely necessary that Mrs. Grandcourt remarry at all?” Deronda said, already ready to point out that Hans’s track record for inventing love lives for other people didn’t give him the right to plot Gwendolen’s future.

“You monster!” Hans laughed, throwing his sketch pencil down playfully. “Do you want her to wear mourning weeds for you for the rest of her life, burn herself on a perpetual suttee pyre while you’re alive and happy somewhere else?”

Deronda said nothing, but he looked so sharply pained that Hans quickly changed the subject, and when he was alone he shrugged to himself, thinking there really must have been something more serious going on between Deronda and the widowed duchess than Mirah would ever be happy to hear about. “Why didn’t she fall in love with me?” he thought, laughing at his own folly. “She’d have had no rivals. No woman has ever wanted to sit and discuss theology with me.”

No wonder Deronda flinched under that light, lashing joke. It touched on sensibilities already quivering in anticipation of the pain he knew he was about to inflict, the kind of remark that makes the thing you’re already dreading feel twice as real, when someone else voices it out loud. But he had come down this time fully resolved not to evade the trial any longer. The next day, he mounted his horse and rode for Offendene. He had sent word ahead that he planned to call, and ask if she could receive him, and he found her waiting for him in the old drawing room, the very room where so many of the most searing crises of her life had unfolded.

She looked far less sad than he’d seen her since her husband’s death. There was no smile on her face, but a quiet, steady self-possession, a world away from the sobbing, despairing mood he’d left her in last time. She noticed the quiet sadness etched into his face right away, and no sooner had they sat down, him across the room from her at a small distance, than she spoke.
“You were afraid to come see me last time, because I was so full of grief and despair. But I’m not like that today. I’ve been sorry for it ever since. I’ve made it a point to keep my hope up, to be as cheerful as I can, so I never give you pain on my account again.”

The unwonted soft sweetness in her voice and her face at that moment made the cruel task ahead of Deronda feel sharper than a knife. But he knew he had to start.
“I *am* in some trouble today,” he said, looking at her with deep, mournful tenderness. “It’s because

The seasons of human experience blur soft as they did in the oldest golden tales: fruit swells heavy on boughs still thick with blossom, the reaper swings his sickle even as another scatters seed, one tends unripe green clusters while another presses grapes into wine. All our lives, harvest and spring twine together, until that great unseen hand gathers us up to sow us new in fields no eye can trace. There is no sweeter gift in love than the quiet, glowing certainty that by weaving the one you love into your life, you can shield their joy, stitch soft comfort over old hardship, and pry open fountains of bright sweet joy over memories of hunger and grief.
Daniel Deronda’s love for Mirah hummed with that exact blessed protectiveness. Even as a small child, her feet had stumbled through brambles, and the very first time he saw her face, it had worn the raw, hollowed look of a girl who had all but given up on hope. Now she bloomed like a delicate flower with dark petal tips and soft ivory-hued skin, warmed through by the sunlight of quiet content. Any grief she could imagine no longer felt like something to fear—it was just a thread in the life she shared with Deronda, a life she could name nothing but entirely good. He watched her slow, happy movements, her soft still poses of rest, with a delight so deep he thought to himself that this was more than enough personal joy for him: to keep her safe from all pain.
Mirah knew nothing of Hans’s quiet, bitter struggle with unrequited love, nothing of the sharp lingering heartache Gwendolen carried for Deronda. Once she learned the secret love Deronda had hidden all this time was for her, she easily wrote off Gwendolen’s old frantic concern for him as the same grateful dependence she had felt, when he had pulled her back from the edge of despair. All Deronda’s few, distant references to Mrs. Grandcourt only reinforced that gentle view of their bond, and Mirah was fully ready to believe he had acted as a rescuing angel for dozens of lost souls just like her. The only miracle left, as far as she was concerned, was that out of all those people, she got the gift of being at his side for every day of the rest of their lives.
So when the bridal veil draped over Mirah’s head, it hid no shaky, doubtful tremors—only a soft, awed thrill, like she was holding a great sacred gift she had been tasked to use well. No velvet wedding canopy ever sheltered a more fair, kind-hearted bride and groom, or one their gathered loved ones could have wished more deeply full, beautiful lives for. No truer lips ever touched the sweet ceremonial marriage wine, no wedding blessing ever landed on a couple more certain to keep every word of their pledge, bound together by perfect, unbroken integrity.
Naturally, they were married under the traditional Jewish rite. No faith has ever demanded that wedding feasts only invite the highest, most noble acquaintances, and so no one should take offense that Deronda’s small, warm wedding feast counted the entire Cohen family among its guests—save for the youngest teething baby, who was fussed over at home by a neighbor. Mordecai would never have allowed the friends who had stood by him through his years of poverty to be barred from sharing his joy. Mrs. Meyrick had long since set aside her old quiet misgivings about the Jewish pawnbroker and his household, and arrived with her three daughters, all of them glowing with the warm knowledge that Mirah’s marriage to Deronda capped a romance they had all gotten to be a gentle, generous part of, a memory they would hold dear for the rest of their lives. Not one of the Meyricks, mother or daughters, had held back from giving Mirah their care and love when she needed it most. They all agreed it was a shame Hans could not be there, but Mab joked that men had no monopoly on silly, unruly hearts—if all four of the Meyrick women had fallen for Deronda, they would never have been so ridiculous as to ruin Mirah’s happy day.
Their willingness to set aside silly prejudice was rewarded when Mr. Cohen gave a speech unlike any standard, stuffy wedding toast, full of rough warm heart. Jacob Cohen ate enough cake for two boys his age, and let out a series of soft, proud snickers through his father’s speech, giddy with the knowledge his family was the center of a little bit of glory, while little Adelaide Rebekah, dressed in a spotless new Sabbath frock, sat through the whole feast with a grave, responsible air, as if she had been tasked to oversee every small part of the celebration. Mordecai’s brilliant eyes, sunken deep in their sockets, drifted over the whole scene with the gentle cherishing warmth of a spirit already half-lifted beyond the mortal world, freed from all selfish small needs but still clinging to every bit of soft, living sympathy. Again and again, his gaze would circle the room before settling back on Deronda, bright with a fresh flicker of that deep, trusting affection he had nurtured for years.
The feast was humble, but Mirah received no shortage of stunning, thoughtful wedding gifts. As soon as their betrothal had been announced, friends had rallied to find thoughtful, beautiful tokens of their joy. Sir Hugo and Lady Mallinger had put great care into putting together a full, perfect set of supplies for their planned travels East, plus a precious carved locket inscribed: *To the bride of our dear Daniel Deronda, all blessings. H. and L. M.* The Klesmers sent a dainty, perfectly crafted engraved watch. But the gift far more precious than gold or jewels arrived for Deronda on the very morning of his wedding, sent from a home near Diplow. It was a short letter, in Gwendolen’s familiar slanted handwriting:
Do not grieve for me on your wedding day. I have remembered your words— that I may live long enough to become one of those good women who make everyone around them glad they were born. I do not see yet how that can come to pass, but you know better than I. If it ever does, it will be all because you helped me. I was selfish once, I only thought of my own pain, and I made you grieve. The thought of your grief still hurts me now. You do not need to grieve for me ever again. It will be better, it shall be better for me, because I knew you.
Preparations for all three of them to depart for the East began immediately. Deronda could not deny Ezra’s wish to set sail right away, so he could travel with them, rather than make them delay their journey to stay and watch over him. He held no illusions that Ezra’s weak body would survive the long voyage; the clear signs of his illness’s final stage had already set in. But Ezra had only smiled and said, “It does not matter where I die, so long as I am with you.”
He never made it to the ship. One early morning, he roused Deronda and Mirah and said softly, “Do not leave my side today. I will be gone before the sun sets.” He insisted they dress him and settle him in his favorite easy chair, Deronda on one side, Mirah on the other. For hours he was unusually quiet, not even making the effort to speak, only glancing at them every now and then with eyes full of a soft, restful meaning, as if to assure them that even when his breath came hard, he floated on an ocean of deep unshakable peace beneath him.
It was not until late afternoon, when the golden light was fading low through the windows, that he took one of each of their hands in his own, looked straight at Deronda, and said, “Death is coming to me like a divine kiss—one that is both a parting and a reunion, that takes me from your sight and lets me live fully in your soul. Wherever you go, Daniel, I shall go. Has it not already begun? Have I not breathed my whole soul into you? We shall live together, always.”
He paused, and Deronda waited, thinking there might be one last gentle word for him. Slow, with great effort, Ezra pressed their hands tight, raised himself a little in his chair, and spoke the ancient Hebrew confession of the Divine Unity that has rested on the lips of dying Israelites for hundreds of generations. He sank gently back into his chair, and never spoke again. It was several more hours before his breath stopped, all the while wrapped safe in the circle of Mirah and Deronda’s arms. There was nothing there for tears, nothing to wail or strike the breast over—no weakness, no blame, no dispraise. Only good, only fair, and all the quiet soft peace that fits such a noble, gentle death.
